Catalog
Toh 4365 (Tōhoku catalogue; also cited as D 4365 of the Derge edition) · Derge Tengyur · Miscellaneous (sna tshogs) · Commentary / Treatise (Śāstra)

About this text

The Commentary on the Ascertainment of Meaning, known in Tibetan as Dön nampar dön mi zawé drelpa and in Sanskrit, judging from its title, as a commentary on the Arthaviniścaya, is an Indian treatise explaining the Ascertainment of Meaning Sūtra, a compact scripture in which the Buddha sets out the core categories of the Dharma, such as the aggregates, the elements, the sense bases, dependent origination, the truths and the path. Opening with homage to the noble Mañjuśrī, the commentator follows the classical Indian method for expounding a sūtra, first setting forth its purpose, connection, setting, occasion, condensed meaning and the meaning of its words, and then working through the sūtra phrase by phrase, drawing throughout on Abhidharma analysis. The commentary closes with the sūtra's own conclusion, explaining the miracle of teaching and how five hundred monks were liberated from the defilements on hearing this discourse. It is Toh 4365 in the Tengyur, where it belongs among the commentarial treatises preserved in Tibetan translation.

Translation

Commentary on the Ascertainment of Meaning (Arthaviniścaya).

Homage to the noble Mañjuśrī.

Paying homage to him, the meaning
Of the sūtra I shall explain clearly.

Now, one who wishes to explain a sūtra must first set forth its purpose, its connection, its setting, its occasion, its condensed meaning, and the meaning of its words: these six kinds of topic must be taught. For otherwise, unless students see the ascertainment of the sūtra's meaning through the three kinds of wisdom, that of hearing, of reflecting, of retaining and so on, those whose wisdom is led by their intellect will think it pointless, like examining a crow's teeth, and will not engage with it at all. That is why it is said that the purpose is explained first. If one asks what the purpose is: it is taught in order that one become skilled in discerning the characteristics of phenomena. And since listeners then apply themselves respectfully to hearing, retaining and the rest, it is said:

Therefore the purpose is explained first.

It is explained so that one may properly discern the meaning of this Dharma discourse, the Ascertainment of Meaning, from beginning to end, and the particular characteristics it sets out; that is the purpose. If someone objects that skill in discerning the characteristics of phenomena could come about by other means as well, that is not so. By other means one cannot become skilled in discerning the workings of this Dharma from beginning to end. Therefore the means of becoming skilled in discerning the characteristics of phenomena is to teach the meaning of the Blessed One's sūtra. Thus, in order to show the result of coming to know the characteristics of phenomena through hearing, reflecting, retaining and the rest, it is said that the purpose of the purpose is explained. And what is the purpose of teaching and explaining in this way? If one is skilled in discerning the characteristics of phenomena, one knows what is to be adopted and what is to be rejected, and so, by taking up what helps and giving up what harms, one comes to abandon the afflictions. Once the afflictions are abandoned, one attains the state of nirvāṇa. This is the purpose of the purpose.

Next, if the connection were not stated, people might think the text is connected with nothing at all, like "five pomegranates and six cakes"; or they might think it is like the precious jewel on the head of the nāga king Takṣaka, which dispels every epidemic, yet there is no way to obtain it. Those whose thinking goes first would then not be willing to engage with it. To make this clear, the connection is explained. It is threefold: the connection of words and meaning, the connection of meaning and practice, and the connection of practice and result. The connection of words and meaning is this: through words such as "What are the five aggregates?", one comes to understand what was explained above, in which all phenomena are summed up through the twenty-six scriptural topics of the sūtra. The connection of meaning and practice is this: seeing the supremely excellent meaning of the sūtra, students feel a special joy, and if one asks whether this is the principal thing to be practiced, it is because they then practice it accordingly. The connection of practice and result is this: if one practices the meaning just as one has understood it, one attains the result, nirvāṇa, whose characteristic is the abandonment of all afflictions.

Next, the setting is the cause from which the sūtra arises. It is taught by the words beginning "Thus did I hear at one time" and ending "he was staying together with twelve hundred and fifty monks." The occasion is what is spoken, fitted to the circumstances, in order to explain the sūtra; that is, it is speaking when the occasion has arrived. For if one were to speak when the occasion had not arrived, it would be like the words of a madman. So speaking fitted to the circumstances, for the sake of what one is to make known, is the occasion. It is taught by the words beginning "At that time the Blessed One" and ending "those monks listened to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One spoke to them as follows." The condensed meaning is explained so that students may easily understand the meaning and the words, and may retain them easily. It is taught by the words beginning "He spoke thus to the monks: What is the Dharma discourse called the Ascertainment of Meaning?" and ending "Monks, this is the scripture of the Dharma discourse of the Ascertainment of Meaning." The meaning of the words is the detailed explanation of the condensed meaning and of the meaning of each individual word. It runs from the words "Monks, what are the five aggregates?" up to the end of the sūtra.

Now I shall explain by analyzing the words of the setting. The setting is fourfold: the perfect time, the perfect teacher, the perfect place, and the perfect assembly.

Among these, the word "thus" applies to many meanings: introducing an example, imparting a reading, settling a question, assenting, and replying to words. Introducing an example is as in "rich like one at ease, brave like a lion, hard like a vajra." Introducing the imparting of a reading is as in "Read it thus!" Introducing the settling of a question is as when someone asks, "What did you hear?" and one replies, "Thus did I hear, and I shall explain it in that way." Assenting is as when someone requests, "Please do it in this way," and one replies, "It shall be done so." A reply to words is as when someone says, "Is this what you heard?" and one replies, "It is so." In this context, by way of analysis, it should be understood as introducing assent. How so? When the noble Kāśyapa and the other five hundred arhats undertook the compiling of the sūtras, the other arhats requested of the noble Nanda, "Please recite it just as you heard it," and he said: "Thus did I hear from the Blessed One, and I shall recite it just as I heard it." It is because he assented in this way.

By the words "did I," others are excluded and direct hearing is shown. The meaning is: "I heard this sūtra directly from the Blessed One; it was not passed down from one hearer to another." "Heard" means that he grasped the meaning and the words without error, by an auditory consciousness possessed of a fully pure ear faculty. That shows that there was nothing he failed to understand.

Why is that? Because when the noble Nanda heard this sūtra directly from the Blessed One, the noble Nanda had not yet realized the truths; he saw the truths only after the Blessed One had passed into nirvāṇa.

"At one time": the expression "at one time" means both time and meeting, and so it is taken in two senses. Taken as time, it is joined with the words "did I hear," and the meaning is: "Since this sūtra is hard to hear, I heard it once, at one time." Taken as meeting, the words "at one time" are joined with "the Blessed One" that follows, and the meaning is: "As rare as a turtle's neck meeting the hole of a yoke, I found a human body and leisure, so hard to find, and I met the appearance of a buddha, so hard to meet, and so I heard this Dharma in Śrāvastī and elsewhere." Alternatively, it shows that the noble Nanda himself was very learned: "I heard this sūtra once, at one time; at other times I heard other sūtras too." And it shows that the Blessed One himself continually accomplished immeasurable benefit for beings: "At this time the Blessed One stayed in this place and taught this Dharma; at other times he stayed elsewhere and taught other Dharmas too." By this, the perfect time is taught.

Next, the word "Blessed One" is taken in three senses: as "one who has conquered," as "one who possesses glory," and as "one who possesses fortune." It is taken as "one who has conquered" because he has conquered the four māras. The four māras are the māra of the divine sons (devaputramāra), the māra of the afflictions, the māra of the lord of death, and the māra of the aggregates. The māra of the divine sons he conquered when, about to awaken fully, he sat on the vajra seat. Though he was assailed in many harmful ways by the weapons, mountains and so forth of the many māras who are divine sons of the desire realm, by the great power of his loving-kindness, compassion, emptiness and the rest he turned even those weapons into flowers; and having subdued them, he conquered them when he became greatly renowned as "the omniscient one, the person without equal." The māra of the afflictions he conquered thus: having conquered the māra of the divine sons in the earlier watch, in the later watch, of the two kinds of affliction, those to be abandoned by the path of seeing and those to be abandoned by the path of meditation, which are set out in nine degrees as great, middling and small, the vajra-like wisdom arose; and because the least of the small afflictions was overcome by the greatest of the great wisdom, he conquered them. Therefore two of the māras are explained as conquered while he sat on the vajra-like seat.

The māra of the lord of death he conquered in the city of Kuśinagara. By the nature of things, buddhas give up one fifth of the human lifespan. Śākyamuni too, having remained in the world for eighty-four years, was by right to pass into nirvāṇa. The other beings who had the fortune to be trained by the Buddha he had already liberated by many means. But when he looked, at the very point of showing his passing into nirvāṇa, he saw that the brahmin called Subhadra, one of those fortunate enough to be trained, had not yet been trained. So he postponed it by three months; delaying three months past the time, it was as though he did not pass into nirvāṇa at once. The māra of the aggregates he also conquered in that same city of Kuśinagara; he conquered it when he relinquished the five aggregates, form and the rest, without remainder. Therefore two of the māras are explained as conquered in the city of Kuśinagara.

The word "Blessed One" is taken as "one who possesses glory" because he possesses six qualities. The six qualities are as it is said:

Perfect lordship,
Form, fame and glory,
Likewise wisdom and effort:
These six are what is called "bhaga."

Here, perfect lordship is because he possesses the perfect riches of the world and of what transcends the world. Perfect form is because his body bears the thirty-two marks of a great person and the eighty excellent minor marks. Perfect fame is because he is renowned throughout every world-realm as possessing perfect qualities. Perfect glory is because, like the wish-fulfilling tree and the wish-fulfilling jewel, he fulfills the wishes of all beings. Perfect wisdom is because he has the wisdom that knows all phenomena without obstruction and without attachment. Perfect effort is because he strives without ever giving up his great diligence for the benefit and happiness of all beings.

The word "Blessed One" is taken as "one who possesses fortune" because he possesses all the dharmas of the thirty-seven factors of awakening: those equal in sets of four, like the four applications of mindfulness and the four right abandonments, and those equal in sets of five, like the five faculties and the five powers. This teaches the perfect teacher.

"Śrāvastī": it is so called because it was made the dwelling of those who go to the abode of the sage named Śrāvasti. The city too is called Śrāvastī, and the particular region marked by that city is also called Śrāvastī. Alternatively, this city is the dwelling of beings who, produced by the accumulations of merit and wisdom, are all endowed with many good qualities, and so it is a city of perfect excellence and wealth. Because it is renowned as a place where one can hear with the ear, and where everything one hears of exists, the name of the city is known as Śrāvastī, "Where All Is to Be Heard." How is that shown? Because it was renowned that everything was to be had there, a certain man entered the city asking, "Is there anyone here who sells human eyes, or not?" Then a deity living in the city changed its own body into a human form and came before him to sell a basin full of freshly extracted human eyes, still quivering. That is the kind of story told. The city of Śrāvastī is very large, and so one would not know in which part of it he was staying. Therefore "the Eastern Park" is mentioned.

"Park" refers to an area of gardens. Their ground is level and even, they are adorned with many lovely things such as flowers, and they are well situated and beautiful in every way; and since beings, all creatures, delight in them, they are called "parks," places of delight. Because that park lies in the eastern part of the city of Śrāvastī, it is called "the Eastern Park." Yet in the eastern quarter of the city of Śrāvastī there are also many parks, so one would not know in which of them he was staying. Therefore it is said specifically, "in the mansion of Mṛgāramātā." Here "Mṛgāramātā" is the name of a laywoman who had seen the truths and entered the path of stream-entry, and the mansion is one she built. As for the meaning of the word "mansion" (prāsāda): it is called a mansion because seeing it makes beings' minds clear and glad (prasanna); this is by way of etymology.

The two places, the Eastern Park of Śrāvastī and the dwelling of Mṛgāramātā, are mentioned in order to benefit both groups, those who have gone forth and those who live in the household. By mentioning the park he benefits those who have gone forth; by mentioning the dwelling of Mṛgāramātā in Śrāvastī he benefits the group who live in the household. Here the perfect place is taught.

Lest one think the Blessed One was staying all alone, to show that this was not so, the words "with a great Saṅgha of monks" and so on are spoken. These words teach five distinctions: being alike in the prātimokṣa vows, being undivided by Māra and the like, possessing distinguished qualities, having destroyed the afflictions, and being many in number.

Because they are alike in the prātimokṣa vows, the first word, "of monks," is stated: they are monks because every one of them was fully ordained through the formal act of motion and resolution and abides in the prātimokṣa vows. The word "Saṅgha" shows that they cannot be divided by Māra and the like; they are a Saṅgha because no one else can turn them from their view, their moral discipline and the rest. The word "great" shows that they possess distinguished qualities: they possess the five qualities of having done what was to be done, having completed their task, having laid down the burden, being thoroughbreds, and having attained their own aim, as well as qualities such as the six superknowledges. The later word "monks" shows that they have abandoned the afflictions: because they have destroyed and abandoned afflictions such as attachment, they are monks in the ultimate sense.

The words "twelve hundred and fifty" show that they were many in number; it shows that when this Sūtra of the Ascertainment of Meaning was taught, there were this many monks in the assembly. The number is spoken of as fixed at that figure because the Tathāgata ordained Uruvilvā Kāśyapa together with his following of five hundred; then Gayā Kāśyapa with his following of two hundred and fifty; after that Nadī Kāśyapa with his following of two hundred and fifty; and then he trained Śāriputra and Maudgalyāyana together with their following of two hundred and fifty, and having ordained them, they always formed the Blessed One's retinue. Therefore the fixed number is mentioned.

As for "was staying," there are three kinds of staying: staying in a posture, staying while teaching the Dharma, and staying in inner absorption. Staying in a posture means sitting, standing, lying down and walking. Staying while teaching the Dharma means proclaiming the meaning of the scriptures within the assembly. Staying in inner absorption means being absorbed in the four immeasurables, loving-kindness, compassion and the rest, or abiding absorbed in emptiness, signlessness, wishlessness and so on. Staying in a posture is the activity of body; staying while teaching the Dharma is the activity of speech; staying in inner absorption is the activity of mind. In this context, it should be seen as staying while teaching the Dharma.

The perfect setting is complete.

With regard to the occasion, the words "Then the Blessed One" and so on are spoken. "Then" means in Śrāvastī, in the Eastern Park, in the dwelling of Mṛgāramātā. Alternatively, "then" serves to lead into the words, or to set down the words. The meaning of "the Blessed One" is as before. Since the Blessed One is the one who explains and the monks are those who listen, it is construed as "the Blessed One addressed the monks." With what kind of speech did he address them? For that reason it is said, "with a voice like Brahmā's, deep like a cloud, sweet and vast." It is the voice of Brahmā because, being exceedingly sweet and gentle like the song of the kalaviṅka bird, it delights the beings to be trained. Because that voice travels far and pervades the whole assembly, it is called "deep like a cloud." Because that voice is the voice of Brahmā, at the time of listening it satisfies the minds of beings and draws their hearts, and so it is sweet. Because that voice pervades the whole assembly like a cloud, so that it is heard alike near and far, it is called vast. With a voice possessing such distinguished qualities he addressed the monks.

"Monks, to you" means "to you who have become disciples who listen." As for "I shall teach the Dharma": the Dharma is the twelve branches of scripture, the sūtras and the rest. Why are they called Dharma? They are called Dharma because they show its kinds. "I shall teach" means "I shall explain."

Lest one wonder by what name the Dharma he was to teach is called, he therefore says, "namely, the Dharma discourse called the Ascertainment of Meaning." "Namely" means "that is to say." Within this Dharma, the ascertainment and settling of the meaning of the dharmas to be understood, to be abandoned, to be cultivated and to be attained is shown; and since beings, on hearing it, come to understand those meanings precisely as settled, this sūtra is therefore called the Dharma discourse of the Ascertainment of Meaning. "Dharma discourse" means a collection of dharmas, a body of Dharma, many dharmas.

Dharma, moreover, is of two kinds: the Dharma to be explained and the Dharma to be realized, and he shows that both these kinds of Dharma are explained within this text.

To show the distinctions and qualities of the Dharma to be explained, he says, "good in the beginning, good in the middle, good in the end." It is called "good in the beginning" because it teaches the training in higher moral discipline. The training in higher moral discipline is as stated in the sūtras: "He is endowed with moral discipline in this way: he is restrained by the prātimokṣa vows, perfect in conduct and in his sphere of activity, sees danger in even the slightest fault, and, having properly taken them up, trains in the points of training."

It is good in the middle because it teaches the training in higher mind. The training in higher mind, too, is as stated in the sūtras: "What are the four meditative concentrations? Secluded from desires, secluded from evil and unwholesome dharmas, with thought and with examination, possessed of the joy and bliss born of seclusion, he enters and abides in the first meditative concentration. Through the stilling of thought and examination, with inner clarity and the mind become one-pointed, without thought and without examination, possessed of the joy and bliss born of samādhi, he enters and abides in the second meditative concentration. Free from attachment to joy, he abides in equanimity, mindful and vigilant, and experiences bliss with the body; that of which the noble ones say, 'equanimous, mindful, abiding in bliss,' he enters and abides in that third meditative concentration. Having abandoned bliss and abandoned suffering as well, with mental pleasure and mental displeasure both having already subsided, he enters and abides in the fourth meditative concentration, neither bliss nor suffering, with equanimity and mindfulness fully purified."

It is called "good in the end" because it teaches the training in higher wisdom. The training in higher wisdom, too, is as stated in the sūtra: "He knows correctly, just as it is: 'This is the noble truth of suffering.' He knows correctly, just as it is: 'This is the noble truth of the origin of suffering; this is the noble truth of the cessation of suffering; this is the noble truth of the path leading to the cessation of suffering.' When he sees in this way, his mind is liberated from the defilement of desire, and his mind is liberated from the defilement of existence and the defilement of ignorance. When liberated, there arises the wisdom-vision that he is liberated."

What then is this Dharma that is good at all three times like? For this reason he says, "excellent in meaning, excellent in wording." It is excellent in meaning because the meaning it expresses is unmistaken and supreme, perfect in meaning. "Wording" refers to the expressive words made of letters such as a and ka; it is excellent in wording because its words are clear and make the meaning quickly understood. This, in short, teaches that the characteristics of the Dharma that is explained are excellent.

And what does a Dharma of this kind accomplish and teach? For this reason he says, "it teaches the pure holy life." Nirvāṇa with remainder of the aggregates and nirvāṇa without remainder are called "brahma." How is it evident that nirvāṇa is called brahma? In the sūtras too, speaking of nirvāṇa, it is said: "It is peace, it has become peace; it is brahma, it has become brahma; it is coolness, it has become coolness." The Dharma is called the holy life (brahmacarya) because it has the characteristic of realizing the noble path that attains such a nirvāṇa. Since such a noble path is supreme among all conditioned dharmas, this teaches the Dharma whose characteristic is ultimate, the Dharma to be realized.

What is that Dharma of ultimate characteristic like? To show its distinctive qualities he says, "unique," and so on. The Dharma of realizing the noble path does not exist in the treatises of others, such as the tīrthikas; it is not shared with others and exists only among the followers of this Dharma, and so it is called "unique." That noble path is called "complete" because it is the antidote to the afflictions of all the realms, the desire realm, the form realm and the formless realm. Since the worldly path does not act as an antidote to the afflictions of the Peak of Existence, it is not called complete. That noble path is liberated because it is liberated by nature; since by nature it is free of afflictions, it is called "pure." That noble path arises from the mental continuum of those whose defilements are exhausted, and because it is purified through the purity of that continuum, it is called "utterly white."

Alternatively, it is good in the beginning because, when one hears it, it gives rise to great faith; good in the middle because, when one reflects on it, it gives rise to great joy; good in the end because, when one meditates on it, it gives rise to great understanding. It is excellent in meaning because the aims of the higher realms and of nirvāṇa come from it. It is excellent in wording because the words that explain it are clear, precise and unmistaken.

Alternatively, the words "good in the beginning" and so on are spoken in order to show the greatness of the nature of the Buddha's Dharma as the antidote to the dharmas of outsiders such as the tīrthikas. For the dharmas of the tīrthikas and the like contain contradictions between what is said earlier and later, and so their dharmas are not good in the beginning, the middle and the end. How do their earlier and later statements contradict one another? They teach that non-harming is the highest dharma, yet they say there is no fault in killing animals for the sake of fire offerings and sacrifices. They say there is no greater sin than lying, yet they say there is no fault in lying at times of play, with women, and in sexual intercourse, when taking a bride, when one's wealth is being stolen, and when one's life is at stake. So they contradict themselves from earlier to later. They are not excellent in meaning, because they teach that a self, an agent and a living being exist. They are not excellent in wording, because their letters are disconnected, unclear and come out as babble. Nor are they unique, because the moral disciplines and the views, such as the view of a self, of the tīrthikas, the skull-bearers, the naked ascetics and the rest, are held in common among them.

Nor are they complete, because their path merely serves as the antidote to the afflictions of the lower grounds, and does not serve as the antidote to all the afflictions of every ground. They are not pure, because all the afflictions of the ground on which one dwells become the basis from which they arise and come forth: for if one dwells in the desire realm, the afflictions that operate in the desire realm become the basis of their origin. They are not utterly clean, because one remains bound up with the latent afflictions of the other grounds. Nor are they the holy life (brahmacarya), because those paths do not bring about the attainment of nirvāṇa.

Alternatively, it is virtuous in the beginning because it is excellent in meaning; it is virtuous in the middle because it is excellent in wording; and it is virtuous in the end because it is unique, complete, pure, utterly clean, and the holy life.

"I shall teach it correctly" means "I shall explain without distortion the dharmas that are to be explained and to be realized."

With the words beginning "Listen to it very well," he directs those to be trained who are listening toward non-distraction, saying, "Listen with undistracted ears." "It" refers to the Dharma discourse called the Ascertainment of Meaning. "Listen very well" means "listen without distortion." "Keep it in mind" means "hold the meaning in its entirety, and do not forget it."

Alternatively, three expressions occur in the sūtra: "listen thoroughly," "listen well," and "keep it in mind." By these, in order, he declared, "Give up the three kinds of faults of a vessel." The three kinds of faults are the fault that is like an upturned vessel, the fault that is like a vessel filled with filth, and the fault that is like a leaking vessel.

Of these, the fault like an upturned vessel is as follows. If a vessel is turned upside down, then even though rain falls from the sky, the water does not collect inside, and the rainfall is to no purpose. In the same way, when the scriptures are being explained, if one is overcome by distraction and torpor, the falling rain of the Dharma becomes pointless. To give up that fault he declared, "Listen thoroughly," meaning "give up distraction and torpor and understand the meaning."

The fault like a vessel filled with filth is as follows. If rain falls into a filthy vessel, the rainwater becomes filthy, and so the rainfall is to no purpose. In the same way, at the time when the rain of the Dharma falls, if one grasps the meaning of the scriptures wrongly through one's own distorted conceptual thought, the falling rain of the Dharma again becomes pointless. To give that up he declared, "Listen well," meaning "listen without distortion."

The fault like a leaking vessel is as follows. Even though the rain from the sky enters a leaking vessel, it immediately leaks out again, so the rainfall is to no purpose. In the same way, when the rain of the sublime Dharma falls, if one understands the meaning, the words and so on at first but later forgets them, the falling rain of the Dharma becomes pointless. As the antidote to that he declared, "Keep it in mind," meaning "do not forget the words and meanings just as you have grasped them."

"I shall explain" is meant in this sense: "If you do so, I shall explain; otherwise the rain of the Dharma I let fall would be pointless, and so I shall not explain."

Alternatively, the Tathāgata has disciples who have gone forth who are like three kinds of sick people, and as the antidote to them he spoke the words beginning "very well."

Of these, the first patient is like this. A certain man is struck by illness, and his father and mother, wishing to help him, give him medicine and food that would benefit him, but he refuses to eat them; in this way he harms the very parents who wish to help him. In the same way, when a person who has gone forth in the Tathāgata's teaching is struck by the illness of the afflictions, and is told, "Listen to the Dharma that is virtuous in the beginning and so on, the medicine that cures the illness of the afflictions," he refuses to listen; in this way he harms the Tathāgata, his father through the Dharma, who means to help him. As the antidote to this he declared, "Listen thoroughly," meaning "listen to the Tathāgata's scriptures."

The second patient is like this. His father and mother, wishing to help him, give him medicine and food that would benefit him, but he does not eat what is beneficial and eats what is not beneficial; in this way he harms the parents who intend to help him. In the same way, when the sublime Dharma has been taught to certain persons who have gone forth in the Tathāgata's scriptures, they do not grasp it without distortion but take it in a distorted way; in this way they harm the Tathāgata, their father through the Dharma, who means to help them. As the antidote to this he declared, "Listen well," meaning "listen without distortion."

The third patient is like this. When his father and mother, intending to help him, give him medicine and food that would benefit him, he eats them as they are given but afterward vomits them up; in this way he harms the parents who intend to help him. Likewise, certain persons who have gone forth in the Tathāgata's teaching listen to the sublime Dharma just as they are instructed, and understand its words and meaning, but later forget them; in this way they harm the Tathāgata, their father through the Dharma, who means to help them. As the antidote to this he declared, "Keep it in mind," meaning "do not forget the meanings and words you have grasped before."

"I shall explain" declares: "Now I am merely the one who explains; it is you who must diligently put it into practice."

Because the Blessed One had placed those monks in an excellent state, the monks in turn said "Excellent!" to the Blessed One. To show that, just as the Blessed One had earlier declared, "Give up the three kinds of fault and listen," they had given up those faults and set about listening, the sūtra says that those monks "listened in response" to the Blessed One. "Listened in response" means "they listened just as the Blessed One had told them."

"He declared this" means he declared what follows below. The setting out of the occasion of the discourse has now been explained. Next, in order to explain the condensed meaning, he said the words beginning, "Monks, what is the Dharma discourse called the Ascertainment of Meaning?"

Because it here teaches, in a definitive way, the dharmas that are to be known, to be abandoned, to be cultivated and to be attained, it is called the Ascertainment of Meaning. "What is it?" asks what that is. To teach it, he said the words beginning "namely"; "namely" means "they are these, as follows." To show that the meaning of the sūtra is complete, entirely and without remainder, in the twenty-six topics from the five aggregates up to the eighty excellent minor marks, he declared, "Monks, this is the scriptural statement of the Dharma discourse of the Ascertainment of Meaning."

Of these, the topics from the five aggregates up to the twenty-two faculties teach three things: what is to be known, what is to be abandoned, and what is to be cultivated. Among those dharmas, whatever has the characteristic of being indeterminate is to be known; whatever has the characteristic of being non-virtuous is to be abandoned; and whatever has the characteristic of being virtuous is to be cultivated. Of these, the topics beginning with the four meditative concentrations and going up to what is called the four limbs of stream-entry teach the dharmas to be cultivated. The topics from what is called the ten powers of a Tathāgata up to what is called the eighty excellent minor marks teach the dharmas to be attained.

And what is the purpose of teaching the condensed meaning? It is to rule out both overstatement and understatement, by making clear that this sūtra explains neither more nor fewer than twenty-six topics. Alternatively, the condensed meaning is stated so that the disciples may easily understand the words and the meaning and easily retain them. For example, when a horse is first shown the racecourse, it afterward runs without obstruction; in the same way, if the disciples first understand the condensed meaning, they will afterward easily understand the meaning of the individual parts. Alternatively, there are two kinds of disciples: those to be trained by a teaching in brief, and those to be trained by a detailed explanation. For the sake of those to be trained by a teaching in brief, he first declared the condensed meaning. For the sake of those to be trained by a detailed explanation, he gave the detailed explanation of the meaning of the words, beginning "namely, the aggregate of form." And to conclude with the condensed meaning, he said the words beginning "Monks, this is."

Now the meaning of the words is to be taught, and therefore he said the words beginning "Of these, monks." "What are the five aggregates?" is a question. To teach the answer just as it was asked, he said the words beginning "namely." Although the word "namely" can also be used as a word introducing an example, in this context the word "namely" serves to introduce the aggregates of form and so on. Therefore "namely" means "the five aggregates are these."

The words "the aggregate of form, the aggregate of feeling," and so on teach the uncontaminated aggregates. For there are thus two kinds of aggregates: uncontaminated aggregates and contaminated aggregates. If one asks, "Here the aggregates are mentioned only in general; how is it evident that they are the uncontaminated aggregates?", it is evident, because below, when the contaminated aggregates are clearly mentioned by name in the words "Of these, monks, what are the five aggregates of appropriation?", it becomes evident that the five aggregates mentioned earlier refer to the uncontaminated aggregates. Were it not so, and both referred to the contaminated aggregates, there would be the fault of repeating the very same contaminated aggregates twice.

Then what is the purpose of teaching the uncontaminated aggregates here? When one sees that there exist uncontaminated aggregates, a state free from all suffering, weariness with the contaminated aggregates arises. Once weariness has arisen, one generates diligence in casting off the contaminated aggregates. When diligence has arisen, one comes to cast off the aggregate of suffering. When the aggregate of suffering has been cast off, one attains the supreme bliss, nirvāṇa. That is the purpose of teaching the uncontaminated aggregates.

Of these, what is the uncontaminated aggregate of form? When a yogin is absorbed in the noble eightfold path, the non-informative form that has the characteristic of being uncontaminated is the aggregate of form. The uncontaminated feeling that arises at that time in that very assemblage is the aggregate of feeling. The perception present in that very assemblage is the aggregate of perception.

Alternatively, the ten kinds of perception of yogins are the aggregate of perception. The ten perceptions are the perception of impermanence, the perception of suffering, the perception of selflessness, the perception of impurity, the perception of the repulsiveness of food, the perception of disenchantment with the whole world, the perception of death, the perception of abandonment, the perception of freedom from attachment, and the perception of cessation.

Of these, to think that things do not endure, since they depend on causes and conditions, is the perception of impermanence. To think that they harm body and mind is the perception of suffering. To think that one has no independence but is under the control of others is the perception of selflessness. To know the nature of the body as having the character of blood, pus and so on is the perception of impurity. To think, "When food is put into the mouth, it mixes with saliva, mucus and so on as it goes down inside, and inside it turns into excrement and urine," is the perception of the repulsiveness of food. To think, "All sentient beings are burned by the fire of the afflictions such as attachment, and tormented by the harms of birth and old age," is the perception of disenchantment with the world. To think, "Like the water in a pot with a hole in its base, the life faculty is used up moment by moment," is the perception of death. At the time of the uninterrupted path at the point of the first fruit, stream-entry, there is the perception of abandonment. At the time of the uninterrupted path at the point of non-returning, there is the perception of freedom from attachment. At the time of the uninterrupted path at the point of arhatship, there is the perception of cessation.

The formations associated with mind present in that very assemblage, such as faith, diligence and volition, and the formations not associated with mind, such as birth and aging, the collection of names and the collection of phrases, are the aggregate of formations. The consciousness present in that very assemblage that takes no delight in the contaminated aggregates and in the five qualities of sense desire is the aggregate of consciousness.

To show that the explanation of the five uncontaminated aggregates is complete, he said, "Monks, these are the five aggregates," meaning "the five uncontaminated aggregates are complete." Here, if one asks, "It would have been enough to say 'the five aggregates'; what need is there to take up the characteristics of form, feeling and the rest?", the characteristics of form and feeling are taken up in order to exclude the other uncontaminated aggregates, such as that of moral discipline.

Since the five aggregates of appropriation are taught after the five aggregates, he said the words beginning "Of these, monks, what are the five aggregates of appropriation?" in order to explain them just as they were taught. And what is the purpose of explaining the five aggregates of appropriation? When the five aggregates of appropriation are explained, one comes to know the four truths, and so the wisdom that knows suchness arises. When wisdom arises, the faults that dwell in the mind are abandoned. When the faults are abandoned, one does not accumulate karma. When karma is not accumulated, one is freed from suffering and attains happiness. That is the purpose.

Alternatively, through the teaching on the aggregates one comes to know, correctly and just as they are, that the aggregates, which are impermanent, unsatisfactory, selfless, without essence, without refuge, without protector and without independence, and are afflicted by endless sufferings such as aging, sickness and death, are like foam, like a water bubble, like a mirage, like the stalk of a water plant, and like a magical illusion. When they know this correctly, just as it is, the noble śrāvakas dispel the darkness of ignorance present in their minds. When the darkness is dispelled, they outshine the splendor of the knowledge of the tīrthikas, which is like a firefly, just as the sun outshines it; they abandon wrong views and the faults born of them, birth, aging and the rest; and they attain the qualities of nirvāṇa. That is the purpose.

The word "of these" is used to single something out from other dharmas when one wishes to teach it, or else to introduce a statement. "Monks" is an address.

As for "appropriation": because this collection of afflictions appropriates future existence, the afflictions such as attachment are called "appropriation," and the aggregates that arise from that appropriation are the aggregates of appropriation. It is a compound in which the middle word is dropped, like "grass fire" and "husk fire": just as a fire arising from grass is called a grass fire, and a fire arising from husks is called a husk fire, with the middle word dropped. Some, not taking it as a compound with the middle word dropped, form it as a genitive compound: the afflictions are called "appropriation," and the aggregates connected with the afflictions are called the aggregates of appropriation, just as fires of grass and of husks are called grass fire and husk fire.

It may be said: It is right that whatever is counted as a sentient being should be called aggregates of appropriation, since they are born from the cause of karma moistened by the afflictions. But how can external things, which are not counted as sentient beings, be called aggregates of appropriation? The reply: It is right that they too, arising from appropriation, are called aggregates of appropriation, since it is taught that the variety of the world is born from karma.

Alternatively, the aggregates that conform to appropriation are called the aggregates of appropriation, like "the king's man": just as a man who conforms to the king's command is called the king's man, the aggregates that conform to the afflictions are the aggregates of appropriation, because by their power one engages with objects and goes to other destinies. Alternatively, among the aggregates, those from which the afflictions arise are called the aggregates of appropriation, like a flowering tree or a fruit tree: just as a tree from which flowers and fruit come is called a flowering tree or a fruit tree, so, because the afflictions arise from the aggregates, they are designated aggregates of appropriation.

The word "five" is stated to rule out more or fewer, for there are some who hold that analytical cessation, non-analytical cessation and space are also aggregates. The word "aggregates" is stated to rule out the five objects, visible form, sound and the rest.

As for "the aggregate of appropriation that is form": form is of two kinds, the four great elements and the form derived from the four great elements. What are the four great elements? The earth element, the water element, the fire element and the wind element. Of these, the nature of the earth element is solidity and roughness. The nature of the water element is moisture and wetness. The nature of the fire element is heat and warmth. The nature of the wind element is lightness and motion. Alternatively, the nature of the earth element is to possess odor; the water element has the nature of possessing taste; the fire element has the nature of possessing visible form; and the wind element has the nature of sound and the tangible. Alternatively, stability, heaviness, firmness, smoothness and roughness are the characteristics of the earth element. Wetness, heaviness, smoothness, coldness and oiliness are the characteristics of the water element. Warmth, lightness, ripening and heat are the characteristics of the fire element. Swiftness, lightness, roughness and motion are the characteristics of the wind element.

What function do these great elements perform? The earth element performs the function of supporting. The water element performs the function of holding together. The fire element performs the function of ripening. The wind element performs the function of growth and increase. Why are just four great elements taught, and not five, six or some other number? Because no more than four are needed as conditions for a sprout to grow, five, six and so on are not taught. Of these, earth serves as the support of the seed; water moistens the seed; fire ripens the seed; and wind makes the seed increase and develop.

Alternatively, since the earth element has the nature of heaviness, those in whom it predominates have heavy bodies and sleep a great deal, and if they are not roused, it turns into illness. Since the water element has the nature of wetness, in those in whom it predominates it turns into phlegm, and if purgatives, emetics and the like are not given, it turns into illness. Since the fire element has the nature of heat, when it predominates it turns into bile, and if, when one is hungry, it is not nourished in time with food and the like, it turns into illness. Since the wind element is motion, in those in whom it predominates, if such a windy person is not rubbed with oil and massaged and so forth, it turns into illness. For this reason too there come to be exactly four great elements. So some masters explain.

Then why are space, feeling and the like not called great elements? The reply: "element" has the sense of arising, and "great" has the sense of pervading. Although feeling and the like arise, they do not pervade, and so they are not great; and although space pervades, it does not arise, and so it is not called a great element. Therefore, because the sense of "element" is that they arise from the karma of sentient beings, and the sense of "great" is that they pervade, they are called great elements.

Then in what way are they great? As it is said in the sūtra: "Nanda, this great earth rests on water, and water rests on wind." It is also said: "When the fire element surges, it burns everything up to the world of Brahmā. When the water element surges, it destroys everything up to the Ābhāsvara class of gods. When the wind element surges, it destroys everything up to the Śubhakṛtsna gods." It is also said: "When the earth element surges, it blocks all the channels of water. When the channels are blocked, wind does not arise. When wind does not arise, the lowlands dry up. When the lowlands dry up, fire arises in the sky and burns." Since this is so, they are called great.

It is asked: Wherever one element is present, are the other elements also present there? Some masters say that they are. How so? Because these four always remain inseparable. If there were no water element in the earth element, the earth element would fall apart into separate bits, like scattered ashes. If there were no earth element in the water element, then even when sugarcane juice and the like are boiled, they would not become solid. If there were no earth element and water element in the fire element, the flames would scatter apart, and flames could not lift leaves and the like. If there were no earth element, fire element and the rest in the wind element, the wind could not carry off grass and leaves, and there would be no such thing as a hot wind or a cold wind.

Furthermore, water arises from earth, for water is produced from a moonstone; likewise fire arises from earth, for fire is produced from a sunstone; and wind arises from earth, for wind rises from clapping the hands or from waving a palm leaf. Earth is present in water, since water holds up leaves and the like; fire is present, since water rots leaves, grass and the like; and wind is present, since water moves and flows downward. Earth is present in fire, since one flame holds onto another and fire holds leaves and the like; water is present, since the flames do not scatter; and wind is present, since fire moves and stirs. Earth is present in wind, since wind lifts leaves and the like; fire is present, since wind dries wet cloth and the like; and water is present, since wind moves and comes forth as one, without scattering apart.

Form derived from the elements is of eleven kinds: the five sense faculties, the five objects, and non-informative form. Of these, the five sense faculties are the eye faculty, the ear faculty, the nose faculty, the tongue faculty and the body faculty. The five objects are visible form, sound, smell, taste and the tangible.

Of these, what is the eye faculty? It is the clear form that serves as the support of eye consciousness. What is the ear faculty? It is the clear form that serves as the support of ear consciousness. What is the nose faculty? It is the clear form that serves as the support of nose consciousness. What is the tongue faculty? It is the clear form that serves as the support of tongue consciousness. What is the body faculty? It is the clear form that serves as the support of body consciousness.

What is visible form? It is of two kinds: that which consists of color and that which consists of shape. Of these, that which consists of color is blue, yellow, red and white. That which consists of shape is long, short, round, circular all round, thin, thick, even and uneven.

What is sound? It is of three kinds: that arising from the cause of appropriated great elements, that arising from the cause of unappropriated great elements, and that arising from both causes. Of these, that arising from the cause of appropriated great elements is the sound of sentient beings. That arising from the cause of unappropriated great elements is the sound of forests and rivers. That arising from both causes is the sound of clay drums, lutes and the like.

Smell is of three kinds: pleasant smell, unpleasant smell, and neutral smell. Of these, pleasant smell is the smell of sandalwood and the like. Unpleasant smell is the smell of filth and the like. Neutral smell is the smell of conch, mother-of-pearl and the like.

What is taste? It is of six kinds: sweet, sour, bitter, pungent, salty and astringent. Of these, sweet is the taste of sugar and the like. Sour is the taste of emblic myrobalan and the like. Bitter is the taste of bile, chiretta and the like. Pungent is the taste of ginger and the like. Salty is the taste of salt. Astringent is the taste of chebulic myrobalan and the like.

Of these, what is the tangible? It too is of eleven kinds: the four great elements, smoothness, roughness, lightness, cold, hunger and thirst.

Of these, what is non-informative form? It too is of three kinds: virtuous, non-virtuous, and having the character of neither. Virtuous non-informative form is in turn of three kinds: that arising from the prātimokṣa vow, that arising from samādhi, and that arising from uncontaminated meditative concentration.

That arising from the prātimokṣa vow is that of monks, nuns, laymen, laywomen, and those who keep the temporary precepts. That arising from meditative concentration is that which arises from the cause of the four meditative concentrations of the form realm. The uncontaminated vow is that which arises from the cause of cultivating the eight limbs of the noble path. Non-virtuous non-informative form is the evil, non-virtuous intentions of fowlers, fishermen, butchers and the like. That which has the character of neither is a pledge such as "I will not eat food without first making a maṇḍala," or "I will not eat food without first beating or killing some sentient being."

Here, how is the aggregate of form to be understood? As is fitting, it is to be understood in eleven distinct ways: it is to be understood as impure, as arising from that, as dwelling in that, as eating that, as delighting in that, as that issuing from it, as without essence, as impermanent, as suffering, as empty, and as selfless.

Of these, to understand it as impure is to understand that this body, from the brain down to the soles of the feet, consists of pus, blood, lymph and the like. To understand it as arising from that is to understand that this body first came about from the mother's blood and the father's semen. To understand it as dwelling in that is to understand that when it dwells in the mother's womb, it remains between the place of digested food and the place of undigested food, surrounded by all kinds of filth. To understand it as eating that is to understand: "It eats the food, drink and so on that the mother has eaten and drunk, turned into saliva, lymph and the like." To understand it as delighting in that is to understand that when the father and mother engage in sexual intercourse, it delights in the blood, the semen and the foul smell that arise from that. To understand it as that issuing from it is to understand: "All kinds of filth issue from this body." To understand it as without essence is to understand: "Even though in the head of an elephant and the like there is an essence the size of a pearl, in this body there is not even a particle of anything worth prizing." To understand it as impermanent is to understand: "It has the nature of perishing moment by moment." To understand it as suffering is to understand: "This body is constantly tormented by aging, death and the like." To understand it as empty is to understand that within this body there is no permanent self such as the tīrthikas imagine. To understand it as selfless is to understand: "This very body does not have the nature of the permanent self that the tīrthikas imagine."

As for "the aggregate of feeling," feeling is of three kinds: pleasant feeling, painful feeling, and feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful. Of these, that which soothes and benefits body and mind is pleasant feeling. That which does not soothe body and mind but harms them is painful feeling. That which neither soothes nor harms body and mind is feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful. Alternatively, experiencing the maturation of virtuous karma is pleasant feeling; experiencing the maturation of non-virtuous karma is painful feeling; and experiencing the maturation of indeterminate karma is feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful.

Here, how is the aggregate of feeling to be understood? It is to be understood in seven ways: it is to be understood as deceptive, as arising from an antidote, as covered over, as arising from conceptualization, as the basis of ruin, as insatiable, and as impermanent.

Of these, to understand it as deceptive is to understand: "Although the noble ones see all feelings as suffering, to the childish they appear to be pleasure." To understand it as arising from an antidote is to understand: "Just as, when a wound is anointed with a cooling salve, one thinks that the mere momentary easing of pain is pleasure, so the notion of pleasure arises when suffering is merely checked by an antidote to suffering." To understand it as covered over is to understand: "The notion of pleasure arises regarding what is by nature suffering merely through clothes, ornaments and the like, and through adjusting one's posture." To understand it as arising from conceptualization is to understand: "This very thought that what is by nature suffering is pleasant arises from distorted conceptualization." To understand it as the basis of ruin is to understand: "Attachment to the pleasure of feeling becomes the cause of the three lower realms." To understand it as insatiable is to understand that, like drinking salt water, even if one indulges in this feeling until the end of an eon, one never becomes satisfied. To understand it as impermanent is to understand that all feelings are momentary by nature.

As for "the aggregate of perception," perception is what apprehends objects such as blue, yellow, long and short as signs and discerns them. It is small, great or immeasurable. Of these, the perception of the desire realm is called small, because it apprehends base things, mere qualities of the five sense pleasures. The perception of the form realm is called great, because it apprehends the sign of the taste of meditative concentration. The perception of the formless realm is called immeasurable, because it takes as its object such things as "space is infinite."

How is the aggregate of perception to be understood? It is to be understood in these ways: as deluding childish beings, as deceptive, as having the characteristic of impermanence, and as that through distorted perception of which other pointless things arise. To understand it as deluding childish beings is to understand: "Just as a mirage, by appearing as water, deceives the childish, so perception deludes the childish by apprehending yellow, blue and the like as signs in objects." To understand it as deceptive is to understand that it is distorted perception that grasps at permanence, happiness, purity and the like.

To understand it as having the characteristic of impermanence is to understand: "It has the characteristic of perishing in every single instant." To understand it as that through distorted perception of which other pointless things arise is to understand: "With distorted perception as the cause, one comes to be born in the three lower realms."

As for "the aggregate of formations": formations are what enact karma characterized as virtuous, nonvirtuous, or indeterminate. They are of two kinds: formations associated with mind and formations not associated with mind.

Of these, the formations associated with mind are: attention, contact, aspiration, conviction, mindfulness, meditative absorption, wisdom, faith, a sense of shame, a sense of embarrassment, non-attachment, non-hatred, non-delusion, diligence, conscientiousness, equanimity, non-harming; attachment, anger, pride, ignorance, doubt, the view of the perishing collection, the view that grasps at extremes, holding views as supreme, holding moral discipline and ascetic observances as supreme, wrong view, wrath, resentment, concealment, spite, jealousy, miserliness, dissimulation, deceit, harmfulness, lack of embarrassment, shamelessness, lethargy, excitement, lack of faith, laziness, non-conscientiousness, forgetfulness, non-vigilance, distraction, sleep, regret, investigation and analysis.

What are the formations not associated with mind? Acquisition, the absorption without perception, the absorption of cessation, the state without perception, the faculty of feeling, similarity of type, birth, aging, duration, impermanence, the collection of names, the collection of phrases, the collection of letters, and so forth.

How is the aggregate of formations to be understood? It is to be understood in six ways: as without core, as not established, as impermanent, as selfless, as suffering, and as empty. Of these, to understand it as without core: just as the trunk of a plantain tree, when its layers are peeled away one by one, has no core, so too these collections of formations, when examined, have no core whatsoever. Not established: just as a city of the gandharvas appears and yet is not established as a city, so too these aggregates of formations are not established in their intrinsic nature. Impermanent, because they perish in every single instant. Suffering, because they possess the qualities of birth, aging and the like. Empty, because those formations lack the self imagined by the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). Selfless, because they themselves are not of the nature of the self imagined by the non-Buddhists.

As for "the aggregate of consciousness": there are six collections of consciousness, from eye consciousness up to mental consciousness. Of these, eye consciousness is that which, relying on the eye and taking form as its object, cognizes the characteristics of form. Ear consciousness is that which, relying on the ear and taking sound as its object, cognizes the characteristics of sound. Nose consciousness is that which, relying on the nose and taking smell as its object, cognizes the characteristics of smell. Tongue consciousness is that which, relying on the tongue and taking taste as its object, cognizes the characteristics of taste. Body consciousness is that which, relying on the body and taking the tangible as its object, cognizes the characteristics of the tangible. What is mental consciousness? It is that which, relying on the mind and taking phenomena as its object, cognizes the characteristics of phenomena.

How, then, is the aggregate of consciousness to be understood? It is to be understood in eleven ways: as not stirring of its own accord, as without independence, as arisen from many conditions, as culminating in a single result, as empty of intrinsic nature, as momentary, as moving swiftly without staying, as stirring toward objects, as seeing impure objects as virtues, as the resting place of the flies of nonvirtue and the like, and as forever separated from the fire of wisdom.

Just as a sprout and a seed and the like are not producer and produced of one another, in that they do not think, "I shall produce," so consciousness does not stir of its own accord. Just as the fire in a mechanical device moves under the force of the wind, so this consciousness too moves under the force of karma and afflictions; therefore it is without independence. Because it arises from many causes and conditions, such as the sense faculty, the object, karma and afflictions, it is arisen from many conditions. Although it is produced in this way by many conditions, no more than a single result, consciousness, arises; therefore it culminates in a single result. Like the hair of a tortoise, it has no intrinsic nature such as a self; therefore it is empty of intrinsic nature. Like the flame of a lamp, it perishes in every single instant; therefore it is impermanent. Like the water of a steep mountain stream, it goes on without remaining even for an instant; therefore it moves swiftly without staying. Like a monkey, it is always attached to objects and has the characteristic of excitement; therefore it stirs toward objects. Like a pig, it holds impure objects to be excellent; therefore it sees impure objects as virtues. Because all nonvirtue arises with consciousness as its cause, it is the resting place of the blue flies of nonvirtue. As long as one is accompanied by consciousness, the wisdom of the Four Noble Truths does not arise; therefore it is separated from the fire of wisdom.

Then why are the five aggregates set out in such an order, first form, after that feeling, after that perception and so on? For three reasons: according to coarseness, according to the way affliction arises, and for the sake of the meaning of the vessel and so forth.

Of these, because form is obstructive and serves as the support of the consciousnesses, it is coarser than feeling and the rest; therefore it is explained first. Feeling is coarser than the other formless aggregates, for one feels, "My hand hurts," and "My foot hurts." To show this, feeling is explained before the remaining three. The aggregate of perception is coarser than the aggregate of formations and the aggregate of consciousness, because it apprehends, "This is a man, this is a woman." Therefore perception is explained before those two. The aggregate of formations is coarser than consciousness, because its characteristic is to bring about virtuous and nonvirtuous karma. Because consciousness is the subtlest of all, it is explained last.

Or else, they are set out in order according to the sequence in which affliction arises. Thus, from beginningless time in saṃsāra, woman and man have been attached to each other's form; therefore form is explained first. Attachment to form, moreover, comes from clinging to pleasant feeling; therefore feeling comes after it. Clinging to that, in turn, comes from the distorted perception that takes what is not lovely to be lovely; therefore perception comes after it. Distorted perception, too, arises from the collection of formations, whose characteristic is affliction; therefore formations come after it. Since these afflict the mind, the aggregate of consciousness is explained last.

Or else, they are also in this order according to the sequence of the vessel and so forth. That which is like the vessel is the aggregate of form, because it is the support of feeling and the rest. That which is like the food is the aggregate of feeling, because pleasant and painful feeling is what is experienced and consumed. That which is like the curry and the seasonings and condiments is the aggregate of perception, because, from the cause of the distorted perception that takes what is unpleasant to be pleasant, feeling becomes attached to perception. That which is like the cook is the aggregate of formations, because, having prepared the maturation of virtuous and nonvirtuous karma, it serves it to consciousness. That which enjoys it all is the aggregate of consciousness, because in the end it is consciousness that experiences the maturation, pleasure, pain and the like.

Or else, the aggregate of consciousness is explained last because it is like the field and the seed. The consciousness of the desire realm relies on the aggregate of form. In the four meditative concentrations of the form realm, consciousness relies on the aggregate of feeling. In the sphere of infinite space, the sphere of infinite consciousness and the sphere of nothingness, consciousness relies on the aggregate of perception. In the sphere of neither nothingness nor not nothingness, which is the Peak of Existence, consciousness relies on the collection of formations, both those not associated with mind and those associated with mind.

What, then, is the meaning of "aggregate"? The meaning of aggregate is the meaning of a heap. Whatever form there may be, whether past, future or present, external or internal, subtle or coarse, fine or inferior, long or short, all of it gathered together into one heap is called an aggregate.

Since, after the aggregates, this is the occasion for explaining the elements, it is with reference to them that the words are spoken: "Here, monks, what are the eighteen elements?"

What, then, is the purpose of teaching the eighteen elements here? It is said: the eighteen elements are taught in order to show those who cling to a self, to something belonging to a self, to an agent and to an experiencer, that relying on the eye and on form there is nothing more than the cognition of form by eye consciousness, and that there is no seer or the like. Or else, the eighteen elements are taught in order to dispel the darkness of not knowing, of doubtful knowing and of wrong knowing, because once the light of the wisdom that knows the eighteen elements has dawned, the darkness of ignorance comes to an end. Or else, it is in order to show the jewel treasure of the qualities of awakening to those who in saṃsāra are bereft of the factors conducive to awakening and have become poor. When the eighteen elements have been explained and one simply recollects the elements, the cultivation of the factors of awakening, such as mindfulness as a limb of awakening, comes to completion.

"Here, what are the eighteen elements?" is the question. To answer it, the word "namely" is spoken. The eye faculty, the eye element and the eye sense source should be understood to differ only in name; their meaning is one.

What, then, is the eye element? It is that which serves as the support of eye consciousness. Its subtle particles, in the black and the dark yellow of the eye, directly facing the object, are clear like crystal and able to apprehend form. Some explain that, like flour sprinkled on the surface of a vessel filled with water, its particles do not sink inside but appear on the surface and remain clear. Some explain that its color abides clear, like an opened flax flower, and that because they are surrounded by the great elements the particles do not scatter apart. Some explain that, like something round, they lie in three parts, an upper layer, a lower layer and a middle, and that being exceedingly clear the three parts do not obscure one another, just like autumn water gathered without turbidity.

What is the form element? It is that which is the object of the eye element: blue, yellow, red and white; long and short; round, circular, thin and thick; even and uneven; high and low; shade, sunlight, light and darkness; cloud, smoke, dust and mist; the open sky; and informative form.

What is the element of eye consciousness? It is that which, relying on the eye element and taking form as its object, comes to know the form just as its aspect is. If the form is blue, it arises in the aspect of blue and knows it as blue.

What is the ear element? It is that which serves as the support of ear consciousness. Its subtle particles are clear like crystal and able to apprehend sounds, and they abide inside the ear like a rolled-up strip of birch bark.

What is the sound element? It is that which is the object of the ear element. It is pleasant, unpleasant, or neither; arisen from the cause of appropriated great elements, arisen from the cause of unappropriated great elements, or arisen from the cause of both; conventional in the world, arisen from the accomplished, or imagined; and the speech of noble ones or the speech of those who are not noble.

Of these, that which benefits and delights the ear faculty and consciousness is called pleasant. That which harms and torments the ear faculty and consciousness is called unpleasant. That which does neither to the faculty and consciousness is called neither pleasant nor unpleasant. The three beginning with that which arises from the four appropriated great elements are as explained before. That which does so is called unpleasant.

Conventional in the world is that which is connected with the language of the world. Arisen from the accomplished is that which the noble ones have taught. Imagined is that which the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) have taught. To speak truthfully about what is seen and so forth, saying "I saw," "I heard," "I know," is the speech of noble ones. The opposite of this is called the speech of those who are not noble.

What is the element of ear consciousness? It is that which, relying on the ear element and taking the sound element as its object, comes to know whatever the characteristics of the sound are.

What is the nose element? It is that which serves as the support of nose consciousness. Its subtle particles are clear like crystal and able to apprehend smells, and they abide on the upper part of the nose, like a split-open bean. Some explain that they lie at the very top of the nose, like a probe.

What is the smell element? It is that which is the object of the nose element. It is fragrant smell, unfragrant smell, and even smell; and innate, compounded, and transformed. Fragrant smell and the rest are as explained before. Innate smell is that of sandalwood and the like. Compounded smell is the smell of incense made from many substances, such as storax. Transformed smell is the smell of fully ripened fruit and the like.

What is the element of nose consciousness? It is that which, relying on the nose element and taking smell as its object, comes to know whatever the nature of the smell is.

What is the tongue element? It is that which serves as the support of tongue consciousness. Its subtle particles are clear like crystal and able to savor taste, and they are arranged like the portions of a half moon. In the middle of the tongue an area the size of the tip of a hair is not pervaded by them, but the rest is pervaded throughout. If it too were pervaded in that way, the brain, constantly experiencing the dripping of fluid, would rot, and one's appetite would always be spoiled.

What is the taste element? It is of six kinds, and their characteristics are as explained before.

What is the element of tongue consciousness? It is that which, relying on the tongue element and taking taste as its object, comes to know sour, sweet and the like.

What is the body element? It is that which serves as the support of body consciousness. Its subtle particles are clear like crystal, abide pervading the whole body like the skin, and are able to apprehend the tangible.

What is the element of the tangible? It is of eleven kinds: the four great elements, smoothness, roughness, lightness, heaviness, cold, hunger and thirst. The characteristics of the four great elements are just as explained before. Where the fire and water elements predominate, there is smoothness. Where earth and wind predominate, there is roughness. Where earth and water predominate, there is heaviness. Where wind predominates, there is lightness. Where fire and water predominate, there is pliancy. Where wind and water predominate, there is cold. From the predominance of fire and wind, when the stomach is empty, hunger and thirst arise.

The element of body consciousness is that which, relying on the body element and taking the tangible as its object, comes to know the characteristics of the tangible.

What is the mind element? It is that which serves as the support of mental consciousness. Whichever of the six kinds of consciousness has just ceased is called the mind element; it is not of the nature of subtle particles.

What is the element of phenomena? It comprises seven kinds of substance: feeling, perception, the aggregate of formations, analytical cessation, non-analytical cessation, space, and non-informative form. Of these, the others have simply been explained already. What is called analytical cessation is this: having examined the four truths with wisdom, thinking, "This is the truth of suffering, this of origin, this of cessation, this of the path," the arising of mind and mental factors comes to cease; this is called analytical cessation.

What is non-analytical cessation? Although the consciousnesses such as eye consciousness arise through the power of conditions, when one consciousness is fixed on its own object, the objects of the other consciousnesses cease, while those consciousnesses, not having met with their conditions, do not arise and are left in the future. This is called non-analytical cessation. For example, when eye consciousness is fixed on form, objects such as sound arise and cease, but ear consciousness, having no object, ceases without having arisen; that very cessation is called non-analytical cessation.

Then what is space? It is that which is unobstructed and gives room for form to arise.

What is the element of mental consciousness? It is that which, relying on the mind element and taking phenomena as its object, makes determinations such as "This is yellow, not blue," or "This is the sound of a vīṇā and nothing else."

Some masters explain that, of the eighteen elements, the eye element is predominantly fire, and because fire predominates it is able to apprehend form; and they say that the ear is of the nature of space, and that being unobstructed it is able to hear sound. But since benefit and harm befall the ear faculty, while space admits of neither benefit nor harm, that is not tenable. Therefore the ear element is of a nature in which wind predominates, and because wind predominates it is able to apprehend sound. The nose element is of a nature in which earth predominates, because earth, too, has smell as its predominant quality, and whatever is predominantly present in a thing enables it to apprehend that very quality. The tongue element is of a nature in which water predominates, because moisture predominates in it and so it is able to apprehend taste; otherwise, just as when two dry stones touch, nothing at all would be experienced. The body element is of the nature of all the great elements.

In order to teach in summary the eighteen elements as they have been explained, he said: "Ascetics, these are called the eighteen elements."

Why, then, are eighteen elements explained, neither more nor fewer? It is because the Blessed One, dividing them into six supports, six things supported, and six objects, taught them as eighteen elements. The six supports are from the eye element up to the mind element. The six things supported are from eye consciousness up to mental consciousness. The six objects are from the form element up to the element of phenomena. Further, the eighteen elements are explained so that one may understand the faults of what is like the vessel holding poisoned food, of what is like the poisoned food, and of what is like the one who eats it.

Here, what is like the vessel holding poisoned food is the six faculties, such as the eye element. What is like the poisoned food is the six objects, such as form. What is like the one who eats it is the six consciousnesses, such as the element of eye consciousness.

The order of the eighteen elements should now be explained. Simply by showing the order of the eye element and the rest, the order of their objects and of the consciousness elements is shown as well.

The five elements such as the eye are taught before the element of mental consciousness because their objects are present only, whereas the mind element is not restricted: the present is its object, and so too are the three times and phenomena that are not of the three times, such as the undefiled.

The four elements such as the eye are taught before the body element because the objects of the four elements such as the eye are derived from the great elements, whereas the object of the body is not restricted: at times it takes the great elements themselves, earth, water and the rest, as its object; at times it takes what is derived from the great elements, heaviness, lightness and the like; and at times it takes both as its object.

The eye and ear elements are explained before the two, tongue and nose, because they engage distant objects: one sees forms located far away, and one also hears the sound of someone calling from afar. The nose and tongue, by contrast, have objects with which they are in contact; since they cannot apprehend until they meet with the object, they do not engage at a distance. The eye engages at an even greater distance than the ear, since even when one sees water falling from far away, the ear does not hear its sound; therefore the eye is taught even before the ear. The nose is taught before the tongue, because even before the tongue has touched the food, the nose first senses its smell, and so engages more quickly.

Or else: in this body the eye element is located highest; below it is the ear, below that the nose, below that the tongue, and below that it is mostly the body that is located. The mind relies on these very things and is located in no place and in no direction; therefore it is explained last.

Now, the meaning of element is the meaning of kind.
