For example, just as when a single mountain contains many kinds, such as gold and silver, these are called its elements, so too, since this single body contains eighteen kinds, they are called the eighteen elements. Or else, the meaning of element is the meaning of a source: just as a place from which jewels and the like come forth is called an element, so too, since they are the place from which many minds and mental factors come forth, they are called elements.

Since, after the elements, this is the occasion for explaining the sense sources, he therefore said, "Here, monks, what are the twelve sense sources?" and so on.

What, again, is the purpose of teaching the twelve sense sources? They are taught so that one may understand the things to which one is attached through attachment to self and attachment to possessions. When one fully understands them, one gives up attachment. When attachment has been given up, one is not reborn in the three realms. That is the purpose of teaching them. Or else, the twelve sense sources are taught in order to abandon the clinging to a self that thinks, "I see, I hear, I taste, I smell, I touch, and with mental consciousness I cling to phenomena."

Of these, the five from the eye sense source up to the body sense source and the five from the form sense source up to the tangible sense source make ten, and their meaning accords with what was explained in the section on the elements. The six consciousness elements together with the mind element, these seven phenomena, are called the mind sense source. The very seven substances explained in the section on the element of phenomena are called the sense source of phenomena.

Here there is an objection to the statement "The eye is an internal sense source." If there were a self, one could well speak of internal and external, calling internal whatever is governed by the self. But Buddhists hold that all phenomena are without self; if there is no self, how can the eye be called an internal sense source? It is said in reply: there is no contradiction. Designating as "self" the very mind that is the basis of conceit, whatever serves as cause and condition for the mind possessed of conceit is called an internal sense source.

Why, then, are the sense sources set out in that order? The sense sources are set out in that order so as to accord with the conventions of the world. In worldly convention, when people first meet they look at one another; accordingly the eye sense source is explained first. After that, having met, they speak; hence the ear sense source. Then they enjoy bathing, perfumes, garlands and the like; hence the nose sense source. Then they enjoy food and drink; hence the tongue sense source. Then they enjoy beds and seats, women and the like; hence the body sense source. The sense source that abides pervading all of these is explained last.

Why, then, are precisely twelve sense sources taught? Exactly twelve sense sources are explained in order to make plain the six dominant conditions and the six objects of the six consciousnesses. Of these, the sense sources from the eye up to the mind are the dominant condition of consciousness. Those from form up to phenomena are the object condition of consciousness.

What is the meaning of sense source? The meaning of a door of arising is the meaning of sense source: because they act as the cause for minds and mental factors to arise and increase, they are called sense sources.

Here there is an objection: if in the section on the elements eighteen are explained in terms of support and object, and in the section on the sense sources they are explained as support and object, why is what is supported not explained? It is said in reply: the Blessed One teaches the Dharma for the sake of training beings. Since he taught twelve sense sources for the sake of training, for the sake of training he included the consciousnesses within the mind sense source and taught twelve.

Why is it that the very phenomena explained in the section on the aggregates are explained again as elements? Of the eleven kinds of form derived from the great elements that were explained under the aggregate of form among the five aggregates, the five objects and the five faculties are explained as ten elements, from the eye element on, and from the form element up to the tangible element. The aggregate of feeling, the aggregate of perception, the aggregate of formations and non-informative form, from the section on the five aggregates, are taught in the section on the elements as the element of phenomena. The aggregate of consciousness is taught in the section on the elements as the elements from the element of eye consciousness up to the element of mental consciousness. The mind faculty among the aggregates is explained in the section on the elements as the mind element. And the very phenomena explained as elements are explained again in the case of the sense sources. How so? The ten that are, among the elements, the five elements such as the eye element and the five such as the form element, are explained in the section on the sense sources as ten sense sources, from the eye sense source on, and the form sense source on. The seven substances, feeling and the rest, that were taught in the section on the elements as the element of phenomena, are taught in the section on the sense sources as the sense source of phenomena. And the seven explained in the section on the elements, the six such as the element of eye consciousness together with the mind element, are taught in the section on the sense sources as the mind sense source. If one asks why this is so:

There are three kinds of confusion, and in order to dispel them those same phenomena are designated by other names and taught again. The three kinds of confusion are: confusion that takes the mind as a single thing, confusion that takes mind and form as a single thing, and confusion that takes form as a single thing. To dispel the confusion that takes the mind as a single thing, the five aggregates are taught: in the section on the five aggregates, the mind is divided into many and taught as the aggregates of feeling, perception, formations and consciousness. To dispel the confusion that takes mind and form as a single thing, the eighteen elements are explained, because there the eye, form and the rest are taught divided into ten, and the mind is taught divided into many, as the element of eye consciousness and so on and the mind element. To dispel the confusion that takes form as a single thing, the twelve sense sources are explained, because in the section on the sense sources form is explained divided into ten, the eye sense source, the form sense source and so on.

Or else, there are three kinds of wish: the wish for a teaching in brief, the wish for a teaching of middling length, and the wish for an extensive explanation. For the sake of those who wish for a teaching in brief, the five aggregates are explained. For those who wish for a teaching of middling length, the twelve sense sources are explained. For those who wish for an extensive explanation, the eighteen elements are explained. Or else, the aggregates and the rest are taught with regard to those of sharp faculties, those of middling faculties, and those of dull faculties: for the sake of those of sharp faculties, the five aggregates are explained; for the sake of those of middling faculties, the twelve sense sources; and for the sake of those of dull faculties, the eighteen elements.

After the sense sources comes the occasion for teaching dependent origination, and with reference to it he said, "Monks, what are the twelve links of dependent origination?" and so on.

What, again, is the purpose of teaching dependent origination? Because they do not understand dependent origination, beings wander in the prison of saṃsāra, are bound by the tight bonds of the afflictions, and are constantly harmed by the many kinds of suffering, such as birth and aging. When they understand it, the darkness of ignorance clears away, they come forth from the prison of the three realms, and they are freed from the many kinds of suffering. Therefore dependent origination was taught. Or else, dependent origination was taught in order to show that these beings do not arise without a cause, nor from a discordant cause, but arise from ignorance as their cause. Or else, in order to show the cause by which one is born in saṃsāra and how one comes to turn back from saṃsāra, dependent origination was taught with two characteristics, the forward order and the reverse order. So that one may understand the cause by which one is born in saṃsāra, it was taught in the forward order that with ignorance as condition formations arise, and so on. So that one may know how one comes to turn back from saṃsāra, it was taught with the characteristic of the reverse order that when ignorance ceases formations cease, and so on.

Here, what is the meaning of dependent origination? The meaning of "dependent" is the meaning of meeting. The meaning of "origination" is the meaning of arising. When cause and condition meet and a result arises, that is called dependent origination. "Twelve links" means twelve parts.

Or else, by the three words "dependent," "connected" and "origination," three kinds of wrong views are refuted. In order to refute the assertion that phenomena arise without a cause, the word "dependent" is used. Its meaning is that phenomena do not arise without a cause, but arise in dependence on causes and conditions. In order to refute those who assert a discordant cause, saying that phenomena arise from a creator such as Īśvara (the Lord) or a self, the word "connected" is used. Its meaning is that phenomena arise from concordant causes, and do not arise from discordant causes such as Īśvara. In order to refute the assertion of annihilation, the word "origination" is used. Its meaning is that it is not the case that one is not born from this world into the next; rather, one is born for as long as the continuum of karma and afflictions has not been cut.

Or else, the word "dependent" refutes the view of permanence: if phenomena were permanent, they would not need to depend on causes and conditions; since they depend on causes and conditions, they are not permanent. The word "connected" refutes the view that results arise from what one has not done. Its meaning is that whoever performs an action experiences it; it is not that one person performs the action and another experiences its result. The word "origination" refutes the view of annihilation.

If one asks how the meaning of dependent origination is to be understood, it is to be understood in eleven ways: as its characteristic, the condensation of its members, the presentation of the conditions of the members, the presentation of the functions of the members, the condensation of the members under affliction, its meaning, its profundity, its divisions, its forward order, its reverse order, and the detailed explanation of the members.

Of these, as for the characteristic, the characteristic of dependent origination is to be understood in three ways: arising from conditions that are without activity, arising from conditions that are impermanent, and arising from conditions that are potent. Arising from conditions without activity is so because the result merely arises through the mere presence of the conditions, and here there is no agent, nor do the conditions themselves think, "I produce these." Arising from impermanent conditions is so because, since the result arises from a cause, these phenomena are impermanent; if they were permanent, nothing whatsoever would arise from anything. Arising from potent conditions is so because ignorance is able to produce formations, and so on up to birth, which in turn is able to give rise to aging and death.

Of these, as for the full condensation of the members, the twelve members of dependent origination are condensed into five members: the projecting member, the projected member, the actualizing member, the actualized member, and the member of adversity. At the time of the cause, they are set out as two: that by which projection is made and that which is projected. At the time of the result, they are set out as two: that by which actualization is made and that which is actualized.

If one asks by what projection is made: projection is made by the three, ignorance, formations, and consciousness. Through the power of ignorance, the latent tendency of having performed virtuous and non-virtuous karma is imprinted on the mental consciousness, and this projects the aggregates of karmic maturation in another life. If one asks what is projected: name and form, the six sense sources, contact, and feeling are projected, because, through the cause of the three above, name and form and the rest arise, each later one in dependence on the one before it.

The actualizing members are the three, craving, grasping, and becoming. This is because, when one has become attached to the three realms through not having abandoned craving for the objects of sense desire, the latent tendency of karma is strengthened, and so at the time of death it becomes able to ripen its result. The actualized member is birth. Aging and death is the member of adversity.

If one asks how the members are presented as conditions: the members are presented as conditions by way of four kinds of condition: the causal condition, the immediately preceding condition, the objective condition, and the dominant condition. Of these, as for the causal condition, the latent tendency of ignorance, placed in the mental consciousness, itself becomes the cause of formations, because through its power formations are actualized. As for the immediately preceding condition, formations arise when the preceding instant of ignorance has ceased; formations do not arise without ignorance having ceased. As for the objective condition, it is because, owing to complete delusion about things, one attends to them as permanent, blissful, and so forth, and thereby forms karma. As for the dominant condition, it is because, just as eye consciousness is produced through the condition of the eye faculty, so formations arise through the power of ignorance. The same is to be applied to the remaining members as well.

If one asks how the functions of the members are presented: ignorance performs two functions. It deludes sentient beings, since it makes them grasp what is impermanent in things as permanent, and it becomes the condition for formations. Of these, it deludes sentient beings because, obscured by ignorance, they do not understand the past, the future, and the present times, and so they doubt, thinking, "Did I exist in the past, or did I not?" and "Will I come to be in the future, or will I not?" It becomes the condition for formations because, when there is ignorance, through its power one forms the karma of renewed existence.

Formations too perform two functions: they connect sentient beings to the destinies, and they become the condition for consciousness. Of these, they connect sentient beings to the destinies because, when there is karma, through its power sentient beings are born in another life into the five destinies; but when there is no karma, even though afflictions are present, they cannot bring about birth, since they lack their companion. They become the condition for consciousness because, through the power of the latent tendency of karma imprinted on the mental consciousness, the mental consciousness, in the future, enters a mother's womb.

Consciousness too performs two functions: it serves as the support of the karma of sentient beings, and it becomes the condition for name and form. Of these, it becomes the condition for the karma of sentient beings because consciousness holds the seeds of the karma that has been formed. It becomes the condition for name and form because, when consciousness transmigrates into a mother's womb, name and form increase and develop.

Name and form too perform two functions: they take up the body of sentient beings, and they become the condition for the sense sources. They take up the body of sentient beings because through their power the limbs, the sense faculties, and so on gradually emerge, and one is born resembling one's own kind. They become the condition for the six sense sources because, in dependence on the earlier member, name and form, as that develops, the later member, the six sense sources such as the eye, arises.

The six sense sources too perform two functions: they take up the body of sentient beings in its completeness, and they become the condition for contact. They take up the body of sentient beings in its completeness in that the six sense sources being unimpaired is itself called the completeness of a sentient being's body. They become the condition for contact because, when the sense sources such as the eye are present, contact is designated from the meeting of the three: name, form, and consciousness.

Contact too performs two functions: it connects sentient beings with unruly enjoyments, and it becomes the condition for feeling. It connects sentient beings with unruly enjoyments because, when these are present, beings come to experience feelings that are pleasant, painful, and neither pleasant nor painful, like a meal mixed of six kinds of food. Contact becomes the condition for feeling because, when there is contact, meeting with a beautiful object gives rise to a pleasant feeling, meeting with a bad object gives rise to a painful feeling, and meeting with an object that is neither gives rise to a feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful.

Feeling too performs two functions: it establishes sentient beings in the full enjoyment of a life, and it becomes the condition for craving. It establishes sentient beings in the full enjoyment of a life because, through the power of feeling, actions are performed, and by that cause one comes to experience in another life karmic maturations that are pleasing, unpleasing, and so forth. It becomes the condition for craving because, through the power of feeling, there arises attachment to not being parted from pleasant feeling and attachment to being parted from painful feeling.

Craving too performs two functions: it keeps the life continuum of sentient beings from being cut off, and, through the power of craving, the continuum of life in another lifetime is not cut off. It becomes the condition for grasping because, when there is craving, desire arises that longs for the five objects of sense desire such as forms, and one does not let go of them.

Grasping too performs two functions: in granting sentient beings renewed existence it makes consciousness furnished with a cause, and it becomes the condition for becoming. In granting sentient beings renewed existence it makes consciousness furnished with a cause because, when there is grasping, which is of the nature of affliction, consciousness is certain to be born into the destinies such as the hells. It becomes the condition for becoming because, through the power of grasping, karma is formed and deposits its latent tendency in the mental consciousness.

Becoming too performs two functions: it turns sentient beings directly toward renewed existence, and it becomes the condition for birth. It turns sentient beings directly toward renewed existence because, once becoming, whose nature is karma, has been brought about, sentient beings go immediately to another destiny. It becomes the condition for birth in that, through the power of becoming, one takes up another body of the same class of beings.

Birth too performs two functions: it makes sentient beings be born in the sequence of name and form, the six sense sources, contact, feeling, and so on, and it becomes the condition for aging and death. Of these, it makes sentient beings be born in the sequence of name and form, the six sense sources, contact, feeling, and so on because, when consciousness has come to birth in a mother's womb, name and form arise after it, the six sense sources after name and form, and contact and the rest after that, one after another. It becomes the condition for aging and death because aging, whose characteristic is the alteration of the aggregates, and death, whose characteristic is the destruction of the aggregates, are certain to occur.

If one asks what function aging and death perform, each of the two performs one function. Aging makes one's prime decline, and death makes the life faculty change. Of these, it makes one's prime decline because the vigor and the clarity of the faculties belonging to youth deteriorate, and the body becomes heavy and the faculties unclear. It makes the life faculty change because, when the life faculty of one class of beings is cut off, a life faculty arises with another class of beings.

If one asks how the members are condensed under affliction: affliction is of three kinds, the affliction of mental afflictions, the affliction of karma, and the affliction of life. Of these, the three, ignorance, craving, and grasping, are condensed under the affliction of mental afflictions. The two, formations and becoming, are condensed under the affliction of karma. The remaining seven, consciousness and the rest, are condensed under the affliction of life.

Thus these members are set out in three segments: the members belonging to the segment of the former life, the members belonging to the segment of this life, and the members belonging to the segment of the later life. Of these, the members belonging to the segment of the former life are two: ignorance and formations. The members belonging to the segment of this life are eight: consciousness, name and form, the six sense sources, contact, feeling, craving, grasping, and becoming. This is because, karma having been formed in the former life through the power of ignorance, the eight phenomena of this life, consciousness and the rest, are born. Further, the two, birth and aging and death, are the members belonging to the segment of the later life, because, through the power of the afflicted craving and grasping in this life, becoming, whose nature is karma, is brought about, and as a result birth and aging and death occur in another life.

Alternatively, these twelve members of dependent origination should be understood, through the example of a poisonous tree, to be of the nature of affliction and suffering. For example, a poisonous tree, covered with manure whose nature is impure, is planted in a field that supports the seed; and when, through the stages of sprout, stalk, and so forth, it has grown into a great trunk, it does harm of many kinds, such as killing. In the same way, these twelve members too are covered with the manure of ignorance; and when the seed of consciousness, accompanied by defilements, is set in the field of the forming of karma, then from the sprout of name and form come the leaves and stalk of the six sense sources. From the leaves and stalk of the six sense sources comes the full blossom of the flower whose character is contact. From the flower whose character is contact, the fruit whose character is feeling is produced. Then, through the power of being seized by craving that clings to feeling, it is thoroughly moistened by the water of grasping; and from consciousness abiding in the field whose character is becoming, in the future the tree of life is produced. Since from it arise immeasurable sufferings such as aging, death, sorrow, lamentation, unhappiness, and distress, it is called "the arising of this entire mass of suffering."

Alternatively, the twelve members are set out in four segments. Ignorance and formations, belonging to the time of the cause, are members of the former life. Consciousness, name, form, the six sense sources, contact, and feeling are the time of the result; being produced by the former life, they are members of this life. Craving, grasping, and becoming are the time of the cause; they are members of this life that become the cause of the next life. Birth and aging and death are the time of the result; they are members of the next life, produced with this life as their cause.

Alternatively, the twelve members are the basis of five kinds of suffering. The five kinds of suffering are: the basis of suffering at the time of formation, the basis of suffering at the time of the womb, the basis of suffering at the time of engaging with objects, the basis of suffering at the time when suffering fully arises, and the basis of suffering in which suffering arises without interruption.

Of these, ignorance and formations are called the basis of suffering at the time of acting, because even when karma is being formed through the power of ignorance, sufferings of many kinds arise, such as killing, cutting, cold, and heat. Consciousness and name and form are called the basis of suffering at the time of the womb, because even when consciousness has transmigrated into the mother's womb and abides in the form of name and form, between the place of digested and undigested food, sufferings such as one like being crushed in a press arise continually. The three, the six sense sources, contact, and feeling, are called the basis of suffering at the time of engaging with objects, because at that time too one constantly experiences sufferings such as meeting with what is disliked, being parted from what is dear, and not obtaining what one wants. The three, craving, grasping, and becoming, are called the basis of suffering at the time when suffering fully arises, because even when, through the power of becoming, one goes searching for objects of desire, one continually experiences the suffering of not finding wealth, goods, and the like; the suffering of their being hard to guard once found; and, once guarded, when they are lost, the suffering of sorrow and the rest; and these also act as the cause of further sufferings such as birth and aging. Birth and aging and death are called the basis of suffering at the time when suffering arises without interruption, because from birth until death there constantly arise sufferings, from sickness, hunger, thirst, and the like, up to the aging of the aggregates and the cutting off of the formations of life.

If one asks how it is understood by way of its meaning: the meaning of being without an agent, the meaning of having causes, the meaning of being without a sentient being, the meaning of dependence on another, the meaning of being without activity, the meaning of impermanence, the meaning of momentariness, the meaning of the unbroken continuity of cause and result, the meaning of the corresponding appropriation of cause and result, the meaning of the variety of causes and results, and the meaning of the definite allotment of causes and results: these are the meaning of dependent origination.

Of these, the meaning of being without an agent is that for all phenomena there is no agent such as Īśvara, and they are not made by an agent. The meaning of having causes is that, since formations arise from the cause, ignorance, so too aging and death arise from the cause, birth. The meaning of being without a sentient being is that ignorance and the rest are not of the nature of a self as imagined by the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). The meaning of dependence on another is that ignorance and the rest cannot arise by themselves but arise through the power of causes and conditions. The meaning of being without activity is that ignorance does not think, "In this way I produce formations," and formations do not think, "In this way I was born from ignorance." The meaning of impermanence is that ignorance and the rest are not everlasting phenomena. The meaning of momentariness is that, having arisen, they perish without remaining for a second instant.

The meaning of the unbroken continuity of cause and result is that, like the rising and falling of the beam of a balance, the instant of the cause ceases and at the very same time the instant of the result arises. The meaning of the correspondence of cause and result is that not everything arises from everything: for example, from a barley seed a barley sprout arises, and not a sprout of wheat or the like; and from giving comes great wealth, and not otherwise; and so forth. The meaning of the variety of causes and results is that from many causes results of many kinds arise as well: for example, as it is said, "From barley comes barley, from wheat comes wheat, from giving comes wealth, and from moral discipline come the higher realms." The meaning of the definite allotment of causes and results is that it is certain that from non-virtue arises the result of unhappy karmic maturation, and from virtue arises the result of happy karmic maturation.

If one asks in what way dependent origination is profound: dependent origination should be understood to be profound through the profundity of its cause, the profundity of its characteristic, the profundity of its arising, the profundity of its abiding, and the profundity of its operation.

Of these, the profundity of the cause is shown by two kinds of remedy, against those who assert discordant causes and against those who assert no cause: by the meaning of being without an agent and by the meaning of having causes. Of these, the meaning of being without an agent is taught as the remedy for those who assert that things arise from discordant causes such as Īśvara. The meaning of having causes is taught as the remedy for those who assert that things arise from no cause.

The profundity of the characteristic is that all dependent origination is without self, and this is shown by the single meaning called "being without a sentient being."

The profundity of arising is that those phenomena of dependent origination arise from conditions, and yet the cause is not the agent of the result. This is shown by the two meanings, dependence on another and being without activity. Of these, since the result arises through the power of the conditions, this is the meaning of dependence on another. Yet the conditions are not agents, and they do not think, "I produce this"; this is the meaning of being without activity.

The profundity of abiding is that even phenomena that do not abide appear to abide. This is shown by the meaning of impermanence and the meaning of momentariness, because, though they are not everlasting and perish in each single instant, they appear as if they abided.

The profundity of operation is that the unbroken arising of cause and result is hard to understand. This is shown in four ways: by the meaning of the unbroken continuity of cause and result, the meaning of the corresponding appropriation of cause and result, the meaning of the variety of causes and results, and the meaning of the definite allotment of causes and results.

The divisions are to be understood as ten: the division of the arising of consciousness, the division of birth and death, the division of the arising of outer grain and the like, the division of formation and destruction, the division taught by way of food, the division of pleasant and unpleasant destinies, the division of purification, and the division of power.

Of these, the division of the arising of consciousness is the teaching that begins, "In dependence on the eye and form, eye consciousness arises." The division of birth and death, with reference to the world of sentient beings, is the series from "With ignorance as condition, formations," up to "With birth as condition, aging and death." The division of the arising of outer grain and the like is the series from "With the seed as condition, the sprout," up to "With the flower as condition, the fruit." The division of formation and destruction is the teaching that begins, "Through the power of the shared karma of all sentient beings, on top of the wind maṇḍala there is the water maṇḍala, and on top of that the golden maṇḍala."

The division taught by way of food is that the sentient beings of the three realms subsist in dependence on four kinds of food. The four foods are morsel food, the food of contact, the food of mental attention, and the food of meditative concentration. Of these, in the desire realm, humans, animals, pretas, and the like mostly depend on morsel food. Animals such as birds, at the time when they are eggs and chicks, depend on the food of contact, the warmth of their parents. As for the food of mental attention, there are some sentient beings who do not die so long as they are not separated from attending in their minds to food. For example, once in the past, at the time of a great famine, a parent, in front of their child, hung up a bag filled with ashes high in the air, said, "There is flour in here; when I come back I will give it to you," and went away. The child did not die for six months; but later, when the child untied it and looked, and saw that it was ashes, the child died. As for the food of meditative concentration, the sentient beings of the form and formless realms, although they do not depend on food, live for many eons through the power of the taste of meditative concentration.

The division of pleasant and unpleasant destinies is being born in the happy destinies in dependence on well-performed actions, and being born in the lower destinies in dependence on badly performed actions.

The division of purification is this: in dependence on the sound of another's explanation, proper attention arises; in dependence on proper attention, the roots of virtue conducive to liberation arise; from the roots of virtue conducive to liberation, the four aids to penetration arise; in dependence on these, the path of seeing arises; in dependence on that, the path of meditation; and from the path of meditation, the result of arhatship is attained.

The division of power is that, in dependence on the realization of the noble truths and on meditative absorption, the six superknowledges and the like arise, and the totality spheres and the like arise.

If one asks how the forward order and the reverse order are to be viewed: of these, the forward order is the side of affliction, namely the series from "With ignorance as condition, formations," up to "With birth as condition, aging and death," and so forth. Of these, not knowing the Four Noble Truths correctly, just as they are, is ignorance. Alternatively, one does not see things that exist as impermanent, suffering, impure, and without self, but sees things as existing as permanent, blissful, pure, and a self, which they are not; this is called ignorance.

In "With ignorance as condition, formations," the formations are of three kinds: formations leading to non-merit, formations leading to merit, and formations leading to the immovable. Of these, the ten non-virtues such as taking life and taking what is not given, actions that, through clinging to the five objects of desire of this life with the thought, "Let me be happy in this life," and through delusion about the results of karma in other lives, cause one to be born in the three lower realms within the desire realm, are called formations leading to non-merit.

Clinging to the result, the happiness of gods and humans in other lives, and understanding that "through the ten virtuous paths of action one is born among gods and humans," one performs the ten virtuous paths of action, such as not taking life and not taking what is not given, which cause one to be born among the gods of the desire realm; these are called formations leading to merit. When, through attachment to the taste of absorption in meditative absorption in other lives, one comes to meditative absorption whose characteristic is one-pointedness of mind, that which causes one to be born in the form and formless realms is called formations leading to the immovable.

These three kinds of karma are abandoned by means of four kinds of result. The four results are the result of stream-entry, the result of once-returning, the result of non-returning, and the result of arhatship. The result of stream-entry abandons the formations leading to non-merit, because once the result of stream-entry has been attained, there is no possibility of being born in the three lower realms. The results of once-returning and non-returning abandon the formations leading to merit: the once-returner abandons the karma that causes birth among the gods and humans of the desire realm, and the non-returner, having abandoned the karma of birth in the desire realm, is called a non-returner because he is not born again in the desire realm. The result of arhatship abandons the formations leading to the immovable, because the result of arhatship is attained by abandoning the latent tendency of karma that clings to the meditative concentrations up to the Peak of Existence.

Of these, since the three kinds of karma are formed when there is ignorance, it is said, "With ignorance as condition, formations." Ignorance becomes the condition for formations as causal condition, immediately preceding condition, objective condition, and dominant condition. For the remaining members as well, it should be understood that the former acts as the condition of the latter by way of the four conditions. How they become conditions by way of the four conditions should be viewed as explained before.

When the three kinds of karma have thus been formed, the mental consciousness first enters a place of birth, such as a mother's womb, and swoons; this is called "With formations as condition, consciousness." When the mental consciousness has thus swooned in the mother's womb in the first instant, from the second instant onward the blood and semen of the father and mother and so forth mingle, and at the time of the embryonic stages called arbuda and kalala, this is called name and form. Of these, the four formless aggregates, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness, are name. The stages of arbuda and kalala are form.

Then, having passed beyond the arbuda and kalala stages, the sense faculties such as the eye and the ear emerge in the mother's womb, and the six faculties come to emerge each in its own place; this is called "With name and form as condition, the six sense sources." Then, once the faculties have emerged, faculty, object, and consciousness come to meet at one time; this is called "With the six sense sources as condition, contact."

Then there arises pleasant feeling, in which, meeting with a pleasing object, body and mind are refreshed; painful feeling, in which, meeting with a displeasing object, body and mind are tormented; and the feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful, in which, meeting with an object that is neither pleasing nor displeasing, body and mind are neither refreshed nor tormented. This is called "With contact as condition, feeling."

Having thus come to feeling, there arises attachment to meeting with pleasant feeling and attachment to being parted from painful feeling; this is called "With feeling as condition, craving." When there is thus craving, one takes up the things that serve as the requisites of pleasant feeling; this is called "With craving as condition, grasping." When there is thus the host of afflictions, craving, grasping, and so on, one forms virtuous and non-virtuous karma; this is called "With grasping as condition, becoming." When there are thus karma and afflictions, the mental consciousness transmigrates into this or that place of birth, and the aggregates, elements, sense sources, and so on come to arise; this is called "With becoming as condition, birth."

When one has thus been born, then, just as a tree that has grown is burned by fire or carried away by water, there arise aging, whose characteristic is the wearing out of the aggregates, and death, whose characteristic is the cutting off of the formations of life; this is called "With birth as condition, aging and death." Since sorrow, lamentation, and the rest are included in the member of aging and death, dependent origination, from "With ignorance as condition, formations" up to "With birth as condition, aging and death," comes down to twelve members.

Of these, aging is of two kinds: the decline of the support and the decline of what is supported. The decline of the support is that, through the decline of the four great elements belonging to the aggregate of form, wrinkles, white hair, and the like appear, and the body becomes heavy in standing up and sitting down. The decline of what is supported is that, the faculties having thus declined, the consciousnesses that depend on them also see and hear forms, sounds, and the like unclearly, and one forgets what one did and said before.

Death is of two kinds: death in due time and untimely death. Of these, death in due time is when the force of the karma of the formations of life that sustain one in this life is exhausted, and warmth, consciousness, and life leave the body. Untimely death is dying suddenly when struck by others with weapons, clubs, and the like, or being killed suddenly when, through craving for evil things, one is taken over by asuras and the like; this is untimely death.

The torment of mind born from being parted from what is dear and meeting with what is disliked is sorrow. Words uttered from the cause of sorrow are lamentation. The harm that comes to the body when the great elements are disturbed is suffering. The harm that comes to the mind at that time is unhappiness. When, remembering harm done by enemies and the like, one thinks, "I have not repaid that harm," and body and mind are harmed, this is called distress.

As for the words, "Thus this entire great mass of suffering arises," as explained above, from ignorance up to aging and death it is nothing but a great collection of suffering. Since there is no self or the like, it is said that it "entirely" arises.

Objection: Since no cause of ignorance is taught, saṃsāra would have a beginning; and since no result of aging and death is taught, saṃsāra would have an end. Or else another link would have to be explained, and another for that one, and yet another for that one would have to be stated, so that the fault of infinite regress would follow. Therefore it is not even proper to state it as twelve links.

Reply: The Blessed One has already taught, in this very dependent origination, that it has neither beginning nor end. How so? Affliction arises from affliction: with craving as condition, grasping arises. Action arises from affliction: with ignorance as condition, formations arise, and with grasping as condition, becoming arises. Suffering arises from action: with formations as condition, consciousness arises, and with becoming as condition, birth arises. Suffering arises from suffering: from consciousness, name and form arise; from the six sense sources, contact arises; and from birth, aging and death arise. Affliction also arises from what is a mere thing, because from feeling, craving arises. Since the Blessed One taught the pattern of the link of craving in this way, ignorance too arises from some other affliction or thing, and so there is no beginning to birth. And since from the thing that is aging and death, too, an affliction or a thing arises, it is established that there is no end, that there is no further link, and that nothing further needs to be stated.

In some sūtras it is explained that improper attention is the cause of ignorance, and that ignorance too is the cause of improper attention. This too is already included within the pattern taught above, of affliction arising from affliction, affliction arising from action, and so on, and so it is not explained separately.

Or else: in the Abhidharma treatises, to the question "What is dependent origination?" the answer is "All conditioned phenomena." Thus dependent origination is explained as of two kinds: that which is presented as pertaining to sentient beings and that which is presented as not pertaining to sentient beings. Why does this sūtra explain only that which pertains to sentient beings? Likewise, in the treatise Jñānaprasthāna, dependent origination is taught in two ways: as characterized by a continuum and as characterized by a moment. Of these, that characterized by a continuum is just what was explained above: "With ignorance as condition, formations," and so on. That characterized by a moment is the twelve-linked dependent origination occurring in a single moment. How so? It is like this. When, under the power of attachment, one takes the life of living beings, the delusion about action and the karmic maturation of action is ignorance. Whatever collection of mental factors is present at that time is formations. The six consciousnesses, eye consciousness and the rest, separately apprehending things at that time, are consciousness. The four aggregates, feeling and the rest, arising together with consciousness, the four great elements, and the forms derived from the elements are name and form. The six faculties, eye and the rest, based on name and form, are the six sense sources. Seeing the objects of the six sense sources is contact. Experiencing pleasure or suffering at the time of contact is feeling. Attachment to flesh and skin at that time is craving. The other entanglements by the afflictions accompanying it are grasping. The mental action produced by grasping at that time is becoming. The arising of those factors, such as the movement of the hand, is birth. Their ripening by turning into another character is aging. The destruction of those factors is death.

In reply: Since the explanation in this sūtra has an underlying intention, there is no fault in its not explaining dependent origination as not pertaining to sentient beings. In order to dispel the delusion of some of those to be trained about entering saṃsāra and turning back from it, what is taught here is only that which pertains to sentient beings, cause and result each producing the next, as in "With ignorance as condition, formations," and so on. As for explaining here the twelve-linked dependent origination characterized by a moment: beginners, hearing that the twelve links exist in a single moment, would be unable to comprehend it. Therefore, for their sake, the Blessed One explained it as connected in terms of a continuum.

Alternatively, thinking accompanied by unhappiness is sorrow. Uttering words at the time of death such as "Alas, father! Alas, mother!" is lamentation. Discomfort connected with the group of the five consciousnesses is suffering. Discomfort in the mental consciousness is unhappiness. When, preceded by sorrow and lamentation, body and mind become weary and exhausted, this is called distress.

"Thus" means as in the words explained above. Because from beginningless time in saṃsāra there is an endless succession of suffering, and because it is a collection of suffering, it is called "a great mass of suffering." Since there is no self and nothing belonging to a self, it is "entire." "Arises" means that it originates in that way.

In order to teach the reverse order, which is the character of purification, it says, "With the cessation of ignorance, formations cease," and so on. Both the forward order and the reverse order are taught because some, not seeing the origin of becoming, cling to annihilation, and to dispel that, the forward order is taught; while some are attached to permanence, and to remove their failure to see the character of the cessation of becoming, the reverse order is taught. Thus, by the two paths of affliction and purification, bondage and liberation are taught.

There, just as when a lamp appears darkness disappears, so when the knowledge arises that sees outer and inner phenomena as impermanent, suffering, impure, and without self, this is called the cessation of ignorance. Just as, for example, without fire smoke does not arise, so when ignorance has ceased, actions not yet done are not formed, and actions being undertaken cannot be carried out, because they lack their assistant.

This is what is meant by "With the cessation of ignorance, formations cease." It is like a single person who, without companions, cannot drag a great tree trunk. And just as, for example, when there is no field there is no support for the seed, so the seed rests on nothing at all, so too, when there are no formations there is no support for consciousness, and so consciousness too comes to be absent. Therefore it is called "With the cessation of formations, consciousness ceases."

Just as, for example, when a seed is burnt no sprout grows, so when the mind has been burnt and made nonexistent by wisdom regarding consciousness, the sprout of name and form does not arise. Therefore it is called "With the cessation of consciousness, name and form cease." Or else, just as when three sticks stand with their tips leaning together, if one stick is missing the other two cannot stand, so consciousness is the support of name and form, and without consciousness the two, name and form, cannot stand. Therefore it is called "With the cessation of consciousness, name and form cease."

Just as, for example, when there is no mirror its reflected brightness does not appear, so when there is no name and form, the brightness of name and form, the eye sense source made of clear form and the rest, does not arise. Therefore it is called "With the cessation of name and form, the six sense sources cease."

Just as, for example, when there is no wall the painting is destroyed, so when the six sense sources, which serve as the support, are absent, contact, which depends on them, comes to be absent. Therefore it is called "With the cessation of the six sense sources, contact ceases."

Just as, for example, when a mine of copper or the like is exhausted and gone, the substances to be produced from it are not produced either, so when contact, the source of feeling, is absent, feeling does not arise. Therefore it is called "With the cessation of contact, feeling ceases."

Just as, for example, when there is no lamp no light appears, so when there is no feeling, attachment does not arise. Thus it is called "With the cessation of feeling, craving ceases."

Just as, if a first son is not born, his later growing up and increasing, as a youth, in the prime of life, and so on, does not take place, so when craving is absent, its increase and growth, grasping, does not arise. This is called "With the cessation of craving, grasping ceases."

Just as, for example, when there is no wood, fire does not arise because its cause is absent, so when affliction, the cause of action, is absent, action is not formed. Therefore it is called "With the cessation of grasping, becoming ceases."

Just as, for example, when the cause of an illness is removed the illness is gone, so when action and affliction, the causes of birth, are removed, birth does not occur. Therefore it is called "With the cessation of becoming, birth ceases."

Just as, for example, if a pot has not been made, its cracking, breaking and so on do not occur, and if a tree has not grown, being burnt in a forest fire does not occur, and if there is no grove, being swept away by water does not occur, so when there is no birth, the sufferings of aging, death and so on do not arise. Therefore it is said, from "With the cessation of birth" up to "this entire great mass of suffering ceases."

With regard to explaining the links in detail, it says, "Here, what is ignorance?" and so on. Here, the negating word in "ignorance," that is, non-knowledge, can carry four meanings: smallness, disparagement, opposition, and absence. As for its carrying the meaning of smallness: for example, a girl with a slender waist is called "waistless." As for its carrying the meaning of disparagement: for example, a bad son is called "no son." As for its carrying the meaning of absence: for example, one says "no food," "no water." As for its carrying the meaning of contradiction: for example, one who is at odds with his companions is called "no friend." Here it should be seen as carrying the meaning of contradiction: what belongs to the side opposed to knowledge is called ignorance, non-knowledge.

In order to teach its characteristic in detail, it says, "Not knowing the prior end," and so on. Here, the prior end is the previous life, and not knowing that there is a previous life is not knowing the prior end. Or else, improperly attending to the formations of past time, thinking, "Did I exist in past time, or did I not exist in past time?" is not knowing the prior end.

As for "not knowing the later end": the later end refers to the next life, in future time; it is not knowing that there is a next life. Or else, improperly conceiving of the formations of future time, thinking, "Will I exist in the future, or will I not exist in the future? Who will I be in the future? How will I be?" is not knowing the later end.

As for "not knowing both the prior and the later end": the prior and later end refers to this present life. Not knowing, in this life, the characteristics of inner and outer phenomena as they are is called not knowing both the prior and the later end. Or else, not knowing the succession that arises through the three times, past, future and present, is called not knowing both the prior and the later end: that is, not knowing that one was born in this life from the actions and afflictions of a previous life, and that the next life will arise from the actions and afflictions of this life. Or else, conceiving improperly about other sentient beings, and being in doubt, thinking, "What does this one have? What is this? From where did these sentient beings come here, and where will they be born after dying and passing from here?" is called not knowing both the prior and the later end.

As for "not knowing the inner": the inner is one's own body, and not knowing that it is of the nature of the five aggregates is called not knowing the inner. Or else, improperly conceiving of what belongs to one's own inner sense sources, thinking, "This is the self," is called not knowing the inner.

As for "not knowing the outer": the outer is form, sound and the like, and it is not knowing that they are of the nature of tiny particles. Or else, imagining outer phenomena not counted as sentient beings to be one's own is called not knowing the outer.

As for "not knowing both the outer and the inner": the outer and inner refers to the formations belonging to the continuum of others. Improperly classing them as friends, enemies and neutrals is not knowing both the outer and the inner.

Not knowing the characteristics of meritorious, non-meritorious and unmoving formations is called not knowing action. Or else, improperly conceiving that action has an agent is called not knowing action.

As for "not knowing maturation": not knowing the nature of the results of those actions, the three lower realms, gods, humans and so on, is called not knowing maturation. As for "not knowing the maturation of action": it is not knowing that if this action is done, this maturation ripens; that is, not knowing that if nonvirtuous action is done it matures in the three lower realms, and if virtuous action is done it matures as gods, humans and so on, is called not knowing maturation. Or else, improperly conceiving that the results of maturation will be experienced by a self or by an experiencer is called not knowing maturation. Wrongly attending to action, the results of action and so on is called not knowing the maturation of action, as for example thinking, "If one kills, one is born in the higher realms," and the like.

Not knowing that refraining from killing, refraining from taking what is not given, and so on are virtuous is not knowing actions well done. Not knowing that killing, taking what is not given and so on are nonvirtuous because of the deed is not knowing actions badly done. Taking what is not given and then giving it to others is a mixed action: not knowing that it is neither purely a virtuous action nor purely a nonvirtuous one is called not knowing actions both well and badly done.

As for "not knowing causes": there are six kinds of cause, namely the homogeneous cause, the pervasive cause, the co-existent cause, the associated cause, the efficient cause, and the maturation cause. Not knowing these six is called not knowing causes.

Of these, the homogeneous cause is this: the phenomena of the desire realm to be abandoned by seeing suffering that arose earlier are the homogeneous cause of the phenomena of the desire realm to be abandoned by seeing suffering that arise later and that will arise. Further, when later roots of virtue grow and arise on the basis of some roots of virtue that existed before, that too is called a homogeneous cause. For example, from the roots of virtue of seeing suffering, the knowledge that sees suffering arises, and likewise, from the roots of virtue of seeing the path, the knowledge that sees the path arises. The four truths of the form and formless realms are to be applied in the same way, each in turn.

What is the pervasive cause? It applies only to afflictive phenomena. Afflictive phenomena that arose earlier are the pervasive cause of the afflictions of their own level that arise later and that will arise. For example, what is associated with wrong view in the desire realm, having arisen earlier, acts as the pervasive cause of the wrong thought, wrong speech, wrong course of action, and wrong feeling that arise later and that will arise.

There, what is the co-existent cause? It is this: when the great elements arise, they depend on one another, each mutually producing the other; and when mind and mental factors arise, they too arise in mutual dependence on one another. This is the co-existent cause.

What is the associated cause? Virtuous, nonvirtuous and indeterminate mind and mental factors are associated causes for one another. A virtuous mind is associated with virtuous mental factors, not with nonvirtuous mental factors; likewise, nonvirtuous and indeterminate mind and mental factors are each associated with their own kind.

There, the efficient cause is that which, when it is present, the other arises, and when it is absent, the other does not arise: when there is a seed a sprout arises, and when there is no seed no sprout arises. Or else, some explain that every phenomenon other than the thing itself that does not obstruct it is its efficient cause; for example, when consciousness arises from the meeting of the eye and its object, all phenomena that do not obstruct it or create an obstacle are its efficient cause.

There, what is the maturation cause? Nonvirtuous phenomena and contaminated virtue project maturation into the lower realms and the rest, and so they are called maturation causes. Indeterminate phenomena, although they do produce concordant results, are weak like a rotten seed and so lack the power to bring maturation; therefore they are not called maturation causes.

Or else, conceiving that things have no cause, or conceiving that their cause is Īśvara, primal nature, the person, or the like, is called not knowing causes.

As for "not knowing results": there are five kinds of result, namely the result made by a person, the concordant result, the maturation result, the dominant result, and the result of separation. Not knowing these five is called not knowing results.

Of these, the result made by a person is whatever result arises from the power of whatever cause; for example, the fruits such as grain that arise from the power of the farmers' own labor are called the result made by a person. The concordant result is one that is made similar to its cause: from a former cause of virtue, the very practice of virtue matures as the result in the future too, and from a former cause of nonvirtue, the very practice of nonvirtue likewise matures as the result. The maturation result is the maturing of the aggregates as gods, humans, hell beings and so on, caused by virtuous and nonvirtuous actions. The dominant result is, for example, eye consciousness arising unimpeded through the power of the eye faculty. The result of separation is the attainment of nirvāṇa by abandoning the afflictions through the power of cultivating the noble path.

As for "not knowing cause and result": not knowing how cause and result unfold, that if this cause is made, this result arises, is not knowing cause and result.

As for "not knowing phenomena arisen from causes": the five aggregates, the eighteen elements, the twelve sense sources and so on are phenomena arisen from causes, whereas cessation through analysis, cessation without analysis, and space are not phenomena arisen from causes. Not knowing this is one way. Or else, outer phenomena such as sprouts and leaves are phenomena arisen from seeds, and inner phenomena, those counted as sentient beings, are likewise of the nature of formations arisen from the cause that is ignorance; they are not born from Īśvara or from a creator. Not knowing this is called not knowing phenomena arisen from causes.

"Not knowing dependent origination" is not knowing how the twelve links of inner dependent origination, from "with ignorance as condition, formations" up to "with birth as condition, aging and death" and so on, arise from one life to the next. "Not knowing dependently originated phenomena" is not knowing that outer dependent origination, from seed to sprout and from flower to fruit, is produced by the seed, the four great elements, the season and so on.

As for "not knowing the Buddha": it is not knowing the phenomenon of the noble path by which one becomes a buddha. Or else, not knowing in this way that the Buddha, the Blessed One, is "the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, endowed with knowledge and conduct," and so forth, and doubting whether he is like that or not, is not knowing the Buddha.

As for "not knowing the Dharma": the Dharma is of three kinds, the Dharma to be explained, the Dharma to be practiced, and the Dharma that is the result. Of these, the Dharma to be explained is the twelve branches of scripture. The Dharma to be practiced is the thirty-seven factors of awakening. The Dharma that is the result is nirvāṇa. Not knowing these three, and doubting them, is called not knowing the Dharma.

As for "not knowing the Saṅgha": the Saṅgha is of two kinds, the noble ones who are persons still in training and the noble ones who are persons beyond training. Of these, the noble ones still in training are the seven, from the stream-enterer up to the one entering upon arhatship. The noble ones beyond training are those who have attained the fruit of arhatship. Not knowing them in the way set out below, "The Saṅgha of the Blessed One's śrāvakas is perfect in faith, perfect in moral discipline, perfect in learning," and so on, and doubting them, is not knowing the Saṅgha.

"Not knowing suffering" is not knowing that the five contaminated aggregates are endowed with the sufferings of birth, aging and so on. Not knowing the origin is called not knowing that actions and afflictions are the causes that produce the aggregates. "Not knowing cessation" is not knowing that nirvāṇa is peace, sublime, and bliss. Not knowing the path is not knowing the noble eightfold path.

As for "not knowing virtuous and nonvirtuous phenomena": virtue is so called because it has gone far from blameworthy things. In the word for virtue, kuśala, kuśa refers to wisdom and la refers to taking: because it takes up the cause of wisdom, it is virtue. The opposite of that is nonvirtue. Not knowing the causes of virtuous and nonvirtuous phenomena is called not knowing virtuous and nonvirtuous phenomena. Or else, virtue is so called because it is blameless, and nonvirtue because it is blameworthy; not knowing them in this way is not knowing virtuous and nonvirtuous phenomena.

As for "blameworthy" and "blameless": a thing to be censured is blameworthy, which means "accompanied by wrongdoing." When things to be censured have been abandoned, that is blameless, which means "accompanied by goodness." Further, the blameworthy is so because it belongs to the dark side, and the blameless because it belongs to the bright side. Not knowing these is called not knowing the blameworthy and the blameless.

As for "not knowing phenomena to be cultivated and not to be cultivated": what proves beneficial, what is to be cultivated, is uncontaminated virtue and the unconditioned. By cultivating it one attains liberation, and so it is a phenomenon to be cultivated. Phenomena that prove harmful are those not to be cultivated, namely nonvirtuous actions that incline one toward the three lower realms. Not knowing these phenomena is called not knowing phenomena to be cultivated and not to be cultivated.

As for "the six sense sources of contact" and so on: eye consciousness merely arises in dependence on the eye and form, and mental consciousness merely arises in dependence on the mind and mental phenomena. Thus, although there is no self that acts, clinging to the existence of a self and the like and being proud about it is called not knowing the six sense sources of contact as they truly are.

Expressions such as "not clearly realizing what is blameworthy" should be understood either as synonyms for "not knowing" itself, or as later words explaining the earlier ones.

In brief, these twenty-eight phrases teach seven kinds of delusion: delusion about time, delusion about things, delusion about transmigration, delusion about the supreme, delusion about reality, delusion about affliction and purification, and delusion through conceit. Of these, the three phrases from "not knowing the prior end" to "not knowing both the prior and the later end" teach delusion about time. The three from "not knowing the inner" to "not knowing both the outer and the inner" teach delusion about things. The phrases from "not knowing action" up to "not knowing dependently originated phenomena" teach delusion about transmigration. The three phrases from "not knowing the Buddha" to "not knowing the Saṅgha" teach delusion about the supreme. The four phrases from "not knowing suffering" to "not knowing the path" teach delusion about reality. The phrases from "not knowing virtuous and nonvirtuous phenomena" up to "not knowing phenomena not to be cultivated" teach delusion about the phenomena of affliction and purification. "Not knowing the six sense sources of contact" teaches the delusion of conceit.

Since there are in this way seven kinds of delusion, seven synonyms for ignorance are also mentioned, beginning with "not knowing." Delusion about time is called "not knowing." Delusion about things is called "not seeing." Delusion about transmigration is called "not clearly realizing." Delusion about the supreme is called "darkness." Delusion about reality is called "bewilderment." Delusion about affliction and purification is called "ignorance." Delusion through conceit is called "pitch darkness."

Or else, wisdom is of three kinds: the wisdom of hearing, the wisdom of reflection, and the wisdom of meditation. Not knowing these as they truly are is called "not knowing." Having shown the nature of ignorance: since that ignorance is the side opposed to the wisdom of hearing, it is "not seeing." Since it is the side opposed to the wisdom of reflection, it is "not clearly realizing." Since it is the foremost of what opposes the wisdom of meditation, it is "darkness." The ignorance that opposes the three kinds of wisdom is also of three kinds: lesser, middling and great. Of these, lesser ignorance is called "bewilderment," middling ignorance is called "ignorance," and great ignorance is called "pitch darkness." The link of ignorance has now been explained.

Next comes the occasion for explaining the link of formations, and so there is the question, "With ignorance as condition, what are formations?" and so on. Teaching just that, it says, "There are three formations: bodily formations," and so on. In brief, this teaches the following: the bodily, verbal and mental actions produced by a mind characterized as meritorious, non-meritorious or unmoving are called formations. Of these, the non-meritorious formations are blameworthy bodily, verbal and mental actions that harm both oneself and others in this life and in other lives, and that lead to the experience of painful feeling. Blameless bodily, verbal and mental actions that harm neither oneself nor others in this life and in other lives, and that bring the experience of pleasant feeling, are meritorious formations. The blameless actions of yogins, which harm neither oneself nor others in this life and in other lives, and which bring the experience of feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful, are the unmoving formations.

In order to explain in detail the three formations mentioned only in brief, it says, "Here, what are bodily formations?" and so on. Exhalation is the breath going out, that is, the air that moves outward. Inhalation is the breath going in, that is, the air that enters inward. "Properties of the body": exhaling, inhaling, and the moving and stirring of the hands and feet are the nature of bodily action, and so they are called properties of the body. Just as, for example, eye consciousness arises while resting on the eye faculty, so exhalation and inhalation too rest on the body, and so they are called "dependent on the body." Just as, for example, when there is a body a shadow arises, the body and the shadow being connected, so too when there is a body, exhalation and inhalation arise; since the two are mutually connected, they are called "connected with the body." Just as, for example, when there is no body the shadow disappears, so too when this body is absent, exhalation and inhalation come to be absent, and so they are called "proceeding while resting on the body."

Explaining exhalation and inhalation as bodily formations is merely by way of illustration; virtuous bodily actions and the like, produced by virtuous and other intentions, are also taught to be bodily action. Otherwise virtuous bodily action and nonvirtuous bodily action would not be bodily formations. In order to show that this is taught by applying the name of the result to the cause, it says, "Exhalation and," and so on. The cause of exhalation and inhalation is the body. Whatever movement there is of the body is called bodily action, and exhalation and inhalation too arise from the body; so, applying the name of the cause, the body, to the result, exhalation and inhalation, exhalation and inhalation are called bodily formations.

To teach the verbal formations, it says, "What are verbal formations?" and so on. As for "conceptual thought," thinking coarsely is called conceptual thought. Thinking finely is analysis. Since one speaks in words after having thought, conceptual thought and analysis, the causes from which words arise, are called verbal formations. "Without conceptual thought and" means that one does not speak in words without thinking coarsely and thinking finely.

Objection: Speech is of the nature of the four great elements, arising from air, while conceptual thought and analysis are mental factors. How then can conceptual thought and analysis be called verbal formations?

Reply: Here too the result is applied to the cause. Here too, virtuous and other conceptual thought and analysis are the causes that produce speech. Therefore, applying the result, speech, to the cause, conceptual thought and analysis, conceptual thought and analysis are also explained as verbal formations. This is seen in the world and in scripture as well. For example, in the world one says, "Fire is pleasant," and in scripture too it says, "The arising of a buddha is bliss." Fire, being of the nature of burning, is not pleasant, yet because its result, warmth, gives rise to pleasure, one says, "Fire is pleasant." And although, since all birth is explained as suffering, there is no bliss in the arising of a buddha, yet because from the cause, the arising of a buddha, comes the result that sentient beings pass into nirvāṇa and attain the bliss of the higher realms, one says, "The arising of a buddha is bliss."

To teach the mental formations, it says, "What are mental formations?" and so on. "A mind of attachment": clinging to one's body and possessions is a mind of attachment. Anger at sentient beings and at things that are not sentient beings is a mind of aversion. Not knowing action, the results of action and the like is a mind of delusion. Whatever attachment a living being has through a mind of attachment, that is meant.

Likewise, whatever aversion there is through a mind with aversion, and that mind possessed of delusion through a mind with bewilderment, is called mental formation.

"These mental factors": the three minds of attachment, aversion and delusion are not mind itself; they are factors that arise from mind, and therefore it says "these mental factors." Their explanation is "dependent on mind." Their explanation, further, is "having mind as cause." Their explanation, further, is "associated with mind." Their explanation, further, is "abiding in mind, they arise."

Alternatively, mind acts as a cause for the mental factors in five ways: generating, supporting, sustaining, stabilizing and increasing. "Mental factors" teaches generating, because if there is no mind, mental factors do not arise either, and when mind arises, mental factors arise as well. "Dependent on mind" teaches supporting, because if mind declines, the mental factors also decline. "Having mind as cause" teaches sustaining, because as long as that mind exists, the mental factor of which it is the cause also remains. "Associated with mind" teaches stabilizing, because mind constantly props up and accompanies the mental factors. "Abiding in mind, they arise" teaches increasing, because when mind flourishes, the mental factors flourish too.

In the desire and form realms, all three are present: bodily, verbal and mental formations. In the formless realm only mental formation is present.

Objection: In other sūtras the Blessed One (the Buddha) explained as formations that by which one practiced virtue and so forth in a past time. Here, since action in general, past, future and present, is explained as formations, there is a contradiction. Reply: although here it is stated in general, the distinction is to be understood from the force of the explanation. Only the virtuous and other actions done in a past time are to be understood as formations; taken thus, it does not conflict with the other scriptures.

Having explained formations, in order to teach the limb of consciousness it says, "Conditioned by formations, consciousness," and so on. Here, cognizing the characteristic of form by relying on the eye faculty and taking form as its object is eye consciousness; apply the same to the remaining consciousnesses. Although this is stated in general, not every consciousness is to be called "conditioned by formations." The intent is that the very mental consciousness which, possessed of accumulated latent tendencies, first links to rebirth in the mother's womb is the consciousness conditioned by formations. If it were not so, and every consciousness were meant, it would contradict the Blessed One's explanation in other sūtras that the swooning mental consciousness at the moment of linking to rebirth in the mother's womb is the limb of consciousness. How is that evident? As in the sūtra: "Nanda, if consciousness had not entered, would name and form arise as the kalala and so on?" "No, Lord." There the intent is mental consciousness.

In the desire realm all six consciousnesses are present. In the form realm, because beings are free of attachment to food eaten in morsels, there is no nose or tongue consciousness, but the four consciousnesses of eye, ear, body and mind are present. In the formless realm, because beings are free of attachment to form, there are no five sense faculties, and so there are no five consciousnesses such as that of the eye; it is explained that only mental consciousness is present.

Having explained consciousness, in order to teach the limb of name and form it says, "Conditioned by consciousness, name and form," and so on. Here, because it makes one incline toward another destiny, it is name; the meaning is that it makes one go to another place of birth. Or else, because it takes things such as form as its objects, it is name; the meaning is that it takes up objects. Because it is obstructive and can be harmed, it is form. When the two are combined into one, it is called name and form.

In order to explain in detail the name and form that were taught in brief, it says, "Name is the four aggregates that are not form," and so on. Experiencing the results of virtuous and nonvirtuous actions is feeling. Grasping the signs of objects is perception. Conditioning the mind is formation. Discerning objects individually is consciousness. These four aggregates that are not form are called name.

As for the form mentioned above, since one does not know what form is or how many kinds there are, doubt arises, and so it says, "What is form?" and so on. It says, "Whatever form there is, all of it is the four great elements and," and so on. "Whatever form there is" means past form, future form, present form, internal form, external form and so forth; all of these are included in two kinds: form that is the four great elements, and form derived from the four great elements. This speaks of two, causal form and resultant form: the four great elements are causal form, and form derived from the four great elements is resultant form. Here, the forms derived from the four great elements are of eleven kinds, as was explained above.

The word "derived" refers to cause: the great elements act in five ways as the cause of the form derived from the elements. It is like saying that fire arises in dependence on wood, which means that fire is produced from wood as its cause. The five causes are generating, basis, support, sustaining and increasing. Generating: because without the elements, derived form does not arise. Basis: because apart from the elements, derived forms have no power to remain or rest anywhere else.

Support: because if the elements decline and change, the derived form that depends on them also becomes otherwise. Sustaining: because when the elements and derived form arise in like manner, the elements hold the causal continuum of derived form so that it is not cut off. Increasing: because when the elements increase, derived form too increases and flourishes.

"What are the four great elements?" To answer this it says, "Namely, the earth element," and so on. The word "element" means holding: it is called an element because it holds its own characteristic and because it holds derived form. Holding its own characteristic means this: the earth element holds the characteristic of hardness, the water element holds the characteristic of wetness, the fire element holds the characteristic of warmth, and the wind element holds the characteristic of motion. Holding derived form means holding it as its cause, because the elements act as the cause of those forms. The great elements are exactly four, not five or three, because more would be pointless and fewer would be incapable, like the legs of a bed.

Since the nature of the great elements was not known, in order to teach it the text says, "What is the earth element?" and so on. In "That which is heavy and hard," heavy is what makes the head of a balance beam go down and its tail go up.
