But there is also derived form called heaviness and lightness, and to set that apart it says "hard." Saying "hard" sets derived form apart; it means rough and solid. Though derived form may be heavy, it does not have the nature of hardness and solidity.

"What is the water element? That which is wet and moist." Its own characteristic is wetness; because it also makes other things wet, it is moist. "What is the fire element? That which is warm and ripens." Because it is itself hot by nature, it is warm; because it also warms and ripens other substances, it ripens. "What is the wind element? That which contracts, extends, is light and moves." What draws the limbs of the body and so forth inward and stretches them outward, and what makes outer things such as leaves move and carries them away, is the wind element. Lightness, however, also exists as derived form, and so that the two are not confused, the synonym "moves" is stated. Here, lightness is the nature of the wind element, and moving is the action of the wind element. Through what "moves" teaches, the nature called "lightness" becomes clear: what is light in the nature of motion is the wind element, while what is merely light without any motion is derived form.

If one asks, "It would be enough to say 'earth'; why is it necessary to say 'element'?" If one said only "earth," then in the world derived forms such as white and red are also called earth; to prevent confusion with that, the word "element" is stated.

In order to teach in summary what was explained above, it says, "This form and," and so on. This form that has the character of the four great elements, combined into one with the four formless aggregates of name explained above, is called name and form. In the Abhidharma, form is of three kinds: visible and obstructive, invisible and obstructive, and both invisible and unobstructive. Name too is threefold: excluding the aggregate of consciousness, it is feeling, perception and formations. The three kinds of form and the three of name combined into one are called name and form. Here, visible and obstructive form is what is the object of the eye faculty, designated as visible form. Invisible and obstructive form is nine substances: the five faculties such as the eye, and the four objects of sound, smell, taste and touch. Form that is both invisible and unobstructive is non-revealing form (avijñapti).

In order to teach the six sense sources, it says, "Conditioned by name and form, the six sense sources," and so on; the sense sources are only six. In order to teach the divisions of the six sense sources that were mentioned merely by name, it says, "The eye sense source," and so on. From the cause, the form of the four great elements, arise the sense sources from the eye sense source up to the body sense source. From the cause, the four aggregates of name, arises the mind sense source. Or else, when the four great elements become limpid and clear, they become the sense sources from the eye up to the body; when the four aggregates of name become clear, they become the mind sense source. Here, the limpid and clear form whose nature is seeing, with visible form as its domain, is the eye sense source. The limpid and clear form whose nature is hearing, with sound as its domain, is the ear sense source. The limpid and clear form whose nature is smelling, with odor as its domain, is the nose sense source. The limpid and clear form whose nature is tasting, with flavor as its domain, is the tongue sense source. The limpid and clear form whose nature is feeling smoothness, roughness and the like, with the tangible as its domain, is the body sense source. That whose nature is knowing and cognizing, having phenomena as its domain, is the mind sense source. In the desire realm all six sense sources are present. In the form realm there are the remaining four, apart from the nose and tongue. In the formless realm only the mind sense source is present.

In order to teach contact, it states the passage, "Conditioned by the six sense sources, contact," and so on. In order to teach how it operates, including the enumeration, it says, "Eye contact," and so on. In dependence on the eye and visible form, eye consciousness arises; therefore the coming together at one time of the three, object, faculty and consciousness, is called contact. Likewise, the coming together at one time of the three, ear, sound and ear consciousness, is called ear contact, and the same is to be applied to the remaining faculties.

Here, the five from eye contact up to body contact are called obstructive contact, because they depend on the obstructive faculties such as the eye. Mental contact is called contact that engages the object further: because the eye and the rest take charge of visible form and so on, and after their seeing the mind examines it, it is called contact that engages the object further.

In order to explain feeling, it says, "Conditioned by contact, feeling," and so on, and with "the six groups of feeling" it teaches their number. In order to teach the divisions through that very statement of number, it says, "feeling arising from eye contact." The feeling that arises from the coming together of the three, eye, object and consciousness, is called feeling arising from eye contact. That, moreover, becomes three kinds of feeling according to the cause, meeting with a pleasing object, an unpleasing object, or an object that is neither: pleasant feeling, painful feeling, and feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful arise. Here, when one meets with a pleasing object, pleasant feeling arises. When one meets with an unpleasing object, painful feeling arises. When one meets with an object that is neither pleasing nor unpleasing, feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful arises. Likewise, by way of meeting with sounds that are sweet, harsh, or neither, three kinds of feeling also arise from ear contact, and the same is to be applied to the remaining faculties.

Here, what refreshes is pleasant feeling; the meaning is that it benefits the faculties and the four great elements. What torments is painful feeling; the meaning is that it harms the faculties and the four great elements. What neither refreshes nor torments is called feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful; the meaning is that it neither benefits nor harms the faculties and the four great elements.

Or else: whatever feeling arises that favors and benefits the body, the faculties and the mind, so that while it arises and while it lasts one thinks, "I am happy," and when parted from it one thinks, "I am suffering," is called pleasant feeling. Whatever arises and harms the body, the faculties and consciousness, so that while it arises and lasts one thinks, "I am suffering," and when parted from it one thinks, "I am happy," is called painful feeling. Whatever arises neither benefiting nor harming the body, the faculties and consciousness, so that while it arises and lasts one thinks neither "I am happy" nor "I am suffering," is called feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful.

As for "the pleasant feeling of mental contact" and so on: the refreshing feeling of eye contact up to body contact is called pleasant feeling, and the tormenting is called painful feeling. But the refreshing feeling of mental contact, which by name would be called pleasant feeling, is called the feeling of mental pleasure, and the tormenting one is not called painful feeling but is called the feeling of mental displeasure. Here, the feeling arising from eye contact up to body contact is called bodily feeling, since it depends on the material faculties. The feeling arising from mental contact is called mental feeling, because it depends on mind.

Objection: Apart from pleasant and painful feeling, how is it evident that a third feeling exists, called neither pleasant nor painful? Reply: it is evident from scripture and from reasoning. From scripture, because it is said, "pleasant, painful, and neither pleasant nor painful," and also, "having abandoned pleasure and abandoned pain, neither pleasant nor painful, possessed of mindfulness," and so on. There is reasoning too: since a mind cannot arise without feeling, at times when pleasant or painful feeling is not experienced, everyone knows for themselves a feeling whose nature is neither pleasant nor painful.

In the desire realm all three feelings are present. In the first and second meditative concentrations of the form realm both pleasant feeling and equanimous feeling are present. From the third meditative concentration of the form realm up to the Peak of Existence only equanimous feeling is present.

In order to teach craving, it says, "Conditioned by feeling, craving." "Craving" belongs among the synonyms attachment, clinging, desire and intense attachment. Attachment to the six objects such as visible form is called craving: craving is never experiencing satisfaction, however constantly one enjoys craving and the objects of craving, like drinking salt water. Just as a moth is attached to the form of fire, its place of ruin, so the attachment of woman and man, each to the other's form, a place of ruin whose nature is impure, is called craving for form. Attachment to various sounds, a place of ruin, like a deer's attachment to song, is called craving for sound. Just as a fly of filth holds dear and is attached to foul smells, so attachment to various odors is called craving for odor. Just as a pig is attached to various impure tastes, so clinging to various tastes is called craving for taste. Just as the mustard-oil insect is attached to the touch of the oil vessel, so attachment to tangibles such as smoothness and roughness is called craving for touch. Longing to meet again with the five qualities of sense pleasure enjoyed before, in the past, and longing to keep company soon with those of the future, attachment to the taste of worldly meditative concentration, and attachment to various wrong views, is called craving for phenomena.

In summary, this is attachment to outer things, attachment to inner things, and attachment to views. Here, attachment to outer things is attachment to the five qualities of sense pleasure. Attachment to inner things is attachment to the five appropriating aggregates.

Attachment to views is clinging to eternalism, clinging to nihilism, and the like.

In order to explain grasping, it says, "With craving as condition, grasping. What is grasping?" With the word "four" it gives their number. It is grasping because it lays hold of renewed existence; or else, it is called grasping because it does not let go of the mind stream of desire, views, and the like, but holds on to it. Or else, "grasping" (upādāna) has the meaning of "fuel": it is called grasping because through it the fire of karma blazes. For example, just as fire blazes because of firewood, so the fire of a person's karma comes to blaze because of the firewood of the afflictions, and that is the sense in which it is called grasping. Or else, "grasping" has the meaning of "entwining": just as the silkworm wraps up its own body, so to bind oneself with the bonds of the afflictions and thereby create an obstacle to liberation is grasping.

In order to set out its divisions, it says "grasping at sense pleasures" and so forth. To be attached to the five qualities of sense pleasure of the desire realm and not let go of them is grasping at sense pleasures. As for "grasping at views": to be attached to the eternalist view, the nihilist view, holding views as supreme, and the like, and not let go of them, is grasping at views. As for "grasping at moral discipline and observances as supreme": when, through the wrong aspiration "May I become a human, a god, Śakra (Indra), or Brahmā," one longs for that and therefore holds moral discipline and observances to be supreme, that is grasping at moral discipline and observances as supreme. As for "grasping at a doctrine of self": to cling to the view of the perishing collection and not let go of it is called grasping at a doctrine of self. Grasping at sense pleasures produces the suffering of the desire realm, while the other three graspings produce the suffering of the three realms.

Alternatively, the attachment, pride, aversion, and ignorance of the desire realm, from what is to be abandoned by seeing suffering up to what is to be abandoned by meditation, are twenty entities. How so? In the desire realm there are five kinds of attachment, from what is to be abandoned by seeing suffering, through the origin, cessation, and the path, up to what is to be abandoned by meditation; five kinds of pride, from what is to be abandoned by seeing suffering up to what is to be abandoned by meditation; five kinds of aversion, from what is to be abandoned by seeing suffering up to what is to be abandoned by meditation; and five kinds of ignorance, from what is to be abandoned by seeing suffering up to what is to be abandoned by meditation. In all, four sets of five make twenty. Because it is impossible for one who has doubt to meditate, there is no doubt on the path of meditation. There are the four kinds of doubt of the desire realm, from what is to be abandoned by seeing suffering up to what is to be abandoned by seeing the path; and on top of these come shamelessness, jealousy, stinginess, dejection, doubt, sleep, anger, and concealment, set out as ten entities. These thirty-four entities in all are called grasping at sense pleasures.

As for "grasping at views": in the three realms, from what is to be abandoned by seeing suffering up to what is to be abandoned by meditation, leaving aside holding moral discipline and observances as supreme, there are ten entities of view in each realm, and these thirty are called grasping at views. How so? In the desire realm, from what is to be abandoned by seeing suffering up to what is to be abandoned by seeing the path, there are four wrong views and four instances of holding views as supreme, making eight. On top of these eight come the view of the perishing collection and the view that holds to extremes, which are to be abandoned by seeing suffering in the desire realm, and that makes ten. Likewise there are ten in the form realm and ten in the formless realm, so they come to thirty entities.

Holding moral discipline and observances as supreme in the three realms, together with that abandoned by seeing the path, six entities in all, is called grasping at moral discipline and observances as supreme. How so? In the desire realm there are two: the holding of moral discipline and observances as supreme that is to be abandoned by seeing suffering, and the holding of moral discipline and observances as supreme that is to be abandoned by seeing the path. Likewise there are two in the form realm and two in the formless realm, two in each, so they come to six entities.

Here, with regard to holding moral discipline and observances as supreme, giving up immoral conduct such as taking life is moral discipline. Engaging by fixed rule in the modes of conduct and the activities of body and speech is observance: regularly performing such acts as shaving the head and washing the hands and feet at set times, or giving up comfort and adopting such ways as the austerities of the five fires and of the dog, is observance. Having firmly regarded base views as virtues, and taking Īśvara and the like, and the view of the perishing collection and the like, as one's starting point, one holds to moral discipline and observances; when these are neither the cause of purification nor the path, one holds them as though they were purification and had the character of the path. That which operates in this distorted way is called grasping at moral discipline and observances as supreme. That is why it is explained as clinging to what is not a cause as a cause: Īśvara, primal matter (pradhāna), and the like are not the cause that produces the worlds, yet because one clings to them as the cause, it is a distortion. As for clinging to what is not the path as the path: the practice of Sāṃkhya gnosis and the like is not the path of liberation, yet one clings to it; because this is distorted, the moral discipline and observances that make it manifest are called grasping at moral discipline and observances as supreme.

Why, then, is holding moral discipline and observances as supreme set apart from the views and explained separately? Because, as a grasping, it is a factor opposed to the path: for although the gnosis of Sāṃkhya yoga and the like is not the path of liberation, it is regarded as the path to liberation. Or else, it deceives both those who live as householders and those who have gone forth. It deceives householders in that, although fasting, making offerings to the fire, and the like are not the path to the higher realms, they take them to be the path. It deceives those who have gone forth too: they are convinced that one becomes pure by giving up the pleasures of the objects of desire, and they are deceived through perceiving what is not the path, the austerity of the five fires, plucking out the hair, eating coarse food, lying on thorns, and so on, as the path.

As for "grasping at a doctrine of self": the attachment, pride, and ignorance that operate in the form and formless realms, from what is to be abandoned by seeing suffering up to what is to be abandoned by meditation, are ten each, making thirty; and on top of these thirty, the doubts of the form and formless realms, four each, make eight. These thirty-eight are called grasping at a doctrine of self. How so? In the form realm, from what is to be abandoned by seeing suffering up to what is to be abandoned by meditation, there are five kinds of attachment, and likewise five in the formless realm, making ten. In the form realm, from what is to be abandoned by seeing suffering up to what is to be abandoned by meditation, there are five kinds of pride, and likewise five in the formless realm, making ten. In the form realm, from what is to be abandoned by seeing suffering up to what is to be abandoned by meditation, there are five kinds of ignorance, and likewise five in the formless realm, making ten. In the formless realm, from what is to be abandoned by seeing suffering up to what is to be abandoned by seeing the path, there are four kinds of doubt, and in the formless realm too there are four kinds of doubt, making eight kinds of doubt. In this way they come to thirty-eight entities.

An objection: although they are alike in being afflictions, the afflictions of the desire realm are explained as the grasping of the desire realm, whereas the afflictions of the form and formless realms are not called the grasping of the form and formless realms. Why is that? The answer, as regards what is called grasping at a doctrine of self, is this: the afflictions of the form and formless realms, and the mind, are turned inward, and so one views those afflictions as a self. Therefore, in order to show that there is no self in them, but that one merely views the afflictions as a self and speaks of them so, it is explained as grasping at a doctrine of self.

Or else: those on the side of householders, because they are attached to objects of desire such as children and wives, grasp at sense pleasures. Those who have gone forth, because they are attached to various views, grasp at views. Because those on the householder side, practicing austerities such as keeping periodic vows and fasting, cling to these as the path to the higher realms and to liberation, and those who have gone forth also think that they will be liberated by those very means, both sides grasp at moral discipline and observances as supreme. The non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) who propound a self grasp at a doctrine of self.

If these are set out in the order of their abandonment, there are three stages: the stage of the path of seeing, the stage of freedom from attachment, and the stage of knowing what has been done. Here, stream-enterers are said to abide in the stage of the path of seeing. At that time there are neither the various distorted views nor the distortion of holding moral discipline and observances to be the path to liberation, and so, on the stage of the path of seeing, grasping at views and grasping at moral discipline and observances as supreme are abandoned. The once-returner is engaged in abandoning the attachment of the desire realm, and the non-returner has abandoned the attachment of the desire realm; for this reason these two are said to abide in the stage of freedom from attachment. At that time, because all the afflictions of the desire realm have been abandoned, grasping at sense pleasures is abandoned. At the time of being an arhat, because one realizes, "What I had to do is done; the burden is laid down," and so on, one is said to abide in the stage of knowing what has been done. Because at the time of being an arhat the afflictions of the form and formless realms are abandoned without remainder, grasping at a doctrine of self is said to be abandoned.

In order to explain existence, it says, "With grasping as condition, existence. What is existence?" and so on. Performing virtuous and non-virtuous actions under the power of grasping is called existence, and the place where existence arises through the power of karma and the afflictions is also called existence. The words "There are three kinds of existence" state their number, and in order to show their divisions it says, "namely, desire existence," and so on. The realm that gives primacy to the five qualities of sense pleasure, such as forms and sounds, is desire existence. The realm in which attachment to the qualities of sense pleasure has been abandoned, but where one cultivates meditative concentration, gives primacy to form, and relies on form, is form existence. To be free of form, such as the five sense faculties, having abandoned attachment to form, is formless existence. In order to show what exactly these are, it says the passage beginning "existence that desires pleasure." Desire existence refers to everything from the hells up to the Paranirmitavaśavartin gods (those who wield power over the emanations of others).

Among these, the hot hells are eight: Avīci (Without Respite), Pratāpana (Intense Heat), Tapana (Heat), Mahāraurava (Great Howling), Raurava (Howling), Saṃghāta (Crushing), Kālasūtra (Black Line), and Saṃjīva (Reviving). The Avīci hell lies twenty thousand leagues beneath this continent of Jambudvīpa. The height of the abode of that Avīci hell is also twenty thousand leagues, and across the middle it is also twenty thousand; in all, their abode comes to forty thousand leagues. Because there is not even the slightest interval, it is without respite, and so it is called Avīci. Or else, it is Avīci because various sufferings occur without interruption: in that hell, great blazing fires arise in the ten directions, and the fiercely blazing fire burns the bodies of the sentient beings; and the hell guardians, holding iron spears, lift them up on the points of their spears and continually boil them in great copper cauldrons. For this reason it is called Avīci. As for the life span in the Avīci hell, one remains there for one eon and then dies.

The seven hells beginning with Pratāpana are stacked in order, one above another, above that Avīci hell: above Avīci is Pratāpana; above Pratāpana is Tapana; above Tapana is Mahāraurava; above Mahāraurava is Raurava; above Raurava is Saṃghāta; above Saṃghāta is Kālasūtra; and above Kālasūtra is Saṃjīva. Some explain that these seven hells are located at the sides of the Avīci hell. Compared with the Tapana hell it is hotter even than that, so that body and mind are intensely tormented; and so, its heat being intense, it is Pratāpana. As for the life span in that Pratāpana hell, one remains there for half an intermediate eon and then dies.

Because their bodies are surrounded by great blazing flames and burned, the beings there are hot and tormented through and through; this is called the Tapana hell. As for its life span: counting the whole life span of the Paranirmitavaśavartin gods, however long it is, as a single day, one remains there for sixteen thousand years and then dies.

As for Mahāraurava: because those there are tormented by many intense and unbearable heats, it is more unbearable even than Raurava. Crying, "Oh, the suffering!" they howl aloud. As for its life span: counting the whole life span of the Nirmāṇarati gods as a single day, it lasts eight thousand years. As for Raurava: harmed by beings who hack at them and by the karmic maturation of terrible deeds, they suffer, and in their misery they howl. As for its life span: counting the whole life span of the Tuṣita gods as a single day, it is four thousand years. Then, as for Saṃghāta: many mountains like sheep, boulders, and the like come together from either side and crush beings to death. As for its life span: counting the whole life span of the Yāma gods as a single day, one remains there for two hundred years and then dies. As for Kālasūtra: just as a carpenter marks wood with a line and then saws the wood, so beings are marked with black lines and then sawn and split, from the head or from the groin. As for its life span: counting the whole life span of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three as a single day, one remains there for a thousand years and then dies. As for Saṃjīva: the beings born there harm one another, hacking and splitting each other. As they lie unconscious, a cold wind blows on them, and from the sky too comes a voice: "Beings, revive!" Then they revive and harm one another. As for its life span: counting the whole life span of the gods of the realm of the Four Great Kings as a single day, one remains there for five hundred years and then dies.

Those eight great hells have four gates, and at each gate there are four additional hells, making sixteen hells. The four additional hells are the hot embers, the swamp of filth, the path of weapons such as razors, and the river of brine. Of these, the hot embers are about knee-deep; the moment beings set their feet in them, their flesh, blood, and skin are stripped away entirely. The swamp of filth is full of a mud of vile filth with an extremely foul smell. In it live worms called kunta, with pale bodies, black heads, and beaks like needles, which pierce right through even the bones of the beings who enter there. As for the great path filled with razor edges: when beings enter it, the path lined with razors strips away all their flesh, blood, and skin the moment they set down their feet; and there is a forest of sword leaves. The sharp edges of those swords also cut the limbs and extremities of those beings into pieces; brindled, tiger-striped dogs gather and devour them; and crows with iron beaks pluck out even their eyes. There are iron trees with sixteen thorns, so that when beings climb up, the points of the thorns turn downward and pierce the beings' bodies, and when they come back down, the points turn upward and pierce their bodies. As for the river of brine: on its banks hell guardians holding weapons, swords, śakti lances, and spears in their hands throw back again even those who come out, and those thrown in are cooked and boiled, as rice, sesame, and the like are boiled in a great cauldron.

There are also eight cold hells, namely Arbuda (Blisters), Nirarbuda (Severe Blisters), Chattering Teeth, Alala, Huhuva, Utpala (Blue Lotus), Padma (Lotus), and Mahāpadma (Great Lotus). Because those beings are so exceedingly cold, their bodies, tormented without respite, turn into blisters and crack open, and from this they are designated Arbuda, Nirarbuda, Utpala, Padma, and Mahāpadma. From the ways in which the sounds of those afflicted by the cold change, they are designated Chattering Teeth, Alala, and Huhuva.

As for the life span in those eight hells, the Blessed One declared it merely by way of an example: "Monks, it is like this. There is now a vāha of sesame of the Middle Country, holding twenty khāris of five bre each; let it be a container for the loads of forty great, strong oxen, and let it be filled heaped high with sesame. Then a man, each time a hundred years have passed, takes away a single grain of that sesame. That vāha of sesame of the Middle Country, holding twenty khāris, would sooner be gradually used up in this way and come to an end; but I say that the life span of the beings born in Arbuda would not be exhausted." What he said there is the life span of Arbuda. Twenty times the life span of Arbuda is the life span of Nirarbuda; twenty times that of Nirarbuda is that of Chattering Teeth; twenty times that of Chattering Teeth is that of Alala; twenty times that of Alala is that of Huhuva; twenty times that of Huhuva is that of Utpala; twenty times that of Utpala is that of Padma; and twenty times that of Padma is the life span of Mahāpadma.

These eight cold hells too lie beneath this very Jambudvīpa, alongside the great hells. If Jambudvīpa itself is not that great in length and breadth, how could there be room for Avīci and the others, so many of them, to be located there? In reply: like a heap of grain, this Jambudvīpa too is broad at the base and narrow at the top, and that is why they are located there.

Now, as for what is called hell: in the language of India it is naraka. The syllable na has the meaning of leading or going: since beings are led by non-meritorious karma to that place of suffering brought on by karma, and go there, it is a hell. Or else, the meaning of naraka is "without delight": those born there are always afflicted by suffering, so that no thought of delight or joy arises in them; hence it is a hell. Or else, it is a hell because beings there undergo many kinds of suffering, such as torment and being hacked apart.

As for what are called hungry ghosts (pretas): the word preta is used for one who has gone far away. Through the power of karma such as avarice, they have gone from this abode of humans for a long time without returning, and so they are pretas; for those born as pretas are known to be exceptionally long-lived.

Or else, they are called hungry ghosts because they are constantly afflicted by hunger, thirst, and the like. Their abodes, too, are mostly in gorges and deserts, and their power is not of one fixed kind: some powerful hungry ghosts enjoy riches like gods, while others cannot find even filth and the like to eat. Among them, Rāhula, the king of the hungry ghosts, is as powerful as Yama, the lord of death; he dwells five thousand leagues beneath this very Jambudvīpa. Or else, some say that Yama himself is the king of the hungry ghosts, and that his abode is just as was explained for the abode of Rāhula. As for the life span of hungry ghosts: counting one human month as one day of the hungry ghosts, they live five hundred years and then die.

As for animals, "those who go aside": they are so called because they have turned aside from the human state and been born elsewhere, or because they go along sideways. Although their abode is certainly the great ocean, they are nevertheless classified in three kinds: those that move in water, those that move on dry land, and those that move in the sky. Those that move in water are the nāga kings, sea monsters, and the like; those that move on dry land are livestock and the like; and those that move in the sky are birds and the like. The life span and power of animals are not of one fixed kind. For example, like the nāga kings Earth Holder, Nanda, and Upananda, there are some whose life span lasts an intermediate eon and who also have power enough to hold up Mount Meru.

As for what are called asuras: they are born from the cause of virtuous karma done with pride or aversion. Their abode is on this great earth, and their life span is said to equal that of the gods of the desire realm.

Now, humans are those who live in the four continents. The meaning explained in the scriptures is that they are humans (manuṣya) because they are born from a mind (manas) of merit and non-merit. Or else, because they are born from the sage Manu and established by him, they are also called manaca, "born of the sons of Manu"; the lord Manu is the one whom the tīrthikas call Prajāpati, Lord of Creatures; and so worldly people say they are humans.

The four continents are eastern Videha (Noble Body), western Godānīya (Cattle Users), northern Kuru (Unpleasant Sound), and southern Jambudvīpa. Of these, the continent of eastern Videha is shaped like a half moon: on three sides of its perimeter there are two thousand leagues, and on the fourth side three hundred and fifty leagues. There is a city there called Videha, and after it the continent too was given the name eastern Videha. Western Godānīya is shaped like a full moon, and its whole circumference is seven thousand five hundred leagues. Because buying and selling there is done with cattle as the goods, the continent too was named after this, western Godānīya. The continent of northern Kuru is square, like a stool: each side is two thousand leagues, making eight thousand leagues in all. Because a harsh sound arises there at the time of death and then one dies, the continent was named northern Kuru, Unpleasant Sound. The shape of Jambudvīpa is like a shoulder blade, broad at the top and narrow at the bottom: each of its three sides is two thousand leagues, and the narrow side is three and a half leagues. In that continent there is a tree called jambu, and after it the continent was named Jambudvīpa.

The people of eastern Videha live two hundred and fifty years, and their bodies measure eight cubits. The people of western Godānīya live five hundred years, and their bodies measure four fathoms, or sixteen cubits. The people of northern Kuru live a thousand years, and their bodies measure eight fathoms, or thirty-two cubits. The life span of the people of southern Jambudvīpa is not fixed, and although their bodies are said to measure one fathom, some are born taller or shorter even than that, so it is not certain.

As for "the six gods who move in the desire realm": these are the six classes of gods belonging to the desire realm, from the class of the Four Great Kings through the Paranirmitavaśavartin gods. They are gods because they are born and arise in the higher realms, or they are gods because they possess excellent bodies and riches.

As for "the heaven of the class of the Four Great Kings": the four world guardians are the Four Great Kings, Virūḍhaka, Virūpākṣa, Vaiśravaṇa, and Dhṛtarāṣṭra. The gods born there, or belonging to their retinue, are called the class of the Four Great Kings. As for their life span: counting fifty human years as one day, they live five hundred years and then die. Their bodies measure one quarter of a krośa (the range of a shout).

As for "the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three": those born there through having performed virtuous karma together in harmony are called the Thirty-Three. To the thirty-two in the retinue of Śakra, lord of the gods, Śakra himself is added, making thirty-three. Of these, the thirty-two ministers are the eight Vasus; twelve sons of the gods called Ādityas (Suns); ten sons of the gods called Rudras (the Fierce); and two called Nabso and Born of Aśvinī: thirty-two in all. The other gods belonging there are also called gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three. As for the life span of those gods: counting a hundred human days as one divine day, they live a thousand years and then die. Their bodies measure half a krośa.

As for "the Yāmas": in the language of India the word is yama. Because they are free from the suffering of strife with the asuras, they are called Free from Strife. Or else, the meaning of the word yama is "going": because one goes there through merit, they are also called Yāma. Or else, because at every watch they call out, "Oh, what bliss! Oh, what bliss!" they are also called Those of the Watches. As for their life span: counting two hundred human days as one divine day, they live two thousand years and then die. Their bodies measure three hundred fathoms.

As for "the Tuṣitas": because they are always accompanied by joy and, compared with the gods of the lower levels, possess the bliss of enjoyments, they are Tuṣita (Joyful). As for their life span: counting four hundred human days as one day, they live four thousand years and then die. Their bodies measure five hundred fathoms, or three quarters of a krośa.

As for "the Nirmāṇarati gods": because each of them delights in the enjoyments of their own emanations, they are Nirmāṇarati (Delighting in Emanation). As for their life span: counting eight hundred human days as one divine day, they live eight thousand years and then die. Their bodies measure six hundred fathoms, or four fifths of a krośa. As for "Paranirmitavaśavartin": because they wield power over sense enjoyments emanated by others, they are Paranirmitavaśavartin. As for their life span: counting one thousand six hundred human days as one divine day, they live sixteen thousand years and then die. Their bodies measure seven hundred and fifty fathoms, or one and a half krośas. The Abhidharma explains that, however long the life span of the gods of the desire realm is, the fathoms and krośas of their body size are equal to the measure of their life span.

Among the gods of the desire realm, the gods of the Four Great Kings and of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three engage in sexual union by coupling in embrace, as humans do; but since they have no semen, their pleasure is fulfilled merely by the release of wind. Their other enjoyments are like those of humans. The Yāmas engage in union by embracing; their pleasure is fulfilled merely by an embrace. The gods of Tuṣita engage in union by holding hands; their pleasure is fulfilled merely by holding hands. The gods of Nirmāṇarati engage in union merely by smiling; their pleasure is fulfilled merely by male and female laughing together. The gods of Paranirmitavaśavartin engage in union merely by looking; their pleasure is fulfilled merely by an exchange of glances.

The sons and daughters of those six kinds of desire-realm gods appear in the laps of their fathers and mothers. Those of the gods of the abode of the Four Great Kings appear as children about five years of age, adorned with clothes and ornaments; those of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three as about six years of age; those of the Yāmas as about seven; those of Tuṣita as about eight; those of Nirmāṇarati as about nine; and those of Paranirmitavaśavartin as about ten years of age.

In order to explain form existence, it says the passage beginning "What is form existence?" The abodes of form existence are seventeen. There are four meditative concentrations: the first concentration has three, the second three, the third three, and the fourth eight, making seventeen. The three stages of the first concentration are those of the Brahmakāyika gods (Brahmā's Class), the Brahmapurohita gods (Brahmā's Close Helpers), and the Mahābrahmā gods (Great Brahmā). The three stages of the second are those of the Parīttābha gods (Lesser Light), the Apramāṇābha gods (Immeasurable Light), and the Ābhāsvara gods (Shining Light). The three stages of the third are those of the Parīttaśubha gods (Lesser Virtue), the Apramāṇaśubha gods (Immeasurable Virtue), and the Śubhakṛtsna gods (Abundant Virtue). The eight abodes of the fourth are from the Anabhraka gods (Cloudless) through the Akaniṣṭha gods of the highest heaven. Of these, the three of Anabhraka, Puṇyaprasava (Increasing Merit), and Bṛhatphala (Great Fruit) are the stages of ordinary beings, and the five above them are the stages of the noble ones.

As for "the Brahmakāyika gods": one born from vast roots of virtue is called Brahmā, and the other Brahmās who are born into his class, or who have become his servants and retinue, are called Brahmakāyika, Brahmā's Class. Some explain that they are called Brahmakāyika because they belong to the retinue of Mahābrahmā. As for their life span: they live half an eon and then die. Their bodies measure half a league.

As for "the Brahmapurohita gods": because they are born into that class, helping Mahābrahmā like ministers, they dwell, more than the other Brahmās, like an inner retinue, and when he rises from meditative concentration they go before him and attend on him; therefore they are called Brahmā's Close Helpers. Or else, because they join their palms before Mahābrahmā and recite praises, they are called Reciters Before Brahmā. As for their life span: they live one eon and then die. Their bodies measure one league.

As for "the Mahābrahmā gods": because, compared with the two other kinds of Brahmās, they are superior in life span, complexion, and power, they are called Mahābrahmā, Great Brahmā. Or else, he is called Mahābrahmā because, surpassing others, he is born from great roots of virtue and has become supreme: having attained a distinction in meditative concentration beyond the ordinary, he is born before all others and dies last; hence he is Mahābrahmā. As for his life span: he lives one and a half eons and then dies. His body measures one and a half leagues.

As for "the Parīttābha gods": compared with the two classes above them, the radiance of splendor and light from their bodies is lesser, so they are Parīttābha, Lesser Light. As for their life span: they live two eons and then die. Their bodies measure two leagues. As for "the Apramāṇābha gods": because their light and splendor cannot be measured or delimited as being just so much, they are Apramāṇābha, Immeasurable Light. As for their life span: they live four eons and then die. Their bodies measure four leagues. As for "the Ābhāsvara gods": because they illuminate with their splendor and light even other abodes that do not belong to their own stage, they are Ābhāsvara, Shining Light. As for their life span: they live eight eons and then die. Their bodies measure eight leagues.

As for "the Parīttaśubha gods": in this context, "virtue" means the bliss of the mental level born of meditative concentration. Because, compared with those above them among the ones belonging to their own stage, their mental bliss is lesser, they are Parīttaśubha, Lesser Virtue. As for their life span: they live sixteen eons and then die. Their bodies measure ten leagues. As for "the Apramāṇaśubha gods": because their virtue cannot be measured, they are Apramāṇaśubha, Immeasurable Virtue; or, because they possess immeasurable mental bliss, they are Apramāṇaśubha. As for their life span: they live thirty-two eons and then die. Their bodies also measure thirty-two leagues. As for "the Śubhakṛtsna gods," Complete Virtue: they are Complete Virtue because they have mental bliss complete without remainder, or because there is no other bliss more excellent than theirs. As for their life span: they live sixty-four eons and then die. Their bodies also measure sixty-four leagues.

As for "the Anabhraka gods": just as there is no covering of cloud over a land, so above that ground there is no other ground obscuring it; hence they are Anabhraka, Cloudless. And, like clouds, they have no connection with the ground, so they are Cloudless: for the celestial palaces of those gods abide in space, and they are born together with their celestial palaces and perish at the same time. As for their life span: they live one hundred and twenty-five eons and then die. Their bodies also measure one hundred and twenty-five leagues.

As for "the Puṇyaprasava gods": because this is the abode in which the highest of the merit belonging to ordinary beings is attained, they are Puṇyaprasava, Increasing Merit. As for their life span: they live two hundred and fifty eons and then die. Their bodies also measure two hundred and fifty leagues.

As for "the Bṛhatphala gods": because they have accumulated the fruit of virtue to a greater degree than in the other abodes, their birth there does not come from inferior karma; since they have attained the supreme fruit, they are Bṛhatphala, Great Fruit. Alternatively, because this is to be attained through a fruit that surpasses the lower grounds of ordinary beings, they are Bṛhatphala. As for their life span: they live five hundred eons and then die. Their bodies also measure five hundred leagues.

As for "the Avṛha gods": among the Pure Abodes this is not the highest; because their merit is smaller than that of the higher grounds of the Pure Abodes, they are Avṛha, Not Great. As for their life span: they live a thousand eons and then die. Their bodies also measure a thousand leagues.

As for "the Atapa gods": because their intention is virtuous, those born there, although they surpass others in power, in the fruit of superior meditative concentration, in life span, and in good qualities, neither harm nor overshadow those of the lower grounds; hence they are Atapa, Not Tormenting. Or else, because they have attained a distinctive meditative concentration, the afflictions are unable to torment them; hence they are Atapa, Not Tormented. As for their life span: they live two thousand eons and then die. Their bodies also measure two thousand leagues.

As for "the Sudarśana gods": because the gods there possess the distinction of utter peace and are endowed with bliss, which others have not attained, they are Sudarśana, Clear Vision. Or else, their view is completely pure: because they see without defilement, they are Sudarśana. As for their life span: they live four thousand eons and then die. Their bodies also measure four thousand leagues.

There are those called "the Sudṛśa gods." Although they cultivate defiled and undefiled meditative concentration, drawing on them at will, they possess the power to keep them from becoming mixed or entangled; because of this they see with the great obscurations purified without remainder, and so they are the Sublime. Or else, they see well and excellently, seeing things as selfless, impermanent, and so forth; hence they are Sudṛśa, Sublime Sight. As for their life span: they live one hundred thousand eons and then die. Their bodies also measure one hundred thousand leagues.

As for "the Akaniṣṭha gods": this is not a lower abode, since above this point in the form realm there is no other realm that possesses form; hence it is Akaniṣṭha, Not Lower. Or else, there is no other abode superior to it, because it has not become smaller or worse than the others; hence it is Akaniṣṭha. Or else, the body in which suffering is accumulated is called āgha, and because this is the culmination of accumulated form, it is called āghaniṣṭha. Or else, āgha is a word for evil: because this is the abode where evil reaches its end and the defilements arise, it is āghaniṣṭha. As for their life span: they live sixteen thousand eons and then die. Their bodies also measure sixteen thousand leagues.

From Avṛha up to the abode of Akaniṣṭha is called the class of the Pure Abodes. Because the only ones born there are noble ones whose mindstreams are pure, for they have washed away the stains of the afflictions with the water of the path, these are the Pure Abodes. Or else, just as one becomes clear of a debt, they have paid off the debt of saṃsāra in full, and because those who are pure are born there, these are the Pure Abodes. Or else, because noble non-returners alone dwell there, unmixed with ordinary beings, these are the Pure Abodes.

To teach formless existence, it states the passage beginning, "What is formless existence?" Because it can be made to appear, it is form; because there is nothing that can be made to appear, it is formless.

As for the passage beginning, "namely, the base of infinite space": when one first trains in the absorption, one takes as one's object, "Space is infinite, space is infinite." Because this itself is the cause and condition for birth there, it is called the base of infinite space. The mind with which they first apply themselves is also described in other sūtras: "Perceptions of form are completely transcended, perceptions of resistance have subsided, perceptions of diversity are not attended to, and with the thought, 'Space is infinite, space is infinite,' one enters the base of infinite space and abides in it."

Its meaning, moreover, is this: these are merely space. One is free of attachment to whatever shapes and colors there are, blue, yellow, red, and white, and to the pleasure and displeasure that arise from associated causes, and since perception does not engage with those colors, that is why it says, "form is completely transcended." Having thus completely transcended perceptions of form, and because these have ceased to appear, one becomes free of perceptions of obstruction and resistance with regard to resistant things, the accumulations of particles that cause colors of every kind. That is why it says, "perceptions of resistance have subsided." Because one is free of that perception, the perceptions that engage the remaining kinds of accumulation not included in it, such as food, drink, vehicles, clothing, ornaments, houses and pleasure groves, armies, and mountains, do not engage at all, in any way whatsoever. That is why it says, "perceptions of diversity are not attended to." Having thus demolished the perceptions of resistance and of diversity, one passes beyond the proximate absorption and, by means of the preparatory absorption, expresses space under the aspect of infinity. That is why it says, "Space is infinite, space is infinite." Then, after attending to the preparatory absorption until it reaches its culmination, one settles in equipoise in the root absorption, the fruit of the culmination of the preparation. That is why it says, "one enters the base of infinite space and abides in it."

Here, for as long as one is entering the root absorption, one abides with space as one's object. Once one has entered the root absorption, one simply abides with the four aggregates of one's own ground as one's object. At the time of the proximate absorption, one attends to the aggregates of the lower grounds and to the many kinds of color as having the characteristic of nonexistence.

As for "the base of infinite consciousness": at the time of the preparatory absorption, the object is infinite consciousness, thus: "The consciousness that takes space as its object under the aspect of infinity is itself infinite, just like space." Because this itself is the cause and condition for birth in that abode, it is called the base of infinite consciousness.

Its meaning is this. At the time of the base of infinite space, consciousness is intent on space under the aspect of infinity; therefore that consciousness itself has the aspect of infinity. One who wishes to enter into infinite consciousness, the consciousness intent on space under the aspect of infinity, turns back from the perceptions of the proximate and root absorptions of the base of infinite space and becomes intent on that very consciousness under the aspect of infinity. That is why other sūtras say, "Having completely transcended the base of infinite space in every respect, one enters the base of infinite consciousness." These are the proximate absorption and the preparatory absorption of the base of infinite consciousness. Rising from the proximate absorption of infinite consciousness and from the absorption of attention at the culmination of the preparation, one enters into equipoise in the root absorption, the fruit of the culmination of the preparation. That is why other sūtras say, "one enters the base of infinite consciousness and abides in it."

As for "the base of nothingness": because at the time of the preparatory absorption one enters equipoise under the aspect, "There is nothing at all," it is called nothingness; and because that itself is the cause and condition for birth in that abode, it is the base of nothingness.

Its meaning, moreover, is this. Building on the base of infinite consciousness, when one searches, "What object is there beyond consciousness?", one does not see any phenomenon at all, whether possessing form or formless. Having seen the object in this way, one rises from the proximate and root absorptions of the base of infinite consciousness and becomes intent on a different object: "There is nothing at all." That is abiding intent on the aspect of the perception of nothingness, and abiding in that way is called the proximate absorption of the base of nothingness and the absorption of attention at the culmination of the preparation. By being intent on that perception again and again, one passes beyond the proximate absorption of the base of nothingness and the absorption of attention at the culmination of the preparation, and enters into equipoise in the root absorption, the fruit of the culmination of the preparation. That is why other sūtras say, "Having completely transcended infinite consciousness in every respect, with the thought, 'There is nothing at all,' one enters the base of nothingness and abides in it." If this is divided up: at the time of the proximate absorption of the base of nothingness, it is "in every respect, infinite consciousness."

As for "the base of neither perception nor non-perception": abiding in a subtle perception, thinking, "Perception is a boil, perception is a thorn, and non-perception, too, is stupor," is called "neither perception nor non-perception." Because that itself is the cause and condition for birth there, it is called the base of neither perception nor non-perception.

Its meaning, moreover, is this. At the time of the base of neither perception nor non-perception, one builds on the base of nothingness. If one asks how, it is by attending to the perception of the base of neither nothingness nor not-nothingness as faulty, like attachment, sickness, a boil, and so forth. At the time of the absorption, through attention at the culmination of the preparation for neither nothingness nor not-nothingness, having perception is unpleasant, like a thorn, and abandoning perception altogether is stupor. One abides in a perception that is subtle in its discernment, and because one has turned away from the perception of the base of nothingness, other sūtras therefore say, "one abides in the base of nothingness." Passing beyond the proximate absorption of the base of neither nothingness nor not-nothingness and the absorption of attention at the culmination of the preparation, one enters into equipoise in the root absorption, the fruit of the culmination of the preparation. That is why the sūtra says, "Having completely transcended the base of nothingness in every respect, one enters the base of neither nothingness nor not-nothingness and abides in it."

In the formless realm, since there are no signs of form, one meditates relying on mind alone; that is why the sūtra speaks of "practicing meditative concentration on mind alone." These, moreover, have no hierarchy of abodes as the desire realm and the form realm do; they are distinguished merely by differences in attainment and differences in being born with longer or shorter lives. That is why the sūtra says, "these four kinds of bases of the formless gods": the four kinds of birth of the formless gods explained above are what is called formless existence. Among them, the gods born in infinite space have a life span of twenty thousand eons, and then they die; infinite consciousness, forty thousand eons; nothingness, sixty thousand; and the last lives eighty thousand. The formless gods, too, simply die and pass away in the very place where they entered the attainment, and are born right there; there is no one who is born elsewhere.

Objection: If in the desire realm and the form realm the mind relies on form, on what do the four name aggregates rely in the formless realm?

Reply: Just as three spear shafts with their tips joined lean on one another, so the four name aggregates themselves rely on one another. Or else, the four name aggregates rely on the faculty of feeling and on the factor of group homogeneity.

The sign that one is attaining a formless attainment is that the impression arises that one's body is falling from the sky.

Objection: Whereas in other sūtras the karma that will take up the fruit of the three realms in the future is denoted by the word "existence," here the desire, form, and formless realms themselves are called existence; therefore this contradicts the other sūtras.

Reply: There is no fault in this. The word "existence" is indeed used for karma, but that karma is the cause that produces future existence, and the three existences are the fruit of karma. Here the name of the cause is applied to the result: because that cause brings about birth in the three realms, the three are designated "existence." So there is no fault.

To teach birth, it states the passage beginning, "With existence as condition, birth." This means that birth arises from existence as its cause.

The words "of whichever beings, these and those" mean these and those beings, gods, humans, animals, and so on. The words "into these and those classes of beings" mean into these and those abodes of the same kind, of gods, humans, and so on, in the next life.

Here, consciousness first becoming manifest in the womb or in the egg is called birth. The major and minor limbs becoming complete, from the time consciousness enters there until the being emerges, is called full birth. Emerging from the womb once the limbs and so forth have become complete in the womb is called transition. For those born from warmth and moisture and for the miraculously born, their own bodies becoming complete all at once is called manifestation. Once they have come forth in this way, their five aggregates being nourished and made to grow by food and so forth is called the appearance of the aggregates. Being helped by other conditions besides food and the like, such as being anointed with butter, massaged, and bathed, is called the acquisition of the sense bases. Their aggregates relying on the causes and conditions of former karma and afflictions is called the acquisition of the elements. Those very aggregates of theirs remaining until the end of life is called the appearance of the life faculty. Not being parted from the homogeneity of the class into which one has been born, among gods, humans, and so on, is called possession of group homogeneity.

Then the sūtra teaches four points: what is born, by what it is sustained in its place of birth, by what it is helped, and what the intrinsic nature of birth is. Of these, the words from "birth" through "manifestation" show what is born. "The appearance of the aggregates" shows its sustenance. "The acquisition of the sense bases" and "the acquisition of the elements" show what helps it. "The appearance of the life faculty" and "possession of group homogeneity" show what the intrinsic nature of birth is.

Alternatively, "of whichever beings, these and those" refers to what belongs to the phase of death; it means these and those beings who have died. "Into these and those classes of beings" refers to what belongs to the phase of birth; it means into these and those abodes of beings where they are to be born. Consciousness, carrying the force of whatever karma, virtuous or otherwise, has been done, arising and swooning in the place where it is to link with the future, is called birth. The remaining words, "full birth" and the rest, are explanations of that very term: each later one should be seen as explaining the earlier.

Alternatively, for the womb-born, consciousness first entering the womb is birth. For the egg-born, consciousness first entering the egg is full birth. For those born from warmth and moisture, consciousness first arising in the warmth and moisture is transition. For the miraculously born, the aggregates appearing all together at one time is manifestation.

Among these, as for womb-born beings: although humans and animals are born from all four modes of birth, most are born from the womb. Among animals, too, the womb-born are such as elephants, horses, cattle, buffaloes, and pigs, and these too come from the mingling of the blood and semen of a male and a female. Among humans, those born from eggs are such as Śaila, Utpala, the five hundred sons of the king of Pañcāla, and the thirty sons of Viśākhā, the mother of Mṛgāra. Among humans, those born from warmth and moisture are such as Māndhātā, who was born from the crown of a head, Cāru and Upacāru, and the girl Āmrapālī, born from a mango fruit, who became the consort of the Licchavi chiefs. Among humans, the miraculously born are such as the humans of the first eon, who were born from the abodes of the gods into the four continents.

That animals are born from all four modes of birth is as taught in the Lokaprajñapti: "Egg-born garuḍas seize and eat egg-born nāgas; likewise, the womb-born seize and eat the womb-born; and likewise, those born from warmth and moisture and the miraculously born also seize and eat those of their own respective modes of birth." The egg-born are mostly birds, such as waterfowl, cranes, peacocks, parrots, and so on. Those born from warmth and moisture, it is said, are mostly ants and beetles, butterflies, flies, and lice. Miraculously born beings, their faculties unimpaired and complete, possessing all their major and minor limbs, are born all at once; such are gods, hell beings, and beings of the intermediate existence.

Among hungry ghosts there are both the womb-born and the miraculously born. An example of womb birth is what a hungry ghost said to the venerable Maudgalyāyana:

At night I give birth to five children,
And by day likewise to others too;
Though I bear them and devour them,
Still I am never satisfied.

Question: It is fine for garuḍas to seize and eat nāgas of a corresponding mode of birth; but if, for all the miraculously born, the aggregates vanish from sight as soon as they cease, how can they eat them?

Reply: Some explain that there is still one who seizes, because the garuḍa eats without understanding its manner of appearing. Other masters explain that, having seized it, the garuḍa may eat it for as long as it has not been parted from life.

With regard to the aggregates, their arising in those modes of birth is called the appearance of the aggregates. At the first moment of rebirth-linking, when the mental consciousness swoons, that is the aggregate of consciousness. Perception associated with the five consciousnesses and accompanied by affliction is the aggregate of perception. The experiencing of feeling at that time is the aggregate of feeling. The jellylike embryo (kalala), characterized by mingled blood and semen, is the aggregate of form. That is why it is called the appearance of the aggregates.

With regard to the sense bases, what is then called the acquisition of the sense bases is this. At the very moment of rebirth-linking, the blood and semen are the body sense base; the mental consciousness there is the mind sense base; and feeling and so forth at that time are the sense base of phenomena. It is these three sense bases that are called the acquisition of the sense bases; the sense bases of the eye and so forth are not present at that time, so the term does not apply to them.

With regard to the elements, what is then called the acquisition of the elements is this. What is there, characterized as blood and semen, is the body element; the mental consciousness at that time is the mind element; and the feeling and so forth present there are the element of phenomena. These three are called the acquisition of the elements; since the other elements are not present at that time, the term does not apply to the others.

"The appearance of the life faculty" is said because the life-formations are first obtained there; and the body faculty, too, should be regarded as maturing there. Why? Because in the desire and form realms the life faculty does not abide without form, since the life faculty operates in dependence on form. When these and those beings who have reached a womb become, from then on, of one kind with this or that class, it is called possession of group homogeneity.

To teach aging, it states the passage beginning, "With birth as condition, aging." This means that aging arises from birth as its cause.

"Baldness" means that because the warmth and strength of the great elements have declined, the hair falls out; or else that, because the body's strength is slight, the hands also tremble. "Grey hair" is the hair turning another color. "Many wrinkles" means the skin becoming wrinkled and shrunken. "Decrepitude" is being unable to enjoy fully the objects of desire, or the fading of luster and radiance. "Infirmity" is having little strength for tasks and being sickly by nature. "Tottering like a bent stick" means that when walking, the body's posture sways to the right and left and totters. "A body wheezing with phlegm" means that when one speaks, being blocked by phlegm and the like, the breath goes in and out only with difficulty. "Hunched" means that, because the great elements in the joints have declined, one cannot hold the body upright and is bent forward. "A body covered with dark spots" means that dark coloring has appeared over most of the face and limbs. "A body stooped forward" means that even when sitting, the body bends forward and cannot stay upright. "Leaning on a staff" means that when rising from a resting posture, one cannot get up without leaning on a staff. "The ripening of the faculties" means that, being feeble by nature, one sees forms, hears sounds, and so on, unclearly. "Decline" is having no diligence for tasks and, because the limbs have broken down, always being lazy. "The formations growing old" means that the five aggregates have reached their last stage, the aggregates themselves having drawn near the time of death. "Breaking down" means that the wish to look at and listen to objects such as forms and sounds no longer arises as before. "Confusion" means that, because the faculty of mindfulness has declined, one forgets and cannot remember what one did and said before. "Weakness" means that when the mind lies down to rest, sleep is heavy and one remains asleep for a long time. "Diminishment" is being unable to rise quickly from lying asleep in this way. "Complete diminishment" means that the life-formations projected by former karma are also coming to their close, so that one is facing the time of death.

Summarizing what was explained above, there are eleven points. The eleven are: change of the basis, change of the hair, change of the skin, change of radiance and strength, change of health, change of complexion, change of deportment, change of the non-physical faculty, change of the physical faculties, the end point, and the exhaustion of the life-formations. Of these, "baldness" shows the change of the basis. "Grey hair" shows the change of the hair. What is designated "many wrinkles" shows the change. "Decrepitude" shows the change of radiance and strength. "Infirmity" shows the change of health. "A body covered with dark spots" shows the change of complexion. "Hunched," "tottering like a bent stick," "a body wheezing with phlegm," "a body stooped forward," and "leaning on a staff" show the alteration of deportment. "Confusion" shows the alteration of the non-physical faculty. "The ripening of the faculties," "decline," "breaking down," "weakness," and "diminishment" show the alteration of the physical faculties. "The formations growing old" shows the end point. "Complete diminishment" shows the exhaustion of life.

To teach death, it states the passage beginning, "What is the teaching on death?" The words "of whichever beings, these and those" mean these and those beings: gods, humans, animals, hell beings, and so on. The words "from these and those classes of beings" mean from these and those abodes of the same kind, of gods, humans, and so on.

Here, the defining characteristic of death is this: when one has been abandoned by warmth, life, and consciousness, that is called dying. Thus other sūtras too say:

When life, warmth, and consciousness
Have abandoned this body,
Like a piece of mindless wood
It falls and lies upon the ground.

Here, when beings die without inwardly experiencing suffering, that is passing away. When beings die while inwardly experiencing suffering, that is coming to pass away. Consciousness leaving the body, so that body and consciousness come apart, is called separation. The cessation of the physical faculties, the eye and so on, is called disappearance. When beings die before their time, that is dying. Those endowed with merit, not long kept company by harm or suffering, swiftly meet the time of death. The time of dying, when the breath moves and the death rattle comes forth, is called the exhaustion of life.

When beings die at their proper time, that is called the cessation of the life faculty, the casting off of the aggregates, and the destruction of the sense bases. Alternatively, the exhaustion of the life-formations is the cessation of the life faculty; being separated from the former aggregates is the casting off of the aggregates; and the sense bases of the former aggregates, the eye and so on, no longer performing their functions, such as seeing, is the destruction of the sense bases.

To conclude what has been explained at length about the twelve links of dependent origination, it says the passage beginning, "Monks, this is..."

Then why are the twelve links, ignorance and the rest, set out in this order?

Reply: At the very first, delusion arises with regard to what is to be known; therefore ignorance is explained first. When one is deluded, one forms mistaken actions with regard to that very thing; so after it come formations. When mistaken actions have been formed, by that cause the mind too becomes mistaken; therefore after that comes consciousness. When the mind has become mistaken, from that it links up with a new life; so after that come name and form. From name and form the faculties arise complete; therefore after that come the six sense bases. Once the faculties are complete, the faculties enjoy objects; therefore after that comes contact. From enjoying objects there arise attachment to pleasant feeling and the wish to be rid of painful feeling; therefore after that comes feeling. From attachment arises the longing to be constantly accompanied by pleasant feeling; therefore after that comes craving. From longing arises the effort to seek it; therefore after that comes grasping. At the time of grasping the afflictions increase, and with the increase of the afflictions one performs the karma of renewed existence; therefore after that comes existence. When karma and afflictions have been accumulated, one comes to be born in the five courses of rebirth; therefore after that comes birth. Once one is born, sufferings of many kinds arise; therefore after that come aging and death. That is why they are set out in that order.

After teaching dependent origination, since the four noble truths had been mentioned, the sūtra, wishing to explain them as they were mentioned, asks, "Now, what are the four noble truths?" and so on. "Now" is a word of distinction: it means that the four truths are singled out and taught distinctly, apart even from the twenty-two faculties and so forth.

If one asks why the Blessed One taught the four noble truths: those who profess to be teachers, such as the wandering mendicants, the naked ascetics, the bearers of human skull cups, and those without scripture, proclaim and teach the special features of their own teachings, saying, "My teaching is the genuine, unerring truth; those of others are not." Seeing that they held conceit about what is in fact mistaken, he taught the passage beginning, "What are the four noble truths?" in order to teach the ultimate to those who are deluded about happiness.

Alternatively, there are those who have entered this teaching, completely given up the signs of a householder, and taken up the signs of a renunciant; who think, "Dwelling in the wilderness, I am a renunciant"; who perfectly uphold the qualities of moral discipline, make do with old requisites, are restrained by shame and conscience, and have shut the doors through which the faculties go out to objects; and who yet, lacking the wisdom that comes from learning, have become convinced, "I am a renunciant." So that they may abandon their conceit and devote themselves to being renunciants in the ultimate sense, he taught the passage beginning, "What are the four noble truths?", intending that in this teaching the realization of the truths is itself what makes a renunciant in the ultimate sense.

Alternatively, for the sake of those fortunate ones who have fully avoided the eight unfree states, whose mindstreams are thoroughly perfumed with the fragrance of the flowers of the roots of virtue that come from honoring the tathāgatas of the past, who have properly gathered the accumulations for attaining the fruit, whose intention and application are thoroughly pure, and who are destined to realize the noble truths, the Blessed One spoke this sūtra beginning, "What are the four noble truths?"

Alternatively, he taught the four truths in order to show the cause of not going there to those whose minds are terrified of meeting the unbearable sufferings of the lower realms. Why? Beings born in the hells, such as Avīci, Saṃghāta, Raurava, Mahāraurava, Tapana, and Pratāpana, helplessly have their mouths forced open with iron tongs, and when iron balls and molten iron are poured in, they experience the suffering of flames blazing up from their noses, the crowns of their heads, and so on. Moreover, those born as hungry ghosts are oppressed by the suffering of thirst and hunger: even if they were to eat and drink without pause, there would be no time when they had enough, let alone an end to it. Because they are tormented by the ripening of their former karma, even when they go in search of water, the waters dry up, or become filled with foul-smelling filth such as pus and lymph; flowering trees become flowerless and fruit trees fruitless; and they experience immeasurable sufferings such as these. And those born as animals undergo the sufferings of being bound, being killed, devouring one another, and so on. To those who live in constant unhappiness, fearing that such sufferings will befall them, he taught the four noble truths in order to show the cause of not going to the three lower realms. Having realized the four truths correctly, just as they are, all of them will be freed from the sufferings of the lower realms.

Now, what is the meaning of "noble truth"? "Noble" refers to one who has gained mastery over oneself. Thus buddhas, the great noble śrāvakas, and so on are called noble because they have gained mastery over their minds, while ordinary beings are called not noble because they have come under the power of the afflictions. "Truth" means what is unerring, or unmistaken, or genuine. The truth by which one becomes noble is the noble truth. Alternatively, the truth explained by the noble ones is the noble truth.

Then, one may ask, would what is explained by ordinary beings not be a noble truth? In reply: all of them, too, explain through the blessing of noble ones such as the Tathāgatas, and they teach the cause of becoming noble; therefore it is called noble truth. Alternatively, because these are true for the noble ones, they are called noble truths.

An objection: Are these then false for ordinary beings? The reply: Whether one is a noble one or a childish being, these are, for all, unerring in their characteristics of being suffering and so on; so for everyone as well they are called noble truths. If they are true for everyone, why then are they called the truths of the noble ones? The reply: The noble ones see, correctly and just as it is, that these are suffering and so on, whereas ordinary beings do not see it correctly, just as it is. Therefore they are called truths for the noble ones. And so it is just as the sūtras state:

What the noble ones declare to be true,
Others know to be suffering;
What others proclaim to be true,
The noble ones know to be suffering.

And it is also said:

Just as a single strand of hair is not felt on the palm of the hand,
Yet if that same strand gets into the eye, discomfort and pain arise,
So the childish, who are like the palm of the hand, do not feel the strand that is the suffering of formations,
But the wise, who are like the eye, grow deeply weary of that very thing.

Thus the noble ones see that the five aggregates of clinging and so on are suffering, but ordinary beings do not see them that way.

Then why is it certain that there are only four noble truths? Because there are four kinds of mistaken view, the truths too are taught as only four, as their antidote. The four mistaken views are the view of the transitory collection, the view of annihilation, the view of eternalism, and the view of non-action. Of these, as the antidote to the view of the transitory collection, which regards the five aggregates as a self, the truth of suffering is taught. As the antidote to the view of annihilation, "When the aggregates perish here, there is no birth in another life," the truth of origin is taught, which shows that as long as there are karma and afflictions, the aggregates continue without interruption. As the antidote to the view, "The five aggregates abide permanently and eternally," the truth of cessation is taught, which shows that when karma and afflictions are abandoned, the continued arising of the aggregates is cut off, and so they are not eternal. As the antidote to the view of non-action, "Even if virtue is done by one person, its result will be experienced by another," the truth of the path is taught: it is the very one who cultivates the path who becomes fully liberated, and one person does not become liberated through another's cultivation of the path. If that were so, there would follow the fault that Yajñadatta would experience the results of the deeds done by Devadatta.

Alternatively, only four truths are taught, as the antidote to four kinds of mistaken understanding. The four kinds of mistake are mistaken understanding of the result, mistaken understanding of the cause, mistaken understanding of liberation, and mistaken understanding of the method for it.

As for what is called mistaken understanding of the result: the aggregates, elements, and sense sources, which have come about as the result of craving, pride, and karma, are like a vessel of filth, by their intrinsic nature impure in manifold ways; they are constantly burned by the fire of the suffering of suffering, the suffering of change, and the suffering of formations; they are continually crushed by the vajra of impermanence; and they are devoid of a self and of what belongs to a self, of an agent, of an experiencer, and so on. Yet they are grasped as pure, as pleasant, as permanent, and as a self. To counter that, the noble truth of suffering was taught.

As for what is called mistaken understanding of the cause: it is the doctrine of discordant causes and of no cause, which says, "All these outer and inner things arise from Brahmā, Īśvara, the Person (puruṣa), Nature, and Time, or without a cause, and so on." To counter that, the truth of origin was taught.
