As for what is called mistaken understanding of liberation: it is the claim that one attains liberation and the higher realms by coming to see, following the Sāṃkhya system, the self and what is called the Principal (pradhāna), that is, the three, rajas, tamas, and sattva, when in equilibrium; or else by pleasing Maheśvara (Śiva). To counter that, the truth of cessation was taught.

As for what is called mistaken understanding of the method for it: it is the claim of those who say, "One will be liberated by eating coarse food such as flowers, fruits, roots, and stalks, and by making one's bed on grass, leaves, planks, thorns, ashes, and the like," and the claim, "One will be liberated through the austerity of the five fires, through fasting, through leaping from cliffs, and so on." To counter that, the truth of the path is taught.

Alternatively, only four noble truths are taught in order to show without error the result and the cause of both thorough affliction and purification. What shows without error the result of thorough affliction is the truth of suffering, for whatever phenomena of thorough affliction there may be, the result of them all is suffering. To show without error the cause of the phenomena of thorough affliction, the truth of origin is taught, for karma and afflictions are the cause of the phenomena of thorough affliction. To show without error the result of the phenomena of purification, the truth of cessation is taught, for nirvāṇa is the result of the factors conducive to awakening. To show without error the cause of the phenomena of purification, the truth of the path is taught, for the noble eightfold path is the cause of nirvāṇa.

Alternatively, it is said in the Abhidharma: "Those who see saṃsāra as fraught with faults and nirvāṇa as endowed with benefits undertake diligence in order to emerge definitively from saṃsāra." Here, what brings about the seeing of saṃsāra as fraught with faults is the seeing of the truth of suffering: when one sees all that belongs to the three realms as possessed of the eight kinds of suffering, one views the phenomena of saṃsāra as fraught with faults. The cause that produces the faults of saṃsāra is the origin, which has the characteristic of becoming, for the sufferings of saṃsāra arise through the power of craving. What brings about the seeing of nirvāṇa as endowed with benefits is the seeing of the truth of cessation: having seen cessation as peace, as excellent, and so on, one regards nirvāṇa as having good qualities. The cause that brings about the attainment of that nirvāṇa is the truth of the path, for nirvāṇa becomes manifest through the power of cultivating the path.

Alternatively, the four truths are taught in order to set out the result and the cause of the worldly and of the world-transcending. Here, the result of worldly phenomena is the truth of suffering, and its cause is the truth of origin. The result of world-transcending phenomena is the truth of cessation, and its cause is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, with reference to clear realization as well, only four truths are taught in the sūtras. How so? It is said, "One who, through correct contact, touches the aggregates as impermanent and so on does not generate the craving not yet generated, and abandons the craving already generated." Here, the aggregates are the truth of suffering; craving is the truth of origin; not generating what has not been generated and abandoning what has been generated are the truth of cessation; and correctly touching them as impermanent and so on is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, it is said, "Those who contemplate the phenomena that fetter as fraught with faults bring craving to cessation without remainder." Here, the phenomena that fetter one to the whole host of afflictions are the truth of suffering; craving is the truth of origin; the cessation of craving without remainder is the truth of cessation; and contemplating them as fraught with faults is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, the four truths are taught in order to show the phenomena that are to be understood, to be abandoned, to be actualized, and to be cultivated. Here, the truth of suffering is to be understood, the truth of origin is to be abandoned, the truth of cessation is to be actualized, and the truth of the path is to be cultivated.

Alternatively, it is said, "When one directly knows and fully knows the intrinsic nature of the aggregates of clinging, one gives up attachment." Here, the aggregates of clinging are the truth of suffering; attachment is the truth of origin; giving up attachment is the truth of cessation. Direct knowledge is the knowledge, on the path of seeing, of the aggregates as impermanent and so on; full knowledge is the knowledge, on the path of meditation, of them as impermanent and so on; and that is the truth of the path.

Then why, among these noble truths, is the noble truth of suffering taught first, after it the truth of origin, then the truth of cessation, and then the path? Why are they set out in this order? In reply: they are set out in order following the sequence of meditation and of coarseness. That the five aggregates have become the basis of manifold sufferings is evident to everyone through direct perception; since it is obvious, the truth of suffering is taught first. When one has seen them thus as suffering, one then wonders, "What is their cause?" Therefore after that comes the truth of origin. Then, as one wonders, "Where do these aggregates of suffering cease?", the truth of cessation comes after that, in order to show, "When one passes into nirvāṇa, suffering ceases." To dispel the wondering, "How is that cessation attained?", the truth of the path is taught after that.

Alternatively, the meaningless mass of birth, aging, and so on throughout beginningless saṃsāra is the truth of suffering. The root of the meaningless mass is the truth of origin. The destruction of the meaningless mass is the truth of cessation. What destroys the root of the meaningless mass is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, what is like a burden is the truth of suffering; taking up the burden is the truth of origin; laying down the burden is the truth of cessation; and casting off the taking up of the burden is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, the aggregates, which are phenomena that fetter, are the truth of suffering; what fetters, such as desire, is the truth of origin; bringing the fetters to exhaustion is the truth of cessation; and seeing danger in the aggregates is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, the five destinies are the truth of suffering; karma and afflictions are the truth of origin; the absence of destinies is the truth of cessation; and destroying karma and afflictions, the causes of the destinies, is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, as when one has entered a wilderness, the terrors of the three lower realms and so on in the wilderness of saṃsāra are the truth of suffering. Attachment to karma, the cause of the various terrors, is the truth of origin. The city free from fear, nirvāṇa, is the truth of cessation. The cause that brings one to the city of nirvāṇa is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, all phenomena that are results are the truth of suffering; all phenomena that are causes are the truth of origin; the destruction of the phenomena that are results is the truth of cessation; and what destroys the phenomena that are causes is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, what is like a sprout is the truth of suffering; what is like a seed is the truth of origin; the state free from the properties of the sprout is the truth of cessation; and what destroys the properties of the seed is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, what is like an illness is the truth of suffering; what is like the causes of illness, such as phlegm and bile, is the truth of origin; what is like freedom from illness is the truth of cessation; and what is like medicine is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, what, like a fire, is full of the various torments of suffering is the truth of suffering; what is like firewood, the cause that produces fire, is the truth of origin; what is like the fire having died down is the truth of cessation; and what is like water, the cause that puts out fire, is the truth of the path.

Again, because the truth of suffering produces manifold sufferings just as an enemy does, what is like an enemy is the truth of suffering. What is like an old grudge is the truth of origin. Because it is free from all the harms of the three realms, what is like being free from an enemy's harm is the truth of cessation. What is like clearing away the grudge is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, because it has become the basis of all faults, what is like a cloth is the truth of suffering; what is like the dirt is the truth of origin; what is like the dirt having been washed away is the truth of cessation; and what is like water and cleanser, the cause that washes away the dirt, is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, what is like being afflicted with the suffering of debt is the truth of suffering; what is like poverty is the truth of origin; what is like having paid off the debt is the truth of cessation; and what is like finding a treasure is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, what is like being wretched with the torment of heat is the truth of suffering; what is like fire, the cause that produces the torment of heat, is the truth of origin; what is like being free from the torment is the truth of cessation; and what is like the remedies that dispel the torment, such as sandalwood and camphor, is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, what is like a person who has eaten poison experiencing the suffering of the poison spreading is the truth of suffering; what is like the poison is the truth of origin; what is like the poison being neutralized is the truth of cessation; and what is like an antidote to poison is the truth of the path.

Alternatively, suffering is of two kinds: meeting with what is unloved and separation from what is loved. These in turn do harm in two places: in the body and in the mind. The truth of origin is also of three kinds: craving, views, and karma. Of these, craving and views act as the cause of birth in the three realms, and virtuous and non-virtuous karma act as the cause of coming to happiness and suffering there. Cessation is also of three kinds: the cessation that consists in the abandonment, by those who abide in views, of the afflictions arisen from causes such as the view of self; the cessation characterized by the abandonment, by those who abide in desire, of attachment to the five sense pleasures of the desire realm; and the cessation characterized by the abandonment, by those who abide in attachment to existence, of the afflictions of the form and formless realms. The path is of four kinds: the path of preparation, the path of seeing, the path of meditation, and the path of knowing what has been done.

Of these, the path of preparation is this: while one is still an ordinary being, there is the gathering of the accumulations, which consists of abiding in moral discipline, having few possessions, knowing moderation in food, and striving in hearing, reflection, and so on; and there are heat, peak, patience, and the supreme worldly phenomenon, these four factors of definite penetration. These are the path of preparation. Seeing the four noble truths without error at the time of attaining the fruit of stream-entry is the path of seeing. At the time of the fruits of once-returner and non-returner, familiarizing oneself with that very seeing of the truths is the path of meditation. At the time of the fruit of arhatship, the understanding, "I have done what was to be done; the burden is laid down; birth is exhausted; I know no existence other than this," is the path of knowing what has been done.

Alternatively, as for the noble truths being set out in this order: the Blessed One too taught them in this order following the order in which yogins examine the noble truths, and whichever truth is examined first is the very one taught first. Here, the yogin first examines the truth of suffering, which has come about as the result of karma and afflictions and is the basis of manifold sufferings. The manner of examining, moreover, concerns what suffering one is attached to, by what one suffers, and from what, amid suffering, the aspiration for liberation arises. What is suffering? The five aggregates are suffering. By what does one suffer? One is harmed and made to suffer by the five aggregates themselves. From what does one wish to be liberated? One wishes to be liberated from suffering itself. Therefore the truth of suffering is taught first. When one examines, "From what has that suffering arisen?", one sees that it has arisen from the causes, karma and afflictions; therefore after that the truth of origin is taught. Then, when one examines, "Where does that suffering cease?", one sees that it ceases when the state of nirvāṇa is attained; therefore after that the truth of cessation is taught. Therefore, when one examines, "By what cause is that cessation attained?", one sees that it is attained by practicing the noble eightfold path; therefore after that the truth of the path was taught.

This is like a physician who, setting about curing a patient's illness, seeks out what the illness is, what the basis of the illness is, what freedom from illness is, and what the medicine is. When the physician first sets about curing the illness, he examines what the illness itself is. After that he examines the basis of the illness, the cause of illness, such as food, from which it has arisen. Then he examines freedom from illness, asking, "Is this illness curable or not?" After that he examines the medicine, asking, "What is the medicine for this illness?"

Likewise, in this sūtra too the order of the truths is explained in connection with this. How so? As it is said: "Just as a physician endowed with four factors is a remover of all darts, worthy of a king, becomes a support for the king, and is counted as what is called a king of physicians. What are the four factors? He is skilled in the ailment, he is skilled in the cause of the ailment, he is skilled in eliminating the ailment, and he is skilled in ensuring that the ailment, once eliminated, does not arise again later. In just the same way, the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the Perfectly Complete Buddha, endowed with four factors, is called the unsurpassed physician, the remover of all darts. What are the four factors? Here, monks, the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the Perfectly Complete Buddha fully knows, correctly and just as it is, 'This is the truth of suffering.' He fully knows, correctly and just as it is, 'This is the truth of the origin of suffering; this is the truth of the cessation of suffering; this is the truth of the path leading to the cessation of suffering.'"

Thus, in order to explain in detail the four truths that were taught only in brief, the passage beginning "What is the noble truth of suffering?" was spoken.

Here one may ask: why, after being taught in brief, are they explained in detail? There are two kinds of disciples: those who delight in a brief teaching and those who delight in a detailed explanation. Of these, for the sake of those who, like the noble Śāriputra, delight in a brief teaching, it was declared in summary, from "What are the four noble truths?" up to "the noble truth of the path leading to the cessation of suffering." For the sake of those who, like the noble Joyful and others, delight in a detailed explanation, the passage beginning "What is the noble truth of suffering?" was spoken at length.

Alternatively, those to be trained are of two kinds, those strong in causes and those strong in conditions. Those like the noble Kāśyapa accumulated the greater part of their roots of virtue in former lives, and their mind streams have fully ripened; since even the mere mention of a word brings them to understand a vast meaning, for these, who are strong in causes, the teaching was given in brief. Those strong in conditions are those who need to be taught by a spiritual friend; since they cannot understand unless there is an extensive explanation, it is explained to them at length.

Alternatively, there are two kinds of faculties, sharp faculties and dull. For those of sharp faculties it was taught in brief; for those of dull faculties it was explained at length. Again, there are two kinds of śrāvakas, those of much learning and those of little learning. To those of much learning it was taught in brief; to those of little learning it was explained at length. This same answer also serves for the occasions when the meaning of dependent origination and so on is taught in brief and explained at length.

As for "the noble truth of suffering": because the noble ones comprehend it as suffering, it is the noble truth of suffering; or, because one who understands suffering in saṃsāra becomes noble, it is the noble truth of suffering. "Truth" means that it is not mistaken as suffering: because it is genuinely suffering, it is the noble truth of suffering.

Now, among the eight sufferings, beginning with birth, why is birth explained first? Just as moral discipline serves as the basis for the first arising of all worldly and world-transcending qualities, so birth too acts as the cause for the first arising of the remaining sufferings, aging, sickness, and so on; therefore it is explained first. Alternatively, suffering is of two kinds: root suffering, and suffering that is like the branches and so on. Of these, root suffering is birth, because it has become the root of the remaining sufferings, aging and so on. Suffering that is like the branches and so on is the remaining ones, the suffering of aging and so on, because they arise from birth as their cause.

What, then, is birth? What was stated above, in the section on dependent origination, in the passage beginning "the birth and coming into being of these various sentient beings in these various classes of sentient beings," is birth. Alternatively, the acquiring of the aggregates, elements, and sense sources in these various classes of beings, through the force of particular karma, virtuous, non-virtuous, and so on, accompanied by the habitual tendencies of afflictions such as craving, which has become the cause of rebirth-linking, is called birth.

Alternatively, birth is of five kinds: birth from understanding the noble Dharma, birth in the abode of the womb, birth into an excellent kind, birth with the characteristic of a shared class, and birth with the characteristic of emergence. Of these, birth from understanding the noble Dharma is being born as a child of the Buddha through realizing the meaning of the Tathāgata's scriptures, as the sūtras say: "born from his heart, born from his mouth, born from the noble Dharma and Vinaya." Birth in the abode of the womb is, from when the mental consciousness first enters the womb until one has come out of the womb, the oval stage arising from the jelly-like stage, the fleshy stage forming from the oval stage, and so on. Birth into an excellent kind is as when one speaks of the kind of a precious jewel as having become an indranī sapphire, or as having become a great indranīla sapphire. Birth of a shared class of sentient beings is that whereby the limbs of the body become alike, as cows share a class with cows, and horses share a class with horses. Birth as emergence is, having been born from the mother's womb, becoming individually complete in the aggregates, elements, sense sources, and so on. Of the five kinds of birth, two are to be considered here: birth in the abode of the womb and birth as emergence.

Why is birth called suffering? The reply: because all births in the three realms are constantly burned by the three kinds of suffering, birth is called suffering. How so? Those born, through causes of demerit, into the classes of the three lower realms and so on are constantly burned by the fire of the suffering of suffering, such as being burned and so on. Those born, through the cause of formations that are meritorious and lead to merit, into the shared classes of humans, gods, and so on in the desire realm are constantly burned by the fire of the suffering of change. Those who arise through the cause of unmoving formations, which have the characteristic of meditative concentration and are other than the formations leading to merit and to demerit, and who are born into the classes of the gods of the form and formless realms, are constantly burned by the fire of the suffering of formations, because they too are subject to impermanence.

Alternatively, just as, once trees have grown, a forest's being burned by fire, a grove's being carried off by water, and so on can occur, but not if the trees have not grown, so when there is birth, the sufferings of being cut, split, bound, and killed, of hunger, thirst, and so on occur, but if there is no birth these do not occur. For this reason too, birth is called suffering.

Alternatively, what is called birth is the source of many sufferings, the wellspring of various sorrows, the root of all that is meaningless, the occasion of bondage to the causes of joy, the breeding ground of various illnesses, the great treasury of many faults, the basis of decline and ruin, the field that produces weariness and discomfort in body and mind, the outflow of various afflictions, what the noble ones revile, what the childish praise, the state that those who delight in existence long for with delight, and the object of the Tathāgatas' compassion; for this reason too, birth is suffering.

Alternatively, from the abode of the womb onward until it reaches the ocean of death, it is constantly accompanied by suffering of various kinds; for this reason too, birth is called suffering. How so? First, as a result of the father and mother engaging in sexual union, the consciousness enters the mingled impurities of blood and semen, and when it swoons, it suffers like one who has fallen from a great precipice into pitch darkness. When, from the blood and semen of the parents, it gradually transforms into the jelly-like stage, the oval stage, the fleshy stage, and so on, and matures, it is filled with suffering like a ripening boil. When, with difficulty, the major and minor limbs each protrude, suffering arises as if its arms and legs were being stretched from opposite sides. When it dwells in the womb between the digested and the undigested food, it suffers as if pressed between two mountains. When the mother eats warm food or cold food and so on, runs, jumps, stretches or bends her limbs, and makes other such movements, it experiences suffering of various kinds: when the mother has eaten hot food, it suffers as if surrounded by fire; when she eats cold food, it suffers as if thrown into a hollow of ice; when she jumps, it suffers as if a powerful man had lifted it up and dashed it violently against the ground. And when it comes out of the mother's womb, it is squeezed out under extreme pressure, as if drawn through a press, so that its suffering is intense. And when it has emerged, its very tender body, soft and raw amid blood, mucus, and the like, is touched by hands, water, and so on, and an unbearable pain arises, like salt water poured into a wound; because of this, birth is suffering.

As for "the suffering of aging," what is aging? What was stated above in the passage beginning "baldness and white hair" is aging. Alternatively, aging is the loss of youthful bloom, the decline of youthful vigor, the diminishing of the great elements, the formations' growing old, the breaking down of the major and minor limbs, the complexion and shape becoming unsightly, the weakening of the faculties, the diminishing of mindfulness and intelligence, and the facing of death.

Alternatively, when the phenomenon called aging presses down, it turns all things into something else. When it presses on the face, it makes it full of wrinkles and covers it with dark blotches. When it presses on the hair, it turns it white and makes one bald. When it presses on the great elements, it makes the limbs heavy and decrepit. When it presses on the sense sources, it weakens the faculties, so that one sees and hears unclearly. When it presses on the body, it makes the body tremble and totter. When it presses on the mind, it deprives one of mindfulness and intelligence, so that one forgets even what one said and did before. When it enters the joints of the back, it makes the body stoop. When it enters the joints of the arms and legs, it makes them wobble like a crooked stick. Because the youthful body is parted from its youth, it also shows the formations to have grown old. That is aging.

Alternatively, aging is of two kinds: that characterized by the decline of the support, and that characterized by the decline of what is supported. Of these, the one characterized by the decline of the support is this: because the flesh, blood, fat, bone, marrow, and so on, which derive from the four great elements, have diminished, one is for that reason unable to perform such actions as running, jumping, stretching, bending, coming, and going, and the body is left without diligence or strength. The decline of what is supported is this: because the faculties, such as the eye, decline, clear seeing with the eye, hearing, smelling, and tasting change; and because, for that reason, mindfulness and intelligence diminish, one forgets even what one said and did before, and the mind is left without diligence or power.

Why is aging called suffering? The reply: when this aging has taken hold, it produces ugliness in the limbs of the body, and, having ruined beauty, strength, diligence, courage, steadiness, intelligence, and discernment, it leads one to the state of death. It is just as executioners cut off and strip away various parts of a man who is to be killed, make his form ugly, and lead him to the place of execution; therefore aging is called suffering.

Alternatively, formerly, in the time of youth, one's very youthful form, beautiful in complexion and shape, gladdened the minds of relatives, kinsfolk, friends, and so on, accorded with the hearts and minds of fair women, and, captivating their minds, was pleasing to many beings. When that form, which was like a lotus, is struck by the hail and frost of aging, it droops, shrivels, decays, and becomes unsightly, so that relatives, kinsfolk, and friends too become displeased, and fair women too forsake one; for this reason too, aging is suffering.

Alternatively, this aging strips of complexion, shape, and beauty what had reached its prime, destroys health, steals strength, diligence, and power, and produces the weakening of the faculties, which is the cause of the decline of mindfulness, intelligence, learning, and wisdom; for this reason too, aging is called suffering.

As for "and the suffering of sickness," what is sickness? When the elements, earth and the rest, are out of balance and become excessive or deficient, that is called sickness; when the elements, earth and the rest, are balanced, there is health. Alternatively, sickness is of two kinds: sickness of the body and sickness of the mind. Of these, sickness of the body is also of two kinds: that arising from internal conditions and that arising from external conditions.

Of these, that arising from internal conditions is whatever harm comes about when the great elements of the body, earth and the rest, are disturbed. That arising from external conditions is whatever harm is done when one is struck by sticks, stones, and the like. This sickness of the body is further classified into many kinds by way of differences in name and in causes and conditions. As for names, there are the many kinds of names given to diseases, such as scabies, swellings, leprosy, pox, boils, fever, and hiccups. As for causes, there are those arising from phlegm, those arising from bile, those arising from wind, and so on. As for bases, there are ailments of the brain, ailments of the eyes, ailments of the throat, and so on. This is sickness of the body. When it is classified in detail, it is said that there are four hundred and four categories of disease.

Sickness of the mind is the harm that comes from a mind born of conceptual thoughts of many different kinds. It too is of two kinds: that arising from focusing on internal things and that arising from focusing on external things. Of these, that arising from focusing on internal things is when, through focusing on one's own body, mental unhappiness and afflictions arise. That arising from focusing on external things is when mental unhappiness and afflictions arise from dwelling again and again in thought on relatives and friends and on the objects of sensual pleasure. This too is classified into many kinds by way of name, cause, and basis. As for names, there are attachment, aversion, delusion, anger, jealousy, resentment, and so on. As for causes, there are those arising from an attractive cause, those arising from a cause for anger, and those arising from a cause that is neither of these. As for bases, there are attachment to forms, attachment to sounds, attachment to smells, attachment to tastes, and so on. Here, however, it is sickness of the body that is intended.

Why is it called suffering? It is like this: when one is seized by this sickness, it produces discomfort of many kinds in one's body, it becomes a hindrance to the life faculty, and it torments one by striking at the vital points. Since it is thus like fire, poison, and weapons, it is called suffering. Alternatively, just as people, elephants, and others caught in the midst of a blazing forest fire, struck by the many sufferings of the flames, cannot bear it and wander to and fro crying out loudly, so too, when struck by the suffering of suffering called sickness, people cannot bear it, and they cry out loudly and weep. For this reason too it is suffering.

As for "and death is suffering," what is death? What was taught above, "the dying and passing on, and the state of having died and passed on, of these various sentient beings from these various classes of sentient beings," is death. Alternatively, when the life faculty, warmth, and consciousness have left the body and the continuum of the life faculty is cut off, that is called death. Alternatively, death is the deterioration of the faculties, the eye and the rest, through the disturbance of the great elements, earth and the rest. When the faculties have deteriorated, the mind and mental factors that depend on them do not arise; and when mind and mental factors do not arise, one is said to be dead.

Alternatively, death is of two kinds: death through the exhaustion of lifespan and death through the exhaustion of karma. Of these, death through the exhaustion of lifespan is dying when the force is spent of the karma, performed in another life, that determines how long the life formations will remain. Death through the exhaustion of karma is dying because, not having performed meritorious actions such as generosity, one lacks in this life the necessities, food, drink, and so on.

Alternatively, death is of three kinds: death in each instant, death through the exhaustion of causes, and death by being struck down. Of these, death in each instant has this characteristic: beginning from the first entry into the womb and continuing until the stream of the life formations of one's class of beings (nikāyasabhāga) is cut off, each earlier instant ceases and a later one arises. Death through the exhaustion of causes is when, because the life formations are exhausted, the stream of the aggregates of one's class of beings is cut off. Death by being struck down is dying upon meeting with conditions such as poison, fire, and weapons.

Why is death called suffering? It is like this. Just as someone endowed with an abundance of grain, precious treasures, possessions, relatives, kinsfolk, friends, and so on may be robbed by the powerful, such as kings, and left with nothing at all, so at the time of death one is surrounded by relatives, kinsfolk, and friends from whom it is hard to part, eyes fill with tears and voices choke with sobbing, and, leaving behind all one's possessions, wealth, treasure, and the rest, one must go alone, joyless, unwilling, and powerless. For this reason too death is suffering. Alternatively, those who have done what is not meritorious experience the unbearable suffering of the ceasing of the five faculties, the eye and the rest; and they are surrounded by the rākṣasas of Yama, by hungry ghosts, and others, who, even before the life faculty has been cut off, inflict on them the sufferings of being bound, threatened, and so on, and who, once the faculty of life has been cut off, lead them helplessly to the abode of the hells. For this reason too death is suffering. Alternatively, this thing called death is, like Mount Meru, unshaken even by the wind; like fire, it consumes everything; like a man filled with hatred, it constantly torments beings with harm; like the waves of the ocean, it never lets its time pass; like a vajra, it shatters the mountain of the aggregates; like the wind, it scatters whatever has been heaped up; like hail, it destroys the life formations; like an evildoer, it has no compassion; like a great elephant, it is hard to conquer; like a hero, it vanquishes others; and like time, it comes without fail. Therefore death is suffering.

If one asks, "Other sūtras speak of death conditioned by birth, death through heedlessness, and death through bad moral discipline; what difference is there among these?" the reply is this. Because the faculty of wisdom is corrupted, there is death through heedlessness. Because one is devoid of pure moral discipline, there is death through bad moral discipline. Because the continuum of the life faculty is cut off, there is death conditioned by birth. Alternatively, because one does not listen to the true Dharma, reflect on it, or meditate on it, there is death through heedlessness. Because one is deprived of the state of a monk, there is death through bad moral discipline. Because one is separated from one's class of beings and one's common lot, there is death conditioned by birth.

As for "and the suffering of separation from what is dear": being separated from pleasing objects, whether they belong to the class of sentient beings or to the class of what are not sentient beings, is called separation from what is dear. Alternatively, being separated from one's own youth, health, long life, family, beauty of form, wealth, and possessions, and being parted from one's relatives, kinsfolk, and friends, is called being separated from what is dear. Alternatively, when the virtuous actions of those who possess moral discipline completely decline, that is separation from what is dear. Alternatively, being separated from pleasant feeling, among the three kinds of feeling, is separation from what is dear. Alternatively, falling from the states of the happy destinies, those of gods, humans, and so on, and dropping into the lower realms is separation from what is dear.

Why is separation from what is dear called suffering? It is like this: because of separation from what is dear, living creatures, as though burned in a forest fire, constantly have their minds scorched by the fire of great grief, and they cry out loudly and weep; therefore separation from what is dear is suffering. Alternatively, because, having passed from happy abodes such as the higher realms and been born in the abodes of the lower realms, such as the hells, one comes to experience suffering of many kinds, it is the suffering of separation from what is dear. Alternatively, because even fearing, hearing of, seeing, or recalling separation from what is dear gives rise to sorrow, lamentation, suffering, mental unhappiness, agitation, and so on, it is again the suffering of separation from what is dear. Alternatively, as though harmed by a seizing spirit (graha) born of separation from relatives such as one's father and brothers, one babbles words of every kind; as though in a faint, one loses mindfulness; as though robbed of everything one owns, the person is thrown into turmoil in every way, and, remembering nothing of what should and should not be done, ends up doing no more than flinging the body down upon the ground. For this reason too, separation from what is dear is suffering.

As for "and the suffering of meeting with what is not dear": meeting with the hells and the like, the results that correspond to non-virtuous karma as their cause, is meeting with what is not dear. All sentient beings want happiness and long to be free of suffering, yet when things that produce painful feeling come near and, because of this, they experience painful feeling, that is meeting with what is not dear. Alternatively, keeping company with hostile beings is meeting with what is not dear. Alternatively, when bad conduct arises in those who possess moral discipline, that is meeting with what is not dear, because those who see danger even in the slightest wrongdoing are tormented in mind by that fault.

Why is meeting with what is not dear called suffering? Because, like a hostile neighbor, it constantly causes harm; like a savage creature, it becomes a source from which anger arises; and it acts as a cause producing joyless conceptual thoughts in the mind; for these reasons meeting with what is not dear is suffering. Alternatively, because of meeting with what is not dear, the mind is scorched by the fire of anger as though burned in a forest fire, and many wishes to be free of it arise; and since producing many such wishes is itself suffering, for that reason meeting with what is not dear is suffering.

As for "not finding what one wants even after seeking it, that too is suffering": what one wants is to be parted from suffering and never meet with it again, and to meet with happiness and never be parted from it again; that things do not turn out this way is itself not finding what one wants after seeking it. Alternatively, one wants not to meet with birth, aging, sickness, death, and so on; not having that wish fulfilled is called not finding what one wants after seeking it. Alternatively, one wants not to meet with what is not dear and not to be separated from what is dear; when this is not accomplished, it is called not finding what one wants after seeking it. Alternatively, one wants one's virtuous deeds to increase and non-virtue to be obstructed; when instead virtue is obstructed and non-virtue increases, it is called not finding what one wants after seeking it. Alternatively, one wants not to be born in the three lower realms and to be born in the higher realms; when instead one falls into the three lower realms and is not born in the higher realms, it is called not finding what one wants after seeking it.

Why is not finding what one wants after seeking it called suffering? "Oh! The thing that was wanted was not found before in this way, it is not found now, and it will not be found later either." Because harm thus arises in all three times, not finding what one wants after seeking it is suffering. Alternatively, because it crushes the hopes in one's mind, becomes a basis for dejection, and on that account constantly torments the mind, not finding what one wants after seeking it is suffering for this reason too.

As for "in brief, the five aggregates of clinging are suffering": the five aggregates of clinging are from the aggregate of clinging that is form up to the aggregate of clinging that is consciousness. The words "in brief" mean that it shows all suffering to be contained in the five aggregates. All suffering is the suffering of the beginning, the suffering of the middle, and the suffering of the end. Of these, the suffering of the beginning is birth. The suffering of the middle is aging, sickness, separation from what is dear, meeting with what is not dear, and not finding what one wants after seeking it. The suffering of the end is death. Since these three sufferings are contained in the five aggregates, it says, "In brief, the five aggregates of clinging are suffering." Alternatively, because the five aggregates are contained in abiding suffering and linking suffering, it says, "In brief, the five aggregates of clinging are suffering." Abiding suffering is aging, sickness, death, separation from what is dear, meeting with what is not dear, and not finding what one wants after seeking it. Linking suffering is birth, because it is connected with the limbs of existence that link one lifetime to the next. Alternatively, the varieties of suffering are measureless; even if one were to describe them until the end of an eon, one could not exhaust them. Therefore, in order to gather the endless sufferings, with their different characteristics, under a general characteristic, it was said, "In brief, the five aggregates of clinging are suffering." Alternatively, that which is like fire is the sufferings of birth and the rest.

That which is like firewood, the cause that produces the sufferings of birth and the rest, is the five aggregates of clinging. Since they act as the cause of all suffering, for this reason too it was said, "In brief, the five aggregates of clinging are suffering." Alternatively, birth and the rest are like seeds, while the five aggregates of clinging are like a field; for this reason too the passage beginning "In brief, the five aggregates of clinging" was spoken. How is this so? The suffering of the arising of the aggregates is birth. The suffering of the full ripening of the aggregates is aging. The suffering of harm to the aggregates is sickness. The suffering of the disintegration of the aggregates is death. The suffering of meeting with unpleasing aggregates is meeting with what is not dear. The suffering of being parted from pleasing aggregates is separation from what is dear. The suffering of not meeting with desired aggregates is not finding what one wants after seeking it.

Why are the five aggregates of clinging called suffering? The reply: the five aggregates of clinging are called suffering because, as long as there are five aggregates produced by karma and afflictions, there arise birth, aging, sickness, death, separation from what is dear, meeting with what is not dear, not finding what one wants after seeking it, sorrow, lamentation, mental unhappiness, agitation, and so on.

An objection: the Blessed One explained feeling as threefold, speaking of pleasant feeling, painful feeling, and feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful. If the aggregates of pleasant feeling and of feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful are not by nature suffering, why did he say here, "In brief, the five aggregates of clinging are suffering"? In reply: because the three kinds of suffering pervade everything conditioned, all the aggregates of clinging are explained as suffering. The aggregates associated with painful feeling are pervaded by the suffering of suffering. The aggregates associated with pleasant feeling are pervaded by the suffering of change. The aggregates of feeling that is neither pleasant nor unpleasant are pervaded by the suffering of formations, that is, of what is produced by causes and conditions and has the characteristic of impermanence.

Alternatively, just as a heap of māṣa beans is called māṣa beans even though there are a few mung beans in it, so, although there is a little happiness in these aggregates, because there is much suffering, all the aggregates are called suffering. Moreover, what wise person would reckon a boil pleasant because the great pain of the boil, once it has broken out, is eased merely by drawing it with mustard oil and lancing it? In the same way, what wise person would regard the five aggregates, that heap of suffering, as happiness because there is also a little happiness in them?

In other sūtras suffering is explained as threefold, while here suffering is taught as eightfold. Should the eight be regarded as included in the three, or should the three be regarded as included in the eight? The reply: the eight should be regarded as included in the three. Birth is suffering, aging is suffering, sickness is suffering, death is suffering, and meeting with what is not dear is suffering: these are included in the suffering of suffering. Separation from what is dear and not finding what one wants after seeking it, these two, are included in the suffering of change. By the words "in brief, the five aggregates of clinging are suffering," the suffering of formations is taught.

In order to teach the truth of the origin, the passage beginning "What is the noble truth of the origin of suffering?" was spoken. Why is the truth of the origin explained after the truth of suffering? Having taught the truth of suffering to those who do not understand the phenomena that are the results of saṃsāra, the truth of the origin is explained after suffering in order to teach the cause to those who do not understand the cause. Alternatively, these worldly people are bound tight by the tight bonds of craving and have not been freed from the prison of saṃsāra; to show the cause of the prison of saṃsāra, the truth of the origin was taught after suffering.

Craving, whose characteristic is attachment, is the cause, the heap of craving from which the heap of suffering originates and arises; it is called the origin of suffering, which means the cause of suffering. Because the noble ones have realized that this craving is the cause of suffering, or because one becomes a noble one by understanding that craving is the cause of suffering, and because it is unmistaken and unerring that this craving is the cause of suffering, it is called the noble truth of the origin of suffering.

And what is that craving? For that reason it is said: "the craving that brings renewed existence, accompanied by the attachment of delight, delighting in this and that." For example, just as a certain man, tormented by severe thirst at midday in springtime and wanting water, is never satisfied even though he drinks salt water continually, so those with attachment to sense pleasures, regarding body and enjoyments as advantages, are never satisfied even though they continually indulge in existence and the requisites of existence; that insatiability is craving.

The words "bringing renewed existence," "accompanied by the attachment of delight," and "delighting in this and that" are either qualifications of craving or synonyms for the word craving. Here, "renewed existence" refers to a future life: that which has the disposition to bring about a future life, or the nature of producing it, or which has become its cause, is called "renewed existence." "Accompanied by the attachment of delight" means having the nature of being attached to and clinging to a future body and enjoyments. "Delighting in this and that" means sentient beings making aspirations regarding many kinds of future states, such as "May I become a god! May I become a human! May I become Brahmā!" Alternatively, craving that becomes the cause of seeking objects not yet obtained, and so on, is called craving that brings renewed existence. Focusing intently on objects that have been obtained and encountered and, wishing not to be separated from them, engaging in activities such as guarding them, is called "accompanied by the attachment of delight."

Becoming miserable through being separated from objects one has encountered, and making aspirations, is called "delighting in this and that." Alternatively, attachment to meeting with pleasant feeling and never being separated from it is called craving that brings renewed existence. Wanting not to meet with painful feeling and to be separated from it is called "accompanied by the attachment of delight." Attachment to the feeling of meditative concentration, equanimity, which is neither pleasant nor painful, is called "delighting in this and that." Alternatively, making aspirations to meet with things not yet obtained is called craving that brings renewed existence. Setting about seeking the things one has aspired to is called "accompanied by the attachment of delight." Setting about increasing what one has sought is called "delighting in this and that."

Alternatively, craving is taught as fourfold with reference to oneself and objects, obtained and not obtained. Of these, not wanting to be parted from the bodies one has obtained is craving. Wanting to meet with and possess bodies one has not yet obtained is "bringing renewed existence." Wanting always to keep company with the objects one has obtained, form and the rest, is "accompanied by the attachment of delight." Wanting to meet with the objects not yet obtained, form and the rest, is called "delighting in this and that."

An objection: in the Abhidharma, the five aggregates that are causes are explained as the truth of the origin, or else karma and afflictions are explained as the truth of the origin. How is this known? Because the sūtra says, "Suffering, origin, world, basis of views, and existence are synonyms." The meaning of this is as follows. The five aggregates that are results are suffering. The five aggregates that are causes are the origin. Because the five aggregates are by nature perishing, they are the world. Because sentient beings view the five aggregates as a self, they are the basis of views. Because they transmigrate and take birth from one life to the next, they are existence. It was also said, "Karma, craving, and ignorance are the cause of future formations." Since, by contrast, this sūtra explains craving alone as the origin, it contradicts the other sūtras. The same sūtra says: "Monks, if these five kinds of seed are unbroken, not destroyed, not rotten, not damaged by wind and the like, and well planted, and if there is the earth element and likewise the water, fire, and wind elements, then these seeds will grow, grow abundantly, and become vast. In the same way, monks, this is set forth as an example so that this meaning may be understood: the expression 'five kinds of seed' is a figurative term for the five aggregates of clinging, and 'the earth element' and the rest are figurative terms for the four stations of consciousness." The four stations of consciousness are consciousness engaged with form, consciousness engaged with feeling, consciousness engaged with perception, and consciousness engaged with formations. Of these, in the desire realm consciousness relies on form, and so it is called consciousness engaged with form. In the four meditative concentrations of the form realm, because consciousness relies on pleasant feeling, it is called consciousness engaged with feeling. In the formless realm, in the base of infinite space, the base of infinite consciousness, and the base of nothingness, because consciousness relies on perceptions that take as their object "space is infinite" and so on, it is called consciousness inwardly engaged with perception. In the Peak of Existence, the base of neither perception nor nonperception, because consciousness relies on formations, both those associated with mind and those not associated with mind, it is called consciousness engaged with formations. The five kinds of seed are seeds from seeds, seeds from roots, seeds from stems, seeds from tops, and seeds.

In reply it is said: that this sūtra explains craving alone, the origin, as the truth of the origin is spoken with reference to the chief cause of all defiled phenomena, and the chief cause is craving. How is it the chief? Because this craving pervades all situations, pervades all times, and pervades all realms. It pervades all situations in that it always follows the three kinds of feeling. How so? When one meets with painful feeling, craving arises that wants to be separated from it, and when one has not met with it, craving arises that wants not to meet with it again; when one meets with pleasant feeling, craving arises that wants not to be separated from it, and when one is separated from it, craving arises that wants to meet with it again; and at the time of feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful, delusion arises in every way. Craving pervades all times because it follows the three times: toward the bodies and enjoyments of the past there arises a craving that longs for them; toward the future and its enjoyments there arises a craving of eager aspiration; and toward the present body and enjoyments there arises a craving to keep company with them always. It pervades all realms because it causes clinging to the things of the three realms: in the desire realm, through the power of craving, one becomes attached to the five objects of sensual pleasure; in the form realm, through the power of craving, one becomes attached to the taste of meditative concentration; and in the formless realm, through the power of craving, one becomes attached to the formless absorptions.

In order to teach the truth of cessation, the passage beginning "What is the noble truth of the cessation of suffering?" was spoken. Being parted from the eight sufferings, birth and the rest, and no longer possessing them, is the cessation of suffering. Because one who understands it in this way becomes a noble one, or because the noble ones have realized it in this way, it is called the noble truth of the cessation of suffering; and because it is unerring and unmistaken that nirvāṇa is the abandonment of all suffering, it is called the noble truth of the cessation of suffering.

Cessation, moreover, is of two kinds: cessation without analysis (apratisaṃkhyānirodha) and cessation through analysis (pratisaṃkhyānirodha). Of these, cessation without analysis is, like a stone thrown into the sky that falls back down, arising in each instant and perishing of its own accord without being destroyed by anything. Cessation through analysis is when, the wood of the afflictions having been burned by the fire of the wisdom that realizes the noble truths, one is no longer born in the three realms. Here it is cessation through analysis that is intended.

The truth of cessation is also taught by way of eleven points: the characteristic, profundity, the conventional, the ultimate, the incomplete, the complete, the unadorned, the adorned, that with remainder, that without remainder, and synonyms.

How is it taught by way of the characteristic? Suchness, the path, and the non-arising of afflictions are the characteristic of cessation. Suchness is the selflessness of persons. The path is the noble eightfold path. The non-arising of afflictions is that the afflictions, attachment and the rest, having been abandoned all together, do not occur again afterward. Thus where they cease, by what they cease, and what ceases are explained to be the characteristic of cessation. Of these, as to where they cease: through understanding the selflessness of persons, they cease in the state of nirvāṇa. As to by what they cease: they cease through the truth of the path. As to what ceases: the host of afflictions ceases. Therefore, with reference to cessation, the Blessed One said:

Eye and ear, and likewise nose,
Tongue and body, likewise mind,
Where name and form and all the rest
Have ceased without remainder.

How is it profound? Because cessation can neither be said to be other than those formations that have ceased, nor be said to be not other. How so? If cessation were something other than the formations, it would not be the cessation that is the ceasing of formations. If cessation were not other than the formations, that cessation would have the characteristic of the afflictions. Therefore it is said to be neither.

How is it conventional? The afflictions suppressed by the mundane path cease for a while but arise again later; that is called conventional cessation. How is it ultimate? The supramundane path destroys the afflictions from the root, and what has once been destroyed does not arise again; that is called ultimate cessation.

How is it incomplete? It is the abandonment of afflictions by persons still in training, those who have attained the fruit of stream-entry, the fruit of once-returning, and the fruit of non-returning; since it is not the abandonment of the afflictions without remainder, it is called incomplete cessation. How is it complete? It is the cessation of persons beyond training who have attained the fruit of arhatship; because they have abandoned all afflictions, it is called complete cessation.

How is it unadorned? The cessation of arhats liberated through wisdom lacks the special distinction of the six superknowledges, and so it is unadorned cessation. How is it adorned? It is the cessation of arhats liberated from both sides, meditative concentration and wisdom; because they possess qualities such as the three knowledges and the six superknowledges, it is called adorned cessation.

How is it with remainder? It is nirvāṇa in which the remainder of the aggregates has not been abandoned, because the remainder of the aggregates is still present. How is it without remainder? It is nirvāṇa with no remainder of the aggregates left at all, because even the mere aggregates have been abandoned.

How is it taught by way of synonyms? For this reason the sūtra says, "that which, of his craving that brings renewed existence," and so on. "That which, of his" refers to that noble one, the arhat who has abandoned the afflictions without remainder; the words are to be construed thus: "the abandonment without remainder of his craving that brings renewed existence and the rest is the truth of cessation."

It is asked: what difference is there among these words, "abandoned without remainder" and the rest? The reply: these are synonyms for nirvāṇa. Although there is no difference, they are designated by these various names according to the vessels to be trained. "Abandoned without remainder" means having abandoned craving through the noble path and attained separation from it. Thus it is as the Blessed One also said: "What is abandoned through the noble path is truly abandoned." What is abandoned through the mundane path is not abandoned without remainder, because there is no attainment of separation, and so it arises again later. "Abandoned individually" means having eliminated every possession of the afflictions; it means that the latent tendencies of the afflictions have been abandoned. "Destroyed" means that, by abandoning the possession of the afflictions, one has reached the end of saṃsāra; that is, one no longer has the phenomena of saṃsāra. "Exhausted" means having abandoned the afflictions other than attachment, such as anger, pride, and ignorance. "Free from attachment" means having abandoned attachment without remainder, that is, having eliminated it. "Cessation" means having abandoned and eliminated the five aggregates of clinging, which have the characteristic of being defiled. "Pacified" means having eliminated all the sufferings of birth, aging, and the rest. "Disappeared" means having cut off the continuum of linking from one lifetime to the next. That which has come about in this way is called the noble truth of the cessation of suffering.

Alternatively, because all the latent tendencies of the afflictions of the desire realm are abandoned, it is called "abandoned without remainder." Because all the latent tendencies of the afflictions of the form realm are abandoned, it is called "abandoned individually." Because the latent tendencies of the afflictions of the formless realm are abandoned without remainder, it is called "destroyed."

That the afflictions which have been abandoned do not arise again afterward is called "exhausted." Because attachment and clinging to the things of the three realms no longer arise, it is called "free from attachment." Because the present aggregates of clinging too will be cast off, it is called "cessation." Because the aggregates of clinging have been cast off, it is called "pacified." Because future aggregates do not arise, it is called "disappeared."

Alternatively, "abandoned without remainder" is set down merely as a summary, and the remaining words, "abandoned individually" and the rest, are its explanation. Because the latent tendency of craving is abandoned, it is "abandoned individually." Because the latent tendencies have been abandoned, the afflictions are no longer active in manifest form, and so it is "destroyed"; therefore it is called "exhausted." Because all afflictive things are seen as faults, it is "free from attachment." Because the continuum in which the afflictions continually arise is cut off, it is "cessation." Because the characteristic of liberation has been attained, it is "pacified." Because there is freedom from grasping at a self, it is "disappeared."

Alternatively, "abandoned without remainder" is the summary, and the rest are its explanation. If one asks how it is abandoned without remainder: it is abandoned without remainder because it is abandoned individually and so on. Since the manifestly active entanglements of the afflictions do not arise, and those that have arisen are abandoned as well, it is "abandoned individually." Since the latent tendencies of the afflictions have been pulled out by the root, so that not even the slightest trace remains, it is "destroyed."

Moreover, by way of the two kinds of remedy, the path of seeing and the path of meditation, it is set out in two ways, as "exhausted" and as "free from attachment." The abandonment, through the path of seeing, of the afflictions to be abandoned by seeing is called "exhausted." When, through the path of meditation, the afflictions to be abandoned by meditation have been abandoned, freedom from all attachment to the three realms is called "free from attachment." Since, by abandoning in this way the afflictions of seeing and of meditation, one has put an end to the continuum by which the five aggregates would arise in the future, it is called "cessation." Since even in this very life none of the things that harm the mind, such as mental unhappiness, arise, it is called "pacified." Since these aggregates too, projected by the momentum of former lives and serving as the basis of all kinds of suffering, will cease to be, it is called "disappeared."

In order to teach the truth of the path, the words "What is the noble truth of the path leading to the cessation of suffering?" and so on were spoken. Here, the ceasing of the eight sufferings explained above is the cessation of suffering, that is, nirvāṇa. The path that brings about its attainment, or that delivers one to it, is called "the path of liberation into the cessation of suffering," and below it is explained to be the noble eightfold path, beginning with right view. Because that path is virtuous in its intrinsic nature, it is called "noble"; or, because that path serves as the remedy for the afflictions, it is called "noble"; or, because it is the path taught by the noble ones, it is called "noble"; or, because by abiding in that path one becomes a noble one, it is the "noble path." That path unerringly and without distortion abandons the afflictions and brings about the attainment of nirvāṇa, and it is called "the noble truth of the path leading to the cessation of suffering."

The sense of "path" also applies to seeking: since by this path yogins seek nirvāṇa and the fruits of the renunciant life, it is called a "path." Alternatively, the sense of "path" applies to going. For example, just as people in the world say, "This road goes to the city of heaven," so too, because the eight branches of the noble path make one go and arrive at the city of nirvāṇa, it is called a "path."

In order to show what it actually is, the words "It is these very branches of the noble path, right view and the rest" were spoken. Right view is of two kinds, worldly right view and transcendent right view. Since the word "noble" is used here, and because it refers to what is undefiled, it does not refer to worldly right view; it refers to transcendent right view. The words "and the rest" include right thought, right speech, and so on. "Branch" refers to a part, or to something that serves as a helper; the meaning is "eight parts," or "eight causes and helpers that bring about the attainment of nirvāṇa." "These very" refers ahead to the eight branches of the noble path given below.

An objection: In the section teaching the truth of cessation, it was explained that cessation is the stopping of craving without remainder, but here, in the section teaching the truth of the path, it is explained as the truth of the cessation of suffering, so the earlier and later statements contradict each other. In reply to one who says this, it is said: Just as smoke does not arise once a fire has been put out, so when the cause, which is characterized as craving, is stopped, the suffering that is characterized as its result also ceases. Since at the time of nirvāṇa suffering too no longer exists, there is no fault in having explained it as "the cessation of suffering."

An objection: Only where there is a chief of the branches can one speak of "branches." Among the eight branches of the noble path, which is the chief of the branches, that these should be called "branches"? The reply: Right view is both the chief of the branches of the path and also a branch, so it is classified in two ways, as the chief of the branches and as a branch. The remaining seven, right thought and the rest, serve as its helpers, so they are classified only as branches.

In order to clarify the meaning of the eight branches of the noble path, a simile is given: the passage beginning "It is like this: some man..." "Some man" means simply any man at all. "Stricken by fever" means stricken by a disease of heat. "A physician praised as skilled" means one renowned in the world as skilled in all the treatises of medicine. "Intelligent" means possessing the oral instructions of the medical treatises. "Experienced in the work" means that those he has treated in the past have been helped, that he is also skilled in combining medicines with one another, and that he has done a great deal of the work of preparing and administering medicines. "Learned in the treatises" means having studied, without exception, all the treatises on medical treatment. "Went into his presence" means went up to where he was. So that the physician in turn may examine that patient for the source of the illness, the passage beginning "That physician in turn, with him, first..." is stated.

The patient, for his part, told the physician the source of the illness: this is the passage beginning "I ate yogurt and cucumber." That the physician, having discussed the source of the illness, told him to take medicine is what is meant by the words "That physician, specifying it precisely, prescribes medicine." "Specifying it precisely, he prescribes medicine" means that he first instructs him, "You drank yogurt, ate cucumber, and drank buttermilk for your thirst. Because of this your phlegm has increased, and so you have become feverish. Therefore, since the increase of phlegm must be calmed, do not eat any food today." After that he prescribes a cooling medicine.

Having stated the simile, in order to show the point itself, the passage beginning "In the same way, tormented by the great fever of the truths and so on" was spoken. "The truths" are the four truths of suffering, origin, cessation, and the path. The very wish to understand them, not yet having understood them, is called "tormented by the great fever of the truths and so on." "The virtuous ones" means this: those persons who wish to realize the four truths are virtuous because in other lives they relied on spiritual friends and accumulated merit through moral discipline, listening, and the like, and because in this life too they inquire into the noble truths in order to pacify the afflictions, such as attachment.

As for "the Tathāgata": he is the Tathāgata because he has understood the four noble truths just as they are. Or else, he is the Tathāgata because, just as the former Tathāgatas abandoned all afflictions and went to the city of nirvāṇa, so too the other Tathāgatas have abandoned all afflictions and gone to the city of nirvāṇa.

As for "the great physician of sentient beings": those who have afflictions such as attachment and aversion, together with mind and mental factors, are called "sentient beings." He is the "great physician" because, in order to pacify the disease of the afflictions, such as attachment, aversion, and delusion, in those sentient beings, he teaches the medicines of the contemplation of impurity, of loving-kindness, of dependent origination, and of the thirty-seven factors of awakening. If one asks why he gives sentient beings the medicine for the afflictions, it is for that reason that "great compassion" was said; it is to be construed as "Because the Tathāgatas possess great compassion, therefore they give the medicine for the afflictions."

What difference is there between compassion and great compassion? Reply: Great compassion is superior to compassion in seven respects. The seven are intrinsic nature, aspect, object, stage, continuum, attainment of freedom from attachment, and protecting and not protecting. Of these, intrinsic nature: because it has the intrinsic nature of non-aversion and non-delusion toward all sentient beings. Aspect: because it has the aspect of tender concern for one, two, or three sufferings, and for countless hundreds of thousands of sufferings. Object: because it takes as its object the sentient beings of one, two, or three realms, and so on up to all the world systems. Stage: because that great compassion belongs to the stage of the fourth meditative concentration. Continuum: because compassion resides in the continuum of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, whereas great compassion resides in the continuum of the Tathāgata. Freedom from attachment: because the compassion of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas goes no further than freedom from the attachment of the desire realm, whereas the great compassion of the Tathāgatas is free from all the attachment of the three realms. Protecting and not protecting: because the compassion of śrāvakas protects only a few sentient beings and does not protect all sentient beings without exception, whereas the great compassion of the Tathāgatas protects all sentient beings without exception.

As for "they ask about the Dharma that removes the fever of suffering": the eight sufferings themselves harm body and mind and so are like a fever; therefore they are "the fever of suffering." In order to abandon it, they ask about the Dharma, such as the four noble truths. "Having shown them the basis, the origin, as it has been taught" means that to those virtuous persons who ask about the Dharma because they wish to abandon the fever of suffering, he showed, specifying precisely, what exactly those karma and afflictions are which other sūtras teach to be the truth of origin, the cause that produces suffering, characterized as karma and afflictions.

If one asks what its medicine is, it is for that reason that the passage beginning "the path, the noble eightfold path" was spoken. The eightfold noble path itself is the medicine that abandons karma and afflictions. Because that path abandons the afflictions, it "brings peace." Because it abandons the mass of suffering, it is "nirvāṇa." "Supreme peace is found": because one attains what has abandoned all karma and afflictions, what is free from all the sufferings of birth, aging, and the rest, and what is supreme above all the happiness of the world, it is called "finding supreme peace."

In order to conclude the four noble truths explained above, the words "Monks, these are called the four noble truths" were spoken. In order to show what those are which were mentioned only by their number in "the noble eightfold path," the words "Now, what is the noble eightfold path?" were spoken.

If one asks why the noble path is explained as having only eight branches, and not nine, seven, or some other number, the reply is: because they make up three aggregates, the noble path has just eight branches, neither more nor fewer. The three aggregates are the aggregate of moral discipline, the aggregate of meditative absorption, and the aggregate of wisdom. Among these, right speech, right action, and right livelihood, since their intrinsic nature is moral discipline, are included in the aggregate of moral discipline. Right mindfulness and right meditative absorption are included in the aggregate of meditative absorption. Although mindfulness is not, strictly speaking, meditative absorption in its essence, the mind of those whose mindfulness is distracted does not rest one-pointedly, and so mindfulness is conducive to meditative absorption; for that reason it belongs to the aggregate of meditative absorption.

Right view, right thought, and right effort, these three, are included in the aggregate of wisdom. Right thought and right effort are by intrinsic nature conceptual thought and diligence, and so, strictly speaking, they are not of the intrinsic nature of wisdom. Nevertheless, since wisdom arises from the causes of unmistaken understanding and of the diligence that accomplishes virtue, they serve as its helpers, and so they belong to the aggregate of wisdom.

Alternatively, because these eight branches of the noble path produce eight qualities, the branches of the path are just eight, neither more nor fewer. The eight qualities are that the mind becomes completely pure, bright, unblemished, free from afflictions, pliant, workable, and steady, and that it attains the unmoving. Among these, freedom from attachment to gains such as robes is the mind's being completely pure. Freedom from attachment to honors such as being bowed to is its being bright. Having neither attachment to one's dwelling place nor flattery is its being unblemished. Having abandoned craving is its being free from afflictions. Having abandoned views is its having become pliant. Having abandoned doubt is its being workable. Not deteriorating afterward is its being steady. The four meditative concentrations are the attainment of the unmoving.

Here, through possessing right view, the mind becomes completely pure. Through possessing right thought it becomes bright, because one does not think about honors and the like. Through possessing right speech it becomes unblemished, because it is free from flattery and the like. Through possessing right action it becomes free from afflictions, because one strives to accomplish virtue and to abandon the afflictions. Through possessing right livelihood it becomes pliant, because one abides in right view and abandons mistaken livelihood. Right effort makes it workable, because, having abandoned conceit, it makes the mind unblemished. Through possessing right mindfulness one makes it steady, because, having abandoned doubt, one settles it without moving. Through possessing right meditative absorption one brings about the attainment of the unmoving, because by the power of meditative absorption one directly attains the levels of the four meditative concentrations of the form realm.

Why, then, are these eight branches of the noble path set out in this order? Reply: First one sees things correctly as impermanent and so on. After that one reflects correctly, "That is like this and like this." Having reflected correctly, one tells others too, "The phenomena are like this," just as one has examined them. Having thus correctly put it into words, one carries out just what one has said. Those who carry out right action have no wrong livelihood, so after that comes right livelihood. In those who live rightly there occurs only the activity of abandoning non-virtue and accomplishing virtue, so next is right effort. In those who abide in right effort, mindfulness carried off by the afflictions does not occur, so next is right mindfulness. When one does not come under the power of the afflictions and one's mindfulness does not lapse, the mind always remains one-pointed, so at the end comes right meditative absorption. That is why they are set out in this order.

In the system of the Great Vehicle, the noble eightfold path is explained as the path of meditation, whereas in the view of the śrāvaka Vaibhāṣikas these eight branches of the noble path are held to be the very nature of the path of seeing. On that view, because these eight branches of the noble path are the very nature of the path of seeing, they are called "the wheel of Dharma." If one asks why, they are called "the wheel of Dharma" because they are comparable to the precious wheel. If one asks how they are comparable to the precious wheel: in that it moves swiftly, lets go and enters, conquers the unconquered, establishes itself in what it has conquered, and rises up and comes down.

For example, just as the precious wheel of a wheel-turning monarch moves swiftly, so too, because the four truths are realized in fifteen moments of mind on the path of seeing, it moves swiftly. The fifteen moments are the forbearance of dharma-knowledge regarding suffering, the wisdom of dharma-knowledge regarding suffering, the forbearance of subsequent knowledge regarding suffering, the wisdom of subsequent knowledge regarding suffering; the forbearance of dharma-knowledge regarding the origin, the wisdom of dharma-knowledge regarding the origin, the forbearance of subsequent knowledge regarding the origin, the wisdom of subsequent knowledge regarding the origin; the forbearance of dharma-knowledge regarding cessation, the wisdom of dharma-knowledge regarding cessation, the forbearance of subsequent knowledge regarding cessation, the wisdom of subsequent knowledge regarding cessation; the forbearance of dharma-knowledge regarding the path, the wisdom of dharma-knowledge regarding the path, and the forbearance of subsequent knowledge regarding the path. Thus there are fifteen. The sixteenth moment, the wisdom of subsequent knowledge regarding the path, belongs to the path of meditation, and so it is not counted as part of the path of seeing.

Here, seeing the truth of suffering of the desire realm as impermanent, suffering, empty, and selfless is called the forbearance of dharma-knowledge regarding suffering. When one sees in this way, one abandons the ten kinds of afflictions of the desire realm that are to be abandoned by seeing suffering: the view of the perishable collection, the view that holds to extremes, wrong view, conceit in views, conceit in holding moral discipline and ascetic vows to be supreme, doubt, attachment, anger, pride, and ignorance. After that, knowing that the afflictions have been abandoned, and making it so that afflictions do not arise later in the continuum, is called the wisdom of dharma-knowledge regarding suffering.

It is like two men, one of whom seizes a thief and puts him out of the house while the other shuts the door. That is, just as the one seizes the thief and goes out through the door together with him, so the forbearance of dharma-knowledge regarding suffering seizes the ten afflictions and ceases together with the afflictions. Then, just as the two men afterward shut the door so that the thief does not get in again, the wisdom of dharma-knowledge regarding suffering makes it so that afflictions do not arise later in the mind's continuum.
