Here, the forbearance is called the uninterrupted path, and the wisdom is called the path of liberation. This example, the way two men drive out a thief, is to be applied as well to the forbearance of subsequent knowledge regarding suffering and the rest.

Here, the setting out of the four aspects, impermanence and the rest, is also the remedy for four kinds of mistaken yogins. The four are two kinds given to attachment and two kinds given to views. The two kinds given to attachment are those overcome by the pride that says "I am," and those attached to laziness. The two kinds given to views are those strongly attached to grasping at a self and to grasping at what is one's own. Of these, those overcome by the pride that says "I am" behave with the conceit that what is impermanent is permanent. As the remedy for that, the truth of suffering is explained as impermanent, because the five aggregates are momentary by intrinsic nature. In order to abandon the holding of that as true and the attachment to it, and because virtue is not practiced, the truth of suffering is explained, as the remedy, as suffering, because all formations possess the three kinds of suffering. As the remedy for grasping at a self, it is explained as selfless, because the self imputed by the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) is not found in the five aggregates. As the remedy for grasping at what is one's own, it is explained as emptiness, because if a self is not established, what is one's own is not established either.

Seeing the truth of suffering of the form and formless realms in the four aspects, impermanence and the rest, is the forbearance of subsequent knowledge regarding suffering. At that time too, of the afflictions of the form and formless realms to be abandoned by seeing suffering, one abandons in the form realm the nine beginning with the view of the perishable collection, excluding anger, and in the formless realm the nine beginning with the view of the perishable collection, excluding anger; altogether, eighteen afflictions are abandoned. There is no aversion in the two realms because the mental continuum is thoroughly moistened by meditative absorption, and because there is no harm to body or mind as the result of non-virtuous karma. After that, knowing that the afflictions have been abandoned, and making it so that afflictions do not arise later in the mind's continuum, is the wisdom of subsequent knowledge regarding suffering.

Then, when one sees the truth of the origin of the desire realm in four aspects, as cause, origin, production, and condition, that is called the forbearance of dharma-knowledge regarding the origin. At that time, of the afflictions of the desire realm to be abandoned by seeing the truth of the origin, one sets aside three, the view of the perishable collection, the view that holds to extremes, and conceit in moral discipline and ascetic vows, and abandons seven: wrong view, conceit in views, doubt, attachment, anger, pride, and ignorance. Then, knowing that the afflictions have been abandoned, and making it so that afflictions do not arise later in the mind's continuum, is the wisdom of dharma-knowledge regarding the origin.

When one sees the truth of the origin of the form and formless realms in the four aspects, cause and the rest, that is called the forbearance of subsequent knowledge regarding the origin. At that time one abandons twelve afflictions: the six of the form realm to be abandoned by seeing the truth of the origin, namely wrong view, conceit in views, doubt, attachment, pride, and ignorance, and likewise six in the formless realm. After that, knowing that the afflictions have been abandoned, and making it so that afflictions do not arise later in the mind's continuum, is called the wisdom of subsequent knowledge regarding the origin.

Because it performs the function of a seed, it is "cause." Because it makes future craving increase, it is "origin." Because it makes the stream of existence arise without break and without interruption, it is "production." Because it accomplishes the result, just as a pot is produced by water, string, the wheel, and clay, it is "condition."

When one sees the truth of cessation of the desire realm as cessation, peace, excellence, and emergence, that is called the forbearance of dharma-knowledge regarding cessation. At that time too one abandons seven afflictions of the desire realm to be abandoned by seeing the truth of cessation: wrong view, conceit in views, doubt, attachment, anger, pride, and ignorance. After that, knowing that the afflictions have been abandoned, and making it so that afflictions do not arise later in the mind's continuum, is the wisdom of dharma-knowledge regarding cessation.

When one sees the truth of cessation of the form and formless realms in the four aspects, cessation and the rest, that is called the forbearance of subsequent knowledge regarding cessation. At that time too one abandons twelve afflictions to be abandoned by seeing the cessation of the two realms: in the form realm six, namely wrong view, conceit in views, doubt, attachment, pride, and ignorance, and in the formless realm six as well. After that, knowing that the afflictions have been abandoned, and making it so that afflictions do not arise later in the mind's continuum, is called the wisdom of subsequent knowledge regarding cessation.

Here, because it is free from the afflictions that are the cause producing the aggregates, it is "cessation." Because it is free from the eight kinds of suffering, it is "peace." Because one has reached a state of virtue and happiness, it is "excellence." Because it is permanent and without equal, it is "emergence."

When one sees the truth of the path of the desire realm in four aspects, as path, reasoning, accomplishment, and deliverance, that is called the forbearance of dharma-knowledge regarding the path. At that time too one abandons the eight afflictions of the desire realm to be abandoned by seeing the path: wrong view, conceit in views, conceit in moral discipline and ascetic vows, doubt, attachment, anger, pride, and ignorance. Then, knowing that the afflictions have been abandoned, and making it so that afflictions do not arise later in the mind's continuum, is called the wisdom of dharma-knowledge regarding the path.

When one sees the truth of the path of the form and formless realms in the four aspects, path and the rest, that is called the forbearance of subsequent knowledge regarding the path. At that time one abandons fourteen afflictions: the seven afflictions of the form realm to be abandoned by seeing the truth of the path, namely wrong view, conceit in views, conceit in moral discipline and ascetic vows, doubt, attachment, pride, and ignorance, and likewise seven in the formless realm. After that, knowing that the afflictions have been abandoned, and making it so that afflictions do not arise later in the mind's continuum, is called the wisdom of subsequent knowledge regarding the path, and it is classed as the path of meditation.

Because it seeks the four noble truths, it is "path." Because it serves as the remedy for the afflictions, it is "reasoning." Because it does away with the distortions, such as holding what is impermanent to be permanent, it is "accomplishment." Because it brings release from the suffering of the three realms, it is "deliverance."

Then, through the path of meditation of the desire realm one abandons four afflictions: attachment, anger, pride, and ignorance. Through the path of meditation of the form realm one abandons three, attachment, pride, and ignorance, and through the path of meditation of the formless realm three as well: six in all.

For example, the precious wheel leaves one land and enters and goes into another. In the same way, on the path of seeing too, one lets go of the forbearance of dharma-knowledge regarding suffering, which is the immediate path, and actualizes and takes up the wisdom of dharma-knowledge regarding suffering, which is the path of liberation. Likewise, letting go and taking up is to be applied also to the forbearance and the wisdom of subsequent knowledge of the form and formless realms. Alternatively, having seen the truth of suffering of the desire realm, one leaves it behind and actualizes the truth of suffering of the form and formless realms, and so it moves on.

For example, the precious wheel conquers the unconquered villages and towns of a region, and establishes itself in those it has conquered. In the same way, the path of seeing, through the immediate path, conquers those not yet conquered among the afflictions such as the view of the perishable collection, because it completely cuts off the acquisition of the afflictions. And it establishes itself in what has been conquered, because through the path of liberation it brings about the characteristic of separation from the afflictions and makes one abide in the state of nirvāṇa.

For example, the precious wheel rises up in some places and in some places comes down again. In the same way, when one focuses again and again on the immediate path: when one focuses on the immediate path of the desire realm, it has come down, and when one abides in the path of liberation, it rises up. Likewise, if one says that it comes down because one actualizes the immediate path of the form and formless realms, then when one abides in the path of liberation it rises up. The same is to be applied to the truth of the origin and the rest. Alternatively, when one focuses on the truth of suffering of the desire realm, it comes down, and when one focuses on the truth of suffering of the form and formless realms, it rises up. The same is to be applied, as appropriate, to the remaining truths.

An objection: Why is the path of seeing called the wheel of Dharma? How is it evident that it is a wheel? That too is evident from what is said in scripture. The scripture says: The noble Ājñāta Kauṇḍinya saw the truths. Then the Blessed One said, "Kauṇḍinya, have you seen the truths?" He replied, "Blessed One, I have seen them." "Kauṇḍinya, have you understood the truths?" He replied, "Blessed One, I have understood them." Therefore, because he had fully understood the truths, he became known as Ājñāta Kauṇḍinya (Kauṇḍinya Who Understood). Because it is said, "When the noble Kauṇḍinya saw the truths, the wheel of Dharma was turned," the path of seeing is called the wheel of Dharma.

Moreover, the Venerable Ājñāta Kauṇḍinya also explains it in this way: these branches of the noble path, which are of the nature of the path of seeing, are called the wheel of Dharma because they resemble the simile of the hub, the spokes, and the rim. Among them, right speech, right action, and right livelihood, these three, being the aggregate of moral discipline, are like the hub: just as the hub of a wheel holds the spokes, so the other qualities too, such as right view, rely on moral discipline, and moral discipline holds them. Right meditative absorption is like the rim: just as the rim of a wheel gathers the spokes so that they do not come apart, so right meditative absorption gathers in all the mind's spreading out. Right view, thought, mindfulness, and effort, these four, are by intrinsic nature spreading out, and so they are like the spokes. Thus, since it is said that the wheel of Dharma was turned because the noble Kauṇḍinya generated the path of seeing, it is evident that the path of seeing is called the wheel of Dharma.

Here, right speech, right action, and right livelihood, these three, belonging to the aggregate of moral discipline, cut the non-virtuous root of aversion. Right meditative absorption, right thought, and right effort, these three, belonging to the aggregate of meditative absorption, cut the non-virtuous root of attachment. Mindfulness endowed with right view, belonging to the aggregate of wisdom, cuts the non-virtuous root of delusion. This too is how some masters have explained it.

Since the meaning of the eight branches of the noble path is explained in detail below in the sūtra itself, I shall give only a summary.

In "right view," "right" means unmistaken, and "view" means seeing: seeing inner and outer things without error as impermanent and so on is right view. Alternatively, "right" refers to reality, and "view" refers to examining: examining the reality of the four truths is right view. Alternatively, "right" refers to what is auspicious, or praised, or virtuous, and "view" refers to illuminating: illuminating what is auspicious or virtuous is right view.

In "right thought," "right" refers to the arising of what had not arisen before, and "thought" refers to apprehension: "This is virtuous; this is non-virtuous." The arising of such apprehension, which had not arisen before, is right thought.

Alternatively, "right" refers to making even or concordant, and "thought" refers to thinking that follows upon it: thinking afterward in accord with what right view has seen, just as it has seen it, is right thought.

As for "right speech": having first properly undertaken the vow of moral discipline and the like, not to break one's pledge, true to one's promise not to do evil and unwholesome things, is right speech. Right speech is of two kinds, the unmanifest (avijñapti) and the manifest (vijñapti). Of these, the unmanifest concerns speech such as lies, harsh words and the like. The manifest is what is called speaking at the proper time, speaking correctly, and speaking the truth.

As for "right action": it is bodily action that is unimpaired, that is, looking closely at taking life, taking what is not given and the like, and not engaging in them.

As for "right livelihood": living in accordance with the Dharma is right livelihood. It is of two kinds, the pure livelihood of householders and the pure livelihood of those who have gone forth. The pure livelihood of householders is turning away from fire, weapons, poison, liquor and the like. The pure livelihood of those who have gone forth is keeping one's requisites few, being content and having few desires, and turning away from hypocrisy and the like.

As for "right effort": the diligence of body and mind that does not produce evil, unwholesome factors that have not arisen, abandons those that have arisen, produces wholesome factors that have not arisen, and increases those that have arisen is right effort.

As for "right mindfulness": keeping the mind from forgetting the things one has previously heard, reflected upon and experienced is right mindfulness. This mindfulness arises from three causes. The three causes are habituation, seeing a sign, and clarity of mind. Of these, habituation: when one has looked again and again at things such as forms, and so on, then later, even though those things such as forms are absent, mindfulness of them arises in the mind, just as, for example, one sees in a dream what one has grown used to during the day. Seeing a sign: mindfulness arises from seeing phenomena that are similar or dissimilar; for example, on seeing someone who resembles one's son, one remembers one's son, and when one suffers from a cold wind, one remembers fire. Clarity of mind: just as the reflection of a face appears in clear water, so when the mind is clear and the share of obscuration is small, one remembers whatever one has done before.

As for "right samādhi": "right" refers to straight, and "samādhi" refers to becoming, or to holding. For example, just as a snake becomes straight when it is put inside the hollow of a bamboo, so turning the mind back from dullness, agitation and the like and holding it straight is right samādhi. Alternatively, "right" refers to evenness, and "samādhi" refers to placing: placing the mind evenly is samādhi, right samādhi.

Now, in order to explain in detail the limbs of the path that had been taught in brief, the passage beginning "Monks, what is right view?" was declared. If one asks why it was declared in detail after having been explained in brief: those to be disciplined are of two kinds, those who understand from a mere mention and those who understand through elaboration. For the sake of those who understand from a mere mention, it was taught in brief, in the passage beginning "namely, right view." Then, since those who understand through elaboration cannot realize the meaning unless it is explained at length, the Tathāgata, who keeps no teacher's closed fist with regard to the Dharma and who possesses great compassion, declared in detail the passage beginning "Therein, monks, what is right view?"

Here, right view is of three kinds: the right view of those in training, the right view of those beyond training, and worldly right view. Of these, the view that persons in training and persons beyond training have while in meditative attainment is called the right view of those in training and of those beyond training. The view one has after rising from samādhi is accompanied by conceptual thought, and so it is called worldly right view. Alternatively, for virtuous persons to see that other lives exist and not to deny karma and the results of karma is worldly right view. It should be understood that here it is worldly right view that is being taught.

In this regard, the view that six factors exist is right view: the view that there exists a basis for transmigration; the view that the seed and the ground, which are its conditions, exist; the view that the good qualities of giving exist; the view that the antidote of giving, moral discipline and meditation and its opposing side exist; the view that the results of the opposing side and of the antidote of those three exist; and the view that there is a complete turning back from transmigration. These are the six.

Of these, the words "This world exists; the world beyond exists" show the view that the basis of transmigration exists. The basis for transmigrating from one life to another is this world and the world beyond. To hold that in this life, too, there are the five aggregates together with karma and afflictions, and to hold that, with the karma and afflictions of this life as the cause, one also takes up aggregates in another lifetime, is called the view that this world exists and the world beyond exists.

The words "There is a mother, there is a father" show that its conditions, the seed and the ground, exist. The one who plants the seed for the birth of the five aggregates is the father; the one who serves as the support of the seed is the mother. The view that both of these exist is called "there is a mother, there is a father."

The words "There is giving, there is fire offering" show the view that giving and offering to the gods exist. The view that when one gives material goods there is a sentient being who receives them, and the view that when one makes a fire offering the god who receives fire offerings receives it there, are called the view that there is giving and there is fire offering.

The words "desired and undesired" show the view that the opposing side of giving, moral discipline and meditation exists. The wholesome side, which has the characteristic of giving, moral discipline and meditation and serves as the antidote, is called "desired." Its opposing side, stinginess, bad conduct and distraction of mind, which belongs to the unwholesome side, is called "undesired." Alternatively, wholesome karma characterized as meritorious and unmoving is "desired."

Unwholesome karma, which has the characteristic of non-merit, is "undesired." The view that these exist is called the view that the desired and the undesired exist.

The words "There is the result and ripening of actions well done and badly done" show the view that the results of the opposing side and of the antidote of those three exist. The view that if one practices giving one will come to great wealth, that if one guards moral discipline one will be born in the higher realms, and that if one cultivates the immeasurables one will attain nirvāṇa; that if one is stingy one will become poor, that if one breaks moral discipline one will be born in the lower realms, and that if one does not cultivate the immeasurables one will not be liberated; or else the view that if one performs meritorious and unmoving karma one is born in the happy destinies, and that if one performs non-meritorious karma one is born in the three lower realms: this is called the view that the result and ripening of actions well done and badly done exist.

The words "In the world there are those who have gone rightly and those who have entered rightly" show the view that there is a complete turning back from transmigration. Transmigration is birth in saṃsāra. Those who have turned back from birth in saṃsāra are the noble ones, stream-enterers and the rest, and the Tathāgatas; the view that they exist is called the view that in the world there are those who have gone rightly and those who have entered rightly. Here, the noble ones such as stream-enterers are those who have gone rightly.

As for "The opposite of this is called wrong view": the opposite of right view is wrong view, which views things as "This world does not exist; the world beyond does not exist," and so on.

In order to explain right thought, it was taught: "What is right thought? Dedicating giving and moral discipline to becoming a buddha and the like, and not dedicating them to becoming a wheel-turning monarch and the like: this is right thought." In terms of meaning, the mind's wholesome thinking, as the antidote to unwholesome thinking and the like, is right thought.

Here, giving is the blameless giving away of things in order to obtain the ornament of the mind, the equipment of the mind, the provisions for yoga, and the highest aim. It is to be viewed in six ways: who gives, where one gives, what one gives, by what means one gives, how one gives, and why one gives.

Who gives? The giver and the patron of the gift give. One who gives things away with one's own hand is the giver; one who has others give is the patron of the gift.

To whom does one give? One gives to four kinds of field: the field of suffering, the field of benefactors, the field of dear ones, and the field of the exceptional. Of these, the field of suffering is travelers, the destitute, beggars, the blind, the deaf, those without a protector, those without a refuge, whoever is tormented by illness, and the like. The field of benefactors is one's father or mother, or else others who have rescued one from the suffering of harm, or who have helped one out of famine, the threat of a foreign king, or being struck by the sickness of bondage, or who have helped one in the work one had to do, or who have brought one past the grief of separation from wealth, relatives, friends and the like, or who have given instructions that led to benefit and happiness. The field of dear ones is whoever are close companions, friends familiar with one another and of one mind, or else others such as kinsfolk, relatives and friends. The field of the exceptional is any brahmin or monk of virtuous nature who has set out to abandon attachment, aversion and delusion.

What does one give? In essence, one gives two kinds of things: things counted as sentient beings and things not counted as sentient beings. Things counted as sentient beings are again of two kinds, external and internal. Of these, external things means giving sons, wives, male and female servants, horses, elephants, goats, sheep and the like. Internal things means giving the feet, hands, head, flesh, blood and so on of one's own body. Things not counted as sentient beings are also of three kinds: things of grain, things of treasure, and things of land. Of these, things of grain are, namely, barley, wheat, rice, sesame and the like. Things of treasure are, namely, gold, silver, pearls, beryl and the like. Things of land are, namely, fields, houses, shops, buildings built for the sake of merit, and the like.

By what means does one give? One gives by means of bodily action and verbal action produced by a cause that is endowed with non-attachment and non-aversion.

How does one give? With a mind of faith, seeing that there is a result in the next life, and with a mind of respect and reverence, one gives respectfully, with one's own hand, at the proper time.

Why does one give? To the field of suffering one gives out of compassion. To the field of benefactors one gives with a grateful mind. To the field of dear ones one gives out of affection. To the field of the exceptional one gives because a sublime result comes from them, who are far nobler than all the world. This is what is called giving.

As for "moral discipline": moral discipline here refers to moral discipline endowed with six limbs. What is moral discipline endowed with six limbs? It is what was taught in the words: "One is endowed with moral discipline, restrained by the vow of individual liberation (prātimokṣa), perfect in conduct and in domain of activity, seeing danger in even the tiniest fault, and one trains in the points one has properly undertaken." Being endowed with moral discipline in this way means not destroying through bodily action, and not destroying through verbal action, the points of training as one has properly undertaken them. How is one restrained by the prātimokṣa vow? The moral discipline of the seven classes is the prātimokṣa vow, and this means being endowed with it. The seven classes are monks, nuns, laymen, laywomen, male novices, female novices, and probationary nuns.

Here, being perfect in conduct means that one's four modes of deportment, one's tasks, and one's engagement in virtuous activities all follow the ways of the world and accord with the world, and follow the Vinaya and accord with the Vinaya. In short, in all one's tasks one acts in a way to which worldly people take no offense and which does not contradict the Vinaya.

How is one perfect in one's domain of activity? One does not enter the five domains that one is not permitted to enter. The five domains are the dwelling of a butcher, the dwelling of a prostitute, the dwelling of a woman who sells liquor, the palace of a king, and the dwelling of a caṇḍāla.

How does one see danger in tiny faults? Tiny faults are those minor points of training which, even if broken, are easy to restore. Yet just as even a small amount of poison can kill a great man, so if these tiny faults occur, one will have no fortune to attain the fruit of an arhat and will fall into the lower realms. Thinking this, one is careful about them and fears them.

How does one train in the points one has properly undertaken? First, when one properly takes the prātimokṣa vow, at the time of full ordination by the formal act consisting of a motion and three proclamations, one hears some of the body of the points of training and is instructed, "Learn the rest from the recitation of the Prātimokṣa Sūtra every half month." Later one listens in detail, from one's companions in the pure life, to how many bases of moral discipline there are, keeps those one has heard unbroken, and restores in accordance with the Dharma those that have been broken.

In short, three characteristics of moral discipline are taught. The three characteristics are the characteristic of being undamaged, the characteristic of its intrinsic nature, and the characteristic of its good qualities in relation to others and to oneself. Here, the words "endowed with moral discipline" teach the characteristic of being undamaged. The words "restrained by the prātimokṣa vow" teach the characteristic of its intrinsic nature. The words "perfect in conduct and in domain of activity" teach the characteristic of its good qualities in relation to others: for if one's deportment is graceful and one does not go to places where one should not go, then others whose minds are without faith will gain faith, those who have faith will grow greatly in faith, and ill report will not be spread in every direction. The two phrases "seeing danger in even an atom's worth of fault" and "training in the points one has properly undertaken" teach the good qualities of moral discipline in relation to oneself: for if even the subtle points of training are not broken, one becomes fortunate enough to attain and to actualize the fruit of an arhat that one has not yet attained and not yet actualized.

"Dedicating it to becoming a buddha" means dedicating thus: "Through the cause of the virtue I have done by way of generosity and moral discipline, may I become a buddha," or, as the word "and so on" indicates, "may I become an arhat," or "a stream-enterer." Not dedicating thus: "May I become a universal monarch," or, as the word "and so on" indicates, "may I become Śakra (Indra) or Brahmā," this is right thought.

A buddha is one who, having abandoned the obscurations of the afflictions, has awoken from the sleep of ignorance just as a person awakens from sleep, to whom phenomena such as the aggregates and the elements have become manifest, and who has become like a lotus that has opened.

As for "universal monarch": one who holds a kingdom and exercises power over beings through the might of the wheel jewel is called a universal monarch. There are four kinds of universal monarch: one with a golden wheel, one with a silver wheel, one with a copper wheel, and one with an iron wheel. Among them, the one with the golden wheel is the highest, the one with the silver wheel is next to the highest, the one with the copper wheel is middling, and the one with the iron wheel is the lowest. Accordingly, in order, the wheel of the one with the golden wheel holds sway over four continents, the one with the silver wheel over three continents, the one with the copper wheel over two continents, and the one with the iron wheel over one continent.

The manner in which they hold sway is as follows. To those who have a golden wheel, the minor kings of the cities come in person to welcome them, come before them, and say: "O lord, these lands are rich and prosperous and flourishing with many people. We too are yours, O lord, so may the lord please command us." Those with a silver wheel set out themselves on the march, and thereupon the minor kings submit. Those with a copper wheel go before the minor kings of the cities, and they submit through the persuasion of words. With the one with the iron wheel, he goes before them, the two sides brandish weapons at one another, and after that they submit.

In order to teach right speech, the passage beginning "What is right speech?" was spoken. As for "lying": it is to hold the perception of the matter to be spoken one way and to speak of it another way; it is a lie once the person addressed has understood the meaning. Therefore the Treasury of the Abhidharma says:

Words spoken with the perception altered,
If spoken to one who grasps the meaning, are a lie.

As for "harsh speech": if, with a mind of aversion, in order to displease another, one speaks words that mention faults of caste, clan, and so on, and the other understands, it is harsh speech. As for "slander": if, with a mind afflicted by attachment and aversion, in order to divide others, one carries words back and forth and the meaning is understood, it is slander. Therefore the Treasury of the Abhidharma also says, "Slander is the words of an afflicted mind, spoken in order to divide." As for "idle chatter": if, with a mind accompanied by afflictions such as attachment, one engages in flattery, songs, and the recounting of non-Buddhist treatises, and another understands the meaning, it is idle chatter. Therefore the Treasury of the Abhidharma also says:

Like flattery, songs, and the words of songs,
And like bad treatises, it is to be given up.

In short, to abandon the four faults of speech and to teach the sublime Dharma, gentle and meaningful, is called right speech. Their characteristics are explained in detail in the section on right action.

In order to teach right action, the passage beginning "What is right action? By body, speech, and mind" was spoken. In short, to abandon the ten nonvirtues of body, speech, and mind, and to accomplish the ten virtuous paths of action with body, speech, and mind, is called right action.

In order to teach what the ten nonvirtues and the ten virtues are, the passage beginning "Of these, there are three kinds of the body" was spoken. Killing, taking what is not given, and sexual misconduct, as actions of the body, are called the three nonvirtues of the body, and abandoning these three is called the three virtues of the body. As for "four of speech": lying, harsh speech, slander, and idle chatter are the four nonvirtues of speech, and abandoning these four is the four virtues of speech. As for "three kinds of the mind": covetousness, malice, and wrong view are the three nonvirtues of the mind, and abandoning these three is the three virtues of the mind. The three nonvirtues of the body, the four nonvirtues of speech, and the three nonvirtues of the mind, taken together, are the ten nonvirtues. The three virtues of the body, the four virtues of speech, and the three virtues of the mind, taken together, are the ten virtues.

These ten paths of action are set out in eight ways: by the completeness of their components, by their divisions, by their causes, by their bases, by their doors, by the greater and lesser paths of action, by their results, and by their synonyms.

Here, with reference to the completeness of the components, the passage beginning "What is killing?" was spoken. When five components are present, the taking of life that is killing becomes complete; if any of them is lacking, it is not the taking of life.

To teach the first component, the words "it is a living being" are stated: what is to be killed is in truth a sentient being, a living creature, and not a mound, a tree stump, or the like.

To teach the second component, the words "and one perceives it as a living being" are stated: it is the taking of life if one knows that what one intends to kill is a living being. If one strikes a living being with the perception that it is a log or the like, it is not the taking of life. In short, this teaches the following: the meaning is that one kills the very one one intended to kill, and does not kill another by mistake.

To teach the third component, the words "and the intention to kill has arisen" are stated. The meaning is this: if one kills after the intention to kill has arisen through the thought "I will kill," it is killing; but if one kills without any thought of killing, with a mind free of affliction, it is not the taking of life.

To teach the fourth component, the words "and one does harm" are stated. The meaning is this: even if the intention to kill is present, if one does not strike with a weapon or the like, it is not the taking of life; but one who intends to kill also strikes with a weapon or the like.

To teach the fifth component, the words "and it dies" were spoken: when the continuum of the life faculty of what was to be killed is cut and it dies, the five components are complete, and so the taking of life is complete.

As for "taking life": the faculty of vitality is life, and to harm or stop its continuum is the taking of life. Even at the very least, if, when the lifespan is entirely exhausted and has ceased, one creates an obstacle to the arising of a single moment of the faculty of vitality, one incurs the fault of taking life; what need is there to speak of anything else?

As for "except when one has been careful and when one has not seen": the meaning is that when a death comes about while one is being careful, when one thinks "I will strike a tree trunk" and hits a person who then dies, and when one does not see that a person is there and a death comes about by one's throwing a stone or the like, these are not the taking of life.

In order to teach taking what is not given, the passage beginning "Here, what is taking what is not given?" was spoken. To take the property of another by force, by deceit, or by stealth is taking what is not given. When it has five components, taking what is not given becomes complete; it becomes theft.

To teach the first component, the words "with the intention to steal" were spoken: it is not taking something with a virtuous mind, thinking, "If the goods are left in this place they will be lost, so I will put them elsewhere," but thinking, out of attachment to another's property, "I will steal it."

To teach the second component, the words "of another" are stated: the meaning is that it is not one's own property but the property of another sentient being.

To teach the third component, the word "property" is stated: goods such as grain and treasure that another sentient being has made his own.

The fourth component taught is the application: from setting out from one's own place, entering the road, and breaking into the house, up to touching the goods with one's hand.

To teach the fifth component, the words "making it one's own" were spoken: when one has moved another's goods from their place and made them one's own property alone, the components are complete and one has taken what was not given.

That being so, if one steals the ornaments of the Blessed One, since there is no owner, from whom has one stolen? It is said: one has stolen from the Buddha, for at the time of his nirvāṇa the Blessed One took possession of and accepted all of them. If one steals the ownerless property of a deceased monk, from whom has one stolen? It is said: if the formal act of motion and proclamation has been performed, one has stolen from the children of the Buddha who belong within that boundary; if the act has not been performed, one has stolen from all the children of the Buddha. If one steals from a stupa, from whom has one stolen? That too is stealing from the Tathāgata himself. If one takes an ownerless treasure, from whom has one stolen? From the lord of the land, that is, from the king.

As for the passage beginning "mother and father": if one takes, out of trusting familiarity, property of one's father, mother, and the like, taking property whose taking will not displease them, it is not taking what is not given. But if one snatches from them an upper or lower garment or the like while they are wearing it on their body, or food while they are eating it, this will displease them, and so it is taking what is not given.

In order to teach sexual misconduct, the passage beginning "What is sexual misconduct?" was spoken; it is to be connected with the words "consorting with another's woman." In short, to engage, by force or by stealth, with women whom another has made his own is called sexual misconduct, and this is set out in five kinds.

What are the five? Going to one who is not to be gone to, going by what is not the proper part, going against one's kin, going in what is not the proper place, and going at what is not the proper time.

Going to one who is not to be gone to is also of five kinds: a woman protected by a husband, one protected by the king, one protected by her parents, one protected by the Dharma, and one protected by the community. Here, one protected by a husband is a woman whom someone has taken as his bride and made his wife. One protected by the king is a woman whose husband has died, a widow, or one without a protector, whom the king's decree has placed under law so that she is not permitted to be enjoyed. One protected by her parents is a girl who has not been given to another, and who remains in her parents' keeping so that she is not allowed to be with others. One protected by the Dharma is a nun, or anyone who has taken vows. One protected by the community is a woman whom many people have placed under a rule, not permitting her to be enjoyed with desire.

Here, if one engages with a woman protected by a husband, it is sexual misconduct against the husband. If one engages with a woman protected by the king, one has robbed the king and engaged in impure conduct, for he will not tolerate it. If the king himself engages with her, against whom does he commit impure conduct? Just as the king himself incurs the fault of impure conduct if he engages with his queen when she has taken the vow of observance, so this case too should be seen as similar. If one engages in impure conduct with a nun, one protected by the Dharma, against whom is the impure conduct committed? It is, in another sense, a theft from the king and impure conduct, for he will not tolerate it. If the king himself engages with her, against whom does he commit impure conduct? Just as when he engages with his queen who has taken the vow of observance, he incurs a fault. If one engages with a woman protected by the community, against whom is the impure conduct committed? One steals from the many people and likewise engages in impure conduct.

Going by what is not the proper part is engaging with one's mouth or by the impure passage, or, even with one's own wife, in places that are not to be used, such as the mouth and the anus. Going against one's kin is going to those connected with one's family line, such as one's mother or sister. Going in what is not the proper place is engaging in a place in plain view of everyone, or in places such as a stupa or a temple. Going at what is not the proper time is engaging by day, or when one's own wife is menstruating, or pregnant, or nursing a small child. If one engages with her when she is pregnant, it harms the one dwelling in the womb; and if one engages with her when a small child is drinking milk, the milk is quickly used up, and even when the child drinks milk its body does not grow and flourish. For these reasons it is not permissible to engage with her.

To sum up the detailed explanation of the three nonvirtues of the body, the words "These are the three kinds of the body" were spoken.

To teach the meaning of the words "sexual misconduct," the passage beginning "desire, desire" was spoken. The word "desire, desire" is base and is censured by the noble ones; therefore it is called wrongful indulgence in desire. It is sexual misconduct when five components are complete. The five components are: it is a woman; her three doors are unimpaired; there is a lustful mind; one has entered one of the three doors; and one experiences pleasure.

To teach lying, the passage beginning "What is lying?" was spoken. Words that are not true are lies. They are set out in two kinds: those concerning worldly matters and those concerning supramundane matters.

Here, with reference to worldly matters, the words "when questioned as a witness, speaking falsely, contrary to what is true" were spoken. When two people have entered into a dispute, some good person is appointed as witness and instructed, "Tell exactly what you heard and saw of what these two people said." If, when that witness is questioned, for his own advantage or in order to deceive others, he does not speak straightforwardly about what he heard, saw, and so on, exactly as it truly was, but speaks otherwise, that is called lying. And if what that lying person says is understood by someone in whose presence he speaks and who grasps the meaning, it becomes a lie, but not otherwise. Therefore the defining characteristic of a lie is explained thus: words spoken with an altered perception in the presence of one who grasps the meaning become a lie.

With reference to supramundane matters, the words "saying of one who is not an arhat, 'He is an arhat'" were spoken. The foes are the afflictions, such as attachment, and one who has destroyed them is a foe-destroyer, an arhat. One who, having become a supreme field of merit, is worthy of the worship of all beings is called an arhat. If, because of attachment to gain and honor, one says "I am an arhat" when one is not an arhat, that is called a lie. The meaning is that what is done in jest is not a lie.

It is asked: if the meaning that is to be conveyed is conveyed by the body, does it become a lie? It is answered: that too becomes one. Therefore it is explained, for example at the time of the poṣadha: "Thus, if one does not utter words, can one incur the fault of lying? One can, if one conveys it by the body."

When four components are present, a lie has been told: for one's own sake, out of attachment to gain and honor; the intention to speak falsely; and others' understanding of it.

To teach slander, the passage beginning "What is slander?" was spoken. Carrying words back and forth with the intention of dividing friends is slander. It is of two kinds: carrying true words and carrying untrue words. Here, the word "true" teaches the carrying of true words: it is true that someone spoke unpleasant words about others, and one carries those very words back and forth without embellishment. The untrue kind is carrying words back and forth with embellishment, even though the one did not speak unpleasantly of the others. If in such ways one speaks words that divide friends and they become divided, it is slander. When four components are present, slander has been committed: one intended to slander, one spoke slanderous words, and the other understood the meaning.

To teach harsh speech, the passage beginning "Here, what is harsh speech?" was spoken. Here, words that insult another are harsh speech.

To teach just that, the passage beginning "May this one suffer" was spoken. Wishing, with a mind of aversion, that another's mind should suffer, to speak words about caste, clan, faults, and so on that are unwelcome to another and displease him is called harsh speech. When four components are present, harsh speech has been spoken: a mind of aversion; the intention to speak harsh words; speaking them; and, because of that, the other's mind coming to suffer.

To teach idle chatter, the passage beginning "What is idle chatter?" was spoken. In short, to speak words that are not talk of the Dharma, whose purpose is neither to attain purification nor to overcome the afflictions, words that give rise to afflictions such as attachment, is called idle chatter. What that is, is taught in detail by the passage beginning "talk of kings."

To say, "In former times there was a king called Śririhariśa, and his retinue, ministers, palace, and so on were like this," is talk of kings. To say, "Some thieves, or a certain band of raiders, plundered another's dwelling and carried off goods and so on like this," is talk of thieves. To say, "When two royal parties fought a battle, there were so many warriors, so many elephants, so many horses, and so many foot soldiers, and the outcome will be like this," is talk of war. To say, "Our parents, relatives, kinsfolk, and friends, at such a time, enjoyed food like this and drink like this, and so lived happily like this," or, "Later we too shall have food and drink like this," is talk of liquor. To say, "Of these two gamblers who played at dice, this one lost and this one won," or, "We too won at gambling like this at such a time," is talk of gambling. To say, "In former times, or now, there are lovely women like these; they always keep company with these men; and I too kept company with so many lovely women like these, and enjoyed them like this," is talk of women. To recount stories of the knowledge and the heroism of kings, ministers, brahmins, householders, and others of former times is the telling of tales. Further, singing songs and expounding the treatises of non-Buddhists and the like are also called idle chatter.

Then, if one asks, "In the time of a universal monarch, is singing songs also idle chatter?" it is not. For words about emerging from saṃsāra and attaining nirvāṇa are set to song and sung, and since they are not composed improperly like the talk of villagers, there is no fault.

When four components are present, idle chatter has been spoken. The four components are: being possessed of an intention of attachment; the intention to speak idle words; speaking idle words; and others hearing them and attachment arising in them.

To sum up the detailed explanation of the actions of speech, the words "These are the four kinds of speech" were spoken.

To teach covetousness, the passage beginning "Here, what is covetousness?" was spoken. As for "May that property of another become mine": to think, out of attachment to the goods of another sentient being, such as grain, treasure, and land, "How can I make these mine?" and to wish to steal another's property by force, by stealth, and so on, and make it one's own, is called covetousness. When three components are present, it becomes covetousness: there is an intention of attachment, one thinks of stealing the property, and one undertakes the application of stealing it.

To teach malice, the passage beginning "Here, what is malice?" was spoken. To intend, with a mind of aversion, to separate another from life, or to harm his body, is malice. To teach the meaning of these two, the words "separating others from life" were spoken. To intend to separate them from life is to intend to kill others. Cutting is intending to sever their limbs with a weapon or the like, or intending to separate them from their property and so on. Tormenting is intending to bind them, beat them, and so on. When three components are present, it becomes malice: being possessed of aversion, intending to do harm, and undertaking the application in order to do harm.

To teach wrong view, the passage beginning "Here, what is wrong view?" was spoken. The reverse of right view is wrong view, namely, the reverse of what was stated above: "There is no this world, there is no other world," and so on. Through wrong view one denies five things: one denies going and not going to the other world; one denies the planting of the seed and the basis; one denies cause; one denies result; and one destroys what is sublime. Here, "There is no this world, there is no other world" denies going and not going to the other world. "There is no mother, there is no father" denies the function of seed and basis. "There is no giving, there is no fire offering, there is nothing desired and nothing undesired" denies cause. "There is no result, no ripening, of deeds well done and deeds badly done" denies result. "In the world there are none who have rightly gone and none who have rightly entered" destroys what is sublime. When three components are present, it is wrong view: being possessed of delusion, the mind being convinced that there is no other world and so on, and likewise putting it into words.

As for the divisions: these ten paths of nonvirtuous action are set out in four aspects: the object, the intention, the application, and the culmination. Here, the object of the ten paths of nonvirtuous action is the thing on the basis of which killing and the rest occur. It is of two kinds: that which belongs to the class of sentient beings and that which belongs to the class of what is not a sentient being. The intention is knowing that it is that object, and the arising of a mind that wishes to perform the act against it. The application is the undertaking and engagement, by oneself or by another, through body, speech, and mind, in order to perform that act. The completion is when, from that application, they bring it to its conclusion at that time, or bring it to its conclusion later.

Here, the object of killing is what belongs to the class of sentient beings, such as humans and livestock. The intention is both knowing that it is a sentient being and intending to kill it. The application is this: someone who wishes to kill an animal rises from his seat, pays the price for it, sets out on the road, leads it away and has it bound, and strikes it with a weapon; performing the acts up to the point at which it is separated from life, by one or two blows of the weapon or however many, is the application. The completion is that living creature dying through that application, either at that time or later.

The object of taking what is not given is a sentient object, such as humans and livestock, or a non-sentient object, such as grain and treasure. The intention is knowing that it is that very object, and wishing to make one's own an object that another has taken possession of. The application is performing the acts from rising from one's seat, setting out on the road, and breaking into the house and so on, up to the point at which, in whatever way, the object has not yet been taken. The completion is making those objects one's own by force or by stealth.

The object of sexual misconduct is solely what belongs to the class of sentient beings, such as one who is not to be gone to. The intention is both knowing that it is a woman or the like and the arising of the wish to engage with her. The application is performing the acts, for the sake of engaging with her, from rising from one's seat and going to the woman's place up to taking hold of her head, hands, feet, and so on. The completion is the union of the two with each other.

The object of lying is what belongs to the class of sentient beings, such as humans; what does not belong to the class of sentient beings, such as grain; and what has been seen, heard, cognized, and known, and what has not been seen, not been heard, not been cognized, and not been known. The intention is, knowingly, to perceive one way and to wish to speak another way. The application extends, for that purpose, from rising from one's seat and setting out on the road up to beginning to speak. The completion is speaking the false words in the midst of the assembled body of witnesses.

The object of slander is sentient beings who are on good terms, sentient beings who are not on good terms, and both parties. Here, those on good terms are those who were friends before; those not on good terms are those who have become divided. The intention is a mind that wishes to divide them, and it is of two kinds: displeasure at their being on good terms and pleasure at their not being on good terms. The application is undertaking acts such as rising from one's seat in order to divide them. The completion is taking words from this side and repeating them to the other side, and taking words from the other side and repeating them to this side, so that they become divided into factions. Here, to those who are friends one speaks divisive words in order to make them unfriendly, and to those who are unfriendly one adds words so that they will not become friends later.

The object of harsh speech is a sentient being who has become a cause of aversion. The intention is the arising of a mind to harm that being, thinking here: "This one harmed me before, harms me now, and will harm me in the future; this one harmed my friend before, harms him now, and will harm him later; this one helped my enemy before, helps him now, and will help him later," and wishing to harm him. The application is undertaking acts such as rising from one's seat in order to harm him. The completion is uttering harsh words that, for that reason, mention his caste, clan, faults, and so on, so that the other's mind suffers.

The object of idle chatter is talk made up of meaningless matters such as kings and thieves. The intention is the arising of the thought and wish to engage in that talk. The application is undertaking acts such as each rising from his own seat in order to talk. The completion is speaking that talk in the midst of a gathered company.

The object of covetousness is of two kinds among things one would make one's own: things one does not give up and things one does not give up entirely. Here, things one does not give up are goods such as wealth and grain. Things one does not give up entirely are objects of especially strong attachment, such as children and wife. The intention is knowing that it is that object and the arising of desire for it. The arising of desire is of three kinds: by way of asking for it, by way of wishing to obtain it, and by way of stealing it. Here, by way of asking for it is thinking, out of attachment to that object, "I will ask for it and obtain it." By way of wishing to obtain it is making the aspiration, "May I somehow obtain this object." By way of stealing it is thinking, "By this means I will obtain or steal this object." The application is seeking various kinds of means by which to make it one's own. The completion is the mind becoming resolved: "I will not let them go without making them mine."

The object of malice is a sentient being who becomes the cause for aversion to arise. The intention is the arising of the thought, "I will harm him." The application is undertaking acts such as rising from one's seat. The completion is resolving in one's mind, "I will not let him go without killing him or beating him."

The object of wrong view is this world, the other world, actions and the results of actions, and so on. The intention is thinking, "Actions, the results of actions, and so on do not exist," and being convinced of this. The application is keeping company with those who listen to and expound treatises that say so. The completion is, having become convinced that they do not exist, also putting into words, "They do not exist."

As for "causes": these ten paths of nonvirtuous action arise from the three roots of nonvirtue as their cause. The three roots of nonvirtue are attachment, the root of nonvirtue; aversion, the root of nonvirtue; and delusion, the root of nonvirtue. Here, killing too is of three kinds: killing out of attachment, killing out of aversion, and killing out of delusion. Here, killing out of attachment is killing because one wants meat, skin, blood, and the like; killing for sport; and killing in order to protect oneself, one's friends, and so on.

Killing out of aversion is killing an enemy in order to retaliate against a foe. Killing out of delusion is, for example, killing with a religious motive, as when those who perform sacrifices kill living beings, saying, "Having made the fire offering, one is born in the higher realms"; and when kings, too, taking what comes from their law books as authoritative, kill, saying, "By inflicting punishments such as executing evildoers, one comes to have a share of merit"; and when the Persians and others say, "Thus, when one's father and mother have grown old and decrepit, or are in danger from a grave illness, they should be killed," and so on. Again, some say this: "Since snakes, scorpions, tryambukas and the like do harm to people, killing them, and since wild animals, livestock, birds, buffaloes and the like are the means of people's sustenance, even killing them does not become a sin," and so on.

Taking what is not given, too, arises from the three: attachment, aversion and delusion. That which arises from attachment is seizing whatever thing one desires, or stealing out of attachment to gain and honor, or seizing wealth in order to protect oneself and one's friends. Stealing out of aversion is seizing from an enemy and stealing from him in order to retaliate against the enemy. That which arises from delusion is as when it is said, "Taking what comes from the law of kings as authoritative, if one seizes the wealth of evildoers in order to punish them, one gains a share of merit"; and as when the brahmins say, "All these things were given to the brahmins by Brahmā; it is because the brahmins have grown weak that the lowborn enjoy these goods. Therefore, even when he takes them, the brahmin takes his own, enjoys his own, dwells in his own, and gives his own; so a brahmin may take them away. Even when he does so, he has no notion whatever that this belongs to another."

Sexual misconduct, too, is of three kinds: arising from desire, arising from aversion, and arising from delusion. Here, that which arises from desire is engaging in it out of attachment to another's woman; engaging in it for the sake of gain and honor, thinking, "If I have intercourse with a certain woman, I will obtain wealth and honor"; and engaging in it in order to protect oneself and others, thinking, "If I have intercourse with a certain woman, protection will come to me and my friends." That which arises from delusion is, for example, saying in the cattle sacrifice, "Gather all your kinsmen and relatives, male and female, and shut them up in a single house. Stripped naked and made to stoop like cattle, drink water, eat grass, and couple with whoever chances to come, and you will go to the higher realms"; and again some saying, "Women are like a mortar, a flower, a fruit, food that has been cooked, a ford, or a road: even if everyone enjoys them, there is no fault"; and the Persians and others saying, "One may have intercourse with one's mother." That which arises from aversion is, in order to retaliate against an enemy, carrying off his wife and seducing her.

Lying, too, arises from attachment, from aversion and from delusion, and so is of three kinds. Here, that which arises from attachment is telling lies out of desire for wealth and in order to protect oneself and one's friends. That which arises from aversion is telling lies in order to retaliate against an enemy. That which arises from delusion is, for example, as it is taught in the treatises of the brahmins:

O king, what is said in jest is no lie,
Nor what is said to women, nor at the time of marriage,
For the sake of one's life, or when all one's wealth would be lost:
These five lies, it is declared, are without sin.

Divisive speech, too, arises from attachment, from aversion and from delusion, and so is of three kinds. That which arises from attachment is speaking divisively out of attachment to wealth and goods, or in order to protect oneself and one's friends. That which arises from aversion is carrying slander between enemies and friends in order to retaliate against an enemy. That which arises from delusion is as when some treatises of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) teach, "Even if one speaks divisively, there is no fault."

Harsh speech, too, is of three: arising from attachment, arising from aversion, and arising from delusion. That which arises from attachment is threatening with harsh words out of desire for another's wealth, and speaking harshly in order to protect oneself and one's friends. That which arises from aversion is speaking harshly in order to retaliate against an enemy. That which arises from delusion is as when it is taught, "Even if one speaks harshly, there is no fault."

Idle talk, too, arises from attachment, from aversion and from delusion, and so is of three kinds. That which arises from attachment is singing songs and telling ancient tales out of attachment to wealth and in order to protect one's friends. That which arises from aversion is, in order to retaliate against an enemy, winning over his friends with idle talk and bringing them under one's own control. That which arises from delusion is saying, "Explaining the treatises on song and dance and the treatises on tales to others becomes merit."

Covetousness, too, arises from attachment, from aversion and from delusion, and so is of three kinds. That which arises from attachment is, out of attachment to another's wealth or for the sake of protecting oneself and one's friends, thinking of taking another's wealth and goods. That which arises from aversion is thinking of seizing and stealing an enemy's wealth in order to retaliate against the enemy. That which arises from delusion is thinking that even if one steals there is no fault.

Malice, too, arises from desire, from aversion and from delusion, and so is of three kinds. Here, that which arises from desire is malice arising toward others out of attachment to another's wealth and goods, or in order to protect oneself and one's friends. That which arises from aversion is malice arising toward an enemy for the sake of retaliation. That which arises from delusion is thinking that even if malice arises, it does not become a fault.

Wrong view, too, arises from attachment, from aversion and from delusion, and so is of three kinds. Here, that which arises from attachment is holding a view because one delights in that very inverted view. That which arises from aversion is taking up the side of wrong view in order to retaliate against an enemy. That which arises from delusion is holding that even if one grasps a wrong view, it does not become a fault.

Among these, as for killing: although for the three, namely malice, harsh speech and killing proper, the cause that produces them is the three roots of nonvirtue, at the end it is aversion that brings them to their conclusion and completes them.

Taking what is not given, sexual misconduct and covetousness, these three as well, have the three roots of nonvirtue as the cause that produces them, but at the end it is attachment that brings them to their conclusion and completes them. Wrong view arises from the three roots, but at the end it is delusion that brings it to its conclusion and completes it. As for the three, lying, idle talk and divisive speech: some are brought to their conclusion by attachment, some are brought to their conclusion by aversion, and some are brought to their conclusion and completed by delusion.

As for "states": these ten paths of nonvirtuous action should be understood as fivefold: the small state, the middling state, the great state, the state of having arisen, and the latent state. Here, the small state is having committed any one of the ten nonvirtuous actions with a small degree of attachment, aversion and delusion. The middling state is having committed any middling path of action in which those three are neither strong nor weak. The great state is having committed any path of action in which attachment, aversion and delusion are extremely abundant. The state of having arisen is when the path of action of the three afflictions has been produced and has ceased. The latent state is when the afflictions and the paths of action abide as habitual tendencies: though it has arisen, it is as if it has not arisen, and though it has ceased, it has not ceased.

As for "doors": one engages in the ten paths of nonvirtuous action through the three doors of body, speech and mind. Some have their fruit ripen in this very life; some have their fruit ripen in the next life; some have their fruit ripen in other lifetimes; some are certain to ripen into fruit; and some are not certain to ripen into fruit.

Here, ripening in this very life means that in whatever life an action was done, its fruit ripens in that same life. This is established as twofold, by distinction of the field and by distinction of the intention. The distinction of the field is like what is told of how, long ago, two monks fell into a dispute; the Saṅgha settled it according to the Vinaya and one of them lost, and because the monk who had lost called the Saṅgha "women," his sex immediately changed. The distinction of the intention is like how, long ago, a eunuch, with a compassionate mind, ransomed and released a plow ox that was to be slaughtered for its meat, and as a result that very eunuch immediately became a man. Furthermore, helping one's father and mother or the Three Jewels with an intention of deep faith, and harming them with a mind of aversion, and the like, also ripen into fruit in this very life.

Ripening into fruit in the next life means experiencing the fruit immediately upon changing lifetimes. Experiencing the ripening in other lifetimes means experiencing it after an interval of two or three lifetimes, or after an interval of ten, a hundred or a thousand.

Among these, being certain to ripen into fruit: it should be understood that whatever is done with intense affliction or intense faith, or is done constantly, or concerns a benefactor, or is harm or help done to a field of qualities, will without doubt have its fruit experienced. Here, what is done with affliction or with intense faith is like the abuse of the Saṅgha mentioned above, or the eunuch's releasing the ox. Doing constantly means that, having made that action habitual again and again, one will certainly experience its fruit. A benefactor is someone like one's father or mother: if one kills them, however one kills them, one will certainly experience the fruit; and if one helps them, too, experiencing the fruit is certain. A field of qualities is the Three Jewels, or certain persons endowed with moral discipline: helping or harming them, too, will certainly ripen into fruit. Not being certain to ripen means that actions other than those explained above may ripen into fruit, or may not ripen into fruit.

The gravity or lightness of paths of action is established in six ways: by the motivation, by habituation, by nature, by the object, by being exclusively on the unfavorable side, and by being overcome through the antidote. Here, as for motivation: paths of action done with intense attachment and aversion are called heavy. Paths of action done with middling attachment, aversion and delusion are called middling. Paths of action done with small attachment, aversion and delusion are called light. As for habituation: any action, whether virtuous or nonvirtuous, done continuously over a long time is heavy; one done for only a short time is middling; one not greatly habituated is light. As for nature: taking what is not given is more blameworthy than sexual misconduct; killing is more blameworthy than taking what is not given; idle talk is more blameworthy than killing; harsh speech is more blameworthy than idle talk; divisive speech is more blameworthy than harsh speech; lying is more blameworthy than divisive speech. Malice is more blameworthy than covetousness; wrong view is more blameworthy than malice. Moral discipline is more blameless than generosity; meditation is more blameless than moral discipline. As for the object: virtuous and nonvirtuous actions of harming or helping one's father and mother and the Three Jewels are established as heavy. As for being exclusively on the unfavorable side: if anyone at all, in a single life, practices nothing but nonvirtue and does not practice virtue, that very action is established as heavy. As for being overcome through the antidote: when one has habituated oneself to virtue, which is the antidote to nonvirtuous action, that virtuous action too is established as heavy.

As for "fruits": by practicing these ten paths of nonvirtuous action, three kinds of fruit ripen: the fruit of maturation, the fruit concordant with its cause, and the dominant fruit. Here, as for the fruit of maturation: if one practices the ten nonvirtues to a great degree, one is born as a hell being; if one practices them to a middling degree, one is born as a hungry ghost; if one practices them to a small degree, one is born in the animal realm. The great and small degrees are distinguished by the intensity or mildness of the afflictions. Alternatively, performing an action has three aspects: the preparation, the main act and the conclusion. Here, the preparation is setting about performing a nonvirtuous action such as killing.

The main act is having performed a nonvirtuous action such as killing. The conclusion is rejoicing in having done them, and afterward continuing to do them in the same way. If an action is done with these three, it is strong, and one is born as a hell being. With two of the causes one is born as a hungry ghost. With one, one is born as an animal.

As for the fruit concordant with its cause: even if one is freed from the three lower realms and by chance is born as a human being, one will experience two ripenings for each single nonvirtue. Here, the fruits concordant with the cause of killing are two: a short life and many illnesses. A short life: because in the past one cut off the continuum of other beings' lives, even when born as a human one dies in the womb, or dies not long after being born. Many illnesses: because in the past, when killing other beings, one also caused them suffering with clubs, weapons and the like, even when born as a human one constantly suffers from various diseases such as leprosy and smallpox.

The fruits concordant with the cause of taking what is not given are also of two kinds: having few possessions, and one's possessions becoming common property. Having few possessions: because in the past one robbed others of their possessions and left them destitute, in this life too one is always destitute and lacking in necessities. One's possessions becoming common property: as the fruit of what one robbed, perhaps robbing it and giving it to others, whatever possessions one may have are always seized by everyone, and one cannot enjoy them alone but enjoys them in common with all.

The results concordant with the cause of sexual misconduct are also two: having servants and retinue who cannot be relied upon, and having a wife shared with rivals. Having servants and retinue who cannot be relied upon means that one's own retinue cannot be trusted, and they do not trust one another. Having a wife shared with rivals means that everyone has designs on one's wife and everyone has illicit intercourse with her.

The fruits concordant with the cause of lying are also two: being much slandered and being deceived by others. Being much slandered means that even when one has done no wrong, others slander one, saying that one has. Being deceived by others means that when they have promised, "I will do such and such," or "I will give it," they give it and yet deceive one.

The fruits concordant with the cause of divisive speech are also two: having a discordant retinue and having a bad retinue. Having a discordant retinue means that one's own retinue is always quarreling and fighting. Having a bad retinue means that whatever retinue one may have is unattractive in bodily features and weak in strength and vigor.

The fruits concordant with the cause of harsh speech are also two: hearing what is unpleasant, and one's words turning into quarrels. Hearing unpleasant words means always hearing nothing but talk that disturbs the mind, and everything one says being unwelcome to others' ears. One's words turning into quarrels means that even when one has not spoken badly, one ends up fighting and quarreling with others.

The fruits concordant with the cause of idle talk are also of two kinds: one's words not being worth accepting, and having unsure eloquence. One's words not being worth accepting means that what one says carries no weight and no one listens to one's words. Having unsure eloquence means that what one says does not hit the mark.

The fruits concordant with the cause of covetousness are also of two kinds: discontent and great desire. Discontent means not being satisfied with what one has. Great desire means hankering after the possessions of others.

The fruits concordant with the cause of malice are also two: not seeking others' benefit, and being harmed by others. Not seeking others' benefit means delighting in harming all others and intending to do them harm. Being harmed by others means that at all times others cause one discomfort, beating, abusing one, and so on.

The fruits concordant with the cause of wrong view are of two kinds: wherever one is born, one is born in places such as those of non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) and inclines toward wrong views; and one is always devoid of honesty and engages in nothing but deceitful and hypocritical deeds.

The dominant fruit of these ten nonvirtuous actions is the decline of outer things. Through killing, things such as grain become weak in potency and poor in luster. Through taking what is not given, they are destroyed by drought and hail. Through sexual misconduct, the ground becomes covered with dust. Through lying, grain and the like become foul-smelling. Through divisive speech, the land becomes very rugged and uneven. Through harsh speech, it becomes parched and saline. Through idle talk, the seasons change, and rain and the like fall out of season. Through covetousness, grain and the like become meager. Through malice, they become pungent and bitter. Through wrong view, fruits become few or there are no fruits at all. The fruits of the ten virtuous paths of action should be understood as the reverse of these.

As for "categories": in the sūtras, speaking with reference to these very ten paths of action, the Buddha spoke of crookedness of body, crookedness of speech and crookedness of mind; spoke of turbidity of body, turbidity of speech and turbidity of mind; spoke of faults of body, faults of speech and faults of mind; and further said, "There is white action with white ripening, black action with black ripening, mixed action with mixed ripening, and action that is neither white nor black, which exhausts the ripening of white and black action." These are the categories. Here, actions of body and speech born from a deceitful mind are crookedness. Actions of body, speech and so on born from attachment are turbidity, and those of body, speech and so on born from the cause of a mind of aversion are faults. The ten nonvirtuous paths of action, which cause birth in the three lower realms, are black actions, and their ripening is also black. Actions that cause birth in the form realm are white, and their ripening is also white. Virtue that causes birth as a human and so on in the desire realm is called mixed white and black action, with mixed white and black ripening. The two, the uninterrupted path and the path of liberation, are neither white nor black action, and are called the path that exhausts white and black ripening.

In order to teach right livelihood, the sūtra says, "What is wrong livelihood?" Once wrong livelihood has been taught, its reverse is right livelihood, and so wrong livelihood is explained first. It is established as twofold: the wrong livelihood of a monk and the wrong livelihood of a layman.

Here, in order to teach the wrong livelihood of a monk, the sūtra says, "Monks, possessed of hypocrisy," and so on. Hypocrisy, flattery, hinting, coercion, and seeking gain by means of gain are the wrong livelihood of a monk; the wrong livelihood of a layman will be explained later.

In order to teach hypocrisy, the sūtra says, "Here, what is hypocrisy?" and so on. Hypocrisy is of three kinds: putting on a deportment; living in a secluded place and displaying few possessions; and making a show of resorting to what is poor and not resorting to what is fine. Here, putting on a deportment means adopting the deportment of meditative absorption, out of attachment to gain and honor, with the intention that others will infer that one has good qualities. Living in a secluded place and displaying few possessions means, with a mind attached to gain and honor, taking up residence in a charnel ground or a wilderness and not keeping many possessions such as begging bowls. Resorting to what is poor and not resorting to what is fine means making a show of using poor food and poor robes and the like, and making a show of not using fine food and good robes and the like; here too one should supply "out of attachment to gain, honor and the like."

To show that, of these three kinds of hypocrisy, this sūtra is speaking with reference to the hypocrisy of putting on a deportment and the manner of living in a secluded place with few possessions, it says, "Monks, when he sees a patron," and so on. Under the sway of attachment to gain and honor, thinking, "May gain and honor come my way," when a monk sees a patron he sits cross-legged on the road or in an empty village, and with the thought that the patrons too will think, "This monk is a meditator, an arhat, worthy of gain and honor," he puts on a deportment: this is called hypocrisy.
