"Sitting cross-legged" stands for the position of whatever deportment one has adopted. Gain means begging bowls, robes and the like. Honor means doing such things as joining the palms and prostrating. "May it come to me" means thinking, "May it be accomplished for me." The words "and so on" include the hypocrisy of resorting to what is poor and not resorting to what is fine.

In order to teach flattery, the sūtra says, "Here, what is flattery?" and so on. It consists of speaking words pleasing to others with one's mind on gain and honor, speaking of one's own good qualities, and speaking the Dharma. Speaking words pleasing to them with one's mind on gain and honor means, because of attachment to gain and honor, saying such words as "You are my father, you are my mother." Speaking of one's own good qualities means saying such things as "I am possessed of faith, possessed of learning, possessed of moral discipline." Speaking the Dharma means expounding the scriptures of the Tathāgata in the presence of others out of attachment to gain and honor. To show that here it is speaking with reference to speaking words that please them, the sūtra says, "You are my father, you are my mother," and so on. Likewise, other "sweet words" are such as "You are my lord, you are my preceptor, you are my master." The words "and so on" in "and so on" include speaking of one's own good qualities and speaking the Dharma.

In order to teach hinting, the sūtra says, "Here, what is hinting?" and so on. It consists of displaying things that harm the body, displaying things that show destitution, and mentioning in words the things one wants, all with the intention of gain. Displaying things that harm the body means, with a mind attached to obtaining robes, food and the like, showing one's own body as afflicted by wounds, sores and the like, and showing it as afflicted by cold and heat. Displaying things that show destitution means showing that one does not have robes, a begging bowl and the like, or appearing as if one had none. Mentioning in words the things one wants means saying, "I lack just this one sort of thing," and, "Excellent robes and the like such as these are obtained from the houses of these excellent householders and are not obtained from those of others." Because this sūtra speaks with reference to wanting things through words, it says, "Monks, having eaten alms," and so on. Having eaten alms at the houses of faithful householders, and having eaten alms, then, under the sway of attachment to gain, saying again and again, "Layman, what is obtained from your house is not obtained from others," is hinting. Speaking straightforwardly without attachment to gain and honor is not a fault; it is not hinting.

In order to teach coercion, the sūtra says, "Here, what is coercion?" and so on. Coercion is also of three kinds: with the intention of gain, disparaging a patron; threatening to beg from other connections; and disparaging those who do not give freely. With the intention of gain, disparaging a patron means, out of attachment to gain and honor, saying, "When a patron does not give gifts such as robes, he becomes poor; and besides, all kinds of misfortune befall him." Threatening to beg from other connections means saying, "If you do not give when it is your turn as my patron, you, patron, are not the only one; there are others too," and so threatening to beg from others. Disparaging those who do not give freely means threatening, "Those who do not practice giving will without doubt fall into the lower realms, and since you too do not practice giving, you will certainly fall into the lower realms." To show that here it is speaking with reference to disparaging those who do not give freely, the sūtra says, "Monks, from whatever house he does not obtain alms," and so on.

Although the words of the sūtra do not explicitly state it, the other two kinds of coercion should also be regarded as included here.

In order to teach seeking gain by means of gain, the sūtra says, "Here, what is seeking by means of gain?" and so on. This too is of three kinds: with the intention of gain, displaying great gains received from others; holding up a patron as generous on a grand scale; and displaying the material goods one has received. With the intention of gain, displaying great gains received from others means that, because of attachment to obtaining robes and the like, one buys fine robes and the like with one's own wealth and then says to one's patron, "I received robes like these as offerings from others," and in this way brings about gain. Holding up a patron as generous on a grand scale means saying, "When such-and-such a patron gives as he pleases, he delights in giving on a grand scale; you are not that kind of patron." Displaying the material goods one has received means that, having gone to a patron's house and having been given food, robes and the like, one tells what material goods are given in the houses of other patrons as well.

Because, of these three kinds of bringing about gain by means of gain, it speaks here with reference to displaying, with the intention of gain, great gains received from others, the sūtra says, "Monks, with his own wealth," and so on. In brief, this means that, having bought robes and the like with one's own wealth, one sits before a patron, a layman, and says, "Another patron gave me robes and the like such as these," and he too, out of embarrassment, gives robes and the like. The other two kinds of bringing about gain by means of gain should also be regarded as included here.

These five kinds of wrong livelihood of a monk are further established as three: actions of body, actions of speech, and actions of both body and speech. Here, hypocrisy is an action of body. Flattery is an action of speech. Hinting, coercion, and bringing about gain by means of gain involve actions of both body and speech. Having abandoned these five kinds of wrong livelihood, monks have right livelihood.

In order to teach what the wrong livelihood of a layman is, the sūtra says, "What is the wrong livelihood of a layman?" and so on. The mention of "layman" is merely illustrative; it also includes laywomen and those who keep the fast-day precepts. Above as well, in the section on monks, "monk" was merely illustrative and also included nuns, male and female renunciants, and female novices.

"Selling beings" means selling human beings. For lay followers that is what is prohibited; it is not that trading in beings such as horses is disallowed. "Pressing grain and mustard seed without examining them" means this: because insects get into grain and mustard seed that have been kept a long time, if one presses grain, mustard seed and the like without examining them, one harms the lives of living creatures, and so this is established as wrong livelihood; the intended meaning is that pressing them after examining them is not wrong livelihood.

This too is established as twofold: wrong livelihood that pertains to what is counted as sentient beings, and wrong livelihood arising from what is not counted as sentient beings. Here, selling beings, selling meat, and selling grain without examining it are wrong livelihood arising from what is counted as sentient beings. Selling poison, selling weapons, and selling liquor are wrong livelihood arising from what is not counted as sentient beings.

In order to teach right effort, the sūtra says, "What is right effort?" and so on. "Speaking courteously" refers to the conduct of respect with regard to those who live as householders: on meeting one's father, mother and the elders of one's family, one bows with the body, greets them with words, and speaks gently. "Prostrating" refers to those who have gone forth: on meeting one's preceptor, master and so on, one bows one's limbs and performs the act of prostration. "Rising" shows the conduct of respect with regard to teachers and others who arrive: when other worthy persons, teachers and the like, come respectfully, one rises from one's seat, goes to meet them, seats them, and serves them. "Performs the act of joining the palms" shows the conduct of respect toward worthy persons dwelling in the same place: when one meets persons living in the same place who share the holy life, one bows the body and performs the act of joining the palms. This is right effort. In brief, abandoning what is unfavorable and striving to cultivate the antidote is right effort.

In order to teach right mindfulness, the sūtra says, "What is right mindfulness? Here, monks," and so on. Here, beings are of three kinds: those whose nature is attachment, those whose nature is aversion, and those whose nature is delusion. The signs of these three should be examined. The signs of one whose nature is attachment are these: by nature restless and of a distracted disposition; delighting in song, dance and the like; weak in mindfulness; always gentle in temperament; very jealous; abiding in much bashfulness. Attachment is hard to abandon but carries little blame. The signs of one whose nature is aversion are these: criticizing and arguing about anything at all; delighting in quarrels; with a frowning face; hard to please; harsh and sharp in temperament; delighting in wrongdoing; inclined to be harsh-minded toward beings and to harm them. The affliction is easy to abandon, but carries great blame. The signs of one whose nature is delusion are these: full of doubt; without zeal for the tasks to be done; much given to sleep and torpor; not of an investigative and tenacious nature; with a mind much befuddled, like cotton or silk floss; very lazy; trusting no one, and that person himself not to be trusted; aversion arising toward virtuous beings; ungrateful. The affliction is hard to abandon and carries great blame.

If one asks how these may be recognized in more detail: the conduct of attachment is to delight in the objects of sensual pleasure and mostly to seek out the good qualities of others, without looking for opportunities to find fault. The conduct of one with aversion is not to be greatly attached to anything and mostly to seek out the flaws and faults of others. The conduct of one with delusion is to settle nowhere, and not to be of a nature given to settling down or to investigating.

If they are examined by way of former conduct: one with attachment, because he mostly performed virtuous actions in the past and so abides in pleasant results, should be seen as having died and passed from a heavenly abode and been born here. One with aversion, because he mostly performed actions such as killing, cutting, and beating in the past, is born here after dying and passing from the hells, from the birthplace of nāgas, or, even in a human birthplace, from a state of constant suffering. Because in the past too his mind was constantly agitated by suffering, wherever he is born, there too his mind should be seen as agitated and his aversion as great. As for the conduct of one with delusion, its former cause should be seen thus: in the past he drank much liquor, or did not perform such activities as listening to and writing out the teachings, and he is born here after dying and passing from the birthplace of animals.

If they are examined by way of the elements as cause: when the four great elements, earth, water, fire, and wind, are in equal proportion, attachment predominates. When the fire element and the wind element predominate, aversion is great. When earth and water predominate, delusion is great.

If they are examined by way of the humors as basis: those in whom wind predominates abide in the conduct of attachment. Those in whom bile predominates abide in great aversion. Those in whom phlegm predominates are those of great delusion.

Further, the three kinds of conduct are examined by way of seven aspects. The seven are: objects seen, afflictions, gait, wearing robes, eating food, doing work, and sleeping.

How is one to examine them by way of objects? Those in whom attachment predominates, when they see some form, look at it as something wondrous and look at it for a long time. Even if some fault appears in it, they do not regard it as a fault, and they make even a slight good quality into a marvel. They do not like to be parted from whatever they see, and even when parted they go away wishing to meet with it again. Those in whom aversion predominates, when they see some form, find nothing in it to marvel at, but look at it with aloofness, as if cutting it off. They do not look for long; they frequently seek out the faults and flaws of others too, and they delight in disparaging. Even when great good qualities and excellences are shown them, they do not trust them. They wish to be parted from whatever they see, and they go away showing their displeasure. Those in whom delusion predominates, even when they see some form, do not recognize its faults or its good qualities. If others disparage it, they disparage it after them; if others praise it, they praise it after them. They do not recognize what is good or bad for themselves.

How is one to examine them by way of afflictions? Those in whom attachment predominates constantly engage in five afflictions: envy, avarice, deceit, pretension, and craving; these are the five. Those in whom aversion predominates also constantly engage in five afflictions. The five are wrath, resentment, concealment, spite, and anger. Those in whom delusion predominates continually engage in five afflictions. The five are laziness, sleep, torpor, doubt, and regret.

How is one to examine them by way of gait? When one whose conduct is attachment walks, he sets his feet down evenly and walks slowly, placing his steps gracefully as he goes. When one whose conduct is aversion walks, by nature he places his steps haphazardly, sets his feet down as if pressing on the ground, and goes leading with his right side. When one whose conduct is delusion walks, by nature, when he sets his feet on the ground, he sets them down in a fluster, and as he walks his two feet scuff the ground and drag against each other.

How is one to examine them by way of wearing robes? One whose conduct is attachment by nature likes robes neither too tight nor too loose. He ties his belt neither tight nor slack, and likes robes of a pleasing length; he puts them on neatly and beautifully arranged. One whose conduct is aversion by nature likes tight robes; he ties his belt tight and wears short robes, and does not dress with an eye solely to beauty. One whose conduct is delusion by nature likes loose robes; he ties his belt slackly, and wears his robes rumpled and unsightly.

How is one to examine them by way of eating food? One whose conduct is attachment by nature likes rich and sweet foods. One whose conduct is aversion likes coarse and sour foods. One whose conduct is delusion has no fixed preference for anything at all. When one whose conduct is attachment eats, he makes moderate mouthfuls and arranges them nicely, and eats slowly and with relish; when he tastes something delicious, a special delight arises in him. When one whose conduct is aversion eats, he makes large mouthfuls that fill his mouth and eats quickly, without relish; even when he has tasted some delicious food, a still greater displeasure arises in him. When one whose conduct is delusion eats, he makes small mouthfuls and makes them untidy, smearing his hands, mouth, and so on with food, and eats without paying much attention to anything.

How is one to examine them by way of work? One whose conduct is attachment takes up the broom and slowly cleans away the sweepings, sweeping so that not a single bit is left. One whose conduct is aversion takes up the broom and, with long strokes, flings the sweepings to both sides, sweeping forcefully and noisily. One whose conduct is delusion takes up the broom and goes about it listlessly; the sweepings are not cleaned away, and he sweeps slowly and untidily. Other work too should be examined in the same way. One whose conduct is attachment does any work beautifully and respectfully. One whose conduct is aversion is steadfast in accomplishing any work properly. One whose conduct is delusion does any work in a muddle, and never keeps to a single task without breaking off.

How is one to examine them by way of sleeping? One whose conduct is attachment prepares his bedding slowly and arranges it nicely, and when he lies down he sleeps with his body curled up. When he rises in the morning, he gets up quickly and goes, or speaks slowly, as though somewhat fearful. One whose conduct is aversion, when he spreads his bedding, prepares it quickly, doing it any which way, and sleeps with his arms and legs stretched out and a scowl on his face. When he rises in the morning, he too gets up quickly and speaks harshly, as though angry. One whose conduct is delusion, having risen from his bedding, prepares it just as it lay when he got up, and when he lies down he sleeps face down.

When he rises in the morning, too, he mumbles words indistinctly, with no sense of what they are, and only gets up from his bed once the time has quite run out and gone by.

Thus, for each of the three kinds of conduct, one must teach what sort of robes, what kind of alms food, what sort of lodging, and which town for the alms round each is to be assigned. Therefore, because those whose conduct is attachment must be set to the practices of meditating on the unattractive, the sūtra says, "Monks, a monk, having seen a woman," and so on. Those whose conduct is aversion are set to meditating on loving-kindness; that will be explained below, where the four immeasurables are taught. For those whose conduct is delusion, dependent origination is assigned; how it works has already been explained above, where the twelve links of dependent origination were taught.

Now, those whose conduct is attachment are given robes that are short and coarse. Those whose conduct is aversion are given robes that are clean, of good color, long, and beautiful. For those whose conduct is delusion, any kind will do. Those whose conduct is attachment are given alms food that is coarse, unappetizing, and scant. Those whose conduct is aversion are given alms food that is soft, clean, and delicious, as much as will satisfy them. For those whose conduct is delusion, any kind will do. For those whose conduct is attachment, the lodging assigned is a charnel ground or a place near a charnel ground, or else a monastery or the like that has not been made fit for use, without fine spreads and coverings. For those whose conduct is aversion, the lodging assigned is a place rich in trees and water, with level ground, and beautiful, or a beautifully appointed monastery, with fine spreads and bedding. For those whose conduct is delusion, anywhere will do. For those whose conduct is attachment, the villages assigned for the alms round are villages where people mostly eat coarse food, and unattractive towns filled with men. For those whose conduct is aversion, the town assigned for the alms round is one with excellent food, a beautiful town, where many people practice with faith. For those whose conduct is delusion, the town for the alms round may be any at all, but it is assigned in a place where mostly learned people live.

Now, the conduct of attachment is of four kinds: attachment to touch, attachment to the natural form, attachment to color, and attachment to enjoyment. Attachment to touch is attachment to soft objects of touch; one with it should meditate on white bones. Attachment to the natural form is attachment to the unimpaired physical form of a woman's body and the like; one with it should meditate on the swollen corpse, the bloated corpse, and so on. Those attached to color should meditate on the discolored corpse and so on. Those attached to enjoyment should meditate on the body of a woman or the like cast away in a charnel ground, mindless, lying like a log.

The disintegration of the outer and inner bodies, one's own and a woman's, becomes the antidote to all four kinds of attachment, and so this sūtra makes that the principal theme and teaches it. Here a woman's body is the outer body, and one's own body is the inner body. Alternatively, head hair, body hair, nails, teeth, dust, grime, and skin are the outer body, and what is listed from flesh and bone up to feces and urine is the inner body. Here, what grows from the head is head hair; what grows from the body, the armpits, the face, and so on, is body hair. If, having examined one's own and others' bodies in this way, one becomes familiar with what one has examined, attachment is crushed. This is called right mindfulness.

In order to teach right concentration, the sūtra says, "What is right concentration?" and so on. One-pointedness of mind on wholesome dharmas is right concentration, and since the four meditative concentrations of the form realm also have the defining mark of one-pointedness of mind on wholesome dharmas, they are called right concentration. The first meditative concentration has five limbs. The five limbs are initial thought, sustained analysis, joy, bliss, and one-pointedness of mind. The second meditative concentration has four limbs. The four limbs are inner clarity, bliss, and one-pointedness of mind. The third meditative concentration has five limbs. The five limbs are equanimity, mindfulness, vigilance, bliss, and one-pointedness of mind. The fourth meditative concentration has four limbs. The four limbs are utterly pure equanimity, utterly pure mindfulness, feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful, and one-pointedness of mind. Thus these four meditative concentrations have eighteen limbs in all. The eighteen limbs are these: five limbs in the first meditative concentration and five in the third, making ten; four limbs in the second and four in the fourth, making eight; so there are eighteen. These four meditative concentrations will be explained in detail later, in the section on meditative concentration; explaining them here would serve little purpose and be a great labor, so they are not explained here.

After the explanation of the Four Noble Truths, the twenty-two faculties are taught; wishing to explain them in the order in which they are taught, the sūtra says, "What are the twenty-two faculties?" and so on. What is the purpose of teaching the faculties? It is said: the faculties are taught in order that one may understand phenomena that are mundane and supramundane, with defilements and without defilements, productive and unproductive, common and uncommon. When one understands those phenomena, one abandons clinging to self and to what belongs to self. When clinging is abandoned, the suffering of saṃsāra ceases. When the suffering of saṃsāra ceases, one attains the bliss of nirvāṇa. Thus the faculties are taught in order that suffering may be abandoned and bliss attained.

Now, the subtle particles of the eye faculty are located within the dark of the eye, like an opened flax flower. The subtle particles of the ear faculty are located inside the ear, like a knot of twisted birch bark. The subtle particles of the nose faculty are located in layers inside the nostrils, like a metal probe. The subtle particles of the tongue faculty are located there like a half moon. The subtle particles of the body faculty are located pervading it in layers, in the shape of the body. The mind faculty does not have the defining mark of an accumulation of subtle particles; its defining mark is the knowing of all phenomena.

The subtle particles of the female faculty are located in the private region, like the face of a drum or like carded cotton. The subtle particles of the male faculty are located in the region of the groin, like a finger. The life faculty is what keeps the aggregates of a given class of beings in existence and holds them so that they do not perish. Some explain that the continuous persistence of the aggregates is the life faculty. Others say that what governs the persistence of the great elements endowed with warmth and consciousness is called the life faculty.

Why are five faculties taught, from the faculty of suffering up to the faculty of equanimity? Those five faculties are what stand opposed to the meditative concentrations and the meditative attainments. Because there are five kinds of opposing factors, the faculties are taught as just five, the faculty of suffering and the rest. The first meditative concentration overcomes the faculty of mental unhappiness. The second meditative concentration overcomes the faculty of suffering. The third meditative concentration overcomes the faculty of pleasure. The fourth meditative concentration, having overcome the faculty of mental happiness, abides in the faculty of equanimity, completely pure.

Now, in the desire realm there are the five consciousnesses, and in the first meditative concentration the eye, ear, and body consciousnesses. Whatever agreeable feeling belongs to those groups of consciousness is called the faculty of pleasure. In the third meditative concentration, the agreeableness of the mind is also called the faculty of pleasure. What is disagreeable to the group of five consciousnesses is the faculty of suffering, and it exists only in the desire realm, not in the other realms. Why is that? Because in the form and formless realms there is no ripening of unwholesome karma. Whatever agreeable feeling there is in the mind is the faculty of mental happiness, and it exists in the desire realm and in the first and second meditative concentrations. The faculty of equanimity exists in the desire, form, and formless realms, that is, on every level.

Alternatively, the three kinds of feeling, pleasant, painful, and neither pleasant nor painful, are set out as five faculties according to the difference of their support, body or mind. When pleasant feeling arises from the body and is connected with the body, it is called the faculty of pleasure; when that same pleasant feeling is connected with the mind, it is called the faculty of mental happiness. When painful feeling is connected with the body and arises from the body, it is called the faculty of suffering; when that same painful feeling is connected with the mind, it is called the faculty of mental unhappiness. What is connected with both body and mind, and conceives of neither pleasure nor suffering, is the faculty of equanimity.

Alternatively, what is based on the five sense faculties, takes an unpleasing object, and is associated with the group of five consciousnesses, without conceptual thought, is the faculty of suffering. What is based on the mind faculty, takes an unpleasing object, involves conceptual thought, and is associated with mental consciousness is the faculty of mental unhappiness. What is based on the five sense faculties, takes a pleasing object, and is associated with the group of five consciousnesses is the faculty of pleasure. What is based on the mind faculty, takes a pleasing object, involves conceptual thought, and is associated with mental consciousness is the faculty of mental happiness. What is based on the six faculties, takes an object that is neither pleasing nor unpleasing, and is associated with the six consciousnesses, whether without conceptual thought or with it, is called the faculty of equanimity.

Alternatively, the experiencing of the ripened result of non-meritorious formations is the faculty of suffering and the faculty of mental unhappiness. The experiencing of the ripened result of meritorious formations is the faculty of pleasure and the faculty of mental happiness. The experiencing of the ripened result of unmoving formations is the faculty of equanimity.

Alternatively, that whose nature is to harm the body, the sense faculties, the mind, and the intellect, which is suffering when it arises and suffering while it lasts, and brings ease when it changes, is called the faculty of suffering and the faculty of mental unhappiness. That whose nature is to benefit the body, the sense faculties, the intellect, and the mind, which is pleasant when it arises and pleasant while it lasts, and painful when it changes, is called the faculty of pleasure. That which neither harms nor benefits the body, the sense faculties, the intellect, and the mind, which is suffering if knowledge has not arisen and bliss if wisdom has arisen, and which lacks such harm or benefit, is the faculty of equanimity.

Alternatively, what is bound up with the suffering of suffering is called the faculty of suffering and the faculty of mental unhappiness. What is bound up with the suffering of change is called the faculty of pleasure and the faculty of mental happiness. What is bound up with the suffering of formations is the faculty of equanimity.

Alternatively, the faculty of suffering and the faculty of mental unhappiness give rise to the craving not to meet and the craving to be parted. The faculty of pleasure and the faculty of mental happiness give rise to sensual craving and the craving to meet. The faculty of equanimity gives rise to the craving of utter delusion.

Alternatively, bodily feeling accompanied by harm is the faculty of suffering. Mental feeling accompanied by harm is the faculty of mental unhappiness. Bodily feeling without harm is the faculty of pleasure. Mental feeling without harm is the faculty of mental happiness. What is the reverse of both is the faculty of equanimity.

Alternatively, bodily feeling devoid of the taste of pleasure, whether born of a disturbance of the elements, or coming from harm done by oneself or another, or the experience of suffering at the time of death, or remaining in an uncomfortable posture, is the faculty of suffering. Mental feeling devoid of pleasure, born of conceptual thoughts such as meeting with what is disliked, being parted from what is loved, weariness, pride, fear, being reviled, and being punished, is the faculty of mental unhappiness.

Bodily feeling that has relish, whether born of the elements having come into balance, or from suffering having been overcome by its antidote, or from remaining in a comfortable posture, or produced by bodily ease, is the feeling of mental happiness. Mental feeling that has relish, arising from having met with a pleasing object and become far removed from unpleasing objects, or from conceptual thought about such good things as fame and praise, is the faculty of mental happiness. Feeling of body or mind that neither benefits nor harms body and mind, and is neither pleasant nor painful, is the faculty of equanimity, which is not fixed one way.

Why are the faculties of faith and the rest taught as just five, and not as six, four, or some other number? It is said: because there are five factors opposed to the powers, faith and the rest are taught as just five, and not as six, four, or some other number. The five factors opposed to the powers are lack of faith, laziness, lack of mindfulness, distraction, and lack of knowledge. The antidote to lack of faith is the faculty of faith. The antidote to laziness is the faculty of diligence. The antidote to lack of mindfulness is the faculty of mindfulness. The antidote to distraction is the faculty of samādhi. The antidote to lack of knowledge is the faculty of wisdom.

Alternatively, five faculties are taught because of five kinds of inclination. The five kinds of inclination are these. Some are inclined to the door of faith, like the noble Vakkali. Some are inclined to the door of diligence, like the noble Śroṇa. Some are inclined to the door of mindfulness, like the noble Mahānāman. Some are inclined to the door of calm abiding, like the noble Revata. Some are inclined to the door of wisdom, like the noble Śāriputra. Those inclined to the door of faith rely on the faculty of faith to emerge from the three realms; they rely on the four limbs of stream entry, which come below. Those inclined to the faculty of diligence rely on the four right efforts and emerge from the three realms through diligence. Those inclined to the faculty of mindfulness rely on the four applications of mindfulness and emerge from the three realms through the faculty of mindfulness. Those inclined to the faculty of samādhi rely on the four meditative concentrations and emerge from the three realms through the faculty of samādhi. Those inclined to the faculty of wisdom rely on knowledge of the Four Noble Truths and emerge from the three realms through wisdom. For this reason too the faculties are taught as just five.

Why are the five faculties set out in this order? It is said: the faculty of faith is the seed of the thirty-seven factors of awakening, and therefore the faculty of faith is placed first. It is like this: just as those who believe that water will come once a well is dug set to work in order to dig the well, so one who sees the faults of saṃsāra and has faith in the good qualities of nirvāṇa undertakes diligence in order to realize nirvāṇa; therefore the faculty of diligence is taught after it. For those who have undertaken diligence, it is impossible for mindfulness to lapse; therefore the faculty of mindfulness comes after that. When mindfulness does not lapse, the mind is always one-pointed; therefore the faculty of samādhi is taught after that. When the mind is undistracted and one-pointed, nirvāṇa is realized; therefore the faculty of wisdom is taught last.

Alternatively, faith in the Four Noble Truths is the faculty of faith. The applied effort of the faithful to realize the truths is the faculty of diligence. The diligent not forgetting the defining marks of the four truths is the faculty of mindfulness. Meditating one-pointedly on the four truths is the faculty of samādhi. Having come to know the four truths is the faculty of wisdom.

Alternatively, a king endowed with five qualities conquers the armies of other kings. The five qualities are these: a wise minister, a strong fortress and territory, armor, sharp weapons, and loyal retainers. Likewise, a yogin endowed with five similar qualities conquers the armies of the afflictions. He is endowed with the faculty of wisdom, which is like the minister; he relies on samādhi as his fortress and territory; he is endowed with the armor of unforgetting mindfulness; he has the sharp weapon of steadfast diligence; and he is endowed with the retinue of unshakable faith.

Now, three faculties are to be explained: the faculty called "coming to know the unknown," the faculty of knowing, and the faculty of being endowed with knowledge. On the path of seeing, nine substances, namely the mind, mental happiness, equanimity, faith, diligence, mindfulness, samādhi, and wisdom, are designated the faculty called "coming to know the unknown." Why is that? Because on the path of seeing those nine substances set to work to know, touch, understand, and realize the four truths that were previously not known at all, the faculty of that person is designated the faculty called "coming to know the unknown." Those same nine substances, at the time of the path of meditation, are designated the faculty of knowing. Why is that? Because on the path of meditation those nine substances set to work to know, become aware of, understand, and realize the four truths that were previously not known at all, the faculty of that person is designated the faculty called "knowing the known." Those same nine substances, at the time of the path of meditation, are designated the faculty of knowing. Why is that? On the path of meditation there is nothing not seen before that is still to be seen; but in order to abandon without remainder the latent tendencies to be abandoned by meditation, one cultivates and knows again and again that very knowledge of the Four Noble Truths gained on the path of seeing, and so the nine faculties of a person on the path of meditation are designated the faculty of knowing. Those same nine substances, at the time of the path of no more learning, are called the faculty of being endowed with knowledge. Why is that? Because at the time of the path of no more learning one understands, "I have fully known the four truths," and possesses the wisdom of insight, it is called the faculty of being endowed with knowledge.

Alternatively, it is the faculty of being endowed with knowledge because it has the character of fully guarding and maintaining the phenomena of complete knowledge. Why is that? Because at the time of the path of no more learning, having attained the knowledge of exhaustion and the knowledge of non-arising, one comes to possess the qualities expressed thus: "Since I have known suffering, there is nothing more to know; since I have abandoned the origin, there is nothing more to abandon; since I have actualized cessation, there is nothing more to actualize; since I have cultivated the path, there is nothing more to cultivate."

This teaches that, on the paths of seeing, of meditation, and of no more learning in turn, those same nine substances are considered in relation to one another, and are not permanently different, one set from another. The statement that the nine substances are present at the time of the three paths is also made with reference to other groupings; the nine substances do not all arise together in a single instant or at a single time. Why is that? Of the feelings of pleasure, mental happiness, and equanimity, any one may arise, but it is impossible for all three to arise together at one time. How so? If one realizes the path on the level of the preparatory stage that is not incapable, the intermediate concentration, the fourth meditative concentration, or the base of infinite space, the base of infinite consciousness, or the base of nothingness, then only the feeling of equanimity is present there, and not the faculties of pleasure and mental happiness. If one is of the level of the first or second meditative concentration, then only the faculty of mental happiness is present there, and not the faculties of pleasure and equanimity. If one is of the level of the third meditative concentration, then only the faculty of pleasure is present there, and not the faculties of mental happiness and equanimity.

In the desire realm, all the other faculties are present except three: the faculty of coming to know the unknown, the faculty of knowing, and the faculty of being endowed with knowledge. In the form realm, the male and female faculties, the nose faculty, and the tongue faculty are absent, because beings there are free from sensual desire, from eating food in morsels, and from attachment. Likewise the faculty of suffering is also absent there, because the body is utterly clear and so is not harmed by external objects, and because there is no ripening of unwholesome karma. The faculty of mental unhappiness is also absent, because the mental continuum of calm abiding is always clear and acts as its antidote. In the formless realm there are eight faculties, the five faculties of faith and the rest, the mind faculty, the faculty of equanimity, and the life faculty, and no others.

Alternatively, when one is on the level of lineage, the five faculties of faith and the rest, engaged in order to realize the as yet unrealized Four Noble Truths, are the faculty called "coming to know the unknown." Why is that? Because they are applied with the thought, "I shall come to know the truths that are unknown." On the level of learning, the five faculties of faith and the rest, by which the four truths come to be understood, are called the faculty of knowing, because what is to be known has come to be known. On the level of no more learning, in order to dwell at ease in this present life, the five faculties of faith and the rest at that time are called the faculty of being endowed with knowledge, because one possesses knowledge of all that is to be known without remainder.

Alternatively, the faculties of persons who follow by faith are designated the faculty called "coming to know the unknown," because they engage in coming to know what is unknown. The faculties of persons from stream entry up to the one entering upon arhatship are designated the faculty of knowing, because they have come to know the truths. The faculties of persons who have attained the fruit of arhatship and abide on the level of no more learning are called the faculty of being endowed with knowledge, endowed with the unequaled quality of complete knowledge.

Why are these three faculties set out in this order? When the wheel of Dharma was first turned in four aspects, "This is suffering, this is the origin, this is cessation, this is the path," that is the first faculty, the faculty of coming to know the unknown. When the wheel of Dharma was turned a second time in four aspects, "Suffering is to be known, the origin is to be abandoned, cessation is to be actualized, the path is to be cultivated," there are two faculties: the faculty of being endowed with knowledge and the faculty of knowing. When the wheel of Dharma was turned a third time in four aspects, "Since suffering has been known, now it need not be known; since the origin has been abandoned, now it need not be abandoned; since cessation has been actualized, now it need not be actualized; since the path has been cultivated, now it need not be cultivated," three faculties are established: the faculty of being endowed with knowledge. Now, the time of the faculty called "coming to know the unknown" is virtuous in the beginning; the time of the faculty of knowing is virtuous in the middle; and the time of being endowed with knowledge is virtuous in the end. For this reason too they are set out in this order. When one abides in the aggregate of moral discipline, the aggregate of samādhi, and the aggregate of wisdom, it is the faculty called "coming to know the unknown." When one abides in the aggregate of liberation, it is the faculty of knowing. When one abides in the aggregate of the knowledge and vision of liberation, it is the faculty of being endowed with knowledge.

What, then, is the meaning of "faculty"? It is said: for the meaning of "faculty," the Indian language has indriya. Indriya means "powerful" or "governing"; the meaning of faculty is that which governs objects such as forms. Then which faculties govern which objects? The five, from the eye faculty up to the body faculty, are faculties because they govern four matters. Here, first of all, the eye and the ear govern four matters. The four matters are: beautifying one's own person, protecting one's own person, producing consciousness together with what accompanies it, and governing an uncommon cause. They govern the beauty of one's own person because without the eye and ear faculties a person is very unsightly, being blind, deaf, and so on. They govern the protection of one's own person because one sees places of danger with the eye and hears of them with the ear, and so avoids them altogether. They govern the production of consciousness together with what accompanies it because by the power of the eye and ear faculties, eye consciousness and ear consciousness arise together with their associated mental factors.

They govern an uncommon cause because form is seen only by the eye faculty and not by any other faculty, and sound too is heard by the ear faculty and is not heard by any other faculty.

The nose, tongue and body faculties also govern four things. They govern the beauty of one's own person because those who lack the nose faculty, the tongue faculty and the body faculty are very unsightly in form. They govern the protection of one's own person because the body is nourished by the particular qualities of morsel food eaten in mouthfuls, of odors, and of tangibles. They govern the production of consciousness together with what accompanies it because by the power of the nose, tongue and the other faculties, nose consciousness and the rest arise together with their associated mental factors. They govern an uncommon cause because odor is sensed only by the nose faculty, taste is savored only by the tongue faculty, and the tangible is felt only by the body faculty, and no other faculty is able to do this.

The four, the mind faculty, the female faculty, the male faculty and the feeling faculty, are faculties because each of them governs two things. Of these, the male and female faculties govern affliction and purification. This is so because those who lack the male and female faculties, the eunuch, the hermaphrodite and the like, do not come to be persons of non-restraint, nor do they commit the acts of immediate retribution, nor do they even cut off the roots of virtue; nor do they come to hold restraint, to attain the fruits, or to be free from attachment.

The life faculty too governs two things. It governs the continuous connection of the equal lot of sentient beings of one and the same class, each in their own kind, and it governs holding that lot without decline. For as long as the life faculty exists, the moments of the equal lot of one and the same class remain within the common class of humans, and it holds the formations of life so that they do not perish.

The mind faculty too governs two things: connection to further existence, and mastery. Connection to further existence is so because the linking of birth takes place by a mind free from attachment or by a mind with aversion. Mastery is so because it is said, "These worlds are led by the mind."

The five beginning with the suffering faculty, and the faculties from the faith faculty up to the faculty of one who has fully known, govern both affliction and purification as phenomena. How so? Of the five beginning with the suffering faculty, the two faculties of pleasure and mental pleasure make attachment lie latent. The two faculties of suffering and mental displeasure make aversion lie latent. The equanimity faculty makes delusion lie latent.

Alternatively, the five from the eye faculty up to the body faculty are faculties because they govern their own respective objects, form and the rest. The mind faculty is a faculty because it acts upon all phenomena by way of taking all phenomena as its object. The female faculty governs the female nature. The female nature is physique, voice, disposition and conduct, and the female faculty governs these. Here, physique is being slender above and broad below, together with breasts. Voice is soft and gentle. Disposition is that all women set their minds on men. Conduct is that all women have impure conduct. The male faculty governs the whole male nature. Here too the male nature is physique, disposition and conduct, and the male faculty governs these. Here, physique is being broad in the upper body. Voice is loud and deep. Disposition is that all men turn their minds to women. Conduct is that all men have pure conduct. The life faculty governs causing the equal lot of one and the same class to remain. The five beginning with the suffering faculty govern affliction; how they govern affliction is as explained above.

Some explain that these five govern purification. Thus, faith arises from the cause of suffering. From pleasure the mind becomes one-pointed. The faculties of mental pleasure and mental displeasure too arise from taking the path to nirvāṇa as one's object. Thus, when one takes the path as one's object, pleasure arises; when one has not taken the path as one's object, mental displeasure arises, with the thought, "I have not done it." When the afflictions have been abandoned and the path is spontaneously accomplished, then neither pleasure nor mental pleasure nor displeasure is apprehended.

The five faculties beginning with faith govern the side of purification. Thus, the five beginning with faith that belong to the worldly path suppress the afflictions. The five beginning with faith that belong to the four aids to penetration cause one to enter the path, because immediately after them the path of seeing arises. The faculty called "I shall come to know what is not yet known" governs the faculty of final knowledge, because after it the faculty of final knowledge arises. The faculty of final knowledge governs the faculty of one who has fully known, because after the faculty of final knowledge the faculty of one who has fully known arises. The faculty of one who has fully known governs nirvāṇa, because without the mind being fully liberated, nirvāṇa is not attained.

Alternatively, as regards abandoning the afflictions to be abandoned by seeing, it is the faculty called "I shall come to know what is not yet known" that governs, because by its power the afflictions to be abandoned by seeing are abandoned. As regards abandoning the afflictions to be abandoned by meditation, it is the faculty of final knowledge that governs, because by its strength the afflictions to be abandoned by meditation come to be absent. As regards dwelling at ease in this very life, it is the faculty of one who has fully known that governs, because at the time of being an arhat there is nothing else to be done but to experience for oneself the bliss of liberation.

If one asks why the faculties are taught as exactly twenty-two, neither more nor fewer, the reply is: the faculties are explained as just twenty-two on account of the support of the mind, conceptual distinction with regard to it, continuance, affliction, the accumulation for purification, and the phenomena of purification. Here, the support of the mind is the six, from the eye faculty up to the mind faculty. Conceptual distinction with regard to it is the two faculties, female and male, because by way of these two faculties one distinguishes, "This is a man, this is a woman." The life faculty brings about continuance. The five beginning with feeling bring about affliction. The five beginning with faith gather the accumulation for purification. The three faculties beginning with the one called "I shall come to know what is not yet known" are purification in its very nature.

Alternatively, the faculties are explained as just twenty-two, neither more nor fewer, on account of the support for engagement, its arising, its continuance and its full enjoyment, and the support for turning away, its arising, its continuance and its full enjoyment. Here, the support for engagement in saṃsāra, or for engagement with objects, is the six, from the eye faculty up to the mind faculty. What brings about arising is the two faculties, male and female, because sentient beings are born from these two. Continuance is the life faculty, since by its power one continues. Full enjoyment is the five beginning with suffering: by the faculties of suffering and mental displeasure one experiences painful feeling; by the faculties of pleasure and mental pleasure one experiences pleasant feeling; and by the equanimity faculty one experiences feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful. The cause of turning away from saṃsāra is the five faculties beginning with faith. The cause of the arising of turning away is the faculty called "I shall come to know what is not yet known," because by its power one turns away from the three realms. Continuance is the faculty of final knowledge, because at that time one abides on the path of meditation, which has the character of turning away from saṃsāra. Full enjoyment is the faculty of one who has fully known, because at that time one experiences for oneself the bliss of liberation. The twenty-two faculties have been explained.

After teaching the twenty-two faculties, the sūtra teaches the meditative concentrations. Therefore, wishing to explain the concentrations just as they are, it asks, in the passage beginning, "What are the four concentrations?" What is the meaning of the word dhyāna, concentration? Because by it one cultivates and contemplates, it is dhyāna; the meaning is "it brings about thorough knowing." In substance, the mind settled in equipoise and placed one-pointedly is concentration. If one asks how it is evident that equipoise is called thorough knowing, it is because it is said, "From a mind in equipoise one knows correctly, just as it is." Although the sense of the word dhyāna refers to the mind, it is right to take the mind itself here as wisdom, because that is how it is according to the tenets of the Buddha. What this teaches is that one-pointedness of mind on virtuous phenomena is concentration.

To teach the first concentration, the sūtra says, in the passage beginning, "Here, monks." "Here" means "in this," that is, a person abiding here in the desire realm. "Monks" is spoken as an address; "monk" means the monk who enters into the absorption of the first concentration. Now, since the first concentration is by nature freedom from attachment to the desire realm, it arises only from the cause that is freedom from attachment to desires, and not from anything else. To show this, the sūtra says, "secluded from desires." This means abandoning attachment to the five qualities of sense pleasure of the desire realm. Here the afflictions of the desire realm mentioned above, attachment and the rest, excluding anger, are expressed by the word "desires," and when these are abandoned one is secluded from desires.

"Secluded from evil, unwholesome phenomena" means having abandoned the aspects of aversion characterized by anger and the like, together with striking, killing and so forth of sentient beings that arise from that cause. Had only "unwholesome" been said here, without the word "evil," then, since the word "unwholesome" also applies to the other afflictions of the desire realm, the same thing would have been said twice. Therefore the words "evil, unwholesome" are used: aversion is called evil because it makes one's own and others' mindstreams savage, and so is gravely blameworthy.

"Accompanied by conceptual thought and accompanied by analysis" means thought and analysis; that is, it is endowed with the thought and analysis that see the phenomena of the desire realm as faulty and harmful. Thinking in a coarse and gross way is conceptual thought. Apprehending and examining in detail is analysis.

In the phrase "with joy and pleasure born of seclusion," the word "seclusion" refers to having abandoned attachment to desires and having abandoned evil, unwholesome phenomena. It is construed thus: endowed with the joy and pleasure born of that, one enters into the absorption of the first concentration and abides there. The words "born of seclusion" show that it refers to the pure concentration and the undefiled concentration. The pure is the worldly concentration in which one perceives the higher as liberation and the lower as a thorn; it is characterized by the absence of attachment to the taste of concentration and of pride. The undefiled is attained by entering by way of the Four Noble Truths and has a supramundane character. The word "joy" refers to mental pleasure. The word "pleasure" refers to the pleasure of ease. Thus it is the concentration in which the mind is workable, and the pleasure of ease that accords with it, that is called pleasure, not pleasant feeling.

Someone says: Then why is "pleasure" stated here only in general terms, without specifying, "It refers to this feeling"? In reply: if you hold that it is the pleasure of feeling, it would have to be either the pleasure of bodily feeling or the pleasure of mental feeling. Now, at the time of absorption bodily pleasure cannot reasonably be present, because in equipoise the group of the five consciousnesses is absent.

Nor does it refer to mental pleasure. Since the word "joy" is mentioned, and joy here refers to mental pleasure, mental pleasure is already explicitly present; so if one held that "pleasure" refers to mental pleasure, the words would be repetitive. Should one say that at that time both pleasure and mental pleasure are present, then the principle of the equality of substances would be violated, because two mental factors of the same substance cannot occur in a single moment. Should one say, "Although two such substances do not occur in a single moment in this way, absorption is by nature made of many moments, so joy comes first and pleasure arises afterward," then it would follow that there is no teaching of five limbs in a single moment of the first concentration, and so that is not reasonable. Therefore the word "joy" refers to mental pleasure, and the word "pleasure" refers to the pleasure of ease. "Having entered into the absorption of the first concentration, one abides" means having settled the mind one-pointedly in equipoise in the first concentration.

To teach the second concentration, the sūtra says, in the passage beginning, "With the stilling of conceptual thought and analysis, he..." The word "he" refers to the yogin; it means "the yogin." "With the stilling of conceptual thought and analysis" means being free of those two. When one has entered the absorption of the second concentration, one is free of the agitation of conceptual thought and analysis, and so it is called "having become unified through inner clarity of mind." The word "inner" is used because these states do not take an external object, but look within the mind. "Clarity" means free of the stain of the agitation of conceptual thought and analysis that is present in the first concentration. Alternatively, the sense of "clarity" refers to faith: because the yogin of the second concentration develops confidence that his attainment of the second concentration also comes about in dependence on the level of equipoise of the first concentration, it is therefore called inner clarity. "Having become unified" is construed with "mind": it means the mind has become one-pointed, or the mind has become gathered on a single object. Because all the concentrations from the second concentration upward and from the Peak of Existence downward are held to be free of conceptual thought and analysis, the sūtra therefore says, "without conceptual thought, without analysis." In "joy and pleasure," joy refers to mental pleasure, and pleasure refers to the pleasure of ease, as explained above. "Having entered into the absorption of the second concentration, he abides" means he abides having settled the mind in equipoise in the second concentration.

To teach the third concentration, the sūtra says, in the passage beginning, "Free of attachment to joy, he..." "He" means the person who has passed beyond the second concentration and enters into the absorption of the third concentration. Because it is through freedom from attachment to the mental pleasure of the second concentration that one enters into the absorption of the third concentration, the sūtra therefore says "free of attachment to joy," meaning free of attachment to the mental pleasure of the second concentration.

In "abiding in equanimity," there are three kinds of equanimity: equanimity of feeling, the equanimity of the immeasurables, and equanimity of formations. Of these, equanimity of feeling is feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful. The equanimity of the immeasurables is characterized by neither attachment nor anger arising toward sentient beings. Equanimity of formations is characterized by engaging the object to be focused on spontaneously, without exertion or effort. Here it is held to refer to equanimity of formations. How so? Because in the third concentration one is endowed with engagement with the object to be focused on that is spontaneous, without exertion or effort, it is said, "abiding in equanimity."

In "mindful and vigilant, he experiences pleasure with the body," mindfulness is not forgetting the sign of being endowed with equanimity. Vigilance refers to wisdom: it is knowing without error the individual characteristics of mindfulness and the other factors that belong to the limbs of the third concentration. In "he experiences pleasure with the body," the word "body" is here intended to mean the mental body. Since in those states the group of the five consciousnesses is absent, the meaning is that he experiences with the mind the pleasure of the third concentration.

To give an illustration of experiencing pleasure with the mind in the third concentration, the sūtra says, in the passage beginning, "that of which the noble ones say, 'Equanimous, mindful, he abides in pleasure.'" Because they have gone far from the mass of afflictions, or from engaging in evil, unwholesome phenomena, they are noble ones, that is, the Tathāgatas, the śrāvakas and the rest. What those noble ones declare, "In equanimity, mindful, he abides in pleasure," is this very third concentration. It is construed thus: in that third concentration which they declared, one settles the mind one-pointedly, and having settled it as one, one abides.

To teach the fourth concentration, the sūtra says, in the passage beginning, "Having abandoned pleasure and abandoned suffering too, he..." Pleasure is characterized by bodily ease. Suffering too is characterized by tormenting this very body. Since these are absent in the fourth concentration, it says "abandoned." Regarding "with the earlier disappearance of mental pleasure and mental displeasure": since with the very attainment of the first concentration the mind has abandoned both delight and torment, it speaks of mental pleasure and mental displeasure as having gone "earlier." Of these, when the second concentration is attained, pleasure is abandoned. When the third concentration is attained, mental displeasure is abandoned. When the fourth concentration is attained, there is no longer even the feeling of mental pleasure.

"Without suffering and without pleasure" is to be taken as meaning endowed with feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful, because when the fourth concentration is attained there is no feeling of pleasure, suffering and the like. Since, moreover, an absorption without any feeling at all is impossible, it is right to take it as meaning that it is endowed with equanimity. The word "pure" mentioned at the end applies to both: purity of equanimity and purity of mindfulness. The word "equanimity" in this context refers to equanimity of formations. The word "mindfulness," too, refers to not forgetting the phenomena that belong to the limbs of the fourth concentration.

Having become free of the eight stains, the faults of the lower levels, it is called purity of equanimity and purity of mindfulness. The eight stains are conceptual thought, analysis, exhalation, inhalation, pleasure, suffering, mental pleasure and mental displeasure: these are the eight. "Having entered into the absorption of the fourth concentration, he abides" means he abides having settled the mind one-pointedly in the fourth concentration.

Objection: since in these four concentrations there are also other factors, faith, diligence and the rest, why are those not explained as limbs of concentration, while conceptual thought, analysis and the rest are explained as limbs? The reply: because with just these the antidote limbs, the benefit limbs and, by that very fact, the basis limbs are understood to be complete, conceptual thought, analysis and the rest alone are explained as limbs; although others are present, they are not set down as limbs.

Here, in the first concentration, conceptual thought and analysis are the antidote limbs, because by conceiving and analyzing the phenomena of the desire realm as a thorn, a boil and the like, they become the antidote to desire, malice and anger, and dispel them. By "joy and pleasure born of seclusion" the benefit limbs are taught, because when desire, anger and the like have been abandoned through conceptual thought and analysis, one experiences for oneself the joy and pleasure born of having abandoned them. By "having entered into the absorption of the first concentration, he abides" the limb that is the basis of both is taught, because by the power of abiding in one-pointedness of mind, the conceptual thought and analysis that refute desire come into play, and one experiences for oneself the joy and pleasure of being secluded from it.

In the second concentration, "having become unified through inner clarity of mind" is the antidote limb, because through inner clarity and the mind having become one-pointed, the conceptual thought and analysis present in the first concentration, which have become the opposing side for the second concentration, are dispelled. Because, once they are cleared away in this way, there is no conceptual thought or analysis in the second concentration, it is called "without conceptual thought, without analysis." The joy and pleasure born of samādhi are the benefit limbs, because when conceptual thought and analysis have been cleared away by inner clarity and having become one-pointed, one experiences for oneself joy and pleasure from a samādhi free of conceptual thought and analysis. By "having entered into the absorption of the second concentration, he abides" the limb that is the basis of both is taught, because in dependence on one-pointedness of mind one becomes inwardly clear and unified, and one experiences for oneself the joy and pleasure born of samādhi.

In the third concentration, the three, abiding in equanimity, mindfulness and vigilance, are the antidote limbs, because they dispel the joy and pleasure of the second concentration. Experiencing pleasure with the body is the benefit limb; that mental pleasure is present there is taught by illustration in the passage beginning, "that of which the noble ones say." By "having entered into the absorption of the third concentration, he abides" the limb that is the basis of both is taught, because having settled in one-pointedness of mind, one engages in equanimity, mindfulness and vigilance, and experiences pleasure with the body.

In the fourth concentration, purity of equanimity and purity of mindfulness are the antidote limbs, because they dispel pleasure, suffering and the like. Feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful is the benefit limb. Having entered into the absorption of the fourth concentration and abiding there is the limb that is the basis of both, because by the power of one-pointedness of mind, equanimity and mindfulness are purified and one comes to experience feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful.

Now, from the first concentration up to the base of neither perception nor non-perception, one cultivates seven kinds of attention. What are the seven kinds of attention? The attention that discerns characteristics, the attention of resolve, the attention of thorough seclusion, the attention that gathers delight, the attention of destruction, the attention that is the culmination of application, and the attention that is the fruit of the culmination of application. Here, when entering into the absorption of the first concentration, the yogin, by means of the seven kinds of attention, attains freedom from attachment to desires. The seven attentions are the attention that discerns characteristics and the rest.

The attention that discerns characteristics is so called because by it one discerns the desires of the desire realm as having the characteristic of coarseness, and discerns the first concentration as having the characteristic of peace. How does one discern the characteristic of coarseness? One examines and investigates the desires of the desire realm under six headings. The six are meaning, thing, characteristic, side, time and understanding. Here one investigates and examines more and more for the sake of understanding. How? Investigating thus, "These desires are accompanied by danger; they bring much harm, much sickness, much unease," is investigating for the sake of understanding.

Next, investigating the thing: the thing that is desire is of two kinds, inner and outer. Investigating the inner thing that is desire is this: when attachment to one's own body and a mind of attachment to desires arise, one notes, "Attachment to the body and a mind of attachment to desires have arisen." Investigating the outer thing is this: when the mind has gone out toward the qualities of sense pleasure, forms, sounds and the rest, one investigates, "The mind has strayed toward these."

The characteristic too is of two kinds: investigating the specific characteristic and investigating the general characteristic. Investigating the specific characteristic: desire has two specific characteristics, desire whose characteristic is affliction and desire whose characteristic is the thing. Desire whose characteristic is affliction is the arising of a mind of attachment. Desire whose characteristic is the thing is the outer objects, women and the like. These two kinds of desire, moreover, become the basis of pleasant feeling, become the basis of suffering, and become the basis of what is neither pleasant nor painful.

When they become the basis of pleasant feeling, they become the support for the arising of attachment, and the support for the occurrence of the inverted perception that takes what is not pleasant to be pleasant. When they have become the basis of painful feeling, they become the ground for the arising of aversion, and the ground for the occurrence of anger and quarrels. When they become the support for the arising of feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful, they become the ground for the arising of bewilderment, deceit, pretense, shamelessness and lack of conscience. Investigating in this way, "From these desires afflictions constantly follow," is investigating the specific characteristic.

How does one investigate the general characteristic? Investigating thus: "All these desires are constantly accompanied by and bound up with the suffering of birth, the suffering of aging, the suffering of sickness, and so on up to the suffering of not getting what one desires; and even those who abide in the enjoyment of desires and possess the perfection of desires are not separated from the nature of birth and the rest, and their company with them lasts only a moment," is investigating the general characteristic.

How does one investigate the side? There are two sides, the white side and the black side, and of these, this mass of desires belongs to the black side. They are like embers, comparable to a most virulent poison, like a dream, like ornaments borrowed for a moment, comparable to the ripe fruit on a tree. Even at the time of seeking those desires, sentient beings experience for themselves the suffering of feeling, and even when they have obtained them, they come to experience the suffering of guarding them, the suffering of separation from them, the suffering of having no control, and so on. Likewise the Blessed One has said that relying on these desires there are five kinds of danger: "These desires have little taste of pleasure and much suffering, and are accompanied by many dangers; even when one relies on them there is no satisfaction, nor any limit to the best of them." In this way, the Buddha and the śrāvakas of the Buddha who have rightly entered, holy beings, have censured them in many ways.

How does one investigate time? Investigating thus: "These attachments to desires, in past, future and present times, always and forever, constantly and perpetually, these desires bring much injury, much suffering, much harm," is investigating time.

How does one investigate by reasoning? These desires have to be achieved through great exertion, great effort, fatigue and exhaustion of many kinds, and crafts of all sorts. Yet what is thus accumulated and striven for serves only as an antidote, to remove mere suffering from one's own body, a coarse thing with form, made of the four great elements, which always needs massaging, washing and nourishing, which is subject to danger, and whose nature is to disperse. To remove the suffering of hunger there is food. To remove the suffering of cold and heat, and of modesty and shame, there is clothing. To remove the suffering of sleep and fatigue there is bedding. To remove the suffering of walking and going there are vehicles. To remove the suffering of sickness there is medicine for the sick. Since these objects of desire are nothing more than an antidote to suffering, one should not be attached to them or cling to them. Rather, one should treat them in the way that sick people afflicted by illness rely on medicine to calm the illness. Investigating thus is investigating by reasoning. In this way, by investigating these objects of desire under six headings, one discerns them as having the characteristic of coarseness, and discerns the first concentration as having the characteristic of peace. This attention should be understood to be mixed with the wisdom of hearing and of reflection.

After that, having gone beyond the wisdom of hearing and reflection, in a solely meditative manner, while cultivating calm abiding and insight that take the desires of the desire realm as coarse and the first concentration as having the characteristic of peace, one resolves again and again on the coarseness and the peace just as they were investigated. This is called the attention of resolve.

Through constant familiarity with this, an attention endowed with the path of abandonment arises for the very first time. Because by it one abandons the great degree of affliction, it is called the attention of thorough seclusion.

Having become free of the afflictions that are its opposing side, that yogin, after that, develops delight in abandonment and an attitude that praises abandonment, and is endowed with a small share of the joy and pleasure born of seclusion. From time to time, by an attention that inspires the mind, whether on the fruits of concentration or on any of the qualities of the Tathāgata and the like, he uplifts himself and arouses enthusiasm. This, done in order to dispel the obscurations of dullness and agitation, is called the attention that gathers delight.

When he has thus rightly engaged, being rightly guided by the roots of virtue, the entanglements of the afflictions of the desire realm do not actually arise, whether he is moving or staying still. At that time he examines it thus: "Is attachment to desires present in me, and do I feel it arising or not arising? Or is it that, being absent, it does not arise, and I do not feel it arising?" And further he becomes deeply confident. This is called attending to the pure signs of virtue. It is called the attention of destruction.

When he has thus destroyed and searched out each side of the antidote and meditated, then for a certain period he is free of all the afflictions of the desire realm, and the antidote attention that is the culmination of the application of the first concentration arises. This is called the attention that is the culmination of application. After that the fundamental first concentration is attained; that is the attention that is the fruit of the culmination of application.

Here, by the attention that discerns characteristics, having correctly understood what is to be abandoned and what is to be attained, one firmly directs the mind toward abandoning what is to be abandoned and attaining what is to be attained. By the attention of resolve one then strives in correct application. By the attention of thorough seclusion one abandons the great degree of affliction.

By the attention that gathers joy, one abandons the middling grade of afflictions. By the attention of examination, one keeps the mind free of pride even with regard to attention to what has been attained. By the attention of the culmination of application, one abandons the weak grade of afflictions. By the attention that is the fruit of the culmination of application, one experiences the fruit of what has been well cultivated. Just as there are seven attentions for the first dhyāna, so too, up to the sphere of nothingness, each level should be understood, as appropriate, to possess seven attentions. Since a full explanation would make the text too long, I have not explained this in detail.

Here, all the lower levels are coarse in two respects. The two are that they abide with the characteristic of not being peaceful, and that their life span is short.

Since the four abodes of Brahmā (brahmavihāras) are taught after the dhyānas, he wished to explain them just as they were taught, and so said, "What are the four abodes of Brahmā?" and so on. "Here" means "here in the desire realm." "Monks" means that he spoke having addressed them, as before. As for "monk," the yogin who cultivates the four immeasurables is a monk. Here "brahma" is nirvāṇa, and the abode that brings about its attainment is the abode of Brahmā. Alternatively, "brahma" is what does not engage in any evil and abandons it, and the four qualities that do not engage in evil are the four abodes of Brahmā.

In "a mind endowed with loving-kindness," although samādhi is not mentioned, the meaning is the samādhi of loving-kindness; it is equivalent to "a mind endowed with the samādhi of loving-kindness." What is it like? To show its distinctive features he said "without enmity" and so on.

Because it is endowed with a mind intent on the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings, making no distinction between friend and enemy, it is without enmity. Establishing beings on the side of virtue and causing them to cultivate the noble path is the mind of benefit, and because it is endowed with such an intention, it is without rivalry. In effect, this means that one thinks of benefiting all beings, without any rivalry between one and another such as there is between senior and junior wives. To think of beings reaching the happy destinies of gods and humans and reaching nirvāṇa is the intention of happiness, and because it is endowed with that intention, it is without harm. Alternatively, because there is no thought of harming even an enemy, it is without harm. At the stage of the beginner, because one pervades a single direction with ever-increasing loving-kindness, it is vast. When loving-kindness has been perfected, because one pervades all ten directions, it has become great. Because the mind is equal toward all, with loving-kindness toward friends and no aversion toward enemies, it is nondual. Because it takes as its object the immeasurable beings of the desire realm, it is immeasurable. Because one has abandoned aversion, the factor opposed to loving-kindness, and cultivated that loving-kindness unmixed with aversion, it is well cultivated.

In "having resolved upon a single direction and pervaded it," a single direction means that at the very outset one focuses on one direction, whether east or west or any direction at all. Here it is taught that, like a mountain going up in flames, one takes as object, by way of the container world, the beings who inhabit that container world. "Resolved" refers to the attention of resolve. Since it takes as object the beings of the desire realm, and does not focus on the thought "all the beings of the three realms are pervaded by suffering," it is not attention to reality. "Pervaded" means "has filled a single direction with loving-kindness." "Having accomplished it, abides" means that one abides having accomplished the mind of loving-kindness. To show that, just as one abides in a single direction, one likewise takes as object the second direction and the rest, he said "likewise the second" and so on. "Above" is the abode of the gods of the desire realm and so on. "Below" is the hell beings and so on. "Intermediate" refers to the beings who dwell in the intermediate directions between the four directions.

Just as the nature of loving-kindness and the characteristics of its cultivation were taught, so, in order to teach compassion and the rest in the same manner, he said "likewise endowed with compassion" and so on; their meaning too should be seen as already explained.
