Some sūtras speak of "the four immeasurables"; the meaning there is that they are called immeasurable because they take immeasurable beings as their object.

Then why are the abodes of Brahmā not taught as five or three or some other number, but only as four? Reply: they are taught as only four because they are the antidotes to four kinds of afflictions and secondary afflictions. The four kinds of afflictions and secondary afflictions are harmfulness, cruelty, discontent, and attachment together with malice. For beings who have much malice, loving-kindness becomes the antidote by which to abandon it. For those who have much cruelty, such as beating and abusing others, compassion becomes the antidote by which to abandon it. For those who have much discontent, joy becomes the antidote by which to abandon it. For those who have much attachment and malice, equanimity becomes the antidote by which to abandon them.

Then, if meditation on the unattractive is also an antidote to attachment, what difference is there between the unattractive and equanimity as antidotes? Reply: meditation on the unattractive is the antidote to the attachment of unchaste conduct, while equanimity is the antidote to the attachment that clings to father, mother, and relatives. So there is a difference.

This shows the characteristics of loving-kindness and the rest. The attention that thinks, "Ah, may these beings be endowed with happiness!" is loving-kindness. The attention that thinks, "Oh, these beings are suffering; may they be free of suffering!" is compassion. The attention that thinks, "May the beings who are endowed with happiness always be happy and joyful!" is joy. Neither generating loving-kindness toward friends nor generating aversion toward enemies, but simply holding the mind evenly, is equanimity.

Then how do beginners first cultivate loving-kindness and the rest? Whether one is oneself endowed with happiness, or even if one has no happiness, when one sees or hears that the buddhas, bodhisattvas, and śrāvakas have attained happiness, one resolves, "May all beings too be endowed with happiness."

If, because of the great strength of the afflictions, that person is unable to resolve that all beings be endowed with the happiness of himself and of the buddhas, having strong aversion toward enemies and strong affection toward friends, he should divide even the class of friends into three kinds: the closest, the middling, and the least. Having made this division, he should first resolve, "May my closest friend be endowed with that happiness." Then, when that is accomplished, he does the same toward the middling friend. Then, when that is accomplished, toward the least friend. Then, when he has accomplished focusing on the three classes of friends with complete equality, he should focus on ordinary people in general. Then, when that is accomplished, he divides enemies into three kinds, the lesser, the middling, and the greatest, and should first resolve toward the lesser, "May he be endowed with happiness." Then toward the middling enemy. Then toward the greatest enemy. Then, when through loving-kindness he has become equal toward both sides, the best of friends and the greatest enemy, wishing "May they be happy," he next focuses step by step, in due order, on his own circle. Then on the town. Then on the realm around the king's palace. Then, gradually making it larger and larger, he should pervade all ten directions with loving-kindness.

Whoever does not disregard even the slight good qualities found in other persons, all of whom have some good qualities, but regards them as endowed with good qualities, will swiftly attain the samādhi of loving-kindness. To state it both ways: when one sets out to recognize good qualities, one can recognize good qualities even in someone whose roots of virtue are severed. Though he remains with his roots of virtue severed, one sees the fruit of his former merit and thinks, for example, "Because you performed meritorious deeds, you now have a beautiful form." One can also recognize faults even in someone who has good qualities. For example, though in someone like a pratyekabuddha not the slightest fault arises through the three doors, one sees the fruit of former demerit and thinks, "Because he formerly performed some slight unmeritorious deed, he now has an unattractive form."

The same should be applied to compassion and the rest, resolving, "These beings, sunk in the mire of manifold attachment, are possessed of every kind of suffering; may they be free of suffering."

Some say that it is the abode of Brahmā because it is an abode that endows one with a great accumulation of merit, since by doing this one's merit increases.

Alternatively, because one has a mind of benefit and a mind of happiness toward beings, it is called "a mind endowed with loving-kindness." Because that loving-kindness is the antidote to three kinds of affliction, it is called without enmity and without rivalry. The three kinds of affliction are aversion toward beings who are equal or superior to oneself in family, good qualities, and the like and who do harm; jealousy toward those who do no harm; and aversion toward inferior beings in both cases. Here, because it is the antidote to aversion toward beings who are equal or superior to oneself in family, good qualities, and the like and who do harm, it is called without enmity. Because it is the antidote to jealousy toward beings who are equal or superior in family, good qualities, and the like and who do no harm, it is without rivalry. Because it is the antidote to aversion toward beings who are inferior to oneself in family, good qualities, and the like, both those who do harm and those who do not, it is without harm. Because cultivating that loving-kindness brings attainment of its great fruit, it is vast. The mind of loving-kindness belongs to the level at which the dhyānas of the form realm are attained, and it arises in a mind-stream that has attained the form realm; therefore it has become great. Some explain that it has become great because it belongs to the level of the formless realm. Because it makes no distinction between enemy and friend, it is nondual. Because it takes as its object the immeasurable beings of the desire realm, it is immeasurable. Because it is utterly victorious over its opposing factor, it is well cultivated; the factor opposed to loving-kindness is aversion.

Thus these words fully teach the antidote, the fruit, the level, the object, and the completely purified mind. The three words "without enmity," "without rivalry," and "without harm" teach it together with its antidotal function. "Vast" teaches the fruit. "Become great" teaches the level. "Nondual" and "immeasurable" teach the object. "Well cultivated" teaches its complete purity.

As for "having resolved upon a single direction and pervaded it, having accomplished it, he abides," and so on: just as, when the grass and trees on a mountain are burning, one says "the mountain is burning," indicating what dwells in a place by the place itself, so here it teaches that one takes as object the beings dwelling in a single direction. "Resolved" teaches the attention of resolve, and not attention to reality, as before. "Pervaded" means that one focuses pervasively, at one time, on all the beings to be focused on. "Having accomplished" means having actualized the four root samādhis, or having actualized the samādhis of loving-kindness and the rest themselves. The word "abides" means that one does not abide for a single instant only, but continues in it without interruption thereafter.

Alternatively, the four abodes of Brahmā are, in order, the characteristic of bringing about happiness, the characteristic of setting about removing suffering, the characteristic of rejoicing that beings are not separated from happiness, and equanimity toward all three. Here, the characteristic of bringing about happiness is loving-kindness. The characteristic of setting about removing suffering is compassion. The characteristic of experiencing joy at beings' not being separated from happiness is joy. The characteristic of not engaging in the manner of all three is equanimity.

And to teach loving-kindness first among the four immeasurables, he said, "Here, monks, a monk with a mind endowed with loving-kindness," and so on. Here, because that loving-kindness abides in the mode of helping beings, it is called "the abode endowed with loving-kindness." The characteristic of helping is of two kinds, bringing about benefit and bringing about happiness, and its meaning is as explained before.

Because it is free of the faults of mind, it is without enmity. Because no one harms him and he harms no one, it is without rivalry. Because he performs no harmful actions, it is without harm. Because the object to be focused on is extensive, it is vast. Because it chiefly brings about benefit and happiness, it becomes great; the meaning of "become great" is that it chiefly brings these about. Because there is neither attachment nor aversion, it is nondual. Because its fruit arises beyond measure, it is immeasurable. Because it is utterly victorious over its opposing factor, it is well cultivated. "Having resolved upon a single direction and pervaded it" means that, being free of aversion, one should pervade a single direction with a resolve that arises from the intention of benefit and happiness toward beings. "Having accomplished it, he abides" means that he abides having made his loving-kindness pure, or complete. The rest should be seen as explained before.

Since the four paths are taught after the abodes of Brahmā, wishing to explain them just as they were taught, he opened in the form of a question and said, "What are the four paths?" and so on. It is a path because it brings about the attainment of nirvāṇa; the meaning is that it accomplishes the state of nirvāṇa. Of these, "There is the painful path with sluggish direct knowledge" is the first path. "There is the painful path with quick direct knowledge" is the second path. "There is the pleasant path with sluggish direct knowledge" is the third path. "There is the pleasant path with quick direct knowledge" is the fourth path.

Of these, the not-unable preparatory stage (anāgamya), the intermediate dhyāna, and the formless attainments are established as the painful path with sluggish direct knowledge and the painful path with quick direct knowledge, because in them calm abiding or insight, respectively, is weak. When those of dull faculties enter absorption in the not-unable stage, the intermediate dhyāna, or the formless attainments, it is called the painful path with sluggish direct knowledge. When those of sharp faculties enter absorption in the not-unable stage, the intermediate dhyāna, or the formless attainments, it is called the painful path with quick direct knowledge. Entering absorption in the four dhyānas of the form realm is established as the pleasant path with sluggish direct knowledge and the pleasant path with quick direct knowledge. When persons of dull faculties enter absorption in the four dhyānas of the form realm, it is called the pleasant path with sluggish direct knowledge. When persons of sharp faculties enter absorption in the four dhyānas of the form realm, it is called the pleasant path with quick direct knowledge.

To teach the first path in detail, he said, "Of these, what is the painful path with sluggish direct knowledge?" and so on. "Here" is equivalent to "here in the desire realm." "Someone" means some man, or some person. "By nature" means by his essential nature. In "of intense attachment," the word "intense" refers to strong attachment: to be excessively attached even to a small desirable thing, and to give rise to a mind of attachment again and again, is intense attachment. Intense aversion is strong aversion: to be agitated by even a slight harm or wrong done, to be full of malice, and to have a mind of aversion arise in one's mind again and again without interruption, is intense aversion. Intense delusion is strong delusion: because delusion is strong, one's grasp of meaning is slight, and to be unable to understand even what is not beyond understanding is intense delusion.

"He" means that person in whom the three afflictions are strong. "Again and again, through intense attachment" means that the attachment of strong attachment arises again and again. "He experiences suffering and mental unhappiness born of attachment" means that he experiences suffering born of the cause, strong attachment, and experiences unhappiness of mind. The same should be applied to aversion and delusion.

"He is sluggish in the five supramundane faculties" means that the person in whom attachment, aversion, and delusion are strong is sluggish in the five noble, undefiled faculties, faith and the rest; that is, those five faculties have little potency, little strength, and little vigor. "Supramundane faculty" is equivalent to "undefiled faculty." If one asks for attaining what they have little potency, he therefore says "for the exhaustion of the defilements": the meaning is that they have little potency for attaining the exhaustion of the defilements. To teach the meaning of little potency, he said, "weak and not quickly attaining." Because even at the time of first entering he is unable to enter quickly, they are weak. Because even at the time of meditation he is unable to attain the fruit, they do not quickly attain. To teach what the five supramundane faculties are, he said, "namely, the faculty of faith," and so on. The characteristics of the five faculties are taught in detail in the section on the thirty-seven factors of awakening.

"He is weak in those supramundane faculties" means this: because his five supramundane faculties have little potency, the person in whom the three afflictions are strong, in order to exhaust the afflictions and the defiled phenomena, enters absorption in the samādhi of the immediate path that abandons the afflictions; and being unable to enter samādhi quickly is called the painful path with sluggish direct knowledge. In effect, because the three afflictions are strong, the five supramundane faculties have little potency, and the faculties are dull. When, with those faculties, one enters absorption and rests in equipoise in the not-unable stage, the intermediate dhyāna, or the formless attainments, that is the painful path with sluggish direct knowledge. Why is that? Because in the not-unable stage and the intermediate dhyāna the limbs are not all gathered, and since calm abiding and insight are weak one must accomplish it with strain; and in the formless realm, too, the limbs are not gathered, and since insight is weak one must accomplish it with strain. Therefore it is painful.

Because a person of dull faculties is unable to attain the fruit quickly, it is sluggish. In effect, because the direct knowledge of his path is sluggish and slight, it is a sluggish path; the meaning is that his wisdom is slight. Since attachment and aversion are mutually incompatible factors and do not occur together, it should be understood that the two occur at different points in the mind-stream: at times attachment arises, at times aversion arises, at times delusion arises.

To teach the second path, he said, "Of these, what is the painful path with quick direct knowledge?" and so on; the other points of meaning are the same as explained before. "Quick direct knowledge" means that one who quickly comes to know the immediately following samādhi has quick direct knowledge; the meaning is that his wisdom is sharp. To show the very meaning of sharp wisdom, he said, "strong, sharp, and quickly attaining." Because it is sharp, it is strong; because it attains quickly, it is sharp. The latter should be seen as explaining the former. Alternatively, there are three paths: the path conducive to penetration, the path of seeing, and the path of meditation. Because one makes the five faculties enter quickly into the path conducive to penetration, they are strong. Because one makes them enter quickly into the path of seeing, they are sharp. Because one makes them enter quickly into the path of meditation, they attain quickly. In effect, when a person in whom the three afflictions are strong, but whose faculties are sharp, rests in equipoise in the absorptions of the not-unable stage, the intermediate dhyāna, or the formless attainments, it is called the painful path with quick direct knowledge. Because in these the limbs are not gathered, and calm abiding and insight are weak, one must accomplish it with strain; therefore it is a painful path. Because a person of sharp wisdom quickly attains the immediately following samādhi, it is quick direct knowledge.

To teach the third path, he said, "Of these, what is the pleasant path with sluggish direct knowledge?" and so on. "Slight attachment" and the rest should be understood as the opposite of "strong attachment" and the rest. In effect, when a person in whom the three afflictions are weak, but whose five supramundane faculties are dull and of little potency, cultivates the first, second, third, and fourth dhyānas of the form realm, it is called the pleasant path with sluggish direct knowledge. Why is that? Because in those dhyānas of the form realm the limbs are gathered, and calm abiding and insight proceed evenly yoked together, one need not accomplish it with strain; therefore it is a pleasant path. Because a person of dull faculties is unable to enter quickly into the immediately following samādhi, it is called sluggish direct knowledge.

To teach the fourth path, he said, "Of these, what is the pleasant path with quick direct knowledge?" and so on; the other word meanings should be seen as explained before. In effect, it teaches this: when a person in whom the three afflictions are weak, and whose five supramundane faculties are powerful and sharp, cultivates the four dhyānas of the form realm, it is called the pleasant path with quick direct knowledge. Why is that? Because in them the limbs are gathered, and calm abiding and insight proceed evenly yoked together, one need not accomplish it with strain; therefore it is a pleasant path. Because a person of sharp faculties quickly actualizes the immediately following samādhi, it is quick direct knowledge.

Since the four cultivations of samādhi are taught after the paths, wishing to explain them just as they were taught, he said, "Monks, what are the four cultivations of samādhi?" and so on. One-pointedness of mind is samādhi, and to familiarize oneself with it again and again is the cultivation of samādhi. This is of three kinds: cultivation as practice, cultivation as close practice, and making much of it. Cultivation by attainment is called practice: the samādhi has not yet become manifest, but merely the acquisition that will make it manifest, or merely its cause, has arisen. Cultivation by manifestation is cultivation as antidote: the samādhi itself has been made directly present. Putting the opposing factors far away is making much of it.

Alternatively, at the time of the path of preparation, that is, the practice through resolve, it is cultivation as practice. At the time of the immediate path, or the path of abandonment, it is cultivation as close practice. At the time of the path of liberation, it is making much of it. Alternatively, it is set out in four words: practice, close practice, cultivation, and making much of it.

The antidotes that abandon the afflictions are likewise four: the antidote of repudiation, the antidote of thorough abandonment, the antidote of support, and the antidote of distancing. Of these, the antidote of repudiation is repudiating desires or formations in such aspects as a disease or a boil; this first repudiation of formations is practice. The antidote of thorough abandonment is the preparatory path and the immediate path, since by them one thoroughly abandons the afflictions; at the time of those two paths it is called close practice. The antidote of support is the path of liberation, and at that time it is called cultivation. The antidote of distancing is the path from then on, in which the earlier afflictions have been abandoned and the later ones never arise in the mind-stream; at that time it is making much of it. Alternatively, at the stage of an ordinary being it is practice. The path of practice through resolve is close practice. The path of seeing is cultivation. The path of meditation is making much of it.

Why does one cultivate it? He said, "it leads to abandoning attachment to sense desires." This is equivalent to saying that there is a cultivation whose purpose is to abandon the attachment of the desire realm. That is the first cultivation of samādhi.

In "there is also one that leads to dwelling at ease in this visible life," the word "visible" refers to what is directly perceived, and the word "phenomenon" here refers to life; in effect, it means "in a directly perceived life," or "in this life." Dwelling at ease is to be free of disturbance in this life and to dwell in peace. One cultivates it for the sake of dwelling at ease in this visible life, not for the sake of abandoning attachment to sense desires; that is the second cultivation of samādhi.

In "there is one that leads to the full attainment of knowledge and vision," this means that it leads to the full attainment of knowledge and to the full attainment of vision. Here, knowledge refers to the wisdom associated with mental consciousness; that is, the conceptual wisdom that knows, "This being is endowed with bad conduct of body; this being is endowed with bad conduct of speech," and so on, and "This being is endowed with good conduct of body," and so on. Vision refers to the nonconceptual wisdom associated with eye consciousness; it refers to the divine eye, which is endowed with the seeing of forms of every variety and of the many kinds of conduct of minds. Cultivating for the sake of attaining that is the third cultivation of samādhi.

In "there is also one that leads to the full attainment of wisdom," to attain a special wisdom is the full attainment of wisdom: one attains the wisdom that knows the Four Noble Truths, or the wisdom that knows the specific and general characteristics of phenomena. The cultivation for the sake of attaining that is the fourth cultivation of samādhi.

With reference to the first cultivation of samādhi, the passage beginning "Here, monks" is spoken. If one asks how one cultivates the abandoning of attachment to sense desires, it is for this reason that the passage beginning "this very body, from the soles of the feet upward to the hair" is spoken. "This body" means one's own body or another's body; it is to be construed with "one examines it with wisdom, correctly, just as it is." How does one examine it? One examines it beginning from the soles of the feet upward as far as the hair and the head, or beginning from the hair and the head downward as far as the soles of the feet. "Bounded by the skin" means that one examines it beginning from the outer hair and skin as far as the inner flesh, blood and bones. "As it is situated" means that one examines how this body, even at the present time, abides with the character of being full of impurities of many kinds. "As it is disposed" means that one examines how this body was formerly projected by virtuous and nonvirtuous karma, and how, having entered the womb, it came about from the impure substances of the father and mother.

To make that very meaning clear, the passage beginning "full of impurities of many kinds" is spoken: one examines how this body, even now, from the head to the soles of the feet, abides full of impurities of many kinds such as blood and lymph, and how formerly too it arose from the blood and semen of the father and mother. "With correct wisdom" means with a wisdom that is conceptual and in accord with the undefiled; such a conceptual wisdom is itself defiled. "One examines thoroughly, correctly, just as it is" means that one examines it as impure only in a way that is unerring and without mistake.

To explain at length the cultivation of the body's impurity and disintegration, which was taught only in brief, the passage beginning "Thus this body" is spoken. "Arising in stages" means that this body first arises as the kalala, then arises as the arbuda, then arises as the peśī, and so youth, the prime of life, old age and the rest arise. "Perishing in stages" means that the kalala and the other stages perish one after another: when the kalala has perished, the arbuda perishes; when the arbuda has perished, the prime of life perishes, and so on up to the perishing of old age. "An accumulation of extremely subtle particles" means that it has come about from the gathering of the minute particles of earth, water, fire and wind. "Flimsy" means that it must be sustained by food and, like an illusory form, is nothing but a hollow appearance. "Rising and falling" means that when one breathes out the body is raised, and when one breathes in the body shrinks and so is lowered. Alternatively, it rises and falls because it must be sustained by food: when it has food it grows stout and so is high, and when it is deprived of food it grows weak and so is low.

"Oozing from the nine wound-openings and the pores" means this. The nine wound-openings are the two eyes, the two ears and the two nostrils, which make six, and with the mouth, the anus and the urethra added, nine. From these, mucus, saliva, phlegm and the like constantly ooze, and from each and every pore of the body hair grime constantly oozes.

"Like an anthill, known as the abode of venomous snakes" means that just as venomous snakes dwell in the filthy abode of an anthill, so in this filthy abode of the five aggregates the venomous snakes of the afflictions dwell. "Ungrateful like a venomous snake" means this: just as a venomous snake, though one has raised it from when it was small, nourishing it with food, will kill one in return if one gives it even the slightest offense, or even if one gives none, so too this aggregate, though one has treated it well by washing it, massaging it and so forth, harms one in return if at some point one does not wash it, massage it and so forth; hence it is ungrateful.

On "treacherous toward a friend, like Ajātaśatru and a monkey": for example, King Ajātaśatru killed his father, who was supreme in kindness; and just as a monkey, when one keeps it and looks after it, grows insolent and does harm, so too this aggregate, though it is produced by its father and mother, held dear by them and well looked after, swells with afflictions and does them harm in return; hence it is treacherous.

"Deceptive like a bad friend" means this: just as a bad friend is gentle in outward manner and speaks with pleasant, soft words, yet inwardly intends harm, so too this aggregate seems beneficial when one keeps company with it for a moment, but when one has relied on it for a long time, harm arises from it later; hence it deceives.

"Feeble by nature, like a mass of foam" means this: just as a lump of foam is by nature feeble and cannot bear being grasped or cut, so too this aggregate of form, when examined with wisdom, cannot bear being grasped and held as having any core; hence it is feeble by nature. "Arising and perishing like a bubble on water" means this: just as a bubble on water arises in a single instant and perishes immediately afterward, so too these aggregates perish as soon as they have arisen; hence they are of the nature of arising and perishing.

"Beguiling like a mirage" means this: just as a mirage, though it is not water, appears like water, so that people who want water come up to it and find there is no water, and thus it deceives others, so too these aggregates, though they are neither pleasant nor pure, appear as if pleasant and pure, and so deceive sentient beings; for this reason they are called beguiling.

"Without core when taken apart, like the trunk of a plantain" means this: just as, when one takes apart the round trunk of a plantain layer by layer of its outer sheaths, there is no core other than the outer sheaths, so too, when these aggregates are taken apart and set aside one by one, there is no essence or core whatsoever.

"Deceptive like an illusion" means this: just as an illusion deceives beings by appearing in various guises, as illusory elephants, as human elephants and as nonhuman elephants, so too, although among the five aggregates there is nothing called a self or a sentient being, they appear as if they were a self and a sentient being, and so deceive beings.

"Full of commands, like a king" means this: just as a king has many commands, so that many tasks must be carried out for him, so too many tasks must be carried out for the sake of this body; hence it is like a king with many commands.

"Watching for an opening, like an enemy" means this: just as an enemy seeks an opening in order to kill one or do one harm, so too this body at all times seeks an opening in order to destroy one's roots of virtue.

"Not to be trusted, like a thief" means this: just as one cannot trust a thief, thinking, "This one will not steal," so too one cannot trust this body, thinking, "I shall always keep company with it," because without doubt it will perish when its time comes.

"Intent on harm, like one who is not a friend" means this: one who is not a friend is one who is not a companion, whose mind and heart have become hostile. Just as one who is not a friend does not intend to benefit one, so too this body does not intend to benefit one.

"Cutting off the life of wisdom, like an executioner" means this: just as executioners who kill beings cut off the lives of beings, so too this body cuts off the life of the wisdom that comes from hearing, reflection and meditation.

"Without sustenance, like an empty village" means this: just as in an empty village there is no wealth of people, livestock or goods, so too within the five aggregates there is, in none of the aggregates, any sustenance having the character of the accumulations of merit and wisdom.

"Ending in destruction, like a potter's vessel" means this: just as a potter's vessels, his bowls and his jars, in the end break and are destroyed, so too this aggregate that has been born does not remain in the end, but destruction comes upon it.

"Full of various impurities, like a cesspit" means this: just as a receptacle and place of filth is full of many impurities such as urine and vomit, so too this body is full of various things such as impurities.

"Oozing impurity, like a vat of fermenting mash" means this: just as the liquid of the mash oozes from a vat of fermenting mash, so too impurities such as mucus and saliva ooze from this body.

"Foul-smelling, like the carcass of a snake, a human or a dog" means this: just as the rotting carcass of a snake, a human corpse and a dog's carcass smell foul, so too a foul smell constantly rises from this body.

"A source of impurity, like a latrine pit" means this: just as impurities of many kinds such as mucus and excrement come forth from it, so too this body serves as a source from which impurities of many kinds come forth.

"Unable to bear being moved, like a wound" means this: just as an open wound hurts when it is disturbed by being grasped, pressed and so forth, so too this body is harmed when it is struck by a stick or the like, however slightly.

"Tormenting, like a thorn" means this: just as when a thorn has pierced one, for as long as that thorn is not pulled out and remains with one, it constantly torments one, so too this body torments one for as long as one keeps company with it.

"Hard to serve, like an untamed master" means this: just as an unruly master is hard to please however one serves him, so too this aggregate is never pleased or satisfied, whatever things one uses to tend and serve it.

"Hard to repair, like a ruined house" means this: just as, when an old house has fallen into ruin, it is hard to restore and repair it, and though one repairs it on one side, it collapses on another, so too this body is hard to sustain constantly with food and drink, and though one sustains it for a while, if it meets with unsuitable food, it will perish at another time.

"Hard, like an unfired pot" means this: just as a potter's vessel that has not been fired is hard to protect, breaking if it is jolted even slightly, so too this body perishes through even the slightest condition; hence it is hard to protect.

"To be guarded constantly, like an unruly friend" means this: an unruly friend must be guarded constantly, for if one does not constantly guard his feelings, he becomes displeased at the slightest thing and goes far away; so too, if this aggregate is not constantly guarded, it becomes an obstacle to one's life, or one becomes parted from the body.

"Shaking and swaying, like a tree on a riverbank" means this: just as a tree on a riverbank shakes and is unstable, so that one day it will fall and be carried off by the water, so too this aggregate abides in destruction and so is unstable, and in the end it will be carried off by the river of impermanence.

"Ending in the ocean of death, like the current of a great river" means this: just as the current of a river in the end reaches the great ocean, so too this river-current of the instants of the aggregates in the end reaches the ocean of death.

"The abode of all suffering, like a makeshift lodging" means this: just as a house made as a temporary shelter is a place of suffering, since it has no bedding and no food, so too these aggregates have no happiness but are subject to birth, old age, sickness and death, and so are the abode of all suffering.

"Unowned, like a place without refuge" means this: just as a house built for travelers on a road or at a crossroads that no owner has taken charge of has no owner to repair it, so too this body has no friend to benefit it and no master to protect it.

"Open to bribes, like a jailer" means this: just as a jailer, when satisfied with a bribe, releases a prisoner, so too this body relies on the five qualities of sense desire, and when it is satisfied it lets go of the roots of virtue. Alternatively, just as a jailer is hard to satisfy with a small bribe, so too this body is hard to satisfy with only a little pleasure.

"Constantly harassed, like a village on the frontier" means this: just as a village in a frontier land has many enemies who overrun and plunder it, so too this body suffers many afflictions: its own troubles such as disease, and outer troubles such as cold and heat.

"Perishing, like a city of sand" means this: just as a house of sand is unstable and by nature perishes, so too this aggregate is by nature momentarily perishing and unstable.

"Insatiable, like fire" means this: just as fire is never satisfied, whatever things one puts into it to burn, but burns them all, so too this body, as if drinking salt water, is never satisfied in enjoying the five qualities of sense desire.

"Hard to cross, like the ocean" means this: just as the great ocean is hard to cross to its far shore because of its many dangers, such as sea monsters, so too this body, being possessed of the many venomous things of the afflictions, is hard to abandon.

"To be diligently avoided, like a basket of snakes" means this: just as those who shrink from the danger of venomous snakes keep far away from a basket of venomous snakes so as not to meet with harm from them, so too those who fear the suffering of birth, old age, sickness and death should keep far away from this aggregate.

"To be guarded constantly, like a young child" means this: just as a small child must be guarded constantly, because if it is not constantly guarded it will go into the fire, fall over a precipice or be pierced by a weapon, so too this body, if it is not guarded in such a way that the doors of the senses do not go out toward their objects, will be pierced by the weapons of the afflictions and the like.

"Always discarded, like a broken vessel" means this: just as water never stays inside a broken vessel, so too virtue never stays in these five aggregates, but leaks away. Alternatively, just as a broken vessel is always thrown away, so too the five aggregates are always cast aside by the noble and the wise.

"Constantly harassed, like a bad country" means this: just as in a bad country those who have no chief or master are oppressed, so too this body is oppressed in many ways by the enemy of disease. Alternatively, just as a bad country, lacking provisions such as food and clothing, is constantly afflicted by harm and want, so too this body is constantly afflicted by sufferings such as not getting what one wants, meeting with what is disliked and being parted from what is dear.

"To be avoided, like poisoned food" means this: just as poisoned food is to be avoided, because if one eats it many kinds of harm befall the body, so too this body is to be abandoned by the wise.

"Needed only for a moment, like a borrowed vessel" means this: just as a vessel borrowed from someone else is only had for a moment and is of no use at all other times, so too this body is needed only when one practices the Dharma, and apart from that it is of no use at all times.

That being so, this body is not to be abandoned altogether. For example, when a chariot has been made out of wood, though the pieces of wood are of no use separately, once they have been made into a chariot they are needed to carry a load. In the same way, although this body is of no use when it is taken apart piece by piece in the manner examined above, it is not to be given up for that reason, since it carries the load of practicing the Dharma. Therefore it is said, "It is to be held solely with a mind set on the Dharma." The meaning is that one should hold this body with a mind that needs it for practicing the Dharma, solely for that purpose and no more.

Wishing to teach yet another way of cultivating the abandoning of attachment to sense desires, the passage beginning "Furthermore, monks" is spoken. Its meaning is that by thoroughly taking the body apart through examining its earlier cause and its later cause, one will abandon attachment to sense desires. "This mere body" means that one should examine this body as something that, both earlier and later, has merely come about from impurity, or as being of nothing but the nature of impurity. If one asks how one examines it, it is for this reason that "one examines its earlier and later causes" is said: the meaning is that one examines how both what is the earlier cause of this body and what is its later cause have come about from nothing but impurity.

To show the earlier cause of the body, the passage beginning "the semen of the father and" is spoken: the earlier cause of this body is the father's semen and the mother's blood, and one examines how from the very beginning it came about from an impure cause. To show the later cause, the passage beginning "the digestion of food and so forth" is spoken: the later cause that makes this body grow is the digestion of the food one has eaten, and the words "and so forth" include everything the sūtra teaches about how food, once digested, moves from one place to another.

To explain what is included there, the passage beginning "as soon as morsel food has been eaten" is spoken. Food is eaten in morsels, and as soon as it has been eaten it first goes to the seat of phlegm. When it has gone to the seat of phlegm, it is softened by the phlegm, and so it turns into a fluid having the character of being mixed with phlegm, and becomes something impure by nature. Having been digested in the seat of phlegm, it then goes to the seat of bile. When it has reached the seat of bile, it is ripened and cooked, digested, and turns sour. When it has turned sour, it goes to the seat of wind. When, having been digested in the seat of phlegm, it has reached the seat of wind, the wind separates it into its parts: it separates out the dregs on one side and the fluid on the other.

From the dregs that have been separated out, urine, excrement and other impurities come about. From the fluid and the essence, blood comes about. From blood, flesh comes about. From flesh, fat comes about. From fat, bone comes about. From bone, marrow comes about. From marrow, in turn, semen and other impurities come about. Thus, since this body, both at its beginning and at its end, has come about from impure causes, the Blessed One declared to the monks, "Regard this body as impure, and reflect on it so."

Furthermore, to teach merely by way of illustration the collection of impurities, the passage beginning "This body, by nine hundred and six bones" is spoken. Just as a house is supported and held up by pillars, so too this body is supported and held up by nine hundred and six bones. The meaning of "like a house of poor timber" is this: just as a house made of poor timber has many joints where the pieces are not bound together, so too this body has many joints where its many bones are fastened together. The meaning of "by many vessels" is this: just as a house has many lattice strips stretched across it, so too in this body four hundred vessels are stretched out, each one like a lattice strip. The meaning of "thoroughly covered by five hundred muscles" is this: just as a house is covered with clay, so it is plastered and covered all over with lumps of five hundred muscles. "Wrapped by six binding sinews" means that, just as the lattice strips and other parts of a house rest on the roof beams, so the other, minor vessels rest on six great vessels. "Stretched across by seven hundred minor vessels" has the same meaning as what was taught above: seven hundred minor vessels are stretched across it like lattice strips. "Stretched across by nine hundred tendons" means that nine hundred tendons serve to hold the joints, the bones and the rest together so that they do not come apart. "With vessels for windows" means that, like the windows of a house, the hollows of the vessels are present in the body like windows. The meaning of "it is like a perforated vessel" is this: just as a perforated vessel is riddled through with holes, so too this body is always riddled with seven hundred vital points that have the character of being vulnerable to harm. The meaning of "koṭis of body hairs" and the rest is this: just as the outside of a poor house is covered with grass, so too this body is covered on the outside by eighty-four hundred thousand koṭis of body hairs.

"Full of the openings of the five senses and of filth and impurity" means that impurity oozes from the sense faculties of the eye, the ear, the nose, the tongue and the body, and that every place within the body too is full of impurity; for this reason the passage beginning "a handful of brain" is spoken. "Each filled equally with these, like granaries" means that, just as granaries are each filled with grain, so too the vessels of impurity are each filled with impurity. "Sustained by the six tastes" means that it is constantly nourished and fattened by the sweet, the sour, the bitter, the pungent, the salty and the astringent.

The meaning of "impurity by the fire of the body" and the rest is this: just as the fire of a fire offering burns the substances offered in the fire offering, so too the fire element present in the great elements constantly burns the impurities as in a fire offering. Becoming thin, weak, old and bent, it always ends in decay. When the limbs of the body are examined one by one, none of them can bear examination, and so all the limbs of the body are repulsive to look at. A stench constantly rises from the mouth, the nose and the openings of impurity, and since impurity abides within the body too, it is said to be foul-smelling and of a putrid nature. Therefore the meaning is that one does not give rise to pride, since in this wretched body there is no basis for taking pride in its being fine and good.

Summarizing in verse the meaning explained above, the Blessed One declared the passage beginning, "Thus examine the body as a heap of impurity." "Thus" means as explained above. As explained above, one examines this body as a heap full of impurities of many kinds; and since it is thus a heap of impurity, the meaning is that it is not fitting to be proud of it as attractive and good. The meaning of the passage beginning "Those who are proud and conceited about their form" is this: the childish who, though knowing that it is thus impure, take pride in this impure body, thinking it fine, and who cling to this body and do not let it go, are like a fool who carries a vessel of filth on his back while holding it to be good and clinging to it. The meaning of "full of something like pus flowing from the nose" is this: since in this body mucus like pus constantly flows from the nose, a foul smell like that of a rotting human corpse constantly comes from the mouth, and from the eyes too comes eye-matter, sweat and pus like worms, how could one feel desire for it or take pride in it? The meaning is that it is not fitting.

Intending that the wise should not merely examine what was explained above concerning impurity, but should examine with their own wisdom whatever other impurities there are, the Blessed One declared the passage beginning "Examine these and the rest." By the words "and the rest" he declared, "Examine still other things with your own wisdom." To show an illustration of this, the passage beginning "Just as, monks, a granary with two doors opened" is spoken. A granary with two doors is one in which the door where the grain is kept is opened and the door where the precious things are kept is opened; that is, the two doors are each opened. Alternatively, "with two doors opened" is merely illustrative, and it should be understood as granaries each having many doors opened. Just as some man possessed of sight, going inside granaries each with many doors opened, knows, "This granary is full of awned grain; this granary is full of the grain of fruits," so too the wise one possessed of wisdom examines this very body in detail: "Even now this body abides full of impurities of many kinds, and formerly too it arose from impure causes."

Having taught the first samādhi, to conclude it the passage beginning "Here, monks" is spoken.

An objection: Other sūtras explain that all defilements are abandoned by the vajra-like samādhi included in the fourth dhyāna, whereas here it is explained that attachment to sense desires is abandoned by cultivating impurity and finding fault with the body. How is this not in contradiction with the others?

Reply: When other sūtras explain the abandoning of all defilements, they speak with the intention that, beginning from finding fault with the body, the defilements are to be abandoned by the vajra-like samādhi included in the fourth dhyāna. Here, by contrast, it is taught that, for those whose attachment is strong, cultivating impurity and finding fault with the body, in order to abandon attachment, becomes the antidote that abandons attachment to sense desires. Since this is explained for the sake of beings who hold their own bodies and the bodies of others to be fine, there is no contradiction.

To teach the second samādhi, the passage beginning "Here, monks, the cultivation and pursuit of samādhi" is spoken. Samādhi is accomplished through two kinds of solitude: solitude of body and solitude of mind. Since one must therefore make the body solitary, the passage beginning "Here, monks, one who dwells in the forest, or at the foot of a tree" is set forth. Places such as the forest and the foot of a tree are places of bodily solitude, since there is no bustle there of children, women and the like. To teach solitude of mind, the passage beginning "this very body of one's own" is spoken; one's own inner body refers to the mental body. "Born of solitude" means born of samādhi; that is, born from having abandoned conceptual thought about sense desires and evil, unwholesome phenomena. If one asks what is born, it is for this reason that "joy and pleasure" is spoken. Joy is mental happiness; pleasure is the pleasure of pliancy; these are to be construed in order. "Suffused" means that through both the mental happiness and the pleasure of pliancy born of samādhi, body and mind are made free of agitation and at peace. To show that this pleasure does not bring peace to only one part, but brings peace to every part of body and mind, "fills it completely, satisfies it completely and pervades it completely" is spoken; these words have one meaning. Here, "suffuses this very inner body of one's own with the joy and pleasure born of solitude" shows that it is pervaded by the joy and pleasure born of the first dhyāna, the second dhyāna, the third dhyāna and so on.

Alternatively, by joy it suffuses; by pleasure it fills completely; by the attention that completes the preparation it satisfies completely; and by the attention that is the fruit of the completed preparation it pervades completely. Thus, at the time of the attention that completes the preparation and the attention that is the fruit of the completed preparation, the joy and pleasure of dhyāna are brought to completion. Therefore, by the attention that completes the preparation there is nothing left unpervaded, and by the attention that is the fruit of the completed preparation it is said that "there is no part that is not completely pervaded."

To establish that very meaning, the Blessed One declared a simile; the passage beginning "It is like this: just as, monks" is spoken. Just as flowers born in water are completely pervaded by the coolness of the water, so too they have body and mind completely pervaded by the pleasure of dhyāna; that is the meaning.

An objection: Why is it not said that, because of the pleasure born of the first, second and third dhyānas and so forth, one will abide in pleasure in the future? Reply: If one falls from those samādhis, one is born in the desire realm and does not experience their pleasure. If one rises to the higher and higher stages, one obtains pleasure from other samādhis, and so their pleasure is absent. And if one passes into nirvāṇa, there too one does not experience the pleasure of samādhi. Therefore it is not called pleasure in a future life; it is called "coming to dwell at ease in this visible life."

To conclude the teaching of the second cultivation, the passage beginning "This is called the cultivation of samādhi" is spoken.

With reference to teaching the third cultivation of samādhi, the passage beginning "Here, monks, the cultivation, pursuit and practice of samādhi" is spoken. In "the perception of light," cultivating the perception is the cause of attaining the divine eye. Cultivating the perception of light is of three kinds: light that is the antidote to darkness, the light of the Dharma, and the light of the body. Light that is the antidote to darkness is, at night, the light of the moon, the stars and so forth; in the daytime, the light of the sun; and at both times, the light of fire, jewels and the like. The light of the Dharma is when a person clearly calls to mind the Dharma teachings, such as the recollection of the Buddha. The light of the body is that of gods and others who are endowed with the radiance of their own bodies. The darkness that is opposed to the first kind of light is also of three kinds: the darkness of the directions, the darkness of clouds, and the darkness of enclosure. The darkness of the directions pervades everywhere; it is night. The darkness of clouds is when, even in the daytime and so on, the directions become dark because they are covered with thick clouds. The darkness of enclosure is when a house or the like is roofed over and has no windows or the like, and so becomes unlit. Of the three kinds of light, the intent here is that if one attends to the light that is the antidote to darkness, one attains the divine eye.

Moreover, for as long as one has not received instructions on meditation from another, the meditation is not accomplished. Therefore, to show how the cultivation of the higher perceptions, for those who have received and retained the instructions, comes to be free of forgetting, the Blessed One taught the passage beginning "the perception of light, very well grasped." The perception of light is to imagine that in all directions there is no darkness and everything is pervaded by light; when the instructions one has received for cultivating it do not decline and are not forgotten, that is called "very well grasped." Its distinctive features are taught in the passage beginning "thoroughly attended to." Because, from having cultivated the perception of light in five ways, one comes to possess a distinctive intention that has arisen, it is called "thoroughly attended to" and "thoroughly practiced."

Because one possesses accomplished meditative absorption, it is called "thoroughly penetrated," which means that the miraculous eye has become manifest. Alternatively, "very well grasped" means grasped at the time of attending to the instructions. "Thoroughly attended to" means well reflected upon at the time of reflection. "Thoroughly practiced" means made habitual again and again at the time of meditation. "Thoroughly penetrated" means that the result of the meditative absorption has been made manifest. Or again, "thoroughly practiced" refers to those who have accomplished the miraculous eye of the mundane path, because for them there is later decline. "Thoroughly penetrated" refers to the attainment of the miraculous eye of the supramundane path, because for it there is no later decline.

If one asks what is to be made manifest, for that reason the sūtra says, "Therefore one establishes the perception of daytime." "The perception of daytime" means attending to the perception accompanied by light. To show that very meaning, it says, "He cultivates his mind as accompanied by radiance." "His" refers to the yogin who wishes to attain the miraculous eye. That yogin, even in the daytime, meditates that everything is filled with light and free of darkness.

To show that this perception of light is to be cultivated equally by day and by night, it says, "As by day, so by night," and so on. Just as one meditates by day that everything is pervaded by light, so too at night one meditates that everything is completely pervaded by light. It teaches that one is to meditate on day and night alike, as having the characteristic of being pervaded by light; with these words it teaches that the meditative absorption of light is to be cultivated at all times.

To teach that, just as one meditates at all times, so too one should meditate on the perception of light as pervading all directions, it says, "As in front, so behind," and so on. Just as one clearly sees the front pervaded by light, so too one meditates that the direction behind is pervaded by light; this teaches meditating on it as equal to the front, having the characteristic of being pervaded by light. Alternatively, "in front" means meditating that everything directly perceived, which is bounded by mountains and the like, is pervaded by light. Likewise, "behind" means meditating that even in places blocked off by mountains and the like, there is no obstruction by mountains, walls and so forth, and that everything right through is made clear by light. "Above" is the direction of the sky where the sun rises. "Below" is right down beneath the great earth. This teaches that, just as the direction above is made full by the perception of being illuminated by the sun, so too one meditates that the entire direction below is pervaded by the perception of light.

To teach in summary the meaning of cultivating the perception of light, it says, "Thus, with a clear mind, unobstructed everywhere," and so on. "Thus" means as explained above. The meditative absorption in which the perception of light has been accomplished is the clear mind. That clear mind, in which the perception of light has been accomplished, is unobstructed in all directions and at all times. It teaches that, having established a perception like the sun risen in the daytime, one should meditate, with the perception accompanied by radiance, that everything right through is pervaded by light.

To illustrate by a simile the meaning of meditating on pervasion by the perception of light, it says, "It is like this: for example," and so on. Regarding "the last month of spring," time is set out as three seasons: spring, summer and winter. Spring has four months, summer also has four months, and winter also has four months. Then the period from the full moon of the first summer month to the full moon of the middle month is called the last month of the spring season; at that time the light of the sun is especially bright. To apply the simile that has been given to its meaning and explain it, it says, "Likewise, monks," and so on; those meanings have simply been explained above.

To conclude the teaching of the three cultivations of meditative absorption, it says, "This is the cultivation of meditative absorption," and so on. In brief, in this sūtra one cultivates attention to the perception of light in order to attain the superknowledge of the miraculous eye, because when one cultivates attention to the perception of light, one comes to attain the superknowledge of the miraculous eye.

With reference to teaching the fourth meditative absorption, it says, "Then, monks," and so on. Wisdom arises from cultivating the fourth dhyāna, and the fourth dhyāna is in turn attained through solitude of body and solitude of mind. Therefore, because one must make the body solitary, it says, "In the wilderness, or at the foot of a tree," and so on. Because one must make the mind solitary, it says, "Having abandoned pleasure and abandoned suffering," and so on; the meaning of these has simply been explained above. In brief, cultivating the fourth dhyāna, which is free of the eight kinds of stain, acts as the cause for the arising of excellent qualities such as virtue with defilement and virtue free of defilement. Therefore, because from cultivating it wisdom too comes to be thoroughly cultivated, cultivating the four dhyānas is called "the distinct attainment of wisdom."

Then, beginning with the four applications of mindfulness and going up to the eight limbs of the path, in order to teach the thirty-seven factors of awakening in sequence, it says, "Monks, what are the four applications of mindfulness?" and so on. Here the thirty-seven factors of awakening are taught as follows: the path of examining things, the path accompanied by exertion, the path that makes meditative absorption workable, the path of applying oneself to clear realization, the path mingled with clear realization, the path of clear realization, and the path of purified deliverance. Among these, the path of examining things is the four applications of mindfulness, because at the very outset one examines their body, feelings, phenomena and mind as things that are impure, suffering, impermanent and without self. The path accompanied by exertion is the four right abandonments, because, having thus examined all things, one then undertakes diligence in order to abandon the obscurations. The path that makes meditative absorption workable is the four bases of miraculous power, because, having thoroughly purified the obscurations, one makes the mind and meditative absorption workable by way of aspiration, diligence, mind and investigation.

The path of applying oneself to clear realization is the five faculties, because, once meditative absorption has been made workable, they become the support for seeing the four noble truths and apply one to the stages of heat and summit. The path that leads to clear realization is the five powers, because, having thus at the time of the faculties obtained the faculties for seeing the truths, one then overcomes what is opposed to realizing the truths, namely lack of faith, laziness, failing mindfulness, distraction and corrupted wisdom, and applies oneself to forbearance and to the highest worldly phenomenon. The path of clear realization is the factors of awakening, because from that time the noble truths are made directly perceived for the very first time. The path of purified deliverance is the eight limbs of the noble path, because thereafter, through cultivating the eight limbs of the noble path, one abandons the afflictions that are to be abandoned, becomes purified, and emerges from saṃsāra. For these reasons, the sequence of the thirty-seven factors of awakening is set out in this way.

Alternatively, at the time of being an ordinary person, one cultivates the four applications of mindfulness. At the time of heat, one cultivates the four right abandonments. At the time of summit, one cultivates the four bases of miraculous power. At the time of forbearance, one cultivates the five faculties. At the time of the highest worldly phenomenon, one cultivates the five powers. At the time of the path of seeing, one cultivates the seven factors of awakening. At the time of the path of cultivation, one cultivates the eightfold noble path. For that reason, too, the sequence is set out in this way.

"Monks, what are the four applications of mindfulness?" is the question. "Here" means here in the desire realm. "A monk" is the monk who is a yogin cultivating the four applications of mindfulness. Of these, the application of mindfulness to the body is explained first. If one asks, "What is the body? What is contemplating the body in the body? What is mindfulness? And what is the meaning of the application of mindfulness?", first I shall state what the body is.

The body is of thirty-five kinds. What are they? The inner, the outer, that which is appropriated by the faculties, that which is not appropriated by the faculties, that which is counted as a sentient being, that which is not counted as a sentient being, that which is accompanied by negative tendencies, that which is accompanied by ease, the body of the elements, the body derived from the elements, the body of name, the body of form, the body of a hell being, that of an animal, that of a hungry ghost, that of a human, that of a god, that which is accompanied by consciousness, that which is without consciousness, the inner body, the outer body, the transformed, the untransformed, the female body, the male body, the neuter body, the body of a friend, the body of one who is not a friend, the body of an ordinary person, the inferior body, the middling body, the excellent body, the companion body, the youthful body and the aged body. These, briefly, are the divisions of the body.

Of these, the inner is one's own body. The outer is another's body. That which is appropriated by the faculties is the body together with the faculties such as the eye. That which is not appropriated by the faculties is whatever has no faculties such as the eye, such as pillars, walls and the like. That which is counted as a sentient being is whatever is appropriated by mind and mental factors. That which is not counted as a sentient being is whatever is not appropriated by mind and mental factors. That which is accompanied by negative tendencies is that of sentient beings who have not abandoned the afflictions. That which is accompanied by ease is that of the noble ones who have abandoned the afflictions. The body of the elements is earth, water, fire and wind. The body derived from the elements is the ten, namely the five faculties and the five sense objects. The body of name is non-informative form, or else the "forms" of feeling, perception, formations and consciousness. The body of form is the aggregate of form itself. The body of a hell being is the form of the hell beings. The body of an animal is that of horses, cattle and the like. The body of a hungry ghost is the form of the hungry ghosts. That of a human is the form of the humans dwelling on the four continents. That of a god is the form of the gods of the desire, form and formless realms. That which is accompanied by consciousness is the forms endowed with the consciousnesses of the eye and so forth. That which is without consciousness is whatever is not endowed with the consciousnesses of the eye and so forth, such as the form of one in the state without perception or in the absorption of cessation. The inner is the form held together by flesh, blood and the like. The outer is the form not held together by flesh, blood and the like. The transformed is the form that, after death, has turned blue and become bloated. The untransformed is the form that has not died and has neither turned blue nor become bloated. The female body is the form endowed with the female faculty. The male body is the form endowed with the male faculty. The neuter body is the form endowed with the signs of a neuter body. The body of a friend is the form of people who are dear to one and have become one's friends. The reverse of that is the body of one who is not a friend, the form of enemies. The body of an ordinary person is the form of people who are neither one's friends nor one's enemies. The inferior body is the form whose color and shape are unattractive. The middling body is the form whose color and shape are neither attractive nor unattractive. The excellent body is that whose color and shape are attractive. The companion body is the body at the time of childhood. The youthful body is the form that has reached the prime of life. The aged form is the body in which the faculties and the great elements have become shrunken and worn out.

When these bodies are summarized, they become three kinds: the inner body, the outer body, and the body that is both outer and inner. Of these, the inner body is one's own body, which is counted as a sentient being. The outer body is forms and so on, which are not counted as sentient beings.

The body that is both outer and inner is the body of other sentient beings, which is counted as a sentient being. Because it is other in relation to oneself, it is called outer; because it is counted as a sentient being, it is called inner.

Alternatively, form appropriated by the faculties is the inner body. Form not appropriated by the faculties is the outer body. Form appropriated by the faculties that belongs to the continua of others is the body that is both outer and inner.

Alternatively, form belonging to the level of meditative equipoise and accompanied by ease is the inner body. One's own body, belonging to the level not in meditative equipoise and accompanied by negative tendencies, is the outer body. The body of another, form accompanied by negative tendencies or accompanied by ease, is the body that is both outer and inner.

Alternatively, the body accompanied by consciousness is the inner body. Form without consciousness that is counted as a sentient being, at the time when it has turned blue, become bloated and so on, is the outer body; it is form without consciousness. Form that in the past was accompanied by consciousness, and form now accompanied by consciousness that in the future will come to be without consciousness, is the inner and outer body.

Alternatively, what possesses head hair, body hair, nails, blood and so on is the inner body. Another's head hair, body hair, nails, blood and so on is the outer body. One's own body at the time when it has become bloated, turned blue and so on is the outer body. This is a summary of the meaning of the divisions of the body.

Now, what is contemplating the body in the body? It is the three kinds of wisdom: the wisdom arising from hearing, the wisdom arising from reflection, and the wisdom arising from meditation, the wisdom by which the nature of the body is understood and realized in all its aspects.

What is mindfulness? It is not forgetting the teachings given with reference to the body that one has taken up, nor the words and meanings of the teachings one has taken up, which one has made directly present through reflection in the mind and through meditation.

What is the meaning of the application of mindfulness? It too is of three kinds: guarding mindfulness, keeping oneself free of affliction toward objects, and settling the mind upon the object of focus. Of these, guarding mindfulness is restraining the faculties and the mind from the very outset so that they do not engage with objects. Keeping oneself free of affliction toward objects is, even having engaged with objects, not grasping forms and the like as signs and not grasping them as attractive. Settling the mind upon the object of focus is placing the mind one-pointedly on the object one is meditating on.

As for "dwelling contemplating the body in the inner body": when, with wisdom, one sees one's own body, the form counted as a sentient being, in terms of its specific and general characteristics, that is called contemplating the body in the inner body. The specific characteristic of the body is that it arises from the accumulation of many minute particles and is filled with various kinds of impurity, such as mucus, saliva, blood and lymph.

When meditating in this way, there are two aspects: what is to be seen clearly by means of the antidote, and dwelling having abandoned the adverse factors. Of these, the antidote is seeing the specific characteristic of the body with wisdom, seeing the general characteristic of the body with wisdom, and placing calm abiding one-pointedly on the phenomenon thus seen with wisdom. The adverse factors are also two: attachment to the body and to objects such as forms, and anger toward those very things.

Seeing the specific characteristic of the body unerringly with wisdom, as impure and so on, is "clear." Seeing the general characteristic of the body unerringly with wisdom is "fully aware." Not forgetting the meaning of that very seeing of the specific and general characteristics of the body with wisdom, and constantly placing the mind of calm abiding one-pointedly upon it, is "possessing mindfulness." Abandoning attachment to the body and to forms and the like, the adverse factor that interrupts the application of mindfulness to the body, is "free of attachment to the world." Abandoning aversion, which interrupts it, is called "having abandoned mental unhappiness." "Dwelling" means meditating as explained above.

When one realizes the specific and general characteristics of the outer body, the forms and so on that are not counted as sentient beings, that is called "dwelling contemplating the body in the outer body." Its specific characteristic is the nature of an accumulation of infinitesimal particles. Its general characteristic is impermanence, suffering, emptiness and selflessness. "Clear" and "fully aware" are also to be applied to it in that sense, and their meaning should be regarded as taught before.

Realizing the specific and general characteristics of the bodies of other sentient beings, which are counted as sentient beings, is called contemplating the body in the inner and outer body; the specific and general characteristics here too should be regarded as explained above.

As for "inner feeling, outer feeling," and so on: what is feeling? It is pleasant feeling, painful feeling, and feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful. Pleasant, painful, and neither pleasant nor painful feelings are, moreover, bodily, and likewise mental. Feelings such as pleasure are, moreover, carnal and non-carnal, based on attachment and based on renunciation. These are the divisions of feeling.

Of these, what gratifies and benefits body and mind is pleasant feeling. What torments body and mind through harm is painful feeling. What neither benefits nor torments body and mind is feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful. These three feelings, when they are connected with the five consciousnesses from eye consciousness to body consciousness, are bodily. These three feelings, when connected with mental consciousness, are mental. When the three feelings come to accord with nirvāṇa, they are non-carnal. All feelings included in the three realms are carnal.

When it comes to accord with the noble path, it is based on renunciation. Clinging to the five qualities of sense pleasure is called based on clinging. The meanings of contemplating, of mindfulness and of the application of mindfulness here should be regarded as explained in the section on the application of mindfulness to the body.

Here, pleasant feeling, painful feeling, and feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful, arising from focusing on one's own body, which is counted as a sentient being, are inner feeling. Pleasant feeling and the like arising from forms and so on, which are not counted as sentient beings, are outer feeling. Pleasant feeling and the like arising from focusing on the bodies of other sentient beings, which are counted as sentient beings, are called inner and outer feeling. Seeing that all three kinds of feeling are bound up with suffering is the application of mindfulness that contemplates feeling in feeling.

As for the three kinds of suffering, there is the suffering of suffering, the suffering of change, and the suffering of formations. Of these, painful feeling is bound up with the suffering of suffering. Pleasant feeling, although it is pleasant for the moment, later becomes a cause of suffering, and so it is bound up with the suffering of change. Feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful possesses the quality of impermanence, and so it is bound up with the suffering of formations. The meaning of "clear" and so on should be regarded as explained above.

As for "the inner mind," and so on: when mind is divided, it is of twenty kinds: the mind with attachment, the mind free of attachment, the mind with aversion, the mind free of aversion, the mind with delusion, the mind free of delusion, the collected mind, the distracted mind, the sunken mind, the elated mind, the agitated mind, the unagitated mind, the calm mind, the uncalm mind, the well-composed mind, the mind that is not well composed, the mind that has well cultivated the path, the mind that has not well cultivated the path, the mind liberated through thorough cultivation, and the mind not liberated through thorough cultivation.

Of these, when one is actively engaged with an object of attachment, entangled by attachment, that is called the mind with attachment. When the entanglement of attachment is merely latent and is not actually engaged, that is called the mind free of attachment. When one is actively engaged with an object of aversion, entangled by aversion, that is called the mind with aversion. When aversion is merely latent and has not actually arisen, that is called the mind free of aversion. When bewilderment arises toward a thing about which one has become deluded, that is called the mind with delusion. When delusion abides as merely latent, that is called the mind free of delusion. These six minds should be understood as established in terms of their occurrence at the time of active engagement. Of them, three belong to the side of affliction, and three to the side of their antidotes.

Then, the mind that abides inwardly in the manner of calm abiding is the collected mind. The mind that follows after the five qualities of sense pleasure is the distracted mind. The mind possessed of sleep and torpor is the sunken mind. The mind made joyful by focusing on some phenomenon that inspires one and dispels sleep and torpor is the elated mind. The mind that, from being uplifted, becomes distracted again is the agitated mind. The mind that has abandoned sinking and agitation and has come to equanimity is the unagitated mind. The mind free of obscurations such as sleep, torpor, regret and doubt is the calm mind. The mind not free of those obscurations is the uncalm mind. The mind that has entered the four root dhyānas is the well-composed mind. The mind that has not entered the four root dhyānas is the mind that is not well composed. The mind that, through thorough cultivation of those very root dhyānas and long cultivation, has gained mastery over them at will is the mind that has well cultivated the path. The mind that, although it has attained the root dhyānas, has not thoroughly cultivated them and, not having cultivated them for long, has not gained mastery at will, is the mind that has not well cultivated the path. The mind in which, through thorough cultivation of the path, all afflictions are purified and thoroughly purified, is the mind liberated through thorough cultivation. The mind in which not all afflictions are purified, nor thoroughly purified, but some afflictions are purified, is the mind not liberated through thorough cultivation. These sixteen minds should be understood as explained with reference to the time of abiding in meditation.

These twenty minds, in brief, are of three kinds: the inner mind, the outer mind, and the outer and inner mind. Of these, the twenty minds that arise from focusing on one's own body, which is counted as a sentient being, are called the inner mind. The twenty minds that arise from focusing on forms and so on, which are not counted as sentient beings, are called the outer mind. The twenty minds that arise when the mind focuses on the forms of other sentient beings, which are counted as other sentient beings, are called the inner and outer mind. The mind, too, is in one instant with attachment and in another instant free of attachment, and so on; realizing that it does not abide even for an instant, but changes its nature again and again, is called contemplating mind in the mind. The other meanings, such as "clear," have simply been explained above.

As for "inner phenomena," and so on: phenomena, when divided into the two sides, the dark side and the bright side, should be understood as twenty kinds.

What are those twenty? Attachment and the taming of attachment, aversion and the taming of aversion, delusion and the taming of delusion, the collected and the distracted, the sunken and the elated, the agitated and the unagitated, the calm and the uncalm, the well composed and the not well composed, the well-cultivated path and the path not well cultivated, liberation through thorough cultivation and the lack of liberation through thorough cultivation. Attachment, aversion, delusion, distraction, sinking, agitation, the uncalm, the uncomposed, the path not well cultivated, and the absence of liberation through good cultivation: these ten are the dark side. The taming of attachment, the taming of aversion, the taming of delusion, the collected, the elated, the unagitated, the calm, the composed, the well-cultivated path, and liberation through good cultivation: these ten are the bright side.

Of these, attachment to forms and to the qualities of sense pleasure is attachment. Meditating on the unattractive is the taming of attachment. Anger toward enemies and the like is aversion. Loving-kindness is the taming of aversion. Not knowing actions and the results of actions is delusion. The wisdom that knows the specific and general characteristics of phenomena is the taming of delusion. The mind abiding one-pointedly within is the collected. The mind engaging with the qualities of sense pleasure is the distracted. The mind overcome by sleep and torpor is the sunken. Dispelling sleep and torpor by reflecting on the results of dhyāna or on the qualities of the Tathāgata is the elated. The mind that, from being uplifted, scatters again is the agitated.
