The mind that has abandoned both sinking and agitation and has become even is the unagitated. Being free of obscurations such as sleep and torpor is the calm. Not being free of the obscurations is the uncalm. Having entered the root dhyānas, that is, the first, second, third and fourth dhyānas, is the well composed. Not having entered the root dhyānas is the not composed. Then, having attained the cultivation of the noble path is the well-cultivated path. Not having attained the cultivation of the noble path is the path not well cultivated. Having abandoned all the afflictions of the three realms so that they do not arise again is liberation through thorough cultivation. Not having abandoned the afflictions without remainder is the absence of liberation through thorough cultivation.

These phenomena too are set out as three kinds: inner phenomena, outer phenomena, and inner and outer phenomena. Of these, dark or bright phenomena that arise from one's own body, which is counted as a sentient being, are inner phenomena. Bright or dark phenomena that arise from focusing on forms and so on, which are not counted as sentient beings, are outer phenomena. Bright or dark phenomena that arise from focusing on the bodies of other sentient beings, which are counted as sentient beings, are called inner and outer phenomena. To see that through dark phenomena there is merely affliction, and through bright phenomena merely purification, but that here there is no self that afflicts or self that purifies, is called contemplating phenomena in phenomena. The meaning of "clear" and so on is as explained above.

The four applications of mindfulness are, moreover, set out in five aspects. The five aspects are: the object of focus, the nature, the companions, the cultivation, and the result of cultivation. Just as the four applications of mindfulness are set out in five aspects, so too the remaining factors of awakening are each set out in five aspects.

What is the object of focus of the applications of mindfulness? It too is fourfold: body, mind, feeling and phenomena. Alternatively, the thing that is the support of the self, the thing by which the self has experience, the thing that is the self itself, and the thing by which the self is afflicted and purified: these four are to be taken as the objects of focus of the applications of mindfulness. Of these, the thing of the self is the body. The thing by which the self has experience is feeling. The thing that is the self itself is mind. The thing by which the self is afflicted and purified is phenomena. And how are they focused on? Childish beings, whose understanding has become distorted, mostly take the body together with its faculties as the support of a self and through it experience pleasure and the like; and that self which thus has experience is in fact the mind; that self is afflicted by attachment and the like and purified by faith and the like. In order that one may realize this, body and the rest are cultivated and set out.

What is the nature? It is wisdom and calm abiding. By saying "contemplating the body," wisdom is stated; by saying "possessing mindfulness" and "dwelling," calm abiding is taught.

The companions are the mind and mental factors associated with them, because wisdom and calm abiding cannot possibly arise alone, unaccompanied by other mental factors such as diligence.

The cultivation is that one should become habituated to the specific and general characteristics of the inner, outer, and both outer and inner body.

The result of cultivation is the abandoning of the four distortions, entry into the four truths, and becoming free of the body and so on. The four distortions are grasping at the impure, at pleasure, at permanence and at self. In order to abandon the distorted grasping of the impure as pure, one cultivates the application of mindfulness to the body, for the Blessed One said that the body is to be meditated on chiefly as having the characteristic of impurity. In order to abandon the distorted grasping of suffering as pleasure, one cultivates the application of mindfulness to feeling, because the Blessed One said, "Whatever feelings there are, all of them are bound up with the three kinds of suffering." As the antidote to the distorted grasping of the impermanent as permanent, one cultivates the application of mindfulness to mind, because this mind too changes, moment by moment, into ever different states, with attachment, without attachment, and so on.

In order to abandon the distorted grasping of what is selfless as a self, one cultivates the application of mindfulness to phenomena: one becomes thoroughly afflicted through attachment and the like, and one becomes purified through faith and the like, and that is all. When one understands that there is here no agent who brings about affliction and brings about purification, and that there is no self, one becomes free of the distortion of grasping at a self.

As for entering the Four Truths: by cultivating the application of mindfulness to the body one enters the truth of suffering, because when one sees the nature of the body, one comes to realize that it is bound up with the many kinds of suffering, such as birth, aging, sickness and death. By cultivating the application of mindfulness to feeling one enters the truth of the origin, because one realizes: "Based on pleasant feeling, craving arises; because of painful feeling, aversion arises; because of feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful, delusion arises; and because of attachment, aversion and delusion, the aggregates of another life come into being." By cultivating the application of mindfulness to mind one enters the truth of cessation, because when one sees that the mind, whose nature is to perish in each and every moment, has the characteristic of selflessness, the fear "In nirvāṇa my self will be annihilated" does not arise. By cultivating the application of mindfulness to phenomena one enters the truth of the path, because, having seen the two sides, that of affliction and that of purification, one relies on the phenomena of purification in order to abandon affliction.

As for freedom: it is so called because by cultivating mindfulness of the body and the rest one becomes free of all dysfunctional tendencies (dauṣṭhulya).

To explain it another way: worldly people, for the most part, do not understand the five aggregates correctly, just as they are, as mere aggregates or mere phenomena. They therefore hold, for instance, that the self dwells in the body; that pleasant and painful feelings afflict it; and that through dharma and non-dharma it becomes purified and afflicted. To turn back this grasping, the four applications of mindfulness were taught.

Among these, to dispel the confusion that the body is the thing that serves as the basis of the self, the application of mindfulness to the body was taught. The meaning is that this body is a thing that is the abode of impurity and suffering, and not a thing belonging to a self. To dispel the confusion that feeling is the thing that the self experiences, the application of mindfulness to feeling was explained. The meaning is that all feelings are suffering, and are not something experienced by a self. To dispel the grasping of mind, mentation and consciousness as the substance of a self, the cultivation of the application of mindfulness to mind was taught. The meaning is that mind is momentary by nature, and not of the nature of a self. Because of the confusion that phenomena make that very self afflicted and purified, the cultivation of the application of mindfulness to phenomena was explained. The point is this: attachment and the like bring about affliction, and faith and the like purify the mind, and that is all; since by nature there is no self, it is impossible for a self to become afflicted or purified.

After teaching the applications of mindfulness, he taught the four right abandonments; and wishing to explain them just as they were taught, he declared the passage beginning, "Monks, what are the four right abandonments?"

The four right abandonments are: not generating evil phenomena that have not arisen; abandoning evil phenomena that have arisen; generating virtuous phenomena that have not arisen; and making virtuous phenomena that have arisen endure.

Objection: Not generating evil, unwholesome phenomena that have not arisen, and abandoning those that have arisen, may fittingly be called right abandonments, since those two concern phenomena that are to be discarded. But generating virtuous phenomena that have not arisen, and making those that have arisen endure, concern phenomena that are to be accomplished. How then can they be designated right abandonments?

Reply: Although it may seem inappropriate to apply the designation to these two, they are the abandoning of what opposes the generation and maintenance of virtue; and since it is through that abandoning that virtuous phenomena arise and endure, there is no fault in calling the abandoning of the opposing side a right abandonment.

To teach the first right abandonment, he spoke the passage beginning, "Monks, here a monk, in order not to generate evil, unwholesome phenomena that have not arisen." Here, evil, unwholesome phenomena are the misdeeds of body, speech and mind in the desire realm: by way of the body, killing, taking what is not given, and unchaste conduct; by way of speech, lying, harsh words, slander and idle talk; by way of the mind, covetousness, malice and wrong view. These ten nonvirtues, and the afflictions that are the causes producing them, attachment, aversion, delusion and the rest, are evil, unwholesome phenomena. They are evil because they are censured by the noble ones; they are unwholesome because their karmic maturation is experienced as unpleasant. Alternatively, what serves as a cause of harm to oneself and others is evil, and it is unwholesome because it is opposed to virtuous phenomena. When those unwholesome phenomena have not become manifest, and one has not encountered them and engaged in them, they are called unarisen: they remain as habitual tendencies.

Alternatively, the decline of the training in higher moral discipline, the training in higher mind and the training in higher wisdom, and the phenomena that are the causes of the decline of the three trainings, are called evil, unwholesome phenomena. Among these, the decline of the training in higher moral discipline is the decline of moral discipline with its six aspects. The causes of the decline of moral discipline are ten: not having taken it up properly from the outset; being slack; holding it wrongly; heedlessness; being in the grip of laziness; wrong aspiration; deterioration of deportment; deterioration of livelihood; falling into the two extremes; not leading to deliverance; and falling away from the commitment one has undertaken.

How is it not taken up properly from the outset? Someone takes it up out of fear of the king, or out of fear of thieves, or for want of a livelihood, or for some other such reason; and, having been taken up in that way, it later declines.

How is one slack? Some, having taken up moral discipline, are without shame, without conscience and with little remorse; they do not guard the points of training strictly, but carry on as their discipline wears away. Being slack in this way, their moral discipline declines.

How does one hold it wrongly? Some are possessed of wrong grasping: they feel remorse over what is no ground for remorse, and because that remorse obstructs virtue, their moral discipline declines.

How is one heedless and in the grip of laziness? Some make no resolve from the outset that no offense shall occur, and when an offense does occur they do not restore themselves again in accordance with the Dharma; and by devoting themselves to the pleasures of lying down, reclining and sleep, their moral discipline declines.

How is there wrong aspiration? Some wrongly aspire, "Through this moral discipline, or this ascetic observance, or this austerity, may I become a god, or one of some other class of gods, or come to have gain or honor!" Because of that wrong aspiration it does not become a cause of liberation, and so their moral discipline declines.

How is deportment deteriorated? Some, in their deportment, such as walking and sitting, and in their spheres of activity, such as villages, stay wherever they happen to land; and their daily tasks and their applications to virtue are out of keeping with the world and contrary to the world, out of keeping with the Vinaya and contrary to the Vinaya. Through this too their moral discipline declines.

How does livelihood deteriorate? Here some are discontented, have many wants, are hard to support and hard to satisfy; by seeking alms bowls, robes and the like in ways contrary to the Dharma, their moral discipline declines.

How does one fall into the two extremes? Some, excessively attached to sensual pleasure, indulge heedlessly in sensual things; while others practice harsh austerities, such as making their bed on thorns or making their bed in ashes, and engage in things not permitted in the Dharma and Vinaya. Through these too moral discipline declines.

How does it fail to lead to deliverance? Some, priding themselves on moral discipline, or ascetic observance, or a view, hold wrongly, "By this very moral discipline, or by this very observance, one will attain release and liberation." Because of this wrong holding it does not become a cause of release and liberation, and so their moral discipline declines.

How does one fall away from the commitment one has undertaken? Some are utterly and in every respect without shame and have no scruple about doing evil, unwholesome things; their moral discipline ruined, they are of evil character, rotten within, trailing along like a donkey, claiming to be ascetics while they are not ascetics.

Next, the decline of the training in higher mind is the decline of the first meditative concentration, the second and so on. The causes of its decline are: much attachment to requisites such as robes and alms bowls; much bodily illness; greed for food; delight in dwelling amid much bustle; delight in many worldly undertakings; fondness for much talk; and delight in lying down, reclining and the like.

The decline of the training in higher wisdom is the view become distorted, such as grasping the impermanent as permanent. The causes of its decline are relying on unwholesome spiritual friends, listening to a doctrine that is not the true Dharma, and attending improperly to the existence of a self, an agent and the like. When these phenomena are not actively engaged in, they are unarisen.

To resolve, "I will not let unarisen unwholesome phenomena arise in the mind in any way whatsoever," and to apply oneself intently from the very beginning, is called generating aspiration. Striving, undertaking diligence and the rest need to be connected in meaning with both right abandonments, and so I shall explain them later.

To teach the second right abandonment, he spoke the passage beginning, "in order to abandon evil, unwholesome phenomena that have arisen." Evil, unwholesome phenomena are those explained above: the misdeeds of body, speech and mind in the desire realm, the ten nonvirtues, or the phenomena that cause the three trainings to decline. When those phenomena have become manifest in the mind and are active in the mind, they are arisen unwholesome phenomena. To generate the wish, "Whatever evil, unwholesome phenomena have arisen in the mind, I will not let them remain in the mind or settle in the mind in any way whatsoever, but will cast them off in every way," is called generating aspiration.

These evil, unwholesome phenomena arise by taking past things as their object, by taking future things as their object, and by taking present objects as their object. Therefore, in brief, they are classed in two kinds: those that arise taking a hidden object, and those that arise taking a manifest object. Of these, those that arise taking past things as their object and those that arise taking future things as their object arise taking a hidden object; those that arise taking a present object take a manifest object.

Here, combining the two right abandonments and summarizing their meaning, the teaching is this. Undertaking diligence in order not to generate unarisen evil, unwholesome phenomena that take a hidden object, and to abandon those that have been generated, is called striving. Striving in order not to generate unarisen evil, unwholesome phenomena that arise taking a manifest object, and to abandon those that have been generated, is called undertaking diligence.

Alternatively, some evil, unwholesome phenomena arise by way of conceptual thought, and there are also those that arise through the power of the object; there are also some evil, unwholesome phenomena that are generated through the power of conceptual thought. Of these, those that arise through the power of conceptual thought arise in the phase of abiding: even without meeting an object, they arise taking past and future things as their object. Those that arise from the power of conceptual thought together with the power of the object arise in the phase of active engagement: they take a thing that is presently occurring as their object, and they arise through the power of the improper attention that also occurs there. Exerting oneself in order not to generate unarisen evil, unwholesome phenomena that arise through the power of conceptual thought, and to abandon those that have arisen, is called striving.

Striving in order not to generate unarisen evil, unwholesome phenomena that arise through the power of conceptual thought and the power of the object, and to abandon those that have arisen, is called undertaking diligence.

Alternatively, afflictions are of three kinds: small, great and middling. Not generating small and middling afflictions that have not arisen, and abandoning those that have arisen, is called striving. Not generating great afflictions that have not arisen, and abandoning those that have arisen, is called undertaking diligence.

Here, when one who has set about not generating unarisen evil, unwholesome phenomena and abandoning those that have arisen sees that the mind has become distracted, then cultivating calm abiding and applying the mind one-pointedly is called holding the mind firmly. When one who has set about not generating unarisen evil, unwholesome phenomena and abandoning those that have arisen sees that the mind is overcome by sleep and torpor and has drawn inward, or when the fear arises that slackness may set in, then attending to things that lift the mind up, the qualities of the Tathāgata or the fruits of meditative concentration, or cultivating insight and dispelling sleep and torpor, is called rightly aspiring.

To teach the third right abandonment, he spoke the passage beginning, "generating virtuous phenomena that have not arisen." Here, virtuous phenomena are the ten virtues, such as refraining from killing; or the three trainings, beginning with moral discipline; or the phenomena that are the causes producing the three trainings. The causes of the three trainings should be understood thus: when one turns back from the causes of the decline of the three trainings, that becomes the cause of their arising. Unarisen means that these phenomena have not become manifest, or have not occurred in the mind. To generate them is to make these phenomena occur manifestly in the mind. Here, when a great wish arises, "May I swiftly attain and make manifest those virtuous phenomena that I have not attained and that have not become manifest," that is called generating aspiration. Striving, undertaking diligence and the rest must be taught in connection with the meaning of the four right abandonments, and so they are to be explained.

To teach the fourth right abandonment, he spoke the passage beginning, "virtuous phenomena that have not arisen." The virtuous phenomena are those explained in the context of the third right abandonment. Making manifest the virtuous phenomena one has attained is called remaining. Making what is to be made manifest clear in the mind, without confusion, is called non-forgetting. That what is not forgotten is not carried off by the opposing side is called non-decline. Thus, cultivating, relying on and making much of the attained virtuous phenomena that have become manifest, completing them and bringing them to perfection, is called cultivating, expanding and completing. When a great wish arises that these arisen virtuous phenomena may remain and be brought to completion, that is generating aspiration.

Here, undertaking diligence in order to actualize the virtuous phenomena one has found is called striving. Striving in order to find the virtuous phenomena one has not found is called undertaking diligence. Making arisen virtuous phenomena remain, unforgotten and undeclining, is striving. Cultivating, expanding and completing those very virtuous phenomena is undertaking diligence.

Alternatively, virtue is of three kinds: small, middling and great. Generating small and middling virtuous phenomena that have not arisen, and making those that have arisen remain, unforgotten and undeclining, is called striving. Generating great virtuous phenomena that have not arisen, and cultivating, expanding and completing those that have arisen, is called undertaking diligence. When, in cultivating these virtuous phenomena, the mind becomes distracted, cultivating calm abiding and settling the mind one-pointedly is called holding the mind firmly. When at that time the mind is overcome by torpor and sleep and has become slack, then attending to the qualities of the Tathāgata or the fruits of meditative concentration and so lifting the mind up, or dispelling torpor and sleep through cultivating insight, is called rightly aspiring.

Here the four right abandonments, in brief, teach the nature of things: the dark side and the white side. Among these, not generating unarisen evil, unwholesome phenomena and abandoning those that have arisen is the dark side. Generating unarisen virtuous phenomena and making those that have arisen remain is the white side.

That being so, by way of the four kinds of abandonment all four become right abandonments. The four kinds of abandonment are: abandonment through restraint, abandonment through abandoning, abandonment through cultivation, and abandonment through guarding.

Among these, the passage beginning "not generating unarisen evil, unwholesome phenomena" teaches abandonment through abandoning. Abandonment here means that evil, unwholesome phenomena occurring manifestly in the mind are absent; and striving so that they do not become manifest is abandonment through abandoning, because they do not arise later.

The passage beginning "in order to abandon evil, unwholesome phenomena that have arisen" teaches abandonment through restraint, because those evil, unwholesome phenomena that have arisen are to be abandoned by way of restraint, so that they never again arise from the mental continuum.

The passage beginning "generating virtuous phenomena that have not arisen" teaches abandonment through cultivation, for in this way unarisen virtuous phenomena are to be generated by relying on them, those that have arisen are to be attained by cultivating them, and once those attained are made manifest, the side opposed to them is transformed.

The passage beginning "making arisen virtuous phenomena remain" teaches abandonment through guarding, because when arisen virtuous phenomena are made to remain, are completed and so on, by abandoning heedlessness, the side opposed to them, and by relying on heedfulness, their ally, the obscurations to them cease to be.

In brief, this teaches the things to be given up and the things to be taken up, the excellence of the intention to give up and to take up, and the excellence of application. Here, the first two right abandonments concern the things to be given up, and the latter ones concern the things to be taken up. The words "generates aspiration" teach the excellence of intention. The words "strives, undertakes diligence, holds the mind firmly and rightly aspires," and so on, teach the excellence of application.

For the four right abandonments there are, further, the object, the nature, the companions, the cultivation and the result of cultivation. What is the object of the right abandonments? The opposing side and the antidote, both arisen and unarisen. The first right abandonment takes as its object the opposing side that has not arisen. The second right abandonment takes as its object the opposing side that has arisen. The third right abandonment takes as its object the antidote that has not arisen. The fourth right abandonment takes as its object the antidote that has arisen. The nature of the right abandonments should be understood to be the nature of diligence. Their companions are the mental factors associated with diligence: aspiration, faith, wisdom, meditative absorption and the like.

As for the cultivation, it is stated: "He generates aspiration, strives, undertakes diligence, holds the mind firmly and rightly aspires." These words teach the cultivation, the undertaking of diligence together with its basis. Here aspiration is the basis of diligence. Why is that? Because when there is desire and aspiration, diligence comes about from that cause.

Here, to cultivate calm abiding and insight alone, without attending to the signs of calm abiding and insight, that is, without attending to the faults of saṃsāra so as to subdue the mind when the mind becomes excited, and without attending to the qualities of the Tathāgata, the sign that lifts the mind up, when the mind becomes torpid, is striving. When the mind becomes excited or torpid, attending, in order to dispel this, to the sign of calm abiding and the sign of insight, which are the antidotes to excitement and slackness, is called undertaking diligence. Distinguishing this further: when the mind becomes distracted, attending to calm abiding and so settling it is holding the mind firmly; when the mind becomes slack, attending to insight and so lifting the mind up is rightly aspiring.

The result of cultivation is that the whole of the opposing side is to be made to decline, and the antidotal phenomena are to be attained and increased. Here, the first two right abandonments teach making the opposing side decline; the third right abandonment teaches attaining the antidotal side; and the fourth right abandonment teaches increasing the antidotal side.

After teaching the four right abandonments he taught the four bases of miraculous power; therefore, since this is the occasion to explain them, he spoke the passage beginning, "What are the four bases of miraculous power?" Here, "miraculous power" refers to the applications of the higher knowledges, such as the divine eye; and the four meditative absorptions, beginning with aspiration, are called bases of miraculous power because they serve as the causes and conditions for the arising of the higher knowledges. Alternatively, just as a person who has legs has the capacity to go, to come and so on, so one who has the four meditative absorptions has the capacity to attain and actualize supramundane attainments; hence they are called the bases, literally the legs, of miraculous power.

To teach the first base of miraculous power, he spoke the passage beginning, "Monks, the base of miraculous power endowed with the meditative absorption of aspiration and the formations of abandonment." When, taking as foremost one's aspiration toward and confidence in suffering, emptiness, selflessness and the like, one generates respect for diligence and, having engaged intently in it, touches one-pointedness of mind, that is called the meditative absorption of aspiration. Alternatively, the one-pointedness of mind that comes from great aspiration and faith in order not to generate unwholesome phenomena that have not arisen, to abandon unwholesome phenomena that have arisen, to generate virtuous phenomena that have not arisen, and to make the virtuous phenomena that have been generated remain, is called the meditative absorption of aspiration.

In cultivating this meditative absorption of aspiration there are five faults, and to abandon the five faults one should cultivate the eight formations of abandonment that are their antidotes. The five faults are laziness, loss of the instructions, slackness and excitement, non-application, and application: these five. Here slackness and excitement are counted together as a single fault.

Among these, laziness is not engaging in abandoning nonvirtue and accomplishing virtue; it is a fault because it obstructs the very occasion of first applying oneself to virtue. Forgetting the instructions is forgetting, in both words and meaning, the meditation instructions as they were taught; it is a fault because, when one has entered on the practice of meditation, forgetting the meaning of the meditation obstructs it. When, having entered on the practice of meditation, the mind has become one-pointed, it is a fault when slackness and excitement break in upon it, for when these two occur the mind becomes unworkable. When slackness and excitement have occurred, not attending to the sign that will dispel them is a fault, because if these two are not cleared away, the mind does not become calm. When the mind, free of slackness and excitement, has become balanced, deliberate application is a fault, because if one moves away from that balance, the mind becomes distracted.

To abandon the five faults, the eight formations of abandonment are aspiration, diligence, faith, pliancy, mindfulness, vigilance, intention and equanimity. Here, aspiration is the arising of the wish, "When shall I perfect meditative absorption? How shall I abandon the latent tendencies? How shall I cast off evil, unwholesome phenomena?" Diligence is engaging uninterruptedly in the cultivation of the antidote for as long as is needed. Faith is the arising of faith and conviction that, by engaging and persisting uninterruptedly, the higher qualities will be attained.

Here, pliancy is this: caused by the manifestation of faith, joy of mind arises. From joy of mind the body becomes blissful. From bliss of body the mind becomes happy. When the mind is happy, the mind becomes workable; and that workable mind's removal of the side of evil, unwholesome phenomena, the dysfunctional tendencies, is pliancy.

Mindfulness, as belonging to the side of calm abiding, is the nine kinds of mental abiding. The nine are: placing the mind inwardly, placing it correctly, placing it thoroughly, placing it closely, taming, pacifying, thoroughly pacifying, unifying, and placing it in equipoise. Here, withdrawing the mind from external objects and, for the very first time, gathering the mind inwardly in a manner free of distraction, is placing. After it has first been gathered, the mind moves toward external objects and coarseness occurs; gathering that coarse mind inward in a subtle way is placing correctly. When, after it has been placed in this way, mindfulness lapses and the mind becomes distracted, gathering it back once again is placing thoroughly. Then, attaining for the first time the characteristic of the mind's not being distracted outward is placing closely. Under the influence of the perception that sees as faulty the objects such as forms, which from the very beginning are the causes of distraction, not letting the mind scatter is taming. Seeing as faulty the conceptual secondary afflictions, the causes of agitation of mind, and not letting the mind scatter, is pacifying. Even when conceptual thoughts have arisen through a lapse of mindfulness, not letting them remain is thoroughly pacifying. Remaining constantly, with effort, in the stream of meditative absorption, without gaps or interruptions, is unifying. Remaining continuously in the stream of meditative absorption, spontaneously and without deliberate application, because meditative absorption has been well cultivated, is placing the mind in equipoise.

Vigilance is the wisdom that belongs to the side of insight. It is of four kinds: analyzing phenomena, ascertaining them, examining them and investigating them. Knowing however many phenomena there are, such as the aggregates and the elements, is analyzing. Knowing those phenomena as they are, impermanent, suffering and so on, is ascertaining. First seeking out the characteristics of phenomena with wisdom accompanied by conceptual thought is examining. Thereafter, scrutinizing them in detail is called investigating.

Intention is the mind's deliberate application: having examined whether obscurations have arisen or not, and whether they have been abandoned or not, it sets about abandoning them. It is the activity of mind in accord with calm abiding and insight. Equanimity is abiding, once the mind has become balanced, with the characteristic of not moving from that balance.

Now, to abandon laziness one cultivates four formations of abandonment: aspiration, diligence, faith and pliancy. Here, aspiration has the characteristic of wishing; diligence has the characteristic of effort; faith has the characteristic of conviction; pliancy has the characteristic of workability. How do these four become antidotes to laziness? They do so by way of relying on the ground, the support, the sign and the result of diligence. The ground of diligence is aspiration. What relies on it is diligence itself, because when there is a wish one undertakes diligence. The sign and cause of the wish is faith, because when there is conviction, wish and aspiration arise. The result of diligence is pliancy: once diligence has been undertaken, the body becomes workable, and so pliancy, which has the characteristic of workability of mind, arises; and because once pliancy has arisen, laziness ceases to be.

As antidotes to the remaining four faults, the other four formations of abandonment are applied in order. The antidote to forgetting the instructions is mindfulness. The antidote to slackness and excitement is vigilance: whenever slackness or excitement of mind occurs, vigilance recognizes it. The antidote to non-application is intention, which dispels slackness and excitement. The antidote to application is equanimity: whenever one is inclined to move, it settles the mind unmoving.

Thus cultivating together with the eight formations of abandonment, in order to abandon the five faults, is called the base of miraculous power endowed with the meditative absorption of aspiration and the formations of abandonment.

To show that this meditative absorption of aspiration is cultivated in connection with the Four Noble Truths, he spoke the passage beginning, "dwelling in solitude, dwelling in freedom from attachment." Here, regarding the truth of suffering: when one sees the domain of suffering, one wishes to be separated from it, and so the time of dwelling on the truth of suffering is called dwelling in solitude. The truth of the origin has the character of attachment and craving; since it is to be abandoned, the time of focusing on it is called dwelling in freedom from attachment. Cessation is bliss, since all suffering has been abandoned in it; the time of focusing on it is called dwelling in cessation. The truth of the path actualizes the truth of cessation and abandons all suffering; focusing on it is called being directed toward relinquishment.

Further, to teach the manner of cultivation by which the opposing side of aspiration and the rest, namely the mind's drawing inward and its distraction, together with their causes, is to be fully abandoned, he said, "He cultivates thinking, 'May my aspiration be neither too weak nor too tightly held.'" Here, the cause of the mind's drawing inward is that, lacking insight, the mind draws inward under the sway of sleep, torpor and laziness and becomes slack. Since that must be abandoned, he said, "May my aspiration not be too weak." The cause of the mind's distraction is that, lacking the perception of impurity, the mind scatters toward objects by grasping at the signs of purity and the like. Since that too must be abandoned, he said, "not too tightly held." As for the antidotes to these two: to abandon the slackness and weakness of the mind, one cultivates insight.

To abandon the mind's distraction and its being too tightly held, one cultivates calm abiding, or the contemplation of the body as impure.

To teach the second basis of miraculous power, he said, "endowed with the samādhi of diligence and the formations of abandonment," and so on. When, for the sake of not generating evil, nonvirtuous dharmas that have not yet arisen, abandoning those that have arisen, generating virtuous dharmas that have not yet arisen, and increasing those that have arisen, one attains one-pointedness of mind through unbroken diligence and constant exertion, that is called the samādhi of diligence. "Endowed with the formations of abandonment" and the rest are as explained before.

To teach the third basis of miraculous power, he said, "endowed with the samādhi of mind and the formations of abandonment," and so on. When, relying on the seeds of samādhi cultivated in other lifetimes, one cultivates samādhi in this life, and when, from cultivating it here, the power of each later moment becomes greater through the force of each earlier one, so that, relying on the growth of the seeds of samādhi, one attains one-pointedness of mind without needing to strive, that is called the samādhi of mind. The rest should be understood as explained above.

To teach the fourth basis of miraculous power, he said, "endowed with the samādhi of investigation and the formations of abandonment," and so on. When, in discerning the characteristics of the dharmas taught in the Dharma teachings of others, one is free of doubt and the mind becomes one-pointed, that is called the samādhi of investigation. Alternatively, when one has found the meaning of the object to be meditated upon by analyzing and searching it out, and the mind then rests one-pointedly on that very thing, that is called the samādhi of investigation. The rest should be understood as explained above.

Here too it is set out under five headings: the object, the nature, the companions, the cultivation, and the result of cultivation. Among these, the object of the bases of miraculous power is the domain of samādhi, or else the miraculous feats to be accomplished through the perfection of samādhi, such as the miraculous eye and ear. What is their nature? It has the characteristic of aspiration, of diligence, of mind, and of investigation. What are their companions? They are the mind and mental factors associated with those four samādhis: desire, non-attachment, non-aversion, and so on. What is their cultivation? It is habituation to the eight formations of abandonment. What is the result of cultivation? It is obtaining whatever one wishes. To conclude the teaching on the four bases of miraculous power, he said, "Monks, these are the four bases of miraculous power."

After teaching the bases of miraculous power, he teaches the five faculties. Wishing to explain them in the order in which they were listed, he said, "Therein, monks, what are the five faculties?" and so on. To set out one by one the names of what was counted as "the five faculties," he spoke from "the faculty of faith" up to "the faculty of wisdom."

Therein, conviction, clear confidence, and longing directed respectively toward what exists, what possesses good qualities, and what is within one's power: that is the faculty of faith. Its characteristic is this: toward what exists there is conviction; toward good qualities the mind is clear and confident; toward what is possible longing arises. Therefore, since longing and aspiration are one in meaning, the faculty of faith governs the samādhi of aspiration. Diligence in accomplishing virtue and abandoning nonvirtue is the faculty of diligence; therefore the faculty of diligence governs the samādhi of diligence. The mind's seeing clearly, and not forgetting, the object on which samādhi is to be cultivated is the faculty of mindfulness; therefore the faculty of mindfulness governs the samādhi of mind. The mind's resting one-pointedly and not being distracted outward is the faculty of samādhi; it too governs the samādhi of mind itself. Discerning the characteristics of dharmas is the faculty of wisdom; therefore the faculty of wisdom governs the samādhi of investigation.

Alternatively, when one has faith and is convinced that saṃsāra is fraught with faults and that nirvāṇa is endowed with good qualities, one undertakes diligence in order to escape from saṃsāra. That conviction is the faculty of faith. Undertaking diligence is the faculty of diligence. Once diligence has been undertaken, higher moral discipline and higher wisdom arise. Therein, mindfulness of the characteristics of what is to be done and what is not to be done is the faculty of mindfulness. The training in higher mind is the faculty of samādhi. The training in higher wisdom is the faculty of wisdom.

Alternatively, they are explained in terms of the four limbs of stream-entry set out in this very sūtra. Abiding in the four kinds of faith born of thorough understanding is the faculty of faith. Abiding in the four right abandonments is the faculty of diligence. Abiding in the four applications of mindfulness is the faculty of mindfulness. Abiding in the four dhyānas is the faculty of samādhi. Knowing the four noble truths is the faculty of wisdom.

The meaning of "faculty" is "that which exercises governance." Over what does each govern? Faith, being the cause for the arising of supramundane dharmas, governs diligence, mindfulness, wisdom and samādhi, because those dharmas arise through the power of faith. Diligence in turn governs the three: mindfulness, samādhi and wisdom. Mindfulness in turn governs the two: wisdom and samādhi. Samādhi in turn governs wisdom. Wisdom governs the actualization of supramundane dharmas. Just as consciousness arises through the power of the faculties such as the eye, so too these dharmas are called faculties because each one gives rise to the next.

Alternatively, "faith" is aspiration toward and faith in the Three Jewels, free of any wavering. Alternatively, faith is the clarity of mind that arises from rejoicing wholeheartedly in good qualities and in those in whom good qualities reside. Alternatively, faith is conviction that from virtuous and nonvirtuous karma one experiences the results of happiness and suffering. Diligence is the mind's enthusiasm, with neither body nor mind flagging, for abandoning nonvirtuous dharmas and actualizing virtuous dharmas.

Alternatively, the actions of body and speech by which one strives at excellent deeds are called diligence. Thus striving to commit evil, nonvirtuous dharmas, being laziness itself, is not diligence.

Not forgetting a thing experienced before is mindfulness. Alternatively, the object of focus being clear to the mind is mindfulness. Alternatively, keeping the sign that one has taken to mind from slipping away, and, if it does slip away, bringing it back into direct presence again, is mindfulness. Alternatively, mindfulness is of four kinds: the mindfulness that holds the words without forgetting them; the mindfulness that, from holding the words, holds them without failing to act on their meaning; the mindfulness that, from holding the meaning, does not give up attending to it continually; and that which, from continual attention, makes the meaning vividly present and sees it: this is mindfulness.

The nine ways of settling the mind are samādhi. Alternatively, samādhi is the mind's flowing as one single stream, unshaken by the winds of the afflictions, like a lamp that does not flicker in a windless room.

Fully understanding the four truths correctly, just as they are, is wisdom. Alternatively, in the language of India wisdom is called prajñā. pra has the sense of "thoroughly," and jñā the sense of "knowing." Thoroughly knowing suffering, thoroughly abandoning its origin, actualizing cessation, and thoroughly cultivating the path: this is wisdom.

Therein, the faculty of wisdom belongs to the side of insight. Since wisdom arises in those who possess mindfulness, the faculty of mindfulness is its cause. The faculty of samādhi belongs to the side of calm abiding. Since those who continually undertake diligence become one-pointed of mind, the faculty of diligence is its cause. Since everything arises from faith as its cause, faith is the cause of both calm abiding and insight.

Alternatively, faith stands for the aggregate of moral discipline, because those who have faith guard their moral discipline. The two faculties of diligence and samādhi teach the aggregate of samādhi together with its cause, because diligence is the cause of samādhi. Mindfulness and wisdom teach the aggregate of wisdom together with its cause, because mindfulness is the cause of the aggregate of wisdom.

Therein, the objects of the five faculties are five: awakening, conduct, the Dharma one has heard, calm abiding, and insight. The object of the faculty of faith is awakening, because through faith clear confidence arises chiefly toward the knowledge of exhaustion and the knowledge of non-arising. The object of the faculty of diligence is conduct, because through diligence one accomplishes the three trainings. The faculty of mindfulness takes as its object the teachings one has heard, because through mindfulness one does not forget the meaning of the Dharma. The faculty of samādhi takes calm abiding as its object, because it applies itself exclusively to the mind's becoming constantly one-pointed. The faculty of wisdom takes insight as its object, because it devotes itself chiefly to the aim of knowing the specific and general characteristics of dharmas.

Why is the order of the faculties arranged in this way? Having developed faith in the result, nirvāṇa, one undertakes diligence; so diligence comes after faith. For those who have undertaken diligence it is impossible for mindfulness to lapse; so mindfulness comes after diligence. When mindfulness does not lapse, the mind becomes one-pointed; so the faculty of samādhi comes after mindfulness. When the mind has become one-pointed, wisdom arises correctly, just as things are; so wisdom is explained after samādhi.

To set out in order the meaning of the five faculties as the sūtra makes it clear, he said, "Therein, what is the faculty of faith?" and so on. Therein, faith and conviction in four things is faith. What are the four? Faith in the mundane right view that operates in saṃsāra is clear confidence; this is the first. "He relies on the maturation of karma" is the second. "Whatever actions he performs, he will experience the maturation of each of those actions individually" is the third. "He does not commit evil actions even for the sake of his life" is the fourth.

As for "operating in saṃsāra": the view regarding the dharmas that exist in saṃsāra, or that belong to saṃsāra, namely "this world exists and the world beyond exists," is called the mundane right view that operates in saṃsāra. When one has the mundane view operating in saṃsāra, which sees that since this life exists a later life exists, that is called faith. "He" means that person who possesses faith. "Relies on the maturation of karma" means accepting that virtuous and nonvirtuous karma have their maturation. "Whatever actions he performs" means whatever actions, virtuous, nonvirtuous and so on, are done by whatever person. "He will experience the maturation of each of those actions individually" means being convinced that when a person does virtuous actions he will experience their maturation as happiness, and when a person does nonvirtuous actions he will experience their maturation as suffering; and that the very one who did an action experiences it, while its maturation is not experienced by anyone else. "He does not commit evil actions even for the sake of his life" is said of that person who sees that this life and later lives exist, sees that karma and the maturation of karma exist, and sees that the maturation of any action is experienced by the one who did it. Being convinced that if one commits evil, nonvirtuous dharmas one will experience suffering as the result, one does not commit evils such as taking what is not given even for the sake of one's life: this is called the faculty of faith.

To teach the faculty of diligence, he said, "Therein, what is the faculty of diligence?" and so on. "In those dharmas that have become the faculty of faith" means "in those four dharmas in which one has come to have faith through the faculty of faith." "Perfectly accomplished through the faculty of diligence" means that, in those dharmas in which one has come to have faith through the faculty of faith, one abandons the nonvirtuous side and accomplishes the virtuous side through the faculty of diligence; that is the faculty of diligence.

To teach the faculty of mindfulness, he said, "Therein, what is the faculty of mindfulness?" and so on.

As for the words "in those dharmas that are perfectly accomplished through the faculty of diligence": through the faculty of diligence one abandons nonvirtuous dharmas and accomplishes virtuous dharmas, and with regard to the virtuous dharmas thus established, the faculty of mindfulness keeps those virtuous dharmas from becoming unclear to the mind and from being forgotten; this is called the faculty of mindfulness.

To teach the faculty of samādhi, he said, "Therein, what is the faculty of samādhi?" and so on. "The dharmas that the faculty of mindfulness does not let go to waste" are the virtuous dharmas that the faculty of mindfulness has kept from being forgotten. "In those dharmas the faculty of samādhi makes the mind one-pointed" means that, with regard to the dharmas the faculty of mindfulness has kept from being forgotten, the faculty of samādhi abandons distraction and focuses on them continually and one-pointedly.

To teach the faculty of wisdom, he said, "Therein, what is the faculty of wisdom?" and so on. "The dharmas on which the faculty of samādhi has made the mind one-pointed" means that, with regard to the virtuous dharmas that the faculty of mindfulness does not forget, the faculty of samādhi settles the mind continually and one-pointedly. The faculty of wisdom's seeing the individual, specific characteristics of those dharmas is "understanding them individually." Its seeing their general characteristics is called "knowing each of those dharmas directly."

The five faculties too are set out under five headings: the object, the nature, the companions, the cultivation, and the result of cultivation. The object of the five faculties is the four noble truths, because even at the stage of the faculties one focuses on those four. Their nature is the very essence of faith, diligence, mindfulness, samādhi and wisdom. Their companions are the other minds and mental factors associated with those dharmas. As for cultivation: through the faculty of faith one cultivates the aspect of full conviction in the four truths. Through the faculty of diligence one cultivates the aspect of diligence that turns one toward the truths. Through mindfulness one cultivates the aspect of not forgetting the truths. Through samādhi one cultivates the aspect of not being distracted from the truths. Through wisdom one cultivates the aspect of realizing their specific and general characteristics. The result is bringing about the realization of the four truths. In the context of the powers, the object and the rest are just the same; they are distinguished only in that they are not overpowered by the adverse side.

To conclude the teaching on the five faculties, he said, "Monks, these are the faculties."

After the five faculties he teaches the five powers, and wishing to explain them in the order in which they were listed, he said, "Therein, monks, what are the five powers?" and so on. The five faculties themselves, once they have become uncrushable and unshakable, are set forth as the five powers. As for the power of faith: for those whose faith has become uncrushable, their faith cannot be turned back by any god or māra in this world, by Brahmā, or by anyone else in the world, whether one in accord with the Dharma or one entangled by afflictions; hence it is the power of faith. The four dharmas, diligence and the rest, that arise from the cause of having actualized the power of faith are also called powers. Because they are endowed with power, they destroy the power of the four kinds of māras, which act as Māra's allies, and actualize the exhaustion of the defilements; hence they are called powers.

Alternatively, at the stage of the faculties, the factors adverse to the five faculties, namely lack of faith, laziness, lapses of mindfulness, distraction and lack of vigilance, still mingle in and arise from time to time. When one no longer lets the adverse factors arise even occasionally, these are called the powers that have arisen as one portion of certain antidotes.

Therein, an intention toward the Three Jewels that cannot be broken is the power of faith. Continually cultivating the factors of awakening is the power of diligence. Never forgetting the Dharma one has heard and reflected on is the power of mindfulness. Not falling under the sway of dullness and agitation is the power of samādhi. Knowing any dharma whatsoever without doubt is the power of wisdom.

Therein, the power of faith abandons attachment to one's own body, which obstructs the attainment of the path of seeing. The power of diligence abandons attachment to one's possessions. The power of mindfulness abandons the mind that dwells in thought on what was done before and on paths that are to be censured. The power of samādhi overcomes the afflictions that arise in gross form, such as dullness and agitation. The power of wisdom uproots all habitual tendencies.

After teaching the five powers he teaches the seven factors of awakening, and wishing to explain them in the order in which they were listed, he said, "Therein, monks, what are the seven factors of awakening?" and so on.

Therein, awakening is the attainment of the knowledge of exhaustion and of non-arising after fully understanding the four truths. Awakening, distinguished according to the person, is set out as three kinds: the awakening of the śrāvakas, and perfect, complete awakening. The dharmas that serve as companions in attaining awakening, or that serve as its support, are the factors of awakening, just as the feet, the hands and so on, which serve as the body's companions or its support, are called the limbs of the body.

Here, which is the principal factor, since these seven are set forth as factors of awakening? The reply: the discrimination of dharmas, being in its very nature the direct realization of suchness, is set forth both as the principal factor and as a factor. The six, mindfulness and the rest, are companions that bring about conformity with it, and so they are set forth as seven factors.

Therein, keeping the dharmas of the thirty-seven factors of awakening that one has cultivated and actualized from going to waste and from being forgotten is called the awakening factor of right mindfulness. The meaning of "dwelling in seclusion" and the rest should be understood as explained before. The awakening factor of the discrimination of dharmas is joined as a pair with the accumulation of insight: it is that by which, on the path of seeing, one is to know the characteristics of the afflicted side and of the purified side without confusing them.

The awakening factor of diligence has the characteristic of taking up the higher, distinguished qualities and abandoning the faults that obscure good qualities. The awakening factor of joy is the suffusing delight of body and mind that is attained on first seeing the characteristic of suchness, the selflessness of the person. The awakening factor of pliancy is the body's becoming serviceable and the mind's becoming serviceable. Because body and mind have been transformed into a body and mind free of all dysfunction, what hinders them in engaging in their own activities is abandoned; and because the mind's basis has been transformed, it engages its object of focus without obstruction. The awakening factor of samādhi is the abiding, constant and unimpaired, of the one-pointed mind that focuses on suchness. The awakening factor of equanimity is abiding constantly without effort and without affliction, since all faults such as dullness and agitation have been abandoned.

The seven factors of awakening are set out as five kinds of factor: the factor of support, the factor of nature, the factor of deliverance, the factor of benefit, and the factor of freedom from affliction. Therein, the awakening factor of mindfulness is the factor of support, because by the power of mindfulness one is kept from straying from whatever object one focuses on to anything else. The awakening factor of the discrimination of dharmas is the factor of nature, because it is by nature the wisdom that knows the specific and general characteristics of the dharmas of awakening. The awakening factor of diligence is the factor that brings deliverance, because by the force of diligence one passes completely beyond the stage of ordinary beings. The awakening factor of joy is the factor of benefit, because it benefits body and mind. The three awakening factors of pliancy, samādhi and equanimity are the factors of freedom from affliction, because those three act as antidotes that destroy the afflictions.

These in turn are set out as three: the cause, the support and the nature of freedom from affliction. Therein, the cause of freedom from affliction is the awakening factor of pliancy, because pliancy acts as the cause that destroys dysfunction and the afflictions that are its causes. How is that so? Thus: the unserviceability of body and mind is dysfunction. When it is present, one-pointedness of mind does not arise, and so the mind becomes distracted. Once the mind is distracted, afflictions such as attachment arise. Therefore dysfunction is the cause of affliction. Pliancy is the serviceability of body and mind, and since it dispels dysfunction, it is set forth as the cause of freedom from affliction. The support of freedom from affliction is the awakening factor of samādhi. Thus, when body and mind are serviceable, the mind comes to rest in equipoise. When the mind rests in equipoise and knowledge arises in accordance with reality, just as things are, affliction is abandoned. Therefore samādhi is the basis of freedom from affliction. The nature of freedom from affliction is the awakening factor of equanimity, because equanimity abandons the afflictions and abides constantly in harmony with freedom from affliction.

These seven factors are further gathered into three: the side of calm abiding, the side of insight, and the side of both. Therein, the three, discrimination of dharmas, diligence and joy, are on the side of insight. The three awakening factors of pliancy, samādhi and equanimity are on the side of calm abiding. Mindfulness is on both sides, and is called "going everywhere."

The factors of awakening too are set out under five headings: the object, the nature, the companions, the cultivation, and the result of cultivation. Therein, the object of the factors of awakening is the four noble truths, because on the path of seeing one knows and focuses on the four truths in accordance with reality, just as they are. Their nature is mindfulness, discrimination of dharmas, diligence, joy, pliancy and equanimity. Their companions are the minds and the other mental factors, such as faith, associated with those factors of awakening. As for cultivation, it is the cultivation of the awakening factor of mindfulness: "he cultivates it as dwelling in seclusion, dwelling in dispassion, dwelling in cessation, and dedicated to relinquishment," and so on; this is said to be the cultivation of the four truths themselves. The result of cultivation is the abandonment of the afflictions to be abandoned by seeing.

After teaching the seven factors of awakening he teaches the eight limbs of the path, and therefore, wishing to explain them in the order in which they were listed, he said, "Monks, what are the eight limbs of the noble path?" and so on. Therein, "noble" refers to persons still in training, such as those at the fruit of stream-entry. Because, in order for them to abandon without remainder the afflictions to be abandoned by meditation and to actualize liberation, it is endowed with eight limbs and is a path comprised of eight limbs, it is called the path of the eight limbs of the noble path.

As for "namely, right view," and so on: having attained, at the stage of the seven factors of awakening, the understanding of the four noble truths, focusing on that very understanding is right view. Thinking correctly about the dharmas that arise with that right view as their chief, abandoning thoughts of desire, malice and harm, and thinking of freedom from desire, freedom from malice and freedom from harm, is called right thought; whenever one wishes to apply the mind to some thought, one thinks about dharmas of that kind. If ever one wishes to apply the mind to speaking, one first makes right view itself manifest, and then speaks to others in conversation about those very dharmas that examine virtuous dharmas; that is called right speech.

If one comes to need requisites such as Dharma robes, alms food, bedding, seats, and medicine as a remedy for illness, then while seeking them, one acts with vigilance when going out and coming back; and when looking ahead and looking around, stretching out and drawing in the limbs, holding the Dharma robes and the alms bowl, eating, drinking and so forth, walking, sitting, lying down and so forth, one acts with vigilance. That is called right action. Seeking the Dharma robes, the medicine as a remedy for illness and the rest, one gives up seeking them through deceit and guile and seeks them in accordance with the Dharma. That is called right livelihood.

When, relying on right view, right thought, right speech, right action and right livelihood, one engages in meditation, one's diligence, in the form of aspiration, vigor, exertion and the like, is unbroken, and one sets about abandoning the afflictions to be abandoned by meditation. That is right effort. The characteristic of clearly holding, without distortion, the things to be gathered in mind, in reliance on the four applications of mindfulness, is called right mindfulness. The nine ways of settling the mind are called right samādhi.

These eight limbs of the noble path are, in brief, set out as two kinds: what is to be done at the time of activity, and what is to be done at the time of abiding. The time of activity is when, apart from meditating in samādhi, one is engaged in postures such as walking and sitting. At that time one should abide in right speech, right action and right livelihood. "At the time of abiding" means when one has entered the absorption of samādhi. Then, too, one does two things: one cultivates calm abiding and one cultivates insight. Therein, the three, right view, right thought and right effort, are the side of insight together with its retinue: right view is insight itself, and right effort and right thought are its side. Right mindfulness and right samādhi are the side of calm abiding together with its retinue: right samādhi is the very gateway of calm abiding, and mindfulness belongs to its side. Thus, in order to abandon without remainder the latent tendencies to be abandoned by meditation, and to actualize the sublime fruit of the arhat, persons still in training rely on right speech, right action and pure livelihood and from time to time cultivate calm abiding and insight.

Alternatively, the absence of non-knowing, of doubting knowledge and of mistaken knowledge is right view. Therein, non-knowing is not having understood the characteristics of the four noble truths. Doubting knowledge is the doubt that wonders whether the four noble truths are so or not. Mistaken knowledge is grasping in an inverted way, taking what is impermanent as permanent and so on.

As for "right thought," in the language of India it occurs as samyag saṃkalpa. samyak has the sense of "undistorted." saṃ has the sense of "even." kalpa has the sense of "thinking things through." Thinking through the dharmas seen by right view without distortion, in accord with the nature of dharmas as empty, impermanent and so on, is right thought.

Abandoning the four faults, such as lying, is right speech. Turning away from the three bad bodily actions, such as killing, is right action. Not making one's living by evil, nonvirtuous means is right livelihood. For those living in a household, such as lay practitioners, it is turning away from trades such as selling poison and weapons. For those who have gone forth, it is turning away from flattery, hypocrisy and the like. Diligence in abandoning evil, nonvirtuous dharmas and in accomplishing virtuous dharmas that is neither too intense nor too slack, but balanced exertion, is right effort.

Not being confused about the object to be focused on, because the mind has become clear, is right mindfulness. Mindfulness arises from six causes. The six causes are seeing something similar, the dissimilar, habituation, understanding things as one in meaning, the power of calm abiding, and the power of causes. Therein, mindfulness arises from seeing something similar, as when, on seeing someone who resembles one's child, one remembers one's child. Mindfulness also arises from what is unlike, as when one remembers heat and cold. Mindfulness also arises from habituation: from having made oneself habituated to reading and so on, mindfulness of whatever one was habituated to arises later as well. Mindfulness arises from understanding things as one in meaning, as when, on seeing a mango fruit, one recalls its sourness. Mindfulness also arises through the power of calm abiding: yogins who have the recollection of former abodes, the knowledge of death and rebirth, and the knowledge of the exhaustion of defilements come to recollect their abodes in former lives. Mindfulness also arises through the power of causes, because those endowed with merit come to remember other lives.

When, having abandoned all distraction and agitation, the mind rests straight or in equipoise, that is right samādhi.

Why are the eight limbs of the noble path explained in this order? Yogins who attain nirvāṇa first look to the four noble truths; therefore right view is taught first. Later, in keeping with what one has seen, there arises the thought, "Is it just so, or not?"; therefore right thought comes after it. As one thinks, so one utters words; therefore right speech comes after that. As one has spoken, so one later acts; therefore right action comes after right speech. Those who act rightly come to have pure livelihood; therefore right livelihood comes after right action. Those endowed with moral discipline undertake diligence in the virtuous side, and their diligence increases; therefore right effort comes after right livelihood. Since it is impossible for those endowed with diligence to have lapses of mindfulness, right mindfulness comes after that. Those endowed with mindfulness become one-pointed of mind, so right samādhi is explained last.

Why is the noble path explained as having eight limbs? There are eight kinds of worldly error, from wrong view up to wrong samādhi; and as their antidote, eight are explained, from right view up to right samādhi.

Alternatively, right view and mindfulness belong to the side of insight; their result is that, being free of ignorance, one is liberated through wisdom. The remaining limbs belong to the side of calm abiding; their result is that, being free of attachment, the mind is liberated. Alternatively, the three, right speech, right action, and right livelihood, are the aggregate of moral discipline; it chiefly cuts the root of non-virtue that is aversion. Right mindfulness and right samādhi are the aggregate of samādhi; it chiefly cuts the root of non-virtue that is attachment. The three, right thought, right effort, and right view, are the aggregate of wisdom; it cuts the root of non-virtue that is delusion.

Here, these eight limbs of the noble path are arranged as six limbs. The six limbs are: the limb of ascertainment, the limb that brings others to attainment, the limb that inspires confidence in others, the limb that purifies the obscuration of the afflictions, the limb that purifies the obscuration of the secondary afflictions, and the limb that thoroughly purifies the obscuration to attaining special qualities.

Among these, right view is the limb of ascertainment, because, having realized on the path of seeing that there is no self, sentient being, life principle, and so on, it ascertains, with regard to that very realization, the nature of the path of seeing, thinking, "I have realized these in such a way." Right thought is the limb that brings others to attainment, because, since one does not utter words without having examined and investigated, one examines the phenomena just as they were seen by right view and then explains precisely that to others. The three, right speech, right action, and right livelihood, are the limb that inspires confidence in others, because they inspire confidence that one's view, moral discipline, and livelihood, respectively, are pure.

Here, right speech gives rise to confidence that one's view is completely pure. How so? When, by means of right speech, one explains to others the phenomena as one has understood them, one answers the objections that others raise and teaches the explanation clearly and without distortion. Considering this, others come to have confidence, thinking, "This person settles the meaning of the Dharma, is free of contradiction, and teaches clearly, and so has understood reality itself." Right action gives rise to confidence that one's moral discipline is completely pure: here, considering that one does not commit misdeeds such as taking life even for the sake of one's own life, others come to have confidence, thinking, "This person possesses the moral discipline pleasing to the noble ones." Right livelihood gives rise to confidence that it is completely pure. How so? Others come to have confidence, thinking, "Since this person seeks even robes and the like in accordance with the Dharma and not through deceit and pretense, and has given up keeping many possessions, this person's livelihood is completely pure."

Here, through right view one is endowed with clear realization, because at the time of the path of seeing one knows without distortion the nature of reality that has been realized. Through right thought one sounds the lion's roar, because one explains to others without hindrance the phenomena just as one has realized them. The three, right speech, right action, and right livelihood, make clearly manifest one's own realization and one's commitment.

Right effort is the limb that purifies the obscuration of the afflictions, because through it one abandons all the afflictions that are to be abandoned by meditation. Right mindfulness is the limb that purifies the secondary afflictions, because, having become aware of laxity and excitement arising in the mind, one afterwards gives them no opportunity to arise. Right samādhi is the limb that thoroughly purifies the obscuration to attaining special qualities, because samādhi purifies the obscurations to attaining the five superknowledges, such as the divine eye, and so brings about the attainment of those qualities.

These eight limbs of the noble path are also arranged in five aspects: object, nature, companions, cultivation, and the result of cultivation. Here, the object of the paths is this: after the path of seeing has arisen, one takes as object, just as it really is, this very truth that was seen at the time of the path of seeing. The nature is the eight limbs, the eight from right view up to right samādhi. The companions are the mind and mental factors associated with them, such as faith and non-attachment. Cultivation is the cultivation of right view as resting on seclusion, resting on dispassion, resting on cessation, and dedicated to relinquishment, and so on. The result of cultivation is ascertainment, bringing others to attainment, inspiring confidence in others, purifying the obscuration of the afflictions, purifying the obscuration of the secondary afflictions, and purifying the obscuration to attaining special qualities.

The Vaibhāṣika śrāvakas hold that this eightfold noble path is cultivated on the path of seeing; but since the system of the Sautrāntika śrāvakas holds it to be the path of meditation, it should be understood as solely the path of meditation.

After teaching the eightfold noble path, the sūtra teaches the sixteenfold mindfulness of breathing out and breathing in. Wishing to explain it just as it was taught, the Blessed One said, "What is the sixteenfold mindfulness of breathing out and breathing in?" and so on.

Here, wind moving outward is breathing out; moving inward is breathing in. The mindfulness that takes as its object the breath going out and the breath drawn in is called mindfulness of breathing out and breathing in; moreover, it is by nature wisdom held by mindfulness. Mindfulness here refers to mindfulness that has become potent, the power of mindfulness: wisdom held by the power of mindfulness takes as its object the wind going out and coming in. Breathing out, moreover, is of two kinds. What are the two? Breathing out and the intermediate breath. Breathing in is likewise of two kinds: breathing in and the intermediate breath. Breathing in is the wind entering inward as far as the navel immediately after the breath has gone out.

The intermediate breath of breathing in is this: after the breath has entered as far as the navel and ceased, for a short while no breath comes forth, and then, without a long pause, an extremely subtle breath arises. The same is to be said of breathing out and the intermediate breath of breathing out, but there is this difference: breathing out is the wind going outward, coming forth from the region of the navel as far as the mouth and nose, and beyond that going out a span, a cubit, and so on. The intermediate breath is this: after the outgoing breath has ceased, for some while no strong breath comes forth, and during that pause, for a single instant, a faint wisp of wind comes forth.

The source of breathing out and breathing in is also of two kinds: the karma that projects it, and the cavity located in the region of the navel. Above that, moreover, there are the cavities of the body.

The basis of breathing out and breathing in is also of two kinds: the body and the mind. Why is that? Because breathing out and breathing in arise in dependence on the body and in dependence on the mind. If they arose in dependence on the body alone, it would absurdly follow that breathing out and breathing in also exist for those who have entered the absorption without perception, for those who have entered the absorption of cessation, and for those born among the gods without perception; since they do not occur for these, they do not depend on the body alone. If they arose in dependence on the mind alone, it would absurdly follow that breathing out and breathing in also occur for beings who have entered the formless absorptions and for beings born in the formless realm; since these have no breathing out and breathing in, they do not depend on the mind alone. Moreover, in certain situations it is not right that they occur in dependence on body and mind: if it were certain that they occur whenever there is dependence on body and mind, it would absurdly follow that breathing out and breathing in also exist for beings absorbed in the four dhyānas and for beings at the time of the kalala stage and the arbuda stage of the embryo; but they do not exist for these. Therefore it is established, as appropriate, that breathing out and breathing in occur in dependence on body and mind at times other than those.

The movement of breathing out and breathing in is also of two kinds. What are the two? Breathing out moves upward; breathing in moves downward.

The places of breathing out and breathing in are also of two kinds: the coarse cavities and openings, and the subtle cavities and openings. Here, the coarse cavities and openings are where the breath comes forth from the region of the navel upward through the openings of the mouth, nose, and so on, and where it travels through the cavities from the openings of the mouth and nose downward as far as the region of the navel. The subtle cavities and openings are all the pores of the hair belonging to the body.

The synonyms for breathing out and breathing in are four: wind, the bodily formation, breathing out and breathing in, and the breath moving out and moving in. Of these, the synonym "wind" is shared with the non-Buddhists, worldly people, and everyone else. The remaining three are not shared, because those synonyms are known in this teaching alone.

The faults that enter into breathing out and breathing in are also of two kinds: applying oneself too loosely, and applying oneself with too much strain. Here, applying oneself too loosely means that laziness arises and the mind is overpowered by sleep and torpor, or else the mind afterwards becomes distracted. Applying oneself with too much strain gives rise to imbalance of body and imbalance of mind. How does imbalance of body arise? When, straining forcefully, one fixes tightly on breathing out and breathing in, unbearable winds arise in the body, and it comes to be greatly stirred by wind. Because it is greatly stirred by wind, agitation arises in the major and minor limbs; therefore it is called bodily excitement. When the wind that has thus agitated the body increases, it spreads within the body into those major and minor limbs; this is imbalance of body. How does imbalance of mind arise? From fixing on the object with strain, the mind becomes distracted. As the distraction of the mind increases, it is overwhelmed by unhappiness and turmoil; this is called the arising of imbalance of mind.

Here, one should understand that there are five ways of becoming familiar with this breathing out and breathing in: becoming thoroughly familiar through counting, becoming thoroughly familiar through skill in the aggregates, becoming thoroughly familiar through engaging with dependent origination, becoming thoroughly familiar through engaging with the truths, and becoming thoroughly familiar in sixteen aspects.

What is becoming thoroughly familiar through counting? In brief it is of four kinds: counting one by one, counting two as one, counting in forward order, and counting in reverse order. What is counting one by one? When the breath has entered inward, then, with mindfulness that takes breathing out and breathing in as its object, one counts "one." When the in-breath has ceased and the out-breath has arisen and passed away, then one counts "two." Counting in this manner up to ten, this is counting two as one.
