What is counting in forward order? It is counting, whether by the counting one by one or the counting two as one explained above, straight through in sequence; this is counting in forward order. What is counting in reverse order? Having reached ten, one counts "one"; at nine one counts "two"; at eight one counts "three"; and so on, until at one one counts "ten." This is counting in reverse order.

When that yogin, through having become thoroughly habituated to counting one by one, counting two as one, counting in forward order, or counting in reverse order, is at that point able to count with the mind undistracted, entirely without distraction, then beyond that one should practise another, special kind of counting. What is that other, special counting? Combining the counting one by one or the counting two as one, one counts "one"

in this way. In the counting two as one, the out-breath and the in-breath, two pairs making four, are counted as one. In the counting one by one, one counts each out-breath and each in-breath singly; here, combining those two into one, one counts in that manner up to ten. When one has become habituated to that as well, one makes ten into one and extends the counting: from counting in this way up to ten, one goes on to make a hundred into one and count up to ten. When the yogin engaged in counting is able, making ten into one, to complete the counting up to ten, and the mind is not distracted in between, then by that much one has become habituated to the method of counting. If, while engaged in counting in this way, the mind becomes disturbed or distracted, one should turn back to the beginning and start the counting again.

What is becoming familiar by engaging with the aggregates? For the yogin who relies much on that practice, cultivates it much, and does it much, pliancy of body and pliancy of mind arise. Now, through the arising of pliancy of body and pliancy of mind, one touches one-pointedness of mind, and the mind delights in remaining constantly on its object. Having thus become thoroughly habituated, one engages with the aggregates by way of attending to the entities of the faculties and their objects. How does one engage with them? Thus: by attending to the thought, "The basis of this breathing out and breathing in is the body; it depends on the body," one engages with and realizes the aggregate of form. To hold and experience their very characteristic without forgetting it, by means of mindfulness that takes those out-breaths and in-breaths as its object, is to engage with the aggregate of feeling. One engages with the aggregate of perception thus: "Whatever recognition is present there is the aggregate of perception." One engages with the aggregate of formations thus: "The mindfulness, samādhi, volition, and wisdom present at that time are the aggregate of formations." One engages with the aggregate of consciousness thus: "The mind, mentation, and consciousness at that time are the aggregate of consciousness." Engaging in this way is called thorough familiarity with the aggregates.

When that yogin sees and thoroughly knows them as mere aggregates, and sees and thoroughly knows them as mere formations and mere things, then one fully engages with the characteristic of dependent origination in those very aggregates. How does one engage with it? The yogin investigates in this way. On examining, "On what does this breathing out and breathing in depend? From what condition has it arisen?" one comes to understand as follows; one realizes: "This breathing out and breathing in depends on the body and has arisen from the condition of the body; it depends on the mind and has arisen from the condition of the mind." On examining, "By what condition do body and mind endure?" one realizes: "They endure on the basis of the present life faculty." On examining, "By what condition has the life faculty arisen?" one realizes that it has arisen by the condition of formations, the virtuous and non-virtuous karma of another life. On examining, "By what condition have those former formations arisen?" one realizes that they have arisen by the condition of ignorance. Thus, by the condition of ignorance, formations previously operated; by the condition of the former formations, the life faculty; from the condition of the life faculty, body and consciousness; and from the condition of body and mind, breathing out and breathing in arise. Then, when ignorance ceases, formations cease; when formations cease, the life faculty ceases; when the life faculty ceases, body and mind cease; and when body and mind cease, breathing out and breathing in cease. In this way one engages with dependent origination.

How does one become thoroughly familiar with engaging with the truths? Having thus become greatly familiar with dependent origination, one understands: "These conditioned phenomena are dependently originated, and therefore impermanent." Because they are impermanent, they arise where there was nothing, and having arisen they cease to be; what is like this is subject to birth, subject to aging, and subject to sickness. Whatever is subject to birth, aging, sickness, and death is suffering. Whatever is suffering is without self, without autonomy, and without owner. In this way one engages with the truth of suffering in its aspects of impermanence, suffering, emptiness, and selflessness. Moreover, one thinks: "The condition that produces the formations of suffering, which have become suffering, become disease, and become result, is craving," and so engages with the truth of origin. One engages with the truth of cessation thus: "The complete abandonment of that craving which produces suffering is peaceful and sublime." One also engages with the truth of the path thus: "Knowing in this way, seeing in this way, and understanding in this way is the truth of the path." In this way one has engaged with the four truths.

Having become familiar with the four truths, that yogin, having completely abandoned the afflictions to be abandoned by seeing, then, in order to abandon the afflictions to be abandoned by meditation, becomes thoroughly familiar with the sixteen aspects. What are the sixteen aspects? They are what the sūtra speaks of, beginning with "When mindfully breathing out, one knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am mindfully breathing out,'" and ending with "When breathing in while contemplating relinquishment, one knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in while contemplating relinquishment.' Monks, these are the sixteen mindfulnesses of breathing out and breathing in."

How are those sixteen phrases distinguished? As described above under becoming familiar with the four truths, the trainee who has seen the four truths and has attained meditation that takes the four applications of mindfulness as its object strives at attending to breathing out and breathing in, in order to abandon the remaining fetters to be abandoned by meditation. When, at the time of breathing out, with an unconfused mind, one knows that the breath is going out, mindful of its characteristic just as it goes out, this is called "When mindfully breathing out, one knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am mindfully breathing out.'"

When, at the time the breath is drawn inward, one knows, "With mindfulness free of distraction I am attending mindfully to just this," that is called "When mindfully breathing in, one knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am mindfully breathing in.'" Of the sixteen, this is the first.

When one takes breathing out and breathing in as one's object, this is called "When breathing out long, one knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing out long,'" and knowing the breath as long, and also "When breathing in long, one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in long.'" This is the second. When one attends to the interval of breathing out and the interval of breathing in, this is called the passage from "breathing out short" up to "one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in short.'" This is the third.

Alternatively, when the yogin, attending to breathing out, takes as object how far the breath, having come forth beginning from the region of the navel, goes out through the openings of the mouth and nose, a span, a cubit, a fathom, and so on, this is called "When breathing out long, one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing out long.'" When the yogin, attending to breathing in, takes as object how the breath has gone downward, beginning from the openings of the mouth and nose as far as the navel, this is called "When breathing in long, one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in long.'" When the yogin takes as object the outgoing breath that, beginning from the navel, comes forth as far as the chest and is cut off, this is called "When breathing out short, one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing out short.'" When the yogin, in breathing in as well, takes as object the breath that, from the openings of the mouth and nose, is cut off at the throat or thereabouts, this is called attending to the short out-breath.

When the yogin attends to the breathing out of the coarse cavities and openings, this is called "When breathing out while experiencing the body, one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing out while experiencing the body.'" When the yogin attends to the breathing in through the coarse cavities and openings of breathing in, this is called "When breathing in while experiencing the body, one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in while experiencing the body.'" In substance, to take as object how the breath goes out beginning from the navel, from the intestines, the bowels, the stomach, and so on, is called "thoroughly knowing correctly, just as it is, breathing out while experiencing the body." When one takes as object how the breath, from the openings of the mouth and nose, travels downward to the throat and so on and enters the intestines, the bowels, and so on, this is called "thoroughly knowing correctly, just as it is, breathing in while experiencing the body." This is called the fourth.

When the yogin knows that this breathing out, too, is a bodily formation, depending on the body and abiding in the body, and at that time takes as object how the breath, depending on the body, goes out, this is called "When breathing out while experiencing the bodily formation, one knows correctly, just as it is, that one is experiencing the bodily formation." When the yogin knows that this breathing in, too, is a bodily formation depending on the body, and that it is drawn in depending on the body, then, at the time of taking as object how the breath is drawn in depending on the body, this is called "When breathing in while experiencing the bodily formation, one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in while experiencing the bodily formation.'" This is the fifth.

When the yogin takes as object how the breath goes out from the subtle cavities and openings, such as the hairs and the pores of the body hair, this is "When breathing out while experiencing the whole body, one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing out while experiencing the whole body.'" When the yogin takes as object how the breath enters inward from the subtle openings and cavities, such as those of the body hair, this is called "When breathing in while experiencing the whole body, one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in while experiencing the whole body.'" This is the sixth.

When the out-breath has ceased and the intermediate breath has ceased as well, and the yogin takes as object the empty state in which no further breathing out and breathing in has arisen, and comes to understand it, this is called "When breathing out with my bodily formation calmed, one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing out with my bodily formation calmed.'" Here "calmed" refers to the state and the time free of breathing out. When the in-breath has ceased and the intermediate breath has ceased as well, the yogin's taking as object the time that has become empty, in which no further breathing out and breathing in has arisen, is called "When breathing in with my bodily formation calmed, one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'My bodily formation is calmed.'" This is the seventh.

Alternatively, the meaning of "calmed" is knowing through having made body and mind serviceable by attending to breathing out and breathing in. Or else, as explained above, because one has relied much on attending to those out-breaths and in-breaths, cultivated it much, and done it much: earlier, when one had not yet become familiar with it, at the time of attending to breathing out and breathing in, the mind grew weary and attended as if in discomfort, and the breathing out and breathing in also arose coarse and heavy. Once that has ceased, being free of it, through the power of habituation the breath too moves subtly, and the mind attending to it, without weariness, attends as if endowed with a pleasant touch. Therefore it is said, "breathing out with my bodily formation calmed," and so on.

If, through the power of having thoroughly applied the practice of mindfulness of breathing out and breathing in in this way, that yogin comes to attain the first dhyāna or the second dhyāna, then to know how, by way of attending to breathing out and breathing in, one has attained the first and second dhyānas is called the passage from "When breathing out while experiencing joy" up to "one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in while experiencing joy.'" This is the eighth. If one comes to experience the bliss of the third dhyāna, which is free of joy, then this is called the passage from "When breathing out while experiencing bliss" up to "one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in while experiencing bliss.'" This is the ninth. Since the fourth dhyāna and above are free of the eight faults, from the fourth dhyāna upward there is no practice of mindfulness of breathing out and breathing in.

If that yogin comes to attain the first, second, or third fundamental dhyāna, then that yogin will without doubt attain the not-incapable stage and the samādhi of the preparation for the first dhyāna. Then, relying on those samādhis, one fully discerns whatever one's mind has come to be and however it has arisen: understanding and knowledge arise that thoroughly comprehend, correctly and just as it is, whether it is with attachment or free of attachment, with aversion or free of aversion, with delusion or free of delusion, collected or distracted, slack or uplifted, excited or not excited, calm or not calm, settled in equipoise or not settled in equipoise, well cultivated or not well cultivated, liberated or not liberated. To know, while attending to breathing out and breathing in, of what kind the nature of the mind is as it arises, is called the passage from "I am breathing out while experiencing the mind" up to "one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in while experiencing the mind.'" This is the tenth.

Since breathing out occurs in dependence on the mind, when the yogin takes as object how the breath goes out in dependence on the mind, this is called "When breathing out while experiencing the mental formation, one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing out while experiencing the mental formation.'" Breathing in, too, is drawing the breath in dependence on the mind; at the time one takes as object how the breath is drawn in dependence on the mind, this is called "When breathing in while correctly experiencing the mental formation, one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in while correctly experiencing the mental formation.'" This is the eleventh.

As one thus comes to experience joy or to experience bliss, if by chance, through confusion of mindfulness, the mind is stirred by deluded notions such as "I am," or "The self is mine," or "I shall come to be," or "I shall not come to be," or "I shall come to have form," or "I shall come to be formless," or "I shall come to be with perception, or without perception, or neither with nor without perception," then, having very swiftly recognized it with wisdom, one attends to both breathing out and breathing in, does not let those thoughts remain, and dispels them by thoroughly abandoning them. This is called the passage from "breathing out with my mental formation calmed" up to "one knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in with my mental formation calmed.'" This is the twelfth.

If, while meditating in this way, the mind is overpowered by the obscurations of sleep and dullness, so that the mind becomes unclear, then one should attend to some object that makes the mind joyful and clear, and so uplift and gladden the mind. When one again attends to the movement out and in of the object, this is called "One thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'My mind rejoicing, I breathe in.'" This is the thirteenth.

By thus relying much on that object, cultivating it, and doing much of attending to the very movement of the breath out and in, the obscurations that actively occur, such as torpor and excitement, are put far away. When one thinks, "My obscurations have been dispelled," this is called the passage from "breathing out with my mind liberated" up to "one knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in with my mind liberated.'" This is the fourteenth.

If, while meditating in this way, one sees that one has become obscured by the obscuration of excitement and the obscuration of doubt, then, attending to both breathing out and breathing in, one gathers in the mind, settles it inwardly, and pacifies it. At the time the mind is settled one-pointedly, this is called the passage from "breathing out with my mind settled in equipoise" up to "one thoroughly knows correctly, just as it is, 'I am breathing in with my mind settled in equipoise.'" This is the fifteenth.

When that yogin's mind has become liberated from the obscurations, there still remain the residual latent tendencies that interrupt the remainder of the meditation; in order to abandon them, one actualizes the path of meditation on the four truths. One does this as follows: one first realizes the truth of suffering, thinking, "Formations are of the nature of impermanence." Therefore it is said, "Likewise contemplating impermanence," and so on.

Previously, that yogin became habituated to calm abiding in dependence on the first, second, and third dhyānas and on the not-incapable stage. Now, through contemplating impermanence, one becomes habituated to insight. Since that yogin's mind has thus become habituated to calm abiding and insight, one thereafter turns one's aspiration toward the three elements. The three elements are the element of dispassion, the element of cessation, and the element of relinquishment. Here, aspiring to abandon all the formations to be abandoned by meditation is contemplating dispassion.

Aspiring to abandon all the aggregates is contemplating cessation. Aspiring to abandon all the formations to be abandoned by seeing is called contemplating relinquishment. Being thus free of confusion when breathing out and breathing in means this: having come to know correctly, just as it is, "I breathe out," in the manner of one contemplating relinquishment, and having come to know correctly, just as it is, "I breathe in," in the manner of one contemplating relinquishment, it is called "knowing thoroughly, correctly, just as it is, 'contemplating relinquishment, I breathe in.'" Alternatively, to view the mass of suffering as something whose nature is to perish is contemplating impermanence. To view the origin as something whose nature is to be abandoned is contemplating dispassion. To view cessation as something whose nature is to be realized is contemplating cessation. To view the truth of the path as that which abandons the afflictions is called contemplating relinquishment.

To conclude the teaching on the sixteenfold meditation on the breath moving out and in, the sūtra says, "Monks, these are the sixteen mindfulnesses of breathing out and breathing in."

After teaching the sixteen aspects of the breath moving out and in, the sūtra wishes to explain the four limbs of stream-entry in the order in which they were listed, and so it says, "What, then, are the four limbs of stream-entry?" and so on. Here "stream" refers to the noble path. Just as the stream of a river flows into the ocean, so that path flows into the city of nirvāṇa; therefore the path is the stream. To have entered that path, or to have attained that path, is stream-entry. The four qualities that serve as companions to the path are called the four limbs of stream-entry; they are also called the four kinds of faith of the noble ones that is born of understanding.

Of these, the passage from "The noble śrāvaka recollects that Blessed One thus" up to "having understood the Buddha, he is endowed with faith" is the first limb of stream-entry. The passage from "The sublime Dharma well spoken by the Blessed One" up to "he is endowed with faith in the sublime Dharma" is the second limb of stream-entry. The passage from "The Saṅgha of the Blessed One's śrāvakas" up to "the eight kinds of persons" is the third limb. The passage from "Thus, being perfectly endowed with faith in the Saṅgha of the Blessed One's śrāvakas" up to "he is endowed with the moral discipline dear to the noble ones and uncensured by the wise" is the fourth limb.

As for "noble śrāvaka": those persons in training, the stream-enterers and so on, who have entered upon the abandonment of attachment, the abandonment of aversion, and the abandonment of delusion, and those who have abandoned attachment, aversion, and delusion, the arhats who abide in the fruit of no more training, are the noble śrāvakas. The meaning of śrāvaka, "hearer," is this: because their seeing of the truths depends on hearing the scriptures and instructions of another, they are hearers. This is to be connected with the words, "They give rise to unshakable faith in the four qualities."

To teach the first limb of stream-entry, the sūtra says, "That Blessed One," and so on. In brief, the first limb teaches six aspects: the vanquishing of obstructors, the perfection of the Teacher, that by which he attained it, how he went, the activity of the Teacher, and those by whom he is embraced.

"Blessed One" teaches the vanquishing of obstructors. Here the obstructors are the evil Māra, who creates obstacles to attaining unsurpassed awakening. Because at the time of his perfect and complete awakening he vanquished the evil Māra and the gods' sons of the desire realm who belong to Māra's retinue, together with their followers, he is the Blessed One, the Vanquisher.

From "Tathāgata, arhat, perfectly complete Buddha" onward, the perfection of the Teacher is taught. This perfection of the Teacher is taught in two ways: as speaking things just as they are, and as true accomplishment. Here "Tathāgata" teaches speaking things just as they are. "Thus" means unerring. "Gone" means teaching, speaking, or proclaiming. Because he proclaims and explains the meaning of things, the four noble truths, just as they are, he is the Tathāgata. True accomplishment is likewise of two kinds: accomplishing perfect abandonment and accomplishing perfect wisdom. Here "arhat" teaches the accomplishment of perfect abandonment: because he has abandoned the afflictions such as attachment, the phenomena to be abandoned by seeing and by meditation, he is an arhat, a foe-destroyer. "Perfectly complete Buddha" teaches the accomplishment of perfect wisdom. "Perfectly" means without error, or without distortion. "Complete" means without remainder. "Buddha" means one who knows. These are to be connected as "perfectly awakened" and "completely awakened." In sum, because he knows without remainder every object there is to be known, the aggregates, the elements, and so on, and because he possesses the wisdom that knows them without distortion as impermanent, suffering, empty, and without self, he is called the perfectly complete Buddha.

Alternatively, the perfection of the Teacher is shown in three ways. The three are speaking without distortion, being free of afflictions, and having comprehended everything there is to be known and expressed. Here "Tathāgata" teaches speaking without distortion: because he has spoken of the objects of knowledge just as they are, he is the Tathāgata. "Arhat" teaches being free of afflictions, because he has abandoned the afflictions and the secondary afflictions, which are like foes. "Perfectly complete Buddha" teaches having comprehended everything there is to be known and expressed, because he has comprehended, completely and without distortion, all things whatsoever that are to be known.

"Endowed with knowledge and conduct" teaches that by which he went. Its meaning is that by the eightfold noble path, beginning with right view, he went to the state of nirvāṇa.

Among the eight limbs of the noble path, right view is called knowledge, because it is through right view that the four noble truths are known correctly, just as they are. The remaining seven limbs, right thought and the rest, are called feet. The meaning is that by the eight limbs of the noble path he went to the state of nirvāṇa.

If one asks why, among the eight limbs of the noble path, right view is explained as knowledge while the remaining limbs are explained as feet: just as one who has no eyes cannot see, and one who has no feet cannot walk and so goes nowhere, but one who sees with the eyes and also has feet is then able to walk, so too, when right view sees the four truths and the remaining limbs put into practice what has been seen, just as it was seen, one then reaches nirvāṇa.

Alternatively, there are the three trainings: the training in higher moral discipline, the training in higher mind, and the training in higher wisdom. Of these, the training in higher wisdom is called knowledge, and the trainings in higher mind and higher moral discipline are called feet. Thus it is taught that by the three trainings he went to the city of nirvāṇa.

An objection: in other scriptures, samādhi is accomplished in dependence on moral discipline, and wisdom is accomplished in dependence on samādhi; that is why moral discipline is mentioned first, samādhi after it, and wisdom after that. Why, then, is wisdom mentioned first here? The reply: when they are embraced by wisdom, moral discipline and samādhi become completely pure, but moral discipline and samādhi without wisdom do not become pure; therefore wisdom is mentioned first. Just as one looks with the eyes and then walks with the feet, so one examines with wisdom, which is like the eyes, and then proceeds by means of moral discipline and samādhi, which are like the feet. Therefore the three trainings are called being endowed with knowledge and conduct.

Alternatively, the three knowledges of one who has no more to learn are called knowledge. The three knowledges are the knowledge called knowledge of former abodes, the knowledge of death and transmigration, and the knowledge of the exhaustion of the defilements. The feet are moral discipline, samādhi, and the remaining three kinds of superknowledge, namely knowledge of others' minds, the divine eye, and the divine ear; these five qualities are called the feet. In this way it is taught that he obtained the perfection of the Teacher, the three knowledges and the rest.

Up to this point, the Blessed One's perfect fulfillment of his own aim, together with its cause, has been taught. The passage from "Blessed One" through "perfectly complete Buddha" teaches the perfect fulfillment of his own aim, and "endowed with knowledge and conduct" teaches its cause.

"Sugata" teaches how he went. Just as a fever that has been completely cured does not return, so, because he has gone thoroughly, never to return to saṃsāra, he is the Sugata, the one gone to bliss. Alternatively, like a vase filled with butter, he has comprehended everything there is to be known, has abandoned without remainder all the obscurations there are to be abandoned, and has gone well as far as there is to go; therefore he is called the Sugata, the one well gone.

In brief, the two explanations show that the Blessed One is distinguished from outsiders who are free of attachment, and from the other arhats. Here, going without return, like a cured fever, shows that he surpasses outsiders who are free of attachment: although outsiders such as the tīrthikas who are free of attachment are separated from attachment for a while, they return again to the three realms, and so they are not without return. Going well, like a full vase, shows that he is distinguished from the arhats: although arhats and the like do not return, they have not realized all that is to be known and have not abandoned all the habitual tendencies without remainder, and so they have not gone well.

"Knower of the world" and so on teach the activity of the Teacher. The activity of the Teacher is of two kinds. The two are surveying the world of those who have the fortune and those who lack it, and training those who are to be trained. "Knower of the world" teaches the surveying of the world of those who have the fortune and those who lack it: three times each day and three times each night he examines the worlds with great compassion and wisdom, and knows, "This being has the fortune for liberation; this one does not have the fortune for liberation." For that reason he is called knower of the world.

"Unsurpassed charioteer who tames persons" teaches the training of those who have the fortune. Having known them in this way, he establishes the fortunate beings on the noble path and tames them; therefore he is the tamer of persons. Here, those who are suitable to be tamed, or who can be tamed, are the ones to be tamed. Because he is like a charioteer, he is a charioteer: just as a charioteer who trains horses tames them by ridding them of their faults and vices and sets them on an easy road, so he too tames the afflictions of beings and sets them on the right path; therefore he is a charioteer. Moreover, those who were difficult to tame, such as Uruvilvā Kāśyapa and the like, and Nanda, Aṅgulimāla, and the like, were dominated by delusion, attachment, and aversion. Because he tames those who are difficult to tame and places them in the supreme state, nirvāṇa, he is called the unsurpassed tamer of persons. Here, Uruvilvā Kāśyapa and the like were dominated by delusion, Nanda was dominated by attachment, and Aṅgulimāla was dominated by aversion.

"Teacher of gods and humans" teaches those by whom he is embraced. If one asks by whom the Tathāgata's teaching is embraced, it is embraced by gods and humans. Although animals and the like are also placed in the higher realms and so on through hearing the sound of the Dharma being taught, it is gods and humans who become vessels for understanding the noble truths and attaining the fruits of the ascetic life. For that reason he is called the teacher of gods and humans.

If one asks who in fact it is that possesses the qualities explained above, then for that reason the sūtra says, "He is the Buddha, the Blessed One." The words "that one" from above are to be connected here, and the meaning is: "That one, of whom the qualities of Blessed One, Tathāgata, arhat and so on were stated, is the Buddha, the Blessed One." Here, because he has woken from the sleep of ignorance and has comprehended everything there is to be known, he is the Buddha, the awakened one. Because he possesses the six qualities, he is the Blessed One. As for the six qualities, it is said: "Perfect lordship, beauty of form, fame, glory, wisdom, and exertion: one who possesses these six is so called."

"Having recognized him as the Buddha, he is endowed with faith" means that, having understood the Tathāgata to possess the qualities explained above, he is accordingly endowed with clear faith and conviction in him.

To teach the second limb of stream-entry, the sūtra says, "The sublime Dharma well spoken by the Blessed One," and so on. The Dharma spoken by the sublime ones is the sublime Dharma, because it was explained by the buddhas, the blessed ones. It is called the Dharma well spoken by the Blessed One because it teaches freedom from the two extremes, abandoning the extreme of suffering and the extreme of clinging to pleasure; because it is undeceiving and unmistaken; and because what he taught was nothing but Dharma that is virtuous in the beginning, the middle, and the end.

Alternatively, it is called the Dharma well spoken by the Blessed One in ten respects. The ten are: produced by the perfect one, done for the sake of others, continuous, openly proclaimed, differentiated, relied upon, made known, set forth, timely, and embracing good qualities. Here, "produced by the perfect one" means that he taught it after he had abandoned all the afflictions without remainder, had comprehended everything there is to be known, and had fully awakened. "Done for the sake of others" means that he taught it acting for the sake of all sentient beings. "Continuous" means that he did not teach only now and then, but taught without interruption. "Openly proclaimed" means that he was not like those who teach nothing, or who teach some and not others, but taught everyone without the closed fist of a teacher. "Differentiated" means that he taught the Dharma attuned to the individual dispositions of beings, in keeping with each of them: to those given to attachment he taught the unattractive, to those given to aversion he taught loving-kindness, and so on. "Relied upon" means that he taught relying wholly on speech endowed with the five qualities. "Made known" means that with a single utterance he made boundless world systems understand and know it, and made every circle of his assembly, near or far alike, understand and know it. "Set forth" means that he taught the path free of the two extremes. The two extremes, which are abandoned, are the extreme of suffering, such as the austerities of the tīrthikas, and clinging to the extreme of pleasure, such as attachment to the pleasures of desire. "Timely" means that he taught in keeping with the right time for those to be brought to maturity. "Embracing good qualities" means that he taught with speech endowed with the sixty qualities of a voice that teaches the Dharma.

"It is visible here and now" means that this Dharma of the Tathāgata's teaching is to be directly perceived. It is established by valid cognition; unlike the doctrines of the tīrthikas, it is not something to be accepted on faith alone, whether it is sound or not. Therefore it is called visible here and now.

Because it serves as the remedy for abandoning the afflictions, the secondary afflictions, and their habitual tendencies, it is free of sickness.

It is timeless, in the sense that there is no time at which, once abandoned, one falls back. When the afflictions are abandoned by the mundane path, one falls back after some time has passed; but when the afflictions are abandoned by the supramundane path, then once they have been abandoned there is never afterward any time of falling back, and so it is timeless. Alternatively, it is timeless because those who have entered upon right practice quickly attain distinction without a long time passing. That is why other sūtras say, "Monks, through applying oneself to proper attention, the defilement of desire that has not arisen does not arise, and those that have arisen are quickly abandoned."

As for "leading onward": because it brings about the attainment of escape from all the suffering of saṃsāra without remainder, or the attainment of nirvāṇa, and leads one to that state, it is leading onward. For by becoming habituated to it one is gradually freed from the suffering of saṃsāra and reaches the state of nirvāṇa.

"Seen herein" means that a Dharma such as this does not exist among those outside it, the tīrthikas and the like. Because it is not shared with others, it is called seen only herein. For such a Dharma is seen to exist only in this Dharma of the Buddha, and not in the other views of the tīrthikas and the like.

"To be known by the wise each for themselves" means that this Dharma is not the domain of logicians and is not realized by logicians, but the wise see and know it by their own inner wisdom. Therefore it is called to be known by the wise each for themselves.

To teach still other qualities, the sūtra says, "It dispels intoxication," and so on. Because it removes attachment to and conceit in high birth, beauty, health, and long life, it dispels intoxication. "It dispels craving" means that it brings about the abandonment of attachment to the qualities of desire. "It destroys every abode" means that it is the means for casting off the five aggregates, the basis of suffering and the afflictions. Alternatively, because it abandons all the latent tendencies of the afflictions, it destroys every abode. Because it cuts off the continuity of life, the rebirth in the five courses of existence, or the uninterrupted rebirth from one life to the next, it cuts off the course. Because it teaches the nonexistence of any entity that is a self, it is emptiness. Because it teaches that even what is called a self is not established, it is nonapprehension. Because it is the cause of abandoning attachment to the body and possessions of other lives, it is the exhaustion of becoming. Because it also brings about the abandonment of attachment to the body and possessions of this life, it is freedom from attachment.

Because it is the path that brings about the attainment of nirvāṇa, in which the afflictions and the whole mass of suffering have been abandoned, it is called cessation and nirvāṇa.

Alternatively, the Dharma is of three kinds: the Dharma of explanation, the Dharma of practice, and the Dharma of the result. Of these, the passage from "The sublime Dharma well spoken" up to "to be known by the wise each for themselves" teaches the qualities of the Dharma of explanation. The passage from "It dispels intoxication" through "freedom from attachment" teaches the qualities of the Dharma of practice. "Cessation, nirvāṇa" teaches the Dharma of the result. To have firm faith in and conviction that these teachings possess those qualities is called being endowed with faith in the sublime Dharma.

This shows that the Dharma of the Buddha is superior to the doctrines of the tīrthikas and others. How so? Because their doctrines are taught in a distorted way, they are not well spoken. Because they amount to nothing more than following faith and cannot bear examination by valid cognition, they are not visible here and now. Because they are not a remedy for the afflictions and the secondary afflictions, they are not free of sickness. Because one later falls back, they are not timeless. Because they do not bring about the attainment of the state of nirvāṇa, they are not leading onward. Because the doctrines of the tīrthikas are held in common with one another, they are not seen herein. Because their doctrines are the domain of logic, they are not to be known by the wise each for themselves. Because they do not remove attachment to birth, beauty, and the like, they do not dispel intoxication. Because they do not remove attachment to the qualities of desire, they do not dispel craving. Because they do not abandon the five aggregates, they do not destroy the abode. Because they do not cut off the five courses of existence and the continuity of life, they do not cut off the course. Because they teach that a self exists, they are not emptiness. Because they teach that a self exists, they do not teach nonapprehension. Because they do not act as a remedy for attachment to future bodies and possessions, they are not the exhaustion of craving. Because they do not abandon attachment to the present aggregates and belongings, they are not freedom from attachment. Because they are not the path to the attainment of nirvāṇa, they are not called cessation, nirvāṇa.

Alternatively, apart from what was explained before: this Dharma of the Buddha may be practiced at whatever time one practices it; because there is no set time for practicing it, it is timeless. That it is easy to practice and can be practiced at any time is as the layman Anāthapiṇḍada explained: "The Dharma of the Buddha is good to practice while walking, good while sitting, good while lying down, and good while pacing back and forth." Moreover, the doctrines taught by the tīrthikas involve a closed fist, because they are not taught to everyone, as when they say, "Teach it to the kṣatriyas and the brahmins, but do not teach it to the śūdras." The sublime Dharma, however, is taught openly to all four castes without any closed fist, and so it is called "seen herein," because it is taught without the closed fist of a teacher, saying, "Come and see!"

To teach the third limb of stream-entry, the sūtra says, "The Saṅgha of the Blessed One's śrāvakas is well engaged," and so on. These are the qualities of the Blessed One's Saṅgha. "Well engaged" is stated in summary. "Engaged in the right way" and the rest are the explanation of how it is well engaged. These should be understood as explained with reference to four meanings: engaged for the sake of the ascetic life, engaged in the ascetic life, engaged in the state of the ascetic life as ascetics, and engaged in what is taught for ascetics.

Here, "engaged in the right way" teaches being engaged for the sake of the ascetic life. "For the sake of the ascetic life" means the aim of the ascetic life, and that is nirvāṇa. Because they are engaged in nirvāṇa, they are called engaged in the right way. If one asks how it is known that nirvāṇa is called the right way, it is because other sūtras say, "The Dharma of the right way is unsurpassed; O, it is that which is to be won."

"Engaged uprightly" teaches being engaged in the ascetic life. Here the ascetic life refers to the eight limbs of the noble path, because they serve as the remedy that brings about the thorough abandonment of the afflictions. To be engaged in that is to be engaged uprightly. If one asks how it is known that the eightfold noble path is called upright, it is because another sūtra says, "The door to the deathless has been opened; the upright path has been fully proclaimed," and others say, "What is upright is the noble path; what is crooked is evil view."

"Engaged harmoniously" is taught as being engaged in the state of the ascetic life. If one asks in what respect they are in harmony: they are in harmony in being endowed with loving bodily action, endowed with loving verbal action, and endowed with loving mental action, and they are engaged harmoniously because they are in accord in possessions, moral discipline, and view.

"Engaged in the Dharma and in the Dharma to be pursued, practicing the Dharma to be pursued" teaches being engaged in what is taught for ascetics. What is taught for ascetics is the Dharma taught for the sake of ascetics, namely the Vinaya, the Sūtras, and the Abhidharma. Here, the scriptures to be explained are the Dharma, and the scriptures to be practiced are the Dharma to be pursued. Because they follow, among the scriptures to be explained, just what was spoken, they are engaged in the Dharma. Because they practice accordingly the teachings to be practiced, they are called practicing the Dharma to be pursued.

"Recognizing it as the Saṅgha, he is endowed with faith" means that through the power of realizing the noble truths, one has conviction and clear faith that the noble Saṅgha possesses the qualities explained above; this is being endowed with faith, having recognized it as the Saṅgha.

Alternatively, "engaged in the right way" and the rest are said with reference to accomplishing the supreme aim, accomplishing it without distortion, accomplishing it without division, and accomplishing it just as it was taught. Here, "engaged in the right way" teaches the accomplishment of the supreme aim. The supreme aim is nirvāṇa, and because they bring it about, they are engaged in the right way.

"Uprightly" teaches accomplishing it without distortion: because they practice the undistorted path, they are engaged uprightly. "Engaged harmoniously" teaches accomplishing it without division: because they practice just as the other noble arhats practice, they are engaged harmoniously. "Engaged in the Dharma and in the Dharma to be pursued" teaches accomplishing it just as it was taught: because they practice just as it is set forth in the scripture of the Blessed One, they are called engaged in the Dharma and in the Dharma to be pursued, and practicing the Dharma to be pursued.

To show who in fact are the persons endowed with such qualities, the sūtra says, "There is the Saṅgha that has entered upon realizing the fruit of stream-entry," and so on. Here the stream is the noble path, and to have entered it is stream-entry. The fruit of stream-entry is the subsequent knowledge of the path, which abides in the sixteenth moment. Having entered upon realizing it is the subsequent forbearance regarding the path, which abides in the fifteenth moment of mind. "There is also the Saṅgha that has entered" refers precisely to the one abiding in the sixteenth moment of mind, the subsequent knowledge of the path; by it, the afflictions of the three realms that are to be abandoned by seeing have been abandoned without remainder.

The one who has entered upon realizing the fruit of once-returning abides in the path that abandons five kinds of afflictions, up to the middling ones, out of the nine afflictions of the desire realm to be abandoned by meditation: the great of the great, the middling of the great, the small of the great, the great of the middling, the middling of the middling, the small of the middling, the great of the small, the middling of the small, and the small of the small. The Saṅgha of once-returners abides in the path that abandons eight kinds, the great of the small and the middling of the small among the afflictions of the desire realm to be abandoned by meditation.

The one who has entered upon realizing the fruit of non-returning abides in the path that abandons two, the great of the small and the middling of the small among the afflictions of the desire realm to be abandoned by meditation. The Saṅgha of non-returners abides in the path that abandons nine kinds, the small of the small among the afflictions of the desire realm to be abandoned by meditation. Because they thus pass into nirvāṇa without coming back to this desire realm, they are non-returners, who do not return even once.

The one who has entered upon realizing the fruit of arhatship: the afflictions of each level of the form realm are divided into nine, and the afflictions of the four meditative attainments of the formless realm are also divided into nine; such a one abides in the path that abandons up to eight kinds, through the middling of the small among the afflictions of the Peak of Existence. The arhat abides in the path that abandons the nine afflictions of the Peak of Existence. Because there the afflictions, which are like foes, have been destroyed without remainder and no longer exist, such a one is called an arhat, a foe-destroyer. Alternatively, it is because such a one is worthy of the offerings and honor of the whole world that one is called an arhat, a worthy one. That is why another sūtra too says, "worthy of the offerings and honor of Indra and Upendra."

"The four pairs of persons" are the four who are entering and the four who have attained the fruits; counted thus, there are eight persons. "Thus" means "as explained above."

"Perfect in faith": because they possess unshakable conviction in karma and its results and in the truths, they are perfect in faith. Alternatively, because they possess the four limbs of stream-entry, they are perfect in faith. Because they possess six-limbed moral discipline, or undefiled moral discipline, they are perfect in moral discipline. Because they have heard most of the sūtras, those that begin "virtuous in the beginning, virtuous in the middle," and so on, and have heard them without distortion, they are perfect in learning. Because they possess undefiled samādhi, whose defining mark is apprehending the selflessness of persons, they are perfect in samādhi. Because they possess the wisdom that comprehends the four noble truths without error, they are perfect in wisdom. Because they have abandoned the afflictions to be abandoned by seeing and those to be abandoned by meditation, they are perfect in liberation. Because after that they possess the knowledge that the afflictions have been abandoned, the knowledge that what had to be done has been done, and the knowledge that afflictions will not arise again in their mind stream, they are called perfect in the vision of liberation.

Because they have become a place to which Śakra, lord of the gods, Brahmā, and others, and kings and ministers among humans and others, are to give and make offerings, they are called worthy of gifts. Because they are worthy of offerings of excellent, fine, and abundant things, they are greatly worthy of gifts. Because they are the ones before whom, again and again, people bow the body and join the palms, this is connected with "fit to be saluted with joined palms." Because they are the ones before whom people show reverence by touching the five limbs of the body to the ground, they are worthy of prostration.

As for "unsurpassed field of merit": when roots of virtue such as generosity are planted there, it becomes a field that brings about the abandonment of all afflictions and the attainment of that whose defining mark is liberation. Because there is no other field more excellent than it, it is the unsurpassed field of merit. Because, if one presents to it gifts such as generosity, or provides it with sustenance, or provides it with requisites such as robes, these continually increase moment by moment and day by day, it is called fit to be the recipient of the world's gifts.

Because they possess the moral discipline dear to the noble ones and the qualities of moral discipline, the sūtra says, "Moreover, their moral discipline is unbroken," and so on. "Moreover" means that the noble Saṅgha not only possesses the qualities explained above, but also possesses the qualities that follow below, such as unbroken moral discipline.

Here moral discipline is of three kinds: the limb of moral discipline, the limb of observance, and the limb of heedfulness. Because they have not fallen away from any of these three, their moral discipline is unbroken. Of these, not committing the defeats, which are evil by nature, is the limb of moral discipline.

Shaving the head and wearing saffron robes is the limb of observance. Not drinking alcohol is called the limb of heedfulness. Because there is no guarding it at some times and at other times giving it up and abandoning it, it is uninterrupted. Because it is not mixed with the stains of bad character, it is undiminished. Because the causes that make moral discipline decline, thoughts of sensual desire and thoughts of aversion, do not arise, it is unmixed. Because only the purely white side arises, and because it acts as a cause of nirvāṇa, it is stainless. Because it possesses the antidote to the decline of moral discipline and upholds it fully, it is free. The antidote to the decline of moral discipline is the power of wisdom and the power of meditation. Because one has trained in these two again and again, one does not fall under the sway of evil, unwholesome phenomena, and bad companions are unable to lead one astray; therefore it is called free. Because it is not mixed with the stains of wrong view and wrong aspiration, it is untainted, for it is dedicated solely to renunciation. Not being mixed with wrong view means not being mixed with stains such as the view of the perishable collection. Not being mixed with wrong aspiration means not being mixed with aspirations such as "May this moral discipline make me become such and such."

"Thoroughly complete" means fully complete, brought to perfection. If one asks how it is brought to perfection, it is perfected because it is unbroken, untorn and undiminished. "Well undertaken" means well accomplished. If one asks how it is well accomplished, it is well accomplished because it is unmixed, stainless, free and untainted. "Praised by the wise" refers to the moral discipline, because the Tathāgata, the noble arhats and others have proclaimed it to be endowed with good qualities. "Not censured by the wise" means that the wise have not declared that this moral discipline has faults or flaws, because it is undefiled. "They possess the moral discipline dear to the noble ones" means that because it is the moral discipline that the noble ones favor, it is a cherished moral discipline. Because that moral discipline has the characteristic of being wholesome and undefiled, they actualize it again and again for the sake of nirvāṇa; therefore it is the moral discipline favored by the noble ones.

In effect, these passages teach the physician, the medicine, the attendant, and the requisites. Here, the Tathāgata is the physician who removes the afflictions. The Dharma is the medicine that removes the afflictions. The Saṅgha is the attendant in removing the afflictions. The moral discipline that pleases the noble ones is the requisite for abandoning the afflictions. To conclude the teaching on the four limbs of stream-entry, the sūtra says, "These are the four limbs of stream-entry."

After teaching the four limbs of stream-entry, the ten powers are taught. In order to explain them in the same order in which they were listed, the sūtra says, "Monks, what are the ten powers of the Tathāgata?" and so on. Whatever he has explained and taught is just so, unmistaken and without error; therefore he is called the Tathāgata. Because he possesses the might that overwhelms all the afflictions and all māras, they are powers.

To teach the first power, the sūtra says, "Monks, the Tathāgata knows perfectly, just as it truly is, what is a ground as a ground and what is not a ground as not a ground." Here "ground" refers to a cause, and "not a ground" refers to what is not a cause. Knowing that wholesome and unwholesome actions are the causes that produce the results of happiness and suffering is called knowing a ground as a ground, just as it truly is. An agent, a self and the like are not causes of phenomena; knowing this is called knowing what is not a ground as not a ground, just as it truly is.

Alternatively, "ground" refers to what is possible and "not a ground" to what is impossible. It is possible to come to great wealth through generosity, but impossible to become poor through generosity. It is possible to be born in the higher realms through moral discipline, but impossible to be born in the lower realms through moral discipline. Conversely, it is impossible to be born in the higher realms by committing evil, but possible to be born in the lower realms by committing evil, unwholesome deeds. It is possible to be born in the higher realms by doing what is wholesome, but impossible to go to the lower realms through what is wholesome. It is possible for a man to become fully awakened, to become a wheel-turning king, or to become a pratyekabuddha, but a woman does not become so. Understanding this is called knowing perfectly, just as it truly is, what is a ground as a ground and what is not a ground as not a ground. Here, knowing everything, knowing without obstruction, and knowing purely is called knowing just as it truly is.

To teach the second power, the sūtra says, "Of the past, the future and the present," and so on. Actions that were done and accumulated and have since ceased are past actions. Actions that have neither been done nor ceased, nor are being done, but will be done in the future, are future actions. Actions that have neither been completed nor ceased, but are now in the process of being done, are called present actions. Moreover, summing these up as three kinds, by way of bodily action, verbal action and mental action, the sūtra speaks of undertaken actions. The ripening from those actions of the results of happiness and suffering is called karmic maturation. Knowing this too in its four kinds is called knowing perfectly, just as it truly is. That is, he understands these kinds just as they are: some actions are pleasant now but ripen as suffering in the future; some actions are painful now but ripen as happiness in the future; some actions are painful now and ripen as suffering in the future as well; and some actions are pleasant now and ripen as happiness in the future as well.

Here, some, because they grew habituated to committing unwholesome actions in other lives, engage in unwholesome actions in this life too as if with delight. Owing to that condition, these actions will ripen in the future in the three lower realms. That is called an action that is pleasant now but ripens as suffering in the future. Some, because in former lives they did not greatly habituate themselves to what is wholesome, engage in this life in wholesome deeds such as guarding moral discipline as if with discomfort. Because of that cause, it ripens in another life in a happy destiny. This is called what is unpleasant here but ripens as happiness in another life. Some beings, because they did not habituate themselves to evil actions in other lives, are made against their will in this life to do evil actions by bad companions. Those actions will ripen in the future in the three lower realms. Therefore they are called unpleasant in this life and ripening as suffering in the future as well. Some, because in former lives they did not greatly habituate themselves to what is wholesome, engage in this life in wholesome deeds such as guarding moral discipline as if with discomfort. Because of that cause, it ripens in another life in a happy destiny. This is called what is unpleasant here but ripens as happiness in another life. Some beings, because they did not habituate themselves to evil actions in other lives, are made against their will in this life to do evil actions through the influence of bad companions. Those actions will ripen in the future in the three lower realms. Therefore they are called unpleasant in this life and ripening as suffering in the future as well. Some, because in former lives they grew habituated to doing wholesome actions, do wholesome actions in the same way in this life too, and in this life attain the four meditative concentrations of the form realm. Because these will ripen in the future in the higher realms as well, they are called pleasant in this life and ripening as happiness in other lives as well.

To teach the third power, the sūtra says, "He knows perfectly, just as they truly are, the many inclinations and the various inclinations of other beings." The many inclinations of beings are lesser inclination, middling inclination and great inclination. Inclination toward the Śrāvaka Vehicle is lesser inclination. Inclination toward the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle is middling inclination. Inclination toward the Great Vehicle is great inclination. Some beings are inclined toward forms but not toward sounds. Some are inclined toward sounds but not toward forms. Some are inclined toward smells but not toward forms. These limitless kinds of inclination are called the various inclinations. Because he knows without error, just as it is, whatever each being is inclined toward, he knows perfectly, just as they truly are, the many inclinations and the various inclinations.

To teach the fourth power, the sūtra says, "He knows perfectly, just as they truly are, the many world elements and the various world elements." Here "element" means the lineage, nature or seed of beings. Those of the śrāvaka lineage, of the pratyekabuddha lineage and of the Great Vehicle lineage are called the many world elements. The eighty-four thousand forms of conduct of beings are called the various world elements. Because he understands without error, just as it is, which lineage beings possess and which conduct each being possesses, it is called knowing just as it truly is.

To teach the fifth power, the sūtra says, "He knows perfectly, just as they truly are, the superior and inferior faculties of other beings." Here the faculties are of three kinds: sharp, dull and middling. Knowing perfectly and without error, "This one possesses sharp faculties of faith and the rest of the five; this one possesses dull faculties of faith and the rest," is called knowing perfectly, just as they truly are, the superior and inferior faculties.

To teach the sixth power, the sūtra says, "He knows perfectly, just as it truly is, the path that leads everywhere." Having known the lineages, the conduct, and the sharp and dull faculties of beings, he knows without error, just as it is, which path and which teaching is fitting to teach to which being. This is called knowing, just as it truly is, the path that leads everywhere. For example, he knows to teach the unattractive to one who is attached, to teach loving-kindness to one who is angry, and so on. Alternatively, seeing and knowing the five courses of rebirth is called knowing, just as it truly is, the path that leads everywhere. Alternatively, there are paths relying on separate positions, those of ascetics, brahmins and others, who are attached to various individual views and conceive of them as the path. Among these, that blameless path which, in this life and in other lives, produces nothing but happiness in every respect is called the path that leads everywhere. Because he knows it without error, it is called knowing just as it truly is.

To teach the seventh power, the sūtra says, "The faculties of other beings," and so on. The faculties are the five faculties, the faculty of faith and the rest. The powers are the five powers, faith and the rest. The limbs of awakening are the seven limbs of awakening, mindfulness and the rest. The meditative concentrations are the four meditative concentrations of the form realm. The liberations are the eight, beginning with one who has form seeing forms. The meditative absorptions are the three: the signless, the wishless, emptiness and so forth. The attainments are the four attainments of the formless realm and the attainment of cessation. Alternatively, when those who have gained mastery over the mind enter a meditative absorption suited to accomplishing whatever aim they wish, that is called entering an attainment.

As for "defilement," in brief there are two kinds of defilement that obstruct the attainment of the meditative concentrations and the rest. For those who have not attained them, there is engaging in an application that is not a skillful means, and the arising of the defilements of dullness and agitation. For those who have attained them, there is the active occurrence of the defilements of that level, or relishing the bliss of meditative concentration. That is called defilement. Being free of these two kinds of defilement is purification.

Among the limitless meditative concentrations, which name belongs to which concentration is called their establishment. Understanding without error the phenomena such as the faculties, their defilement and purification, and what their names are, is called knowing perfectly, just as it truly is.

To teach the eighth power, the sūtra says, "The many kinds of former abodes," and so on. There are the various abodes of beings, this one and that, namely east, west, north, south and so forth. There are one's own bodies and those of others, which followed the eight bases of conventional designation that differ in various places, names and so on. Recollecting, in brief, the six modes of usage regarding these is called correctly recollecting the many kinds of former abodes, with their aspects, with their locales, and with their signs.

The eight bases of conventional designation are: what the name was, what the caste was, what the clan was, what conventions were used, what happiness and suffering were experienced, how long or short the life span was, for how long one remained, and what the end of life was. The six modes of usage are: the designation by which one is called; the caste, such as the warrior caste and so forth; who one's father and mother were; the provisions of food and drink; prosperity and decline; and the various formations of life. For in this way people in the world, relying on the eight bases of conventional designation, speak and converse about themselves and others by means of the six modes of usage.

Here, who one was means which person one was in that life and in those lives. Caste means which one was born into, whether the warrior caste, the brahmin caste, the merchant caste, the servant caste or another. Father and mother means who was one's mother and who was one's father in each of those lives of each individual being. The provisions of food and drink means whatever food one ate in each of those lives, such as millet, rice and so on. Prosperity and decline means whatever hardship one experienced and whatever happiness one experienced in each of those lives. The various formations of life means how long the life span was in each of those lives, whether a hundred years, a thousand, ten or another number; the phases in those lives of the time of childhood, the time of youth and the time of old age; and after how much of the formations of life had elapsed, at what point one died. Because he understands without error all these conventions of himself and others, it is called correctly recollecting the many kinds of former abodes, with their aspects, with their locales, and with their signs.

Here, in "with their aspects," the aspects are recollecting the forms, recollecting the labels, and recollecting the signs. Recollecting the forms means recollecting whatever oneself and others did, starting from a single life up to beyond many hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of eons. Recollecting the labels means recollecting also the name of each life, or the individual names, in all those lives: recollecting, "In this life the name was this; in this life the name was this." Recollecting the signs means recollecting the time and the caste in each of those lives, and whatever prosperity and decline were experienced. "With their locales" means recollecting the clan and the caste in each of those lives, which land one lived in, and where one was born after dying and passing on from there. "With their signs" means recollecting who one's father and mother were in each of those lives, and recollecting for how long the formations of life endured.

Alternatively, recollecting, when born in each of those lives, what time it was for the father and mother, who the father and mother were, what happiness and suffering were experienced, what food was eaten, and for how long or short a time one remained, is called recollection with aspects. Recollecting in which land one was born in each of those lives, and how one was born in succession from one life to the next, is with locales. Recollecting what name one was given in each of those lives is called correctly recollecting with signs.

Moreover, in brief this teaches three points: by what one recollects, what one recollects, and how one recollects. Here, that by which one recollects is the very wisdom of recollecting former abodes; it is by this that one recollects. What one recollects ranges from recollecting even a single life up to recollecting many hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of eons. How one recollects is recollecting with aspects, with locales and with signs.

To teach the ninth power, the sūtra says, "With the purified divine eye that far surpasses the human," and so on. Here, "divine" refers to the first meditative concentration, the second and so on. That divine eye is called the divine eye because it relies on just those meditative concentrations, is their result, and is encompassed by them. Because it is the result of perfected meditative concentration and of liberation, it is called liberated. Because humans do not have even the name of that divine eye, it is called far surpassing the human. Thus the gods born in the desire realm have a divine eye obtained through birth, and so it is called a divine eye corresponding to that; but because humans do not have even that much, it far surpasses the human.

"Beings who engage in wholesome conduct of body, speech and mind" means that the ten wholesome actions, such as refraining from killing, are to be practiced by means of the three, body, speech and mind. Engaging in unwholesome conduct means engaging in the ten unwholesome actions, such as killing, by way of the three, body, speech and mind. Being born among gods and humans through practicing the ten wholesome actions is rebirth in a happy destiny. Falling into the three lower realms through practicing the ten unwholesome actions is called rebirth in a bad destiny. When a being abandons the life faculty, that is dying. Being born into the intermediate state is passing on. Coming into existence in a class such as gods or humans is called being reborn. Seeing with the divine eye how beings are reborn in other lives, from where to where, is called the power of recollecting other lives.

To teach the tenth power, the sūtra says, "Because of being free of defilements through the wisdom of the exhaustion of defilements, he knows the liberation of mind just as it truly is." Having abandoned without remainder the latent tendencies to be eliminated by the paths of meditation and of seeing, attaining undefiled moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom and liberation is the exhaustion of defilements. Then, once the defilements are exhausted, attaining the wisdom of liberation, "In my own continuum the defilements are exhausted, the afflictions are abandoned, and I will not be born into another existence," is called knowing just as it truly is the liberation that is free of defilements through wisdom. Just as he knows that the defilements in his own continuum are exhausted, knowing how to bring about the exhaustion of the defilements in the continua of other beings as well is called the power of the exhaustion of defilements.

Now the nature of these ten powers, their divisions, their being unshared, their equality, their activity, their sequence and their distinctive features are to be explained.

What is the nature of the ten powers? Their nature should be understood to be the nature of the five undefiled faculties, faith and the rest. Alternatively, since among these wisdom is the chief, they should be understood to be of the nature of wisdom.

As for the divisions: dividing these ten powers into three kinds, they should be understood to be limitless in their very nature. The three kinds are the division by time, the division by aspect, and the division by continuum. The division by time is so called because he understands all knowable things belonging to the past, the future and the present. The division by aspect is so called because, even with regard to a single conditioned thing, he understands all the aspects it has, such as its particular characteristic and its general characteristic, its having form or being formless, and so on. The division by continuum is so called because he understands without remainder what each individual continuum is like of the beings who dwell in the limitless world systems of the ten directions. Because he thus understands these three divisions, these ten powers should be understood to be limitless by nature.

As for being unshared: these ten powers are possessed by the Tathāgata alone, and others such as śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas do not have them.

As for equality: all tathāgatas are alike in having gained these ten powers, for there is no difference among them of greater or lesser. Therefore all tathāgatas are said to abide in equality. The only difference is that, in keeping with the aims of beings, on some occasions some of them make certain powers manifest, and on other occasions some of them do not make certain powers manifest.

As for activity: which activity does each of these ten powers perform? By the power of knowing what is possible and what is impossible, he knows perfectly, just as it truly is, that a cause stands as a cause and a result stands as a result, and he refutes the ascetics and brahmins who teach that there is no cause or who teach an incompatible cause. By the power of the wisdom that knows one's own actions, he knows just as it truly is that those who have done their own actions themselves experience the results, and he refutes the ascetics and brahmins who assert that the merit of generosity and so forth can be transferred. By the power of knowing the meditative concentrations, liberations, meditative absorptions and attainments, he tames beings by the miracle of teaching, the miracle of instruction and the miracle of supernormal power, and then teaches the genuine instructions; and he refutes the brahmins and ascetics who hold their own discordant views, speak in contradiction, and argue against him. By the power of knowing superior and inferior faculties, he knows just as it truly is the sharp, middling, lowest and other faculties of beings, and teaches the sublime Dharma to each of them individually as is fitting. By the power of knowing the various inclinations, he knows just as it truly is the great, middling, lesser and other inclinations toward wholesome and unwholesome phenomena; he makes the wholesomeness of those inclined toward the wholesome increase, and he leads those inclined toward the unwholesome to abandon the unwholesome. By the power of knowing the various elements, he knows just as it truly is the lesser, middling and great lineages, and trains them, each as is fitting, through the particular doors by which they are to be delivered, in accordance with their faculties, their dispositions and their latent tendencies. By the wisdom that knows the path that leads everywhere, he understands just as it truly is the path that brings about complete deliverance from all suffering, and understands just as it truly is the path that does not bring about deliverance from all suffering; then, having set aside the path that does not bring deliverance, he places beings correctly on the path that brings deliverance. By the power of recollecting former abodes, he recollects and makes known how beings were born and came into being in the past, and having generated disenchantment and faith, he teaches the Dharma, and refutes the ascetics and brahmins who teach eternalism. By the wisdom that knows death and rebirth, the Tathāgata foretells how śrāvakas of the past and future passed away and how they will later be born, and refutes the ascetics and brahmins who teach annihilationism. By the power of knowing the exhaustion of defilements, he has no doubt and no hesitation about his own liberation, and he refutes the ascetics and brahmins who are filled with the conceit of imagining that they have attained arhatship. This is the activity of the ten powers of the Tathāgata.

Now how should the sequence of these ten powers be understood? When the tathāgatas become fully awakened in unsurpassed awakening, they gain all these ten powers at one time; having gained them at one time, they make them manifest in sequence. Here, immediately upon becoming fully and perfectly awakened, the tathāgatas first of all look, with the wisdom that knows what is possible and what is impossible, upon whatever establishment there is of the causes and results of phenomena.

Having looked in this way, by the power of the wisdom that knows one's own actions, he teaches the beings of the desire realm who wish to obtain pleasing results the means of abandoning unwholesome actions and accomplishing wholesome actions. Further, for those beings who wish to abandon attachment by means of the worldly path, by the power of knowing the meditative concentrations, liberations, meditative absorptions and attainments, he teaches the genuine instructions just as they are, so that they may become free of worldly attachment and attain the worldly path. For those who wish to become free of attachment by means of the path that transcends the world, he teaches the Dharma by means of the remaining seven powers, so that they may attain freedom from attachment that transcends the world.

How is this so? First, by the power of knowing superior and inferior faculties, he looks at the faculties of those who wish to attain freedom from attachment that transcends the world. Then, after that, having examined their faculties, by the power of knowing the various inclinations, he looks closely at their dispositions. Then, after that, by the power of knowing the various elements, he looks closely at their latent tendencies. Having thus known their faculties, dispositions and latent tendencies, by the power of knowing the path that leads everywhere, he delivers them, as is fitting, through the meditative object and through the door by which they are to be delivered. Then, after that, by the power of recollecting former abodes and the power of knowing death and rebirth, having applied them thoroughly to the meditative object and the door of deliverance, he makes their minds stable and purifies their conduct, and then rightly teaches the Middle Way path, which abandons the two extremes, the views of eternalism and annihilationism that arise from the root, the view of the perishable collection. Then, after that, by the power of knowing the exhaustion of defilements, he removes the conceit of those who, having entered correctly in this way and become steady through calm abiding, have not abandoned the latent tendencies of the afflictions but are merely free of their manifest activity, and who, though they have not done what is to be done, give rise to the conceit of having done what is to be done. This, then, is one form of the sequence of the powers.

What is the difference between the power of knowing what is possible and what is impossible and the power of knowing one's own actions? It is answered: by the power of knowing what is possible and what is impossible, he understands that pleasing and unpleasing results arise from wholesome and unwholesome actions; by the power of knowing one's own actions, he understands that pleasing and unpleasing results arise for the very one who did the wholesome or unwholesome actions. So there is a difference.

Further, by the power of knowing one's own actions, he understands, "One obtains it through that very meditative concentration, liberation, meditative absorption or attainment"; by the power of attaining the meditative concentrations, liberations and meditative absorptions, relying on those very meditative concentrations and the rest, he teaches those to be trained by means of the three kinds of miracle.

Further, by the power of attaining the meditative concentrations, liberations and meditative absorptions, he knows the mind, together with faith and the rest, only in a general way; by the power of knowing superior and inferior faculties, he distinguishes individually the natures of that very faith and the rest as lesser, middling and great.
