Further, by the power of knowing superior and inferior faculties, he knows the dispositions, how the dispositions of those very faculties, faith and the rest, were directed in former lives toward this or that phenomenon; by the power of knowing the various inclinations, he knows how to distinguish those very dispositions into various kinds: "This one is accompanied by a disposition of attachment; this one is accompanied by a disposition of aversion."

In brief, disposition has six divisions: the disposition that does not lead to deliverance, the disposition that leads to deliverance, the disposition far from purification, the disposition near to purification, the disposition wishing to attain nirvāṇa in this very life, and the disposition wishing to attain nirvāṇa in the future. Here, the disposition that does not lead to deliverance is the disposition of those inclined toward the teachings of the Mighty One, Brahmā and others. The disposition that leads to deliverance is the disposition of those inclined toward the three vehicles. The disposition far from purification belongs to those who abide in giving at the level of lesser and middling roots of virtue. The disposition near to purification belongs to those who abide in great roots of virtue. The disposition wishing to attain nirvāṇa in this very life belongs to the noble śrāvakas and others. The disposition wishing to attain nirvāṇa in the future is that of the tathāgatas.

Further, by the wisdom that knows the various inclinations, he understands them merely as seeds arising from the cause, inclination. By the wisdom that knows the various elements, he analyzes those very seeds and knows them in their many kinds. When the elements are analyzed, in brief they are of four kinds: the naturally abiding seed, the seed arisen from habituation, the seed that will become purified, and the seed that will not become purified. Here, the naturally abiding seed is the lineage of the three vehicles, present by nature in saṃsāra without beginning. The seed arisen from habituation is the seed that ripens into the lineage, nourished and accumulated through habituation to the roots of virtue. The seed that will become purified belongs to those whose nature is to attain complete nirvāṇa. The seed that will not become purified belongs to those who will not attain complete nirvāṇa. Here, by the power of knowing the various elements, he knows by leading them onto the path that accords with their seed. By the power of knowing the path that leads everywhere, he knows without remainder all the divisions of the path there are: "This path is defiled; this one possesses purification; this one will become thoroughly purified; this one will become purified momentarily."

Here, by the power of knowing the path that leads everywhere, he knows the past, and the five courses of rebirth of the past together with their causes. By the power of recollecting former lives, he knows the past as well, distinguishing six kinds of conduct connected with eight kinds of conventional designation. Here, by the power of recollecting former lives, he knows, starting from the past, the death and transmigration of beings; and by the power of knowing death and transmigration, he knows how beings will be born and how they will die in the future. Here, by the power of knowing death and transmigration, he knows that those who have not brought their own aim to completion will link up with rebirth again in the future. By the power of knowing the exhaustion of the defilements, he knows that those whose own task is completed and whose minds are liberated attain nirvāṇa in this very life. Thus it should be understood how these ten powers are both without distinction and with distinction from one another.

Since the four fearlessnesses are taught after the ten powers, wishing to explain them in the order in which they are taught, he said, "What are the four fearlessnesses of the Tathāgata?" and so on. Here "fearlessness" means being without timidity, or without apprehension. That is, he has made four kinds of claim: he has claimed in the assembly to be liberated from the obscuration of the knowable, which is not shared with the śrāvakas, and to have fully awakened to all phenomena in all their aspects; he has claimed in the assembly to be liberated from the obscuration of the afflictions, which is shared with the śrāvakas; he has claimed to teach the path of deliverance so that beings who wish for liberation may pass beyond suffering; and he has claimed that whatever becomes an obstacle to attaining that very path is to be abandoned. Even if gods, humans and others, whether excellent or not excellent, challenge these claims, he sees no occasion and no sign by which their challenge could stand, and so he abides without timidity. This is called the four fearlessnesses of the Tathāgata.

Here, the passage from "The Blessed One is perfectly and fully awakened" up to "he turns the wheel of Brahmā, which no ascetic or brahmin in the world has turned in accordance with the Dharma" teaches fearlessness with regard to the claim to be liberated from the obscuration of the knowable, which is not shared with the śrāvakas, and to have awakened to phenomena in all their aspects. This is the first fearlessness. The passage from "Whatever the Tathāgata has declared to be obstructive phenomena" up to "how could that be?", where "that, by whom" and the rest is as before, teaches the claim that whatever becomes an obstacle to attaining that very path is to be abandoned. This is the second fearlessness. The passage from "That by which noble ones will come forth" up to "as before" teaches the claim to teach the path so that beings who wish for liberation may completely pass beyond suffering. This is the third fearlessness. The passage from "The defilements are exhausted" up to "this turning of the wheel of Brahmā" teaches the claim to be liberated from the obscuration of the afflictions, which is shared with the śrāvakas. This is the fourth fearlessness.

"I fully know, 'This Blessed One is perfectly and fully awakened'": having abandoned all obscurations of the knowable, he has himself understood that he has made directly evident, by wisdom, the aspects of phenomena such as the aggregates, the elements and the sense sources. This is what is called "I fully know that he is perfectly and fully awakened."

As for the passage "'You have fully awakened to this Dharma': with regard to this, the world with its gods, with its māras," and so on, it is to be construed thus: even if gods and humans, excellent and not excellent, challenge the claim that he who has fully and perfectly awakened to phenomena is awakened, he sees the sign of non-awakening they allege and sees that it cannot stand, and so, free from all fear and without apprehension, he roars the lion's roar in the assembly.

Here "the world with its gods" indicates gods and humans in general, both excellent and not excellent. The excellent gods are Māra, the sovereign among the gods of the desire realm, and Brahmā, the sovereign of yoga. The excellent humans are the ascetics and brahmins, because they possess knowledge and are worthy of veneration even by kings. Gods and humans who are not excellent are the other gods, humans and so on. Even when these dispute and challenge him, saying that he is not fully and perfectly awakened, he does not see that they have any sign of having seen to the end of phenomena, and he does not see their challenge standing by reasoning; and so it says, "not seeing any sign for disputing." "Since he does not see a sign" means since he does not see any cause by which the challenge would stand by reasoning.

Because he has attained freedom from the bristling of the body's hair and from trembling of speech, it is said, "The Tathāgata has found ease." Because he has found a mind without apprehension, it is said, "he abides having found fearlessness."

Nirvāṇa is the unsurpassed state. It is attained by cultivating the noble path, and once it has been attained one is free from all harm and all fear. Therefore nirvāṇa is the place of the bull, the leader of the herd; and because he has made that nirvāṇa directly evident, it is said, "he fully knows the place of the bull."

When he professes and declares himself to be perfect in his claim to be the unsurpassed Teacher, he subdues the other teachers, terrifies all opponents, and proclaims his great speech in the assembly without apprehension; therefore it is said, "having entered the assembly, he roars the true lion's roar."

Just as he himself has made directly evident the state of nirvāṇa, so, out of compassion, he explains it so that others may attain it; therefore it is said, "he turns the wheel which no ascetic or brahmin in the world has turned in accordance with the Dharma."

In brief, these words teach two things: that he has attained the perfection of his own aim, and that he brings about the perfection of the aim of others.

Here, the words "he fully knows the place of the bull" teach that he has attained the perfection of his own aim, and the words "having entered the assembly, he roars the true lion's roar" and "he turns the wheel of Brahmā" teach that he brings about the perfection of the aim of others.

Alternatively, because he pacifies every illness of the afflictions, it is said, "he fully knows all the places of the bull." Bad physicians, not knowing the medicine for the patients' illness of the afflictions, claim that theirs is the true medicine; because he refutes them and himself claims to be the true physician, it is said, "he roars the true lion's roar." Because he teaches the means for pacifying the illness of the afflictions, it is said, "he turns the wheel of Brahmā."

"Whatever the Tathāgata has declared to be obstructive phenomena": the phenomena that obstruct the cultivation of the noble path are indulgence in the five strands of sense pleasure and the like. "Will not become obstructive" refers to the assertion that even if one indulges in the five strands of sense pleasure, they will not become a hindrance to liberation. "Here, by whom," with "in full," means that, regarding the assertion "they will not become obstructive," made against his explanation that those phenomena are obstructive, one should construe in full from "the world with its gods" up to "turns the wheel of Brahmā."

In "And whatever he has declared to be the path by which noble ones come forth," "he" means the Tathāgata. The path by which noble ones come forth is the path relying on which the noble ones attain the path of liberation and come forth from the suffering of saṃsāra, that is, the eightfold noble path and the rest. "Declared to be the path" means declared to be the cause of attaining nirvāṇa. "Even one who relies on it will not come forth" means: even if one meditates on that path, one will not come forth from the three realms, so how could one ever be free of the suffering of birth, old age, sickness, death and the rest? Here "the world with its gods" and so on is to be construed as before.

In "I fully know myself that the defilements are exhausted," the defilements are the host of afflictions to be abandoned by seeing and by meditation; he knows that he himself has made directly evident their abandonment without remainder. For the rest of the explanation, the meaning should be seen as before.

In brief, these four fearlessnesses should be seen as perfectly attained with respect to his own aim and the aim of others. His own aim has two aspects, and these two are the distinction of wisdom and the distinction of abandonment. The aim of others also has two aspects: abandoning what is unfavorable and relying on the antidote. Here the words "has perfectly awakened to phenomena in all their aspects" show that he possesses the distinction of the wisdom that has realized all that is knowable. The words "I fully know myself that the defilements are exhausted" show that he possesses the distinction of abandonment. The words "declared to be obstructive phenomena" teach the abandonment of what is unfavorable. The words "declared to be the path of coming forth" teach reliance on the antidote.

After the four fearlessnesses are taught, the four analytical knowledges are taught, and therefore he said, "What are the four analytical knowledges of the Tathāgata?" and so on.

Here, knowing phenomena as emptiness, signlessness and wishlessness is the analytical knowledge of meaning. Alternatively, knowing them as suffering, impermanent, empty and selfless is called knowing the analytical knowledge of meaning. Knowing the divisions of phenomena is the analytical knowledge of dharma: knowing the limitless divisions of phenomena, such as virtuous and nonvirtuous, blameworthy and blameless, conditioned and unconditioned, defiled and undefiled, mundane and supramundane, good and bad, afflicted and purified, having form and formless, and so on, is the analytical knowledge of dharma. Knowing the eighty-four thousand kinds of conduct of beings, and their antidote, the eighty-four thousand heaps of Dharma, is the analytical knowledge of meaning. Knowing all the languages, however many there are, belonging to whatever is counted as a sentient being, gods, nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, asuras, kinnaras and the rest, and knowing all that is treated in the sciences of language and grammar, is knowing the analytical knowledge of language. Making all beings understand by a single utterance, and knowing the divisions of a single thing so that, even if one spoke of them until the end of an eon, there would be no end, is the analytical knowledge of eloquence.

In essence, knowing the two, what is to be taught and that by which it is taught, endlessly and without obstruction, is called analytical knowledge. What is to be taught has two aspects: the basis and what rests on it. The basis is dharma; what rests on it is meaning. That by which it is taught has two aspects: unobstructed words and unobstructed wisdom. Here the analytical knowledge of language is unobstructed wisdom; the meaning is that it is taught by these two.

After the four analytical knowledges are taught, the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha are taught, and wishing to explain them in the order in which they are taught, he said, "What are the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha?" and so on. These eighteen are present only in the mindstream of the Tathāgata himself, and not in others such as śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas; because they are not shared, they are called the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha.

The meaning of these eighteen is set out as follows. When an arhat monk whose defilements are exhausted enters a town for alms, at times he goes along in company with a fierce elephant; at times he goes along in company with fierce horses, elephants, cattle or dogs; at times he sets his foot among dense thorns; at times he sets his foot where a venomous snake lives; he enters places that should not be entered, where there are women and the like, and is drawn into improper talk; having gone to a wilderness place, he leaves the road and goes the wrong way; he goes along together with thieves, or lions, tigers, bears and the like. Such things are called the stumblings of the noble arhats. Because the Tathāgata has nothing of the sort at all, in any way whatever, the Tathāgata is said to be without stumbling.

Further, when a noble arhat looks for the road to a wilderness place and does not see the road, or enters an empty house and makes a noise, saying, "What is this?", and calls out loudly, or heaves great sighs, or, from the fault of habitual tendencies, lets out a horse laugh, that is the clamor of the noble arhats. Because the Tathāgata has nothing of this kind at all, in any way whatever, he is said to be without clamor.

The Tathāgata has no confusion of mindfulness. Because he does not forget even things done long ago or things said long ago, he is said to be without loss of mindfulness.

Again, a noble arhat is in meditative equipoise when he has entered samādhi, but when he has not entered samādhi he is not in equipoise. The Tathāgatas, however, have minds always in equipoise, and have nothing of that kind at all, in any way whatever; therefore he is said to have no mind that is out of equipoise. That is why the sūtras say:

The great elephant moves in equipoise,
The great elephant stands in equipoise,
The great elephant looks in equipoise,
And when he sits, he sits in equipoise.

Further, a noble arhat gives rise solely to the perception of weariness toward the aggregates, and gives rise to the perception of peace toward nirvāṇa, in which they are abandoned without remainder. The Tathāgatas have no perception of difference between the two, the aggregates and nirvāṇa; therefore he is said to have no perception of diversity, because he abides in the characteristic of supreme equanimity.

Further, a noble arhat, without examining whether beings have the good fortune or not, leaves the welfare of beings in equanimity. The Tathāgatas examine whether beings have the good fortune or not, and leave in equanimity those who lack it; leaving them in equanimity without examining is absent from them in every way. Therefore he is said to have no equanimity that is without examination.

Further, a noble arhat, with regard to abandoning the obscuration of the knowable, falls short without having attained the wisdom purified of the obscuration of the knowable, and so suffers decline of aspiration, decline of diligence, decline of mindfulness, decline of wisdom, decline of liberation, and decline of the wisdom of liberation; in that respect all six are lacking in him. The Tathāgata has none of these six kinds of decline. Because he does not decline, from aspiration toward abandoning the obscuration of the knowable up to the teaching of the wisdom of liberation, he is said to have no diminution of aspiration, and so on up to no diminution of the teaching of the wisdom of liberation.

Further, noble arhats do not comprehend the knowable things of the three times by a mere effort, whereas the wisdom that does is called unattached. They do not comprehend everything, whereas the wisdom that does is called unobstructed. The Tathāgatas comprehend the things of the three times without effort, and so are unattached; and because they comprehend everything, they are unobstructed. Therefore it is said, "seeing with wisdom unattached and unobstructed with regard to the past," and so on up to "seeing with wisdom unattached and unobstructed with regard to the present."

Further, noble arhats do not send wisdom ahead when they engage in actions of body, speech and mind, and so at times they engage in virtuous bodily action, and at times in indeterminate action. Likewise they engage in verbal and mental action as above: they engage in mixed action. For the Tathāgatas, in all three kinds of action, wisdom goes before and they follow wisdom, so that only virtuous action occurs, and indeterminate action does not occur. Therefore it is said, "all bodily action is preceded by wisdom," and so on up to "all mental action is preceded by wisdom and follows wisdom." Here, because it is set in motion by wisdom, it is called "preceded by wisdom"; because wisdom arises at the same moment, it is called "following wisdom."

In essence, these eighteen unshared qualities teach the perfection of the pure, unshared actions of body, speech and mind; the perfection of never falling away, short of attainment, from the Tathāgata's faculties together with their basis and result; the perfection of abiding in unshared wisdom; and the perfection of engaging in unshared action. Here, "without stumbling" teaches the purity of the body. "Without clamor" teaches the purity of verbal action. The four, without loss of mindfulness, without a mind out of equipoise, without perception of diversity, and without equanimity that lacks examination, teach the purity of mental action. The six, from "no diminution of aspiration" up to "no diminution of the teaching of the wisdom of liberation," teach that there is no falling away, short of attainment, from the Tathāgata's faculties together with their basis and result. Here the basis of the faculties is aspiration. The result is liberation and the teaching of the wisdom of liberation. The faculties themselves are diligence, mindfulness and wisdom, three in all.

The three phrases from "unattached and unobstructed with regard to the past" up to "unattached and unobstructed with regard to the present" teach the perfection of abiding in unshared wisdom. The three phrases from "all bodily action is preceded by wisdom" up to "all mental action is preceded by wisdom and follows wisdom" teach the perfection of carrying out unshared action.

After the eighteen unshared qualities have been taught, the thirty-two marks of a great man are mentioned, and to explain them he said, "What, then, are the thirty-two marks of the Tathāgata?" and so on. They are the marks of a great man because, when someone possesses them, one comes to understand, "This is a great man." There are thirty-two of them, and the eighty minor marks belong to them as their retinue.

"His feet are well planted" means that the soles of both feet are full and are set down evenly on the ground, without hollows, bulges and the like.

"Beneath the soles of both feet, on the soles of the feet, marks of wheels" is said because on the soles of both feet there are wheels with a thousand spokes, together with their hubs, complete in every aspect.

"Broad heels and high insteps" means that the heels are wide and thick and the upper part of the foot is high.

"Webbed feet and hands" means that the spaces between the fingers and toes of both feet and both hands are joined by something thin, like a net.

"Soft and tender hands and feet" means that the skin of the hands and feet is soft and yet firm and close-textured.

"Seven parts of the body elevated" means that the backs of the two hands are raised, the soles and the tops of the feet are raised, the two shoulders are raised, and the nape of the neck is full: these are the seven.

"Shanks like those of the eṇeya antelope" means that, starting from the thighs, the body gradually tapers downward.

"The private part withdrawn within" means that the private part is withdrawn within the skin and is not visible.

"The upper body like a lion's" means that the chest is broad and the waist narrow.

"The space between the collarbones is full" means that the space between the collarbones is filled out.

"The arms, when stretched out, are very long" means that when he stretches out his hands, they reach past the knees.

"The body is pure" means that no dirt clings to the body.

"The throat like a conch" means that the neck has folds like a conch.

"The jaws like a lion's" means that the jaws are large and full.

"Forty even teeth" means that the upper and lower teeth are equal in number, twenty and twenty.

"The teeth without gaps and even" means that the teeth fit closely together and none is larger or smaller.

"The large tongue" means that when he stretches out his tongue it covers his face.

"The supreme sense of taste" means that even if rotten barley falls on his tongue, he tastes it as the supreme flavor of the food of the gods.

"The voice of Brahmā, the sound of the kalaviṅka bird" means that his speech is gentle and soft, and so captivates the minds of those to be trained.

"Eyes deep blue" means that above the white of the eye the color is a deep blue.

"Eyelashes like a cow's" means that his eyelashes are like a cow's, even and fine.

"The uṣṇīṣa on the head" means that at the center of his head there is a protuberance of flesh which cannot be seen even when looked at from the world of Brahmā.

"Round and beautiful like a nyagrodha tree" means that the height and breadth of his body are equal, and his limbs are in proportion to his body, none larger or smaller.

Having taught the characteristics of the thirty-two marks of a great man, then, for the sake of beings who aspire to attain the marks and so wish to strive earnestly to accomplish their causes, he spoke in order to teach the causes of the marks: "This mark of a great man of the Tathāgata, the well-planted feet, was accomplished by his having formerly been very steadfast in his vows," and so on. Alternatively, the marks are of the nature of a result of maturation, and since a result without a cause is impossible, he teaches their cause.

"Accomplished by having formerly been very steadfast in his vows": formerly, when he was practicing the conduct of a bodhisattva, he never parted from the vows he had undertaken to do virtue and the like, and kept them earnestly and with respect; from that the well-planted feet were accomplished.

"Accomplished by having formerly put other beings at ease" means accomplished by not causing fright or fear to beings of little power and little strength.

"He gave protection and shelter by means of the Dharma" means accomplished by having formerly guarded himself from doing nonvirtuous actions such as killing. Alternatively, "protection, shelter and concealment by means of the Dharma" means accomplished by benefiting persons who practice the Dharma when they face fear and hardship, by being their companion at the times of listening, reflecting, meditating and so on. Here, helping at the time of listening is protecting; helping at the time of reflecting is sheltering; helping at the time of meditating is concealing.

"Accomplished by formerly not dividing retinues" means accomplished by not luring away the servants and retinues of others.

"Accomplished by formerly giving various garments" means accomplished by giving robes, beds, seats and the like.

"Accomplished by formerly upholding all the teachings of the buddhas" means accomplished by first listening to the sublime Dharma, then reflecting on it, and then meditating on it.

"Accomplished by formerly guarding secrets and abandoning sexual intercourse" means accomplished by guarding what must not be taught to those who are unfit vessels and not teaching it to them, by guarding the secrets of others and not proclaiming them to anyone else, and by abandoning the practices of unchastity.

"Accomplished by formerly practicing virtuous qualities" means accomplished by practicing moral discipline, samādhi and wisdom.

"Accomplished by giving others the relief of fearlessness" means accomplished by protecting beings oppressed by fear of kings, thieves and the like from that fear.

"Accomplished by formerly encouraging the tasks of others" means accomplished by inspiring enthusiasm in those who were doing them and by helping them.

"Accomplished by formerly bringing to full completion his application to the roots of virtue" means accomplished by bringing to their conclusion whatever virtuous deeds he had begun.

"Accomplished by formerly giving relief to beings" means accomplished by protecting beings born in the lower realms.

"Accomplished by formerly guarding the actions of body, speech and mind" means accomplished by guarding the three, body, speech and mind, so that they did not engage in sinful and nonvirtuous deeds.

"Formerly relying on an immeasurable heap of merit" means arising from merit amassed beyond measure, such as generosity and moral discipline. "Accomplished by formerly being without artifice in intention" means accomplished by keeping the mind free of deceit. "Accomplished as a mind fit for work while rightly practicing the Dharma" means accomplished by intention when, in examining the individual and general characteristics of phenomena, sleep, dullness, conceptual thought and analysis have been cleared away. "Accomplished by formerly abandoning busyness" means accomplished by formerly abandoning living in the midst of many beings. "Accomplished by formerly speaking the praise of those worthy of being proclaimed" means accomplished by making known the good qualities of those whose good qualities deserve to be proclaimed.

In "wholly beautiful as a mighty champion," "mighty champion" is a measure of strength. If one asks how great that measure of strength is: the strength of ten ordinary bull elephants is found in one scent elephant. The strength of ten scent elephants is found in one Mahānāgana. The strength of ten of these is found in one Srakanta. The strength of ten Srakantas is found in one Paraṅga. The strength of ten Paraṅgas is found in one Canūraga. The strength of ten Canūragas is found in one mighty champion. Because each joint of the Blessed One's body has the strength of a mighty champion, he is called "wholly beautiful as a mighty champion." That is why, when the Blessed One flung with his big toe the elephant that Devadatta had killed, so that it cleared seven walls, he was called "like a mighty champion."

After the marks, the eighty minor marks are taught, and wishing to explain them he said, "What are the eighty excellent minor marks?" and so on.

"The nails slightly red" means like the color of copper. "The nails glossy" means shining on the outside. "The nails raised" means the middle of the nail is set high and arched. "The nails rounded" means not flat. "The nails full" means without sparse gaps. "The nails gradually tapering" means the tips gradually narrow. "The ankle bones hidden" means the flesh of the ankle bones is sunk within. "The feet even" means the two feet are not unequal in size. "The gait of a lion" means he walks without fear or timidity. "The gait of a bull" means he walks as though pressing down the great earth. "The gait of an elephant" means he walks majestically. "The gait of a swan" means the steps of his right and left feet are always even. "Going to the right" means he steps first with the right foot. "Going beautifully" means his gait is graceful. "Going without swaying" means he sets down his feet without his body swaying. "The body rounded" means it is endowed with beauty. "The body glossy" means the reflection of a face appears in the skin of his body. "The body well proportioned" means it is large where it should be large and small where it should be small. "Height and breadth equal" means he has height and breadth alike. "All the minor marks beautiful" means that whatever should be a mark or minor mark is present without being fragmentary or incomplete. "Walking evenly" means without stooping forward or leaning back. "The body clean" means that nothing unclean or the like arises from it. "The body pure" means it has no stain. "The body not dejected" means he does not remain downcast. "The seven high" means surpassing others, or that the breasts and the like are high. "Limbs and lesser limbs well distinguished" means that the hands and feet are each distinct, not merged, and beautifully set without being joined together. "Vision clear and without dimness" means that his eyes are free of cataract and the like and he sees clearly. "The belly rounded" means it is not sagging. "The belly smooth" means its skin is smooth. "The belly not misshapen" means it is not thick above and thin in the middle. "The belly not sunken" means it is not drawn inward. "The navel deep" means it is sunk into the body. "Wholly beautiful" means he is surrounded on all sides by a halo a fathom wide, which makes him beautiful. "Clean in conduct" means that he never touches an unclean place with his right hand. "The lines of the hands deep" means the lines go inward. "The lines of the hands long" means they are unbroken. "The face not too long" means the face is not long in length. "Form and reflection appearing" means that the upper and lower lips of the mouth are not unequal in size, or that reflections appear on his body. "A voice resounding like a bull elephant and like the clouds" means his voice carries far. "The canine teeth even" means the four canine teeth are not unequal in size. "The canine teeth gradually tapering" means their tips are fine. "The nose clean" means no mucus drips from it. "The eyes wide" means they are large in breadth too. "The eyes long" means they are long in length too. "Like the petals of an opened utpala lotus" means they are gradually blue and are set long and slender. "Long and thick" means not sparse. "The eyebrows long" means the eyebrows are long in extent, without a break between them. "The hairs of the eyebrows even" means no hair is long or short. "The ears full and long" means the ears are large, not thin and bony. "The ears even" means the right and left ears are not unequal in size. "The faculty of hearing unimpaired" means no condition whatever can make him deaf. "Slender yet full" means not bony, and well filled with flesh. "The excellent limbs all full" means the whole body is beautiful. "The hair not tangled" means it is not mixed together and matted.

Thinking, "The Ascertainment of Meaning explained above has now been taught without omission," he spoke the words from "Monks" up to "I have taught." He declared that the Dharma as taught is not to be held as mere words but must be practiced earnestly, saying the words from "Monks, places of wilderness" up to "this is my teaching." "Monks, be heedful" means "Do not engage in nonvirtuous phenomena." Thinking that if one did engage in them, there would be torment of mind even at the moment of death and harm afterward as well, he declared, "Do not come to regret."

Regarding "This is my teaching": there are three miracles of the Tathāgata, the miracle of declaration, the miracle of instruction, and the miracle of magical power. The miracle of declaration is saying, "Meditate in this way." The miracle of instruction is telling someone, "Your thought right now is this and this." The miracle of magical power is displaying such things as flying through the sky.

Here, in order to explain the miracle of teaching, he said, "This is my teaching," because that miracle is superior to the other two miracles.

To show the result of explaining the sūtra, it says, "When this Dharma discourse was explained, the minds of five hundred monks were liberated from the defilements without appropriation." The result of hearing the sūtra is the abandonment of the rebirth-linking of the aggregates into one existence after another, and freedom from the afflictions to be abandoned by seeing and by meditation. Here, because the linking from one life to another has been abandoned, it is called "without appropriation." Because they are free from the afflictions to be abandoned by seeing and by meditation, it is called "the mind liberated."

The Commentary on the Ascertainment of Meaning is complete.

Translator's notes

The text is the commentary on the Arthaviniścaya Sūtra; the Tibetan don rnam par gdon mi za ba is rendered here as "Ascertainment of Meaning." "Five pomegranates and six cakes" is the stock Indian example of an utterance whose parts have no connection. Evident scribal slips read as intended: ཛྭཤེས as ཤེས, སྨ་བ as སྨྲ་བ, ལྷོགས as ཀློགས ("read"), and ལུང་འབོགས་སྡེ་པར as ལུང་འབོགས་པར. The example "rich like one at ease" (ངལ་བསོ་བ) is obscure; it may conceal the name of a wealthy figure such as Vaiśravaṇa, and is translated literally. Note that this commentary names Nanda, not Ānanda, as the reciter of the sūtra; this is kept as written. The Tibetan gloss of the six qualities ends literally "these six are explained as 'bcom'," the Tibetan rendering of bhaga in bhagavat; it is given as "bhaga" for sense. The figure of eighty-four years is kept as the text gives it. The etymology of Śrāvastī plays on the Tibetan name, literally "where there is hearing," together with the sense "where everything exists." རི་དགས་མ is identified as Mṛgāramātā (Migāra's mother, the laywoman Viśākhā), in whose mansion in the Eastern Park this sūtra is set; the commentary's etymology of "mansion" plays on prāsāda and prasanna. Scribal slips read as intended: ཀུན་འགའ as ཀུན་དགའ, སྒོགས་པ as སྒྲོགས་པ, and བསྟན་པ་བྱས་ཏེ as བསྟན་པར་བྱ་སྟེ. Evident scribal slips read as intended: ནང་དང་ཞིང as ནང་རབ་ཏུ་དང་ཞིང ("inner clarity") and སྤང as སྤངས. The phrase ཆད་ཀྱིས is obscure and is taken as "in short." In the second patient, the Tibetan reads literally "does not eat what is beneficial, eats the beneficial"; the sense plainly requires "what is not beneficial" (mi phan pa) in the second clause, and it is translated that way. In the leaking-vessel example, rjed pa is read as brjed pa, "forget," as the context requires. "The holy life" renders tshangs par spyod pa (brahmacarya), the last of the five excellences, here explained as leading to nirvāṇa. The phrase rendered "to rule out both overstatement and understatement" reads bskyed pa dang skur ba spang ba; bskyed pa is taken in the sense of sgro btags pa, "adding, superimposition," the usual pair with skur ba, "denial." In the perception of impurity, zhes pa is read as shes pa, "to know," as the context requires. "One comes to know the four truths" reads bden pa bzhi zhes par, taken as bden pa bzhi shes par, as the sense requires. "External things, which are not counted as sentient beings" follows the evident sense; the Tibetan as written lacks the negative (sems can gyi grangs su gtogs pa), but the question contrasts them with what is counted as sentient beings. The two grammatical analyses of "aggregates of appropriation" are a compound with the middle word dropped (madhyamapadalopin) and a genitive (sixth-case) tatpuruṣa compound. The list of tangibles is said to have eleven items but names only ten as written; heaviness, which the standard Abhidharma list includes, is absent here, and it is translated as it stands. Among the shapes, sdom pa is read as sbom pa, "thick," the natural pair with phra ba, "thin." "Virtuous non-informative form" renders dge ba'i rnam par rig byed, where the context (the subject being non-informative form) requires the negative ma yin pa, evidently dropped. In the account of the elements interpenetrating, the second "me la yod de" is read as "me yod de," "fire is present," matching the pattern of the other clauses. The word rendered "lowlands" (shol) is uncertain. The pungent substance sge'u gsher is tentatively identified as ginger. Several evident scribal slips are read according to sense: sams can as sems can, "sentient being"; dga' ba, "joy," in the list of torments of the body as rga ba, "aging," beside death; and rtag pa, "permanence," in the fourth way of understanding feeling as rtog pa, "conceptualization," as the text's own explanation ("arises from distorted conceptualization") requires. "Those who keep the temporary precepts" renders dus khrims pa, holders of the one-day (upavāsa) vow. In the comparison of coarseness, the Tibetan as written says that "the aggregate of formations" is coarser than formations and consciousness; the reasoning and the conclusion ("perception is explained before those two") show that perception (འདུ་ཤེས) is meant, a slip for འདུ་བྱེད, and it is translated so. Among the formations not associated with mind, the text reads "the faculty of feeling" where the standard lists have the life faculty (srog gi dbang po); it is translated as written. "Holding moral discipline and ascetic observances as supreme" is punctuated in the Tibetan as if two items, but is the single standard item śīlavrataparāmarśa. The enumeration of the eleven ways lists "momentary" and "flies and the like", while the explanation reads "impermanent" and "blue flies"; each is translated as written. Several readings have been taken as copyists' slips: "four meditative concentrations" reads བསམ་གཏན་བཞིན་ ("like the concentrations") for བཞི་ན; "present" reads དེ་ལྟར་གྱི for ད་ལྟར་གྱི; "rolled-up strip of birch bark" reads གྲོག for གྲོ་ག (the classical Abhidharma image for the ear organ); and "imagined" reads ཀུན་ཏུ་རྟག་པ ("permanent") for ཀུན་ཏུ་བརྟགས་པ, as the next passage, which explains "the imagined" as what the tīrthikas posit, confirms. The pair ring ba and thung ba in the heap formula is translated "long or short" as written; the familiar formula has "far or near". The phrase rendered "the curry and the seasonings and condiments" (ཚོད་མ་དང་ཚ་སྤོད་ཚག་ཚེག) is partly uncertain. The description of the eye particles "in the black and the dark yellow" is somewhat obscure. The last sentence repeats the definition of "unpleasant" in isolation, with ཉན for སྙན; it is translated as an evident stray repetition. In the account of the tangible, "where fire and water predominate" is given twice, first for smoothness and then for a quality written གཉེན་པ, which is not in the list of eleven and whose meaning is unclear; it is rendered tentatively as "pliancy". In the tongue passage, ཟག་པ is the everyday "dripping, leaking" and ཡི་ག is read as ཡི་ག(འ) "appetite". The sutra quotation addresses the hearers as དགེ་སྦྱོང་དག ("ascetics", śramaṇas), whereas the earlier quotation had དགེ་སློང་དག ("monks"); it is translated as written. The text compares the five sense elements with "the element of mental consciousness" where the reasoning concerns the mind element; it is translated as written. ལྟུང་བ in "food and drink" is read as བཏུང་བ ("drink"), an evident slip. The sense source "that abides pervading all of these" and is explained last is evidently the mind sense source, though the text does not name it. The long opening question lists the correspondences between aggregates, elements and sense sources; in it "the five elements such as the mind element" is evidently a slip for the eye element (the five sense elements), and the range of the ten sense sources is given loosely ("from the eye sense source ... the form sense source"); the sense has been kept as plain as the wording allows. The text spells "dependent" as བརྟན་པ ("firm") in the gloss, an evident slip for བརྟེན་པ. In "they do not understand the past, the future, and the present," the text reads de ltar ("thus"); I read it as da ltar ("the present"), which the triad of times plainly requires. In "the latent tendency of karma imprinted on the mental consciousness," the text reads dgos ("needed"), evidently a slip for bsgos ("imprinted"), the verb used in the parallel statement above. The text first names consciousness's function as being "the support" of beings' karma and then, in explaining it, calls it "the condition" for their karma; both are translated as written. The three characteristics (without activity, impermanent, potent conditions) correspond to the Abhidharmasamuccaya's nirīha, anitya and samartha pratyaya. The Tibetan repeatedly confuses sred pa ("craving") with srid pa ("becoming, existence"), which differ by one vowel. Where the sense plainly requires it, I have read srid pa: "renewed existence" (yang srid pa) in the functions of grasping and becoming, "condition for becoming" as the second function of grasping (its explanation confirms this), "becoming, whose nature is karma" (karmabhava) twice, and "formations and becoming" under the affliction of karma, as the standard scheme requires. In the account of craving, the second function (being the condition for grasping) is not named in the opening statement; the text's wording is kept as it stands. "Unruly enjoyments" (longs spyod ma rungs pa) and the simile "like a meal mixed of six kinds of food" (kha zas drug dang 'dres pa) are rendered literally; their precise intent is uncertain. Several readings are corrected as evident slips. "Planted in a field" reads zhing ("field") for shing ("tree") in the phrase sa bon gyi rten gyi shing, as the parallel "field of the forming of karma" requires. In the list of meanings, the third item reads sems can gyi dbang ("dependence on beings"), but its explanation is headed sems can med pa ("without a sentient being," Skt. niḥsattva), which I follow; the list also inserts "is the meaning of dependent origination" after the first item, which I have folded into the closing predicate. In the balance-beam simile the text reads that the result instant "does not arise" (mi 'byung) simultaneously; the simile and the doctrine (cause ceasing and result arising at once, as the beam's ends fall and rise together) require "arises," which I have translated. The heading "correspondence of cause and result" is given as "non-correspondence" (rjes su mi mthun pa) at its explanation, against the list and the explanation's own content; I follow the list. The image rendered "like being crushed in a press" (ris brtsir ba) is uncertain in its exact sense. The first of the five sufferings is named "at the time of formation" in the list and "at the time of acting" in its explanation; both are kept as written. The divisions are announced as ten, but the text lists and explains eight; this is translated as it stands. Under the profundity of arising, the text names the first meaning as sems kyi dbang ("dependence on mind"), evidently a slip for gzhan gyi dbang ("dependence on another"), as the immediately following explanation shows; I have translated the latter. In the famine story, the phrase thal ba 'phur ma cig gnam la gtor is obscure; I have rendered it, in line with the well-known Abhidharma story of the bag of ashes hung on the wall, as hanging a bag of ashes up high. "Totality spheres" renders mtha' dag skye mched (Skt. kṛtsnāyatana). The opening phrase lha dang mi'i bde ba phyir mi 'ong ba'i 'bras bu ("the result of non-returning of the happiness of gods and humans") is obscure, since non-returning makes no sense in a description of meritorious formations; I have rendered the evident sense, clinging to the result consisting of the happiness of gods and humans. In "aging, whose characteristic is the wearing out of the aggregates," and in "aging is of two kinds," the text reads dga' ba ("joy"), a slip for the like-sounding rga ba ("aging"). "Unhappiness" renders slong ba, apparently a slip for the standard member yid mi bde ba (daurmanasya) in the list sorrow, lamentation, suffering, unhappiness, distress. "Arbuda and kalala" render nur nur po and mer mer po, the first embryonic stages. The text reads མི་སློང་བ for the fourth item of the list (sorrow, lamentation, suffering, unhappiness, distress); this makes no sense in context and is evidently a slip for ཡིད་མི་བདེ་བ, "unhappiness," which is translated. The Sanskrit-in-Tibetan title ཛྙཱ་ན་པྲ་སྟ་ན is identified as the Sarvāstivāda Abhidharma treatise Jñānaprasthāna. འདུལ་བ་ཁ་ཅིག is taken here in the sense of "some of those to be trained" (vineya) rather than Vinaya. In "affliction also arises from what is a mere thing," དངོས་པོ (vastu) refers to the links that are mere results (suffering), such as feeling. In the three-sticks simile the text reads རྣམ་པར་ཤེས་པ་གཉིས་ལ, which does not construe; it is translated in the evident sense "without consciousness the two, name and form, cannot stand." In the pot simile, སྲེད་པ་དང་ཆགས་པ ("craving and attachment") fits poorly beside the parallel images of decay and destruction; it is taken as a slip for words meaning cracking and breaking (ཆག་པ), and translated so. སྔོན་གྱི་ལས ("former action") at the start of the explanation of the prior end is evidently a slip for སྔོན་གྱི་མཐའ ("the prior end") and is translated accordingly. བདག་གི་བར་དུ་དུཀུན་ཏུ་རྟོག་པ is read as བདག་གི་བར་ཀུན་ཏུ་རྟོག་པ, "imagining to be one's own." The "unmoving" formations are the imperturbable (āneñjya) actions leading to the form and formless realms. The six causes and five results are those of Sarvāstivāda Abhidharma (sabhāgahetu, sarvatragahetu, sahabhūhetu, saṃprayuktakahetu, kāraṇahetu, vipākahetu; puruṣakāra-, niṣyanda-, vipāka-, adhipati- and visaṃyogaphala). ལས་འདྲེན་མ་ཡིན་ཏེ is read as ལས་འདྲེས་མ་ཡིན་ཏེ, "is a mixed action," which the following clause requires. In the sentence on indeterminate phenomena the text reads ནུས་པའི་ཕྱིར་རྣམ་པར་སྨིན་རྒྱུ་ཞེས་བྱའོ ("because they are able, they are called maturation causes"), which contradicts its own reasoning ("weak like a rotten seed") and standard doctrine; a negation appears to have dropped out, and the evident sense, that they lack the power and are not called maturation causes, is translated. རང་བཞིན here is the Sāṃkhya primal nature (prakṛti), and སྐྱེས་བུ the Sāṃkhya person (puruṣa). In "the sufferings of birth, aging and so on," the text reads དགའ་བ ("joy"), which makes no sense among sufferings; it is evidently a slip for རྒ་བ ("aging") and is translated so. The etymology of virtue plays on the Sanskrit kuśala; the Tibetan writes ཀུན for the first element, apparently representing Sanskrit kuśa, glossed as wisdom, with la glossed as "taking." བསྟན་པར་བྱ་བ in the section on what is to be cultivated is read as བསྟེན་པར་བྱ་བ, as the heading of that section has it. The phrase ཁ་ན་མ་ཐོ་བའི་མངོན་པར་མི་རྟོགས་པ is taken as citing one of the sūtra's further synonyms for not knowing. The text reads མཐོང་བའོ ("is seeing") where the list of synonyms and the parallel clauses require མ་མཐོང་བའོ, "is not seeing"; the negation has evidently dropped out and is restored. In the example from the world, the text reads འཇིག་རྟེན་ནི་མི་བདེ་བ, but the explanation that follows ("fire ... is not pleasant, yet ... one says, 'Fire is pleasant'") shows the saying is "fire is pleasant" (མེ་བདེ་བ), which is translated. The last sentence is cut off by the division into pieces and is completed by the sūtra phrasing that continues in the next piece. "They are not mind itself; they are factors that arise from mind": the Tibetan has a negative in both clauses ("are not dharmas of mind, are not dharmas arisen from mind"), which is self-contradictory; the second negative is taken as a slip for the affirmative. In the fivefold scheme the list reads "stabilizing" (brtan pa) but the gloss on "associated with mind" repeats "supporting" (brten pa); it is read as "stabilizing", and the sūtra word "dependent on mind" is written brtan for brten. "Otherwise it would not contradict the other scriptures" (gzhan du na) is read in its evident sense, that this interpretation avoids conflict. "mdog zhan" is read as mdo gzhan, "other sūtras". The five causes of derived form correspond to the Abhidharma list of generation, basis, support, sustenance and growth. Several evident scribal slips are read as follows: rtag par ("permanent") as rtas par ("flourishes"); yang na ni as yang ba ni ("lightness"); de las ("from that") and 'jig rten nas as referring to sa, "earth"; ming gang gzugs as ming dang gzugs ("name and form"); nyams pa as nyan pa ("hearing"). The reasons "pointless" and "incapable" are taken to apply respectively to more than four and fewer than four elements. The phrase rendered "to teach how it operates, including the enumeration" (grangs smos pa'i bar du byed pa) is obscure, and the rendering is tentative. "Contact that engages the object further" renders lhag par yul la 'jug pa'i reg pa, which corresponds to what the Abhidharma calls designation contact (adhivacana-saṃsparśa); the commentary glosses it in its own way. Evident slips read: las bstan pa as las skyes pa ("arising from"), gnyig ma yin pa as gnyis ka ma yin pa ("neither"), lud as lung ("scripture"). The statement that only equanimous feeling is present from the third concentration upward is translated as the text gives it, though standard Abhidharma assigns pleasant feeling to the third concentration as well. The "mustard-oil insect" (yungs mar gyi sgang bu) is an unidentified creature. In the sūtra quotation the Tibetan reads srid pa, "becoming"; it is taken as sred pa, "craving", since the formula here is "with craving as condition, grasping" and the preceding section treats craving. Likewise bsten is read as bstan, "to explain", as in the same formula later in the passage; the doubled bzhi bzhi is taken as a single "four"; and khyem is read as khyim, "householder". The further afflictions said to be ten, which bring the count to thirty-four, are named as only eight, with doubt listed again; the list is translated as it stands. The counts of entities follow the Abhidharma reckoning of the latent afflictions abandoned by seeing the four truths and by meditation, as in the Abhidharmakośa (34, 30 and 6 entities for the first three graspings). In the sentence on the eight doubts the Tibetan names the formless realm in both clauses; the first is evidently meant to be the form realm, as the parallel sentences and the total of eight require. It is translated as written. For the life span in Avīci the Tibetan writes che'i tshad; it is read as tshe'i tshad, "life span", as in the parallel sentence on Pratāpana. The counts (thirty-eight entities for grasping at a doctrine of self) and the scheme of three stages follow the Abhidharmakośa's treatment of the four graspings. The life span in Saṃghāta is given as two hundred years (nyis brgya); the doubling series of the other hells, and the Abhidharmakośa, would lead one to expect two thousand. It is translated as written. In the first list of the cold hells the Tibetan writes o sgra tham tham and sa la la; the text itself later spells them so sgra tham tham, "teeth chattering", and a la la, and those readings are followed. Since their Sanskrit equivalents cannot be fixed with confidence from this text's order, these two names are rendered from the Tibetan. zung ma, "twins", is taken as a rendering of Yāma (Skt. yama, "twin"), the gods of the third heaven of the desire realm. gshegs pa in the descriptions of Kālasūtra and Saṃjīva is read as gshags pa, "to split"; zhabs yang in the simile of the heap is read as zhabs yangs, "broad at the base". Rāhula (sgra gcan 'dzin) is here a king of the hungry ghosts, not the Buddha's son; the text's second opinion makes Yama that king. His abode is placed five thousand leagues down, where the Abhidharmakośa has five hundred; translated as written. The nāga king sa 'dzin, "Earth Holder", could not be identified with confidence (possibly Dharaṇīṃdhara). The passage on Manu is compressed: shed bu and shed bdag are the Tibetan lexical renderings of mānava, "son of Manu", and of Manu. ngal so po and gnas srung ba po are unusual Tibetan forms for two of the Four Great Kings, identified by elimination as Vaiśravaṇa and Dhṛtarāṣṭra. Of the last two of Śakra's ministers, nab so is unidentified (it is the Tibetan name of the lunar mansion Punarvasu), and tha skar skyes, "born of Aśvinī", evidently refers to the Aśvins. For the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, the Yāmas, and the Tuṣitas the text reckons in human days (nyin zhag) where the Abhidharmakośa has human years; translated as written. thun spyoms, "Those of the Watches", plays on yāma in the sense of a watch of the day. che'i tshad is again read as tshe'i tshad, "life span", for northern Kuru and the Four Great Kings. khrom dgongs, used here for the body sizes of the form-realm gods, is evidently an older term for the yojana (its figures match the Abhidharmakośa's yojanas); it is rendered "league", like dpag tshad. For the Parīttaśubha gods the body size is given as ten leagues, where the doubling series would give sixteen; translated as written. In the explanation of the Anabhraka gods, sprin gyi mchod pa, "offering of clouds", makes no sense; the evident sense, a covering of cloud, is translated. che'i tshad is again read as tshe'i tshad, "life span", for the Nirmāṇarati gods. In the Puṇyaprasava section the Tibetan reads ཆེའི་ཚད for ཚེའི་ཚད, evidently a slip; translated as "life span", as in every other section. In the second explanation of the Pure Abodes, གསང་བ ("secret") is read as གཙང་བ ("pure"), which the etymology requires. This text treats Sudarśana (ཤིན་ཏུ་མཐོང) before Sudṛśa (གྱ་ནོམ་སྣང), the reverse of the usual order, and gives Sudṛśa one hundred thousand (བརྒྱ་སྟོང) eons and leagues. The doubling series, up to sixteen thousand for Akaniṣṭha, and the standard Abhidharma figure suggest eight thousand (བརྒྱད་སྟོང), but the number is translated as written. In the second Sanskrit etymology of Akaniṣṭha the Tibetan says it is where the defilements (ཟག་པ) arise; translated as it stands, though the intended sense is unclear. Here སེར་བ is the color yellow and སེམས་ཀྱི་རྒྱུད is "mindstream"; the glossary senses "hail" and "tantra" do not apply. Two evident slips are corrected. Before the question about what the four name aggregates rely on, བསྐལ་པ ("eon") is read as བརྒལ་བ ("objection"), the text's own term for an objection; and in that question གཅིག་ལ ("on one") is read as ཅི་ལ ("on what"), which the reply requires. The section on the fourth formless base is uneven in the Tibetan: besides its standard name, the text three times calls it "neither nothingness nor not-nothingness" (ཅུང་ཟད་མེད་ཅུང་ཟད་མེད་མིན) and once shortens this; the analysis beginning "If this is divided up" gives only its first stage; and the sūtra phrase quoted after "subtle in its discernment" names the base of nothingness. All are translated as written. In the reply on the support of mind in the formless realm, the text names the faculty of feeling (ཚོར་བའི་དབང་པོ), where the Abhidharmakośa names the life faculty together with group homogeneity; translated as written. Glossary senses that do not apply here: ལྟུང་བ is the verb "fall" and དབང་པོ is "faculty". In the list of the four points the sūtra teaches, བསྟན་པ ("taught") is read as བརྟན་པ ("sustained"), which the explanation that follows (ཉེ་བར་བརྟན་པ, "sustenance") requires. ཤེལ is taken as Śaila, the egg-born man whom the Abhidharmakośa names together with Upaśaila; this text pairs him with Utpala, and gives thirty sons of Viśākhā where the Kośa has thirty-two. The first reply about miraculously born nāgas is obscure: literally the garuḍa eats "not knowing its going into appearance"; translated closely. Glossary senses that do not apply here: བདག in the verse is the pronoun "I", དབང་པོ is "faculty", and ཀུན་ཏུ in "full birth" (ཀུན་ཏུ་སྐྱེ་བ) is not the name kunta. Several evident slips are read in their intended sense: ཚོར་བའི་དབང་པོ ("faculty of feeling") as འཚོ་བའི་དབང་པོ ("life faculty"), as the sūtra term and the surrounding sentences require (the same reading probably applies to the reply about the formless realm in an earlier passage, rendered there as "faculty of feeling"); ཆེའི as ཚེའི ("life"); ཐོས་པ ("heard") as ཐོབ་པ ("obtained, reached"), twice; སྒྲ ("sound") as སྐྲ ("hair") and ལྟས ("omen") as ལྤགས ("skin") in the list of eleven points, as the explanations of grey hair and wrinkles show; བར་བ as ཁར་བ ("staff"); and ལས་སུས as ལས་སུ ("tasks"). Glossary senses that do not apply here: དབང in དབང་དུ and དབང་པོ means "with regard to" and "faculty", and བྱེ་བ means "come apart". Evident slips are read in their intended sense: ཆེའི as ཚེའི ("life"); ཞེས་བྱ as ཤེས་བྱ ("what is to be known") at the start of the reply on the order of the links; སྲིད་པ ("existence") as སྲེད་པ ("craving") where the sequence runs feeling, craving, grasping, existence; and དགེ་སྦྱོར as དགེ་སྦྱོང ("renunciant"). གཞུང་མེད་པ, literally "those without scripture", is not identified with confidence; it may render Nirgrantha understood as "without texts". རབ་ཏུ་འཇོམས་པ is taken as the Saṃghāta hell, called འཇོམས་པ earlier in this text. Glossary senses that do not apply here: ལམ་རྒྱུད in ལམ་རྒྱུད་ལྔ means "courses (of rebirth)" and རྒྱུད in དྲིས་རྒྱུད means "mindstream"; ལྟ་བ is "seeing"; སྟོན་པ refers to non-Buddhist teachers; ཀུན་ཏུ in ཀུན་ཏུ་རྒྱུ ("wandering mendicants") is not the name kunta. The first verse reads "true" (bden par) in both lines. In its familiar form, as quoted together with the following simile of the hair in the Abhidharmakośabhāṣya (chapter six), the word is "happiness" (bde bar): what the noble ones call happiness, others know as suffering; what others call happiness, the noble ones know as suffering. Bden par may be a slip for bde bar, but since it is a real word it is translated as written. The school called gtogs 'dod is not a standard Tibetan name, but the doctrine ascribed to it, liberation through seeing the self and the Principal (pradhāna), defined as the three guṇas rajas, tamas and sattva in equilibrium, is that of the Sāṃkhya, so it is rendered Sāṃkhya. In that sentence sems can, normally "sentient being", renders the guṇa sattva. In the list of false causes, rang bzhin names a non-Buddhist first cause (prakṛti, or possibly svabhāva) and is rendered "Nature". Evident slips read: lta bu as lta ba ("view"), and skye bo as skye'o ("arise"). The origin is said to have "the characteristic of becoming" (srid pa); since the next clause speaks of craving, sred pa, "craving", may be the intended reading, but srid pa is translated as written. In "one then wonders", rtogs pas is read as rtog pas. In this piece 'joms pa is the verb "destroy", kun tu means "fully" (in "full knowledge") or is part of the word for "fetter", and the letters sa ga occur only inside khams gsum, "three realms"; none of these is a name here. In "an old grudge", kho na is read as khon, "grudge", the word the next sentence uses; kho na ("only") makes no sense here. Here tsha ba is ordinary "heat", not the hell Tapana, and the letters sa ga occur only inside khams gsum, "three realms"; neither is a name in this piece. In the physician simile, "a support for the king" renders rgyal po'i rkyen, literally the king's "condition", in the sense of a requisite or support. The disciple dga' ba can, who delights in detailed explanation, could not be identified with confidence (perhaps Nanda or Nandika) and is translated literally as "Joyful". The Tibetan names both kinds of trainee and then gives the comparison with Kāśyapa, which the following lines apply to those strong in causes; it is translated accordingly. In the list of five births, rab tu byung ba means coming forth from the womb, as the text itself explains, not monastic going forth. Evident slips read: 'dul ba as gdul ba ("those to be trained"); dga' ba ("joy") as rga ba ("aging") in "the remaining sufferings, aging and so on"; gshegs pa as gshags pa ("split"); skye na as zhe na ("if one asks"); and kyis as kyi in "the basis for the first arising of all qualities". The letters sa ga occur here only inside khams gsum, "three realms", and are not the name Viśākhā. Evident slips read: brtan pa as brten pa, "what is supported", as the text itself spells it a few lines later; bstan pa ("teaching") as brtan pa ("steadiness") in the list of qualities that aging ruins; and gang as dang in "strength, diligence, and power". In "the breeding ground of various illnesses", skye mched is used in its literal sense of a place where things arise, not as "sense source". The rare word btsa' btsa' ba, describing the newborn's body, is rendered "raw". The words matched to earlier names are ordinary words here: 'joms pa "destroy", padmo "lotus", mdzes pa "beauty", tsha ba "hot", kun tu "fully", and tshangs pa inside mi tshangs par spyod pa, "sexual union"; sa ga occurs only across a word boundary. None of them is a name in this piece. Evident copyists' slips, translated as corrected: lus kyis la sogs pa'i 'byung ba chen po is read as lus kyi sa la sogs pa'i, "the great elements of the body, earth and the rest," matching the same phrase later in the passage; che'i 'du byed is read as tshe'i 'du byed, "the life formations"; and bral bar dga' ba, "who delight in parting," which contradicts the weeping relatives, is read as bral bar dka' ba, "from whom it is hard to part." In the forest fire simile, mi dang dbang po ("people and faculties" or "people and lords") makes no sense; it is tentatively read as mi dang glang po, "people and elephants," and this conjecture is uncertain. The disease name sgong po is not securely identified and is rendered "swellings." Some words here are not the names they stand for elsewhere: mdzes pa is "attractive" (the alluring object that provokes desire, set against what provokes anger), not Cāru; 'joms pa is the verb "shatter, vanquish," not the hell Saṃghāta; ma mos is "unwilling," not mātṛkā; and gdon mi za bar is "without fail," not graha. Three evident copyists' slips are translated as corrected: tshig rnam pa sna tshogs bla ba is read as bzla ba, "babbling words of every kind"; gang smos pa mang du skyed pa is read as smon pa, "producing many wishes," as in the clause just before; and in the account of what one wants, sdug bsngal ba dang ma bral zhing, "not parted from suffering," is read without the negative, "to be parted from suffering and never meet with it again," as the parallel "to meet with happiness and never be parted from it again" requires. The exclamation e ma'o here voices the seeker's dismay and is rendered "Oh!" The word 'joms pa is here the verb "crush," not the hell Saṃghāta. Evident copyists' slips, translated as corrected: in the sūtra quotation, dag pa'i 'dod chags is read as dga' ba'i 'dod chags, "the attachment of delight," and de yang der mngon pa dga' ba as de dang der mngon par dga' ba, "delighting in this and that," as the commentary itself glosses these phrases just below; and at the end of the salt water simile, srid pa'o is read as sred pa'o, "that insatiability is craving." The beans sran ma sha and mungga are taken as māṣa (black gram) and mudga (mung). Evident copyists' slips, translated as corrected: yid 'byung ba can gyi sred pa is read as yang 'byung ba can, "craving that brings renewed existence." The text confuses sred pa "craving" and srid pa "existence" in several places, and each is read as its context requires: in the list of synonyms for the aggregates, sred pa is read as srid pa, "existence," since its gloss, "because they transmigrate and take birth from one life to the next," explains existence, not craving; and in "the chief cause is srid pa" and "in the form realm, through the power of srid pa," srid pa is read as sred pa, "craving," as the argument and the parallel clauses require. Of the seeds, ma grub pa gives no clear sense and is tentatively taken as ma grum pa, "unbroken." The phrase bdag gi yul dang is taken as "oneself and objects," since the fourfold division that follows covers both one's own body and objects. The list of the five kinds of seed names four kinds and then plain "seeds" as the fifth; it is translated as it stands. Evident copyists' slips, translated as corrected: under the characteristic, las dang nyon mongs pa mi skye ba is read as lam dang, nyon mongs pa mi skye ba, "the path, and the non-arising of afflictions," since the explanation that follows treats suchness, the path, and the non-arising of afflictions as its three items; under completeness, the text twice has yongs su ma rdzogs pa, "incomplete," where the list of eleven points and the sense (arhats who have abandoned all afflictions) require yongs su rdzogs pa, "complete"; rigs pa gsum, "three reasonings," is read as rig pa gsum, "the three knowledges"; da dud is read as da dung, "still"; and in the sūtra quotation, gyis srid pa is read as gyi sred pa, "craving," as the commentary's own gloss shows. In the second explanation the last term is written nus pa ("capacity"), which makes no sense in this list; it is translated as nub pa, "disappeared," the sūtra's eighth term. The objection's "len du" is read as lan du, "in reply." The path is once called "the path of liberation (grol ba) into the cessation of suffering"; this is kept as written, though the sūtra phrase quoted on either side is "the path leading ('gro ba) to the cessation of suffering," so grol may be a slip for 'gro. Here mdo and mdor mean "summary," not "sūtra"; mthong ba'i chos nyid la is "in this very life" (dṛṣṭa eva dharme), not dharmatā; lhag ma bdun is "the remaining seven," not the seven mothers. Tsha ba occurs only in tsha ba'i nad, "a disease of heat" (fever), and sa ga only inside khams gsum, so neither Tapana nor Viśākhā is named in this piece. The two senses of "path," seeking and going, play on Sanskrit mārga, traditionally derived from mārg-, "to seek." In the lemma on the origin, bzhi ("four") makes no sense and is read as gzhi, "basis," matching the simile's "source (gzhi) of the illness"; the commentary that follows explains only the basis, the origin, and never a "four." Here rgyud is a practitioner's mental continuum, not "tantra"; las kyi mtha' is right action (karmānta), not an "extreme"; sdug bsngal gyi phung po is "the mass of suffering." Tsha bar gyur pa means "you have become feverish," not the name Tapana, and sa ga occurs only inside khams gsum, so neither Tapana nor Viśākhā is named in this piece. The stray syllable phya'i in byed phya'i phyir is read as byed pa'i phyir. Lta ba snyems pa and tshul khrims dang brtul zhugs mchog tu 'dzin pa snyems pa name the afflictions usually called holding views as supreme and holding moral discipline and vows as supreme; snyems pa is rendered literally as "conceit." In the thief simile the text says "the two men" (mi gnyis po) shut the door, although it first gives that task to one of them; this is kept as written. Kun ta and sa ga occur only as fragments (kun tu, "completely," and pas gnas), so neither name appears here; rgyud is the mental continuum, dbang "power," rgyal ba "conquer," and ltung ba "fall," not their glossary senses. In the remedy for laziness, bden par 'dzin ("holding as true") is kept as written; the parallel with the other three aspects (holding the impermanent as permanent, and so on) suggests that bde bar 'dzin, "holding as pleasant," may have been meant. In the upper realms khong khro ba spangs te means "excluding anger," and in the origin section bkol te means "setting aside," since those afflictions do not belong to that set. Among the aspects of cessation, 'byung ba stands for nges par 'byung ba (niḥsaraṇa), rendered "emergence." Kun ta and sa ga occur only as fragments of kun tu 'byung ba and khams gnyis, so neither name appears here. De ma thag pa'i lam, "the immediate path," is the same as the uninterrupted path named at the start of this section (ānantaryamārga). In the hub simile, gzugs ("form") in "the hub makes the gzugs of the spokes" is read as gzungs, "holds," matching the next clause, where moral discipline is their gzungs, their holder; gzungs here is not "dhāraṇī." In the gloss on right thought the Tibetan says 'jigs pa, literally "fear," twice; it is rendered "apprehension." In the passage on the wheel coming down and rising up, the words "if one says" (zhe na) are kept as written although no reply follows. Mdo tsam means "a summary," and yul gzhan "another land"; sa ga occurs only inside khams gsum, so Viśākhā is not named here. In right speech the Tibetan says only that the unmanifest kind concerns speech such as lies and harsh words; in context this means the unseen restraint from such speech, as against the manifest act of speaking truthfully. "Right action" renders ལས་ཀྱི་མཐའ (karmānta), so མཐའ is not "extreme" here; likewise མདོ་ཙམ་དུ means "in brief", not "sūtra", and འདུལ་བ here means those to be trained (vineya), not the Vinaya. I read རྟོག་པ ("conceptual thought") for the written རྟོགས་པ ("realization"), which would not explain why the view after rising from samādhi is called worldly, and གཞི ("ground") for the written བཞི ("four"), as in the list of six just before. In ས་བོན་གྱི་རྟེན་བྱེད་པ་ནི་མ་ཡིན, མ་ཡིན means "is the mother", parallel to ཕ་ཡིན "is the father", not a negation. "The unmoving" is āniñjya karma, the wholesome karma that ripens in the form and formless realms. In "the mind's wholesome thinking" I read རྟོག་པ ("thinking") for the written རྟོགས་པ ("realizing"), since the sentence defines right thought. In the list of benefactors, མྱ་ངན་ལས་འདས་པ is used in its everyday sense of getting past grief, not nirvāṇa; the Tibetan is intransitive ("passed beyond the grief"), and I take the benefactor as the one who brought this about. "The sickness of bondage" renders བཅིངས་པའི་ནད as written; it may stand for "imprisonment or sickness". The six limbs of moral discipline follow the standard formula (śīlavān, prātimokṣasaṃvarasaṃvṛta, ācāragocarasaṃpanna, aṇumātreṣv avadyeṣu bhayadarśī, samādāya śikṣate śikṣāpadeṣu). "Take no offense" renders mig mi 'gag pa, literally "the eye is not blocked," apparently meaning that worldly people find nothing objectionable; the sense is inferred. "The formal act consisting of a motion and three proclamations" renders gsol ba dang zhu ba'i las bzhi (Skt. jñapticaturtha karman), the fourfold act of ordination. "Right thought" (yang dag pa'i rtog pa) is the second limb of the eightfold path. ཆེས་མངོན་པའི་མཛོད in the last citation is read as chos mngon pa'i mdzod, the Abhidharmakośa. The second Kośa quotation is close to but not identical with the known Tibetan of Kośa IV.76, and its sense is that such speech is idle chatter and to be abandoned. In the second component of killing, the Tibetan reads that striking a living being while perceiving it as a log "is" killing (srog bcad pa yin no); this contradicts the point being made and the explanation that follows, so it is read as "is not" (ma yin no). "Mound" renders mtho, whose sense here is uncertain. In "if one takes an ownerless treasure," the Tibetan has byin ("gives"), which makes no sense in a list of thefts; it is read as a slip for a verb of taking or finding. "Deceased monk" renders dge slong zhi ba. "The formal act of motion and proclamation" (gsol ba dang zhu ba'i las) is the saṅgha's act distributing a dead monk's goods within the monastic boundary (sīmā). "The community" renders 'gro ba here, the many people whose rule protects the woman. In the first comparison with the king's queen the Tibetan reads dus khrims ma nos pa ("has not taken the vow of observance"); this is read as mnos pa ("has taken"), as in the parallel sentence that follows and as the sense requires. "In another sense, a theft from the king" renders the obscure rgyal po las gzhan du brkus. In the case of the community the Tibetan mtshungs par spyod pa is read as "likewise engages in impure conduct" (mi tshangs par). "In plain view of everyone" renders g.yel ba kun gyis mthong ba, whose first word is uncertain. "Can one incur" reads rigs pa as reg pa. The lists of four components for lying and slander name only three items each, as in the Tibetan. The king's name is given in transliterated Sanskrit (shri ri ha ri sha); it is reproduced letter for letter as Śririhariśa, since it cannot be identified with confidence. "There is nothing desired and nothing undesired" renders 'dod pa dang mi 'dod pa med literally; it likely reflects the Sanskrit iṣṭa ("sacrifice") of the stock formula of wrong view (no gift, no sacrifice, no oblation). "The seed and the basis" refers to the father and mother as the seed and the field of birth. In the application of killing, "leads it away and has it bound" renders the obscure bsnams khrid bran khrims bcug, and "strikes" reads bsdun as bsnun. The completions of covetousness and malice (bdag gir mi bya mi gtong ngo; mi gsad mi rdeg tu mi gtong ngo) are read as double negatives: "I will not let it go without making it mine," "I will not let him go without killing or beating him." Here མཆོད་སྦྱིན means "sacrifice" (yajña), not the personal name Yajñadatta used elsewhere. The examples closely parallel Vasubandhu's Abhidharmakośabhāṣya on the ten paths of action (the Persians, the brahmins' claim that all was given them by Brahmā, the cattle sacrifice or gosava, the simile of women as mortar, flower and so on, and the verse on the five sinless lies, which is also found in the Mahābhārata). "Tryambuka" is a transliterated name for a kind of stinging insect, kept as is. The sentence on the brahmins taking property is textually rough (བྲམ་དམས་པ read as "the brahmins having grown weak," and the clause before "a brahmin may take them away" rendered by sense following the Kośa parallel). In the final sentence the Tibetan lists malice, harsh speech and killing proper under "killing," evidently meaning that these three are completed by aversion. In the story of the two monks, ཕམ་པ means losing the dispute, not the pārājika offense. In the ranking "by nature," the Tibetan as it stands places idle talk as more blameworthy than killing, which is unusual (one would expect the speech acts to be ranked separately from the bodily ones); it is translated as written. "A wife shared with rivals" renders བསྟོད་པ་དང་བཅས་པའི་ཆུང་མ, read in light of the commentary's own gloss and the standard list in which this result is a wife who has rivals or paramours; the word བསྟོད་པ ("praise") is evidently a corruption here. In the list of categories, "white action with white ripening" and so on follow the standard fourfold classification of karma. The five forms of a monk's wrong livelihood correspond to the standard list kuhanā (hypocrisy), lapanā (flattery), naimittikatā (hinting), naiṣpeṣikatā (coercion), and lābhena lābhaniścikīrṣā (seeking gain by means of gain). སྡང་བ ("hating") in the explanation of right effort is evidently a slip for ལྡང་བ ("rising"), as the gloss about rising from one's seat shows; it is translated accordingly. In the remark on selling beings, the Tibetan says the prohibition applies to "laywomen" (དགེ་བསྙེན་མ); this seems to stand for lay followers in general and is rendered so. "Humors" renders ཉེས་པ here in its medical sense (doṣa): wind, bile, and phlegm. Before the delusion-type's list of afflictions the text has a stray syllable རི, which yields no sense and is left untranslated. Three phrases are uncertain: the aversion-type "goes leading with his right side" (གཡས་ཕྱོགས་ཀྱིས་འགྲོ), the delusion-type sweeping "listlessly" (སྙོམས་པའི་ཚུལ་དུ, taken as from སྙོམ་ལས), and the delusion-type "never keeps to a single task without breaking off" (གཅིག་ཏུ་མི་བྱར་མི་རུང་བར་ཆད་པ་མེད་པ), whose construction is obscure. The whole typology closely parallels the account of temperaments in Buddhaghosa's Visuddhimagga and the Vimuttimagga. "Filled with men" takes ཕོས་གང་བ as "filled with males," which suits the remedy for attachment; the phrase is uncertain. In the second meditative concentration the text announces four limbs but names only three, omitting joy; the translation follows the text. In the fourth, the text reads རབ་ཏུ་དགའ་བ, "utter joy," twice, which is impossible for the fourth concentration; it is read as རབ་ཏུ་དག་པ, "utterly pure," the standard limbs being purity of equanimity and purity of mindfulness. The similes for the sense faculties follow the Abhidharmakośa tradition (flax flower, birch bark, probe, half moon); "the dark of the eye" renders the obscure "cloud of the eye," and "in layers" the uncertain ཐོག་ཐག་ཏུ. In the account of the desire realm and the first meditative concentration, the text simply juxtaposes "the five consciousnesses" and "the first meditative concentration's eye, ear, and body consciousnesses"; the translation supplies "in" to make the sense explicit. In the definition of the equanimity faculty by harm and benefit, the closing phrase དེ་ལྟ་བུ་མེད་པ is taken as "lacks such harm or benefit"; the construction is somewhat loose. In the first sentence the text calls the pleasant bodily feeling "the feeling of mental happiness," where the faculty of pleasure would be expected; the translation follows the text. The text names eight items for the "nine substances"; the ninth in the standard list is pleasure. The account of the path of meditation is given twice, the first time with wording that repeats the reasoning for the path of seeing; this looks like a scribal duplication, and both versions are translated as they stand. The exemplars of the five inclinations are identified as Vakkali (faith), Śroṇa (diligence), Mahānāman (mindfulness), Revata (calm abiding), and Śāriputra (wisdom); the identifications of ཐོས་ན་རྣ་བ as Śroṇa and མིང་ཆེན as Mahānāman are probable rather than certain. "The preparatory stage that is not incapable" renders ལམ་མི་ཐོབ་ཏུ་མི་རུང་བ, evidently the anāgamya preparatory concentration (usually མི་ལྕོགས་མེད).
