The phrase rendered "are not permanently different, one set from another" (མི་གཞན་དང་གཞན་དུ་རྟག་པ) is obscure. "On the level of lineage" reads རིགས་ཀྱི་ས་ལ་གནས་པའི་ཚེ for the written གནོད་པའི་ཚེ ("when harming"), which makes no sense here. In the account of the three turnings, the text assigns two faculties to the second turning and closes the third with only one name; the translation follows the text as it stands. The male and female faculties are introduced with ཐང་ཅིག ("for a moment"), which makes no sense here and is evidently a stray syllable; it is left untranslated. The list of four faculties each governing two things names a "feeling faculty" (ཚོར་བ) where the discussion that follows treats the life faculty (འཚོ་བ); this is probably a scribal slip, but it is translated as written. The text says rebirth is linked "by a mind free from attachment or by a mind with aversion"; one would expect "a mind with attachment," and this may be a slip, but it is translated as written. The definition of the male nature lists physique, disposition and conduct but then also explains voice; this is also translated as written. The three pure faculties are rendered "I shall come to know what is not yet known" (anājñātam ājñāsyāmīndriya), "final knowledge" (ājñendriya), and "one who has fully known" (ājñātāvīndriya). In the first list the text reads "the five beginning with life" (འཚོ་བ) as what brings about affliction, and in the second list "the feeling faculty" (ཚོར་བ) as continuance. Since the five that bring about affliction are the feeling faculties and continuance belongs to the life faculty, as the text itself says just before, the two similar words have evidently been confused by a copyist; the evident readings are translated. The syllable སྩོགས in "gather the accumulation for purification" is taken as a form of "gather." "The pleasure of ease" renders བག་ཡངས, here evidently the bliss of pliancy or tranquility (praśrabdhi) rather than pleasant feeling, as the text itself argues. In the definition of mindfulness the text reads མ་བརྗོད་པ ("not expressing"), which makes no sense here; the evident reading is མ་བརྗེད་པ, "not forgetting," as in the definition of mindfulness at the end of the piece, and this is translated. The syllables ཤེས་པ་ལ before "the first concentration" are unclear and are left untranslated. The text's sequence of what is abandoned in the second, third and fourth concentrations differs from the usual Abhidharma scheme; it is translated as it stands. Of the six headings of investigation, the text reads རིག་པ ("understanding") where the familiar list in the Śrāvakabhūmi has "reasoning" (yukti, རིགས་པ), and its first heading, "meaning," is then illustrated as investigating "for the sake of understanding"; the text is translated as written. Among the seven attentions, "the attention of destruction" (འཇིག་པ) stands where the usual list has the attention of investigation (mīmāṃsā); it is translated as written. In the third concentration the text reads ཚོལ་བ ("seeks") for "experiences pleasure with the body"; the evident reading ཚོར་བ, "experiences," as elsewhere in the passage, is translated. In the attention of resolve the text reads རག་ལས་པ ("dependent on"), which does not fit; the evident reading is རགས་པ, "coarse," which matches the rest of the sentence, and this is translated. In the attention that gathers delight, "arouses enthusiasm" leaves aside སེམས་ཅན ("sentient beings"), which seems to stand for སེམས ("mind"). The yogin's self-examination in the attention of destruction is compressed and somewhat unclear in the Tibetan; it is rendered by its evident sense. "The attention of destruction" (འཇིག་པ) stands where the usual list has the attention of investigation; it is kept as written, consistent with the previous piece. "Up to the sphere of nothingness" renders ཅུང་ཟད་མེད་མིན་སྐྱེ་མཆེད, which seems compressed; it may intend the whole series up to the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception, the summit of existence. "Life span" reads ཚེའི་འདུ་བྱེད for the written ཆེའི་འདུ་བྱེད; the lower levels are coarse because their abiding is less peaceful and their life span shorter. "Having resolved upon" reads མོས for the written སྨོས ("mentioned"), as the commentary itself next glosses "resolved" (མོས). "Without rivalry" glosses the Sanskrit asapatna, whose root sense is a co-wife, which is why the commentary invokes senior and junior wives. The list of four afflictions names five words; the last two, attachment and malice, form the single fourth item countered by equanimity, as the following lines show. "Brahma" in the two etymologies is taken in the sense of the pure or supreme (nirvāṇa, freedom from evil), not the god. "Without enmity and without rivalry" takes the Tibetan's "because it is without enmity, it is called without rivalry" as a list, which the following explanation of each term requires. In the explanation of "without enmity," the Tibetan reads ཉམས་པ ("declined, inferior") where the parallel clauses have མཉམ་པ ("equal"); "equal" is read, since inferior beings are treated separately under "without harm." "Step by step, in due order, on his own circle" renders the terse བདག་གི་རིམས་ཙམ་གྱིས་འཁོར; the sense is a widening sequence from one's own surroundings to the town and the realm. "Some say ... merit increases" uses the ordinary words for "merit increases"; it is not the name of the Puṇyaprasava heaven. The four paths are the four modes of practice (pratipad): painful with sluggish direct knowledge (duḥkhā dhandhābhijñā), painful with quick direct knowledge, pleasant with sluggish, and pleasant with quick. In the first listing the Tibetan has མངོན་པར་ཞེན་པ ("clinging") for མངོན་པར་ཤེས་པ ("direct knowledge"), which is read throughout; in one later mention of the pleasant path the word "path" has dropped out and is supplied. "The not-unable preparatory stage" renders མི་ཐོབ་ཏུ་མི་རུང་བ (anāgamya), the access stage of the first dhyāna. "One's grasp of meaning is slight" renders the compressed དོན་བསྟན་པ་ཆུང, literally "the showing of meaning is slight." "Practice," "close practice," "cultivation," and "making much of it" render བསྟེན་པ, བསྙེན་པ, བསྒོམ་པ and མང་དུ་བྱས་པ (cf. āsevita, bhāvita, bahulīkṛta). Where the Tibetan writes བསྟན་པ ("taught") for the first of these, and མངོན་དུ་བྱས་པ ("made manifest") for the last in the final list, the evident readings བསྟེན་པ and མང་དུ་བྱས་པ are followed. The four antidotes correspond to the Abhidharma set of repudiation (vidūṣaṇā), abandonment (prahāṇa), support (ādhāra), and distancing (dūrībhāva); "support" reads རྟེན for the written བསྟེན. "Visible life" renders ལྟ་བའི་ཆོས (dṛṣṭadharma), whose two parts the commentary glosses separately. In the introduction of the fourth path the Tibetan has "four paths" and "fourth pleasant path" where "the fourth path" is plainly meant. The opening gloss reads literally "the non-attainment of knowledge-and-vision and the attainment of vision"; the negative particle makes no sense in context and has been read as "the attainment of knowledge and the attainment of vision." Likewise "leads to the full attainment of wisdom" follows the evident reading of the quoted sūtra phrase. In the gloss on "rising and falling," the text reads "the body is obtained" (thob) on exhalation; this has been read as "raised" (mtho), which the contrast with "lowered" requires. "As it is situated" and "as it is disposed" render the familiar Abhidharma and sūtra pair corresponding to Pāli yathāṭhitaṃ yathāpaṇihitaṃ; the commentary takes "disposed" as the body's projection by karma. Kalala, arbuda and peśī are the first three stages of embryonic development. In the gloss on the illusion simile the Tibetan lists "illusory elephants, human elephants and nonhuman elephants"; the repetition of "elephant" is odd and may reflect a scribal error for a list of illusory elephants, humans and nonhumans, but it is translated as it stands. The object of the "cesspit" simile (bang ba) is uncertain; the gloss shows it is a receptacle full of filth. The "makeshift lodging" renders a phrase meaning a house resorted to as a temporary expedient, as the gloss explains. "A borrowed vessel" takes snod brnyas pa in the sense of a vessel borrowed from another, as the gloss requires. The word ཚ་བ here is the ordinary word "heat," paired with cold, not the name Tapana. The number of body hairs, literally "a koṭi, eighty-four hundred-thousands," is ambiguous in its arithmetic; it has been taken as eighty-four hundred thousand koṭis. "Pungent" here translates ཚ་བ, one of the six tastes, not the name Tapana. In the preceding piece the simile བང་བ was rendered "cesspit"; this passage shows it means a granary or storehouse. The lemma read here as "fills it completely" is written kun rdzob pa, evidently for kun rdzogs pa, which the commentary itself uses a few lines later. "The attention that completes the preparation" and "the attention that is the fruit of the completed preparation" are two of the seven attentions (manaskāra) of the Abhidharma and Yogācāra meditation manuals. The phrase "in five ways" (lngar) in the final sentence is unclear in its reference and is translated as it stands. "The miraculous eye" renders the Tibetan literally; in context it is the divine eye, the clairvoyant superknowledge (divyacakṣus) attained through the perception of light. In the alternative explanation, the third term is written "thoroughly faulty" in the Tibetan, an evident slip for "thoroughly practiced," the term given just before and just after; it is translated accordingly. The words for "in front" are twice written in non-standard spellings and are taken as "front"; likewise "meditating on it as equal to the front" reads the written "declined" as a slip for "equal," which the sense requires. The sūtra's four cultivations of meditative absorption are being commented on; "the three cultivations" is translated as written, though the context (the following "fourth") suggests "the third" may be meant. The "eight kinds of stain" of the fourth dhyāna are traditionally initial thought, sustained thought, pleasure, pain, mental joy, mental unhappiness, inhalation and exhalation. The seasonal reckoning of the simile is given as the text states it. The path linked to the five powers is called here "the path that leads to clear realization," whereas in the list given just before it was called "the path mingled with clear realization"; the two Tibetan words differ by one letter, and either may be the original. In the list of thirty-five bodies, the item rendered "the companion body" is glossed by the commentary itself as the body of childhood; the Tibetan word for "companion" may stand for a word meaning "child." "Negative tendencies" renders the Abhidharma term dauṣṭhulya, and "ease" its opposite, pliancy. Several evident slips in the Tibetan are read by sense: "outer characteristic" as "general characteristic," and "the karma of other sentient beings" as "the bodies of other sentient beings." The fifth alternative classes one's own decomposing body as "outer," as the text states. The quoted terms "clear," "fully aware," "possessing mindfulness," "free of attachment to the world" and "having abandoned mental unhappiness" gloss the sūtra's formula for the applications of mindfulness. "Based on renunciation" renders a word meaning literally "emergence," the counterpart of feeling "based on attachment" (Pāli nekkhammasita versus gehasita). The commentary explains "the mind free of attachment" (and likewise of aversion and delusion) as a mind in which the affliction is only latent, not active; this is its own interpretation and is translated as it stands. Two evident slips in the Tibetan are read by sense: in "not being free of the obscurations is the calm," the negative has dropped out and "the uncalm" is meant; and "entered the root dhyānas into the path" is read as "not entered the root dhyānas," since the two spellings differ by a single letter and the definition must be the opposite of the one before it. In the list of the four distortions, the first item reads "the impure" where "the pure" would be expected; the explanation that follows makes clear that grasping the impure as pure is meant. "The fear ... does not arise" reads the Tibetan འཇིག་པ ("destruction") as འཇིགས་པ ("fear"), which the sense plainly requires. The list of causes for the decline of moral discipline is announced as ten but the Tibetan gives eleven phrases; heedlessness and being in the grip of laziness are most likely meant as a single cause (as in the Śrāvakabhūmi's list), but both are translated as they stand. "Not leading to deliverance" renders འབྱུང་བར་མི་བྱེད་པ, understood as Sanskrit anairyāṇika. "Dharma and non-dharma" here are the merit and demerit that non-Buddhist schools attribute to a self. Where the Tibetan lists past things twice as the object of what arises with a hidden object, the second is evidently a slip for future things, as the preceding sentence shows, and is translated so. The second "generating aspiration" reads སྨོས་པ as མོས་པ, as in the parallel phrase just before it. In "To teach the second right abandonment," གཉིས་པོ ("both") is read as གཉིས་པ ("second"), since the quoted sūtra phrase is the second abandonment. "Trailing along like a donkey" alludes to the familiar simile of the donkey that follows a herd of cattle claiming to be one of them. Several evident slips in the Tibetan are read by sense: སེམས་གཉིས ("two minds") as གཉིད ("sleep"), beside torpor; མི་མཉམ་པ ("unequal") as མི་ཉམས་པ ("non-decline"), the standard term in the sūtra's list "remaining, non-forgetting, non-decline"; and in the summary of the dark side "generating unarisen evil" as "not generating," as the four abandonments require. In the second description of uplifting the slack mind the Tibetan has ཡང་དག་པར་སྤོང་བ ("right abandonment"); it is read as ཡང་དག་པར་སྨོན་པ ("rightly aspiring"), matching the identical description earlier. The quotation introducing the fourth right abandonment reads "virtuous phenomena that have not arisen" in the Tibetan, although its explanation concerns arisen ones; it is translated as it stands. The four kinds of abandonment correspond to the Sanskrit saṃvara-, prahāṇa-, bhāvanā- and anurakṣaṇā-prahāṇa. Some slips are read by sense: in the second definition of the meditative absorption of aspiration the Tibetan has "not generating unwholesome phenomena that have arisen," read as "that have not arisen"; and where the second fault is explained the Tibetan has "not forgetting the instructions," read as "forgetting," as the fault and its explanation require. The eight formations of abandonment match the list familiar from Maitreya's works (chanda, vyāyāma, śraddhā, praśrabdhi, smṛti, saṃprajanya, cetanā, upekṣā); accordingly བག་ཡངས is rendered "pliancy" (praśrabdhi) and སེམས་པ "intention" (cetanā). Two slips are read by sense: གདམས་ངག་བརྗོད་པ ("stating the instructions") as བརྗེད་པ ("forgetting the instructions"), the fault named earlier; and in the first quotation of the sūtra ཞེན་པ ("clinging") as ཞན་པ ("weak"), as the Tibetan itself reads when the phrase is repeated. The four kinds of vigilance correspond to the four mental acts of insight in the Yogācāra treatises (vicinoti, pravicinoti, parivitarkayati, parimīmāṃsate); they are translated here from the Tibetan as it stands. In the definition of the faculty of faith, the third object is written "the faculty of capability" and, a line later, "toward sinking" (nub pa); both are read here in light of the standard Abhidharma definition of faith as conviction, clarity and longing toward what exists, what has qualities, and what is possible (nus pa), and translated accordingly. In the passage on what faith governs, the Tibetan reads as if "faith" were listed among the things faith governs, with "diligence too" following; it is rendered as faith governing diligence, mindfulness, wisdom and samādhi, which the following sentence ("because those dharmas arise through the power of faith") and the graded sequence after it require. "Faith born of thorough understanding" renders kun tu rig pa'i dad pa, apparently the four kinds of verified faith (avetyaprasāda) that are limbs of stream-entry. "The knowledge of exhaustion and the knowledge of non-arising" renders zad la mi skye ba'i ye shes, taken as kṣayajñāna and anutpādajñāna, the two knowledges that mark arhatship. In "to set out in order the meaning of the five faculties," the Tibetan reads rim pa gnyis ("two stages"), which yields no clear sense here; it is read as rim pa bzhin ("in order"). The fourth kind of mindfulness ends with "is mindfulness" rather than parallel to the first three; it is rendered as it stands. Awakening is said to be set out as three kinds, but only two are named (the awakening of the śrāvakas and perfect, complete awakening); the pratyekabuddha's awakening, the usual third, is evidently understood or lost, and the passage is rendered as it stands. "Diligence that turns one toward the truths" renders bden pa la bzlog pa'i brtson 'grus, whose exact sense is not certain. "The powers that have arisen as one portion of certain antidotes" renders a phrase whose construction is compressed. The awakening factor rendered "pliancy" (praśrabdhi) is written in three ways in this passage (shin tu sbyangs pa, shin tu byang ba, and once bag yangs); all are taken as the same factor. In the clause on pliancy, the phrase rendered "what hinders them in engaging in their own activities is abandoned" is compressed in the Tibetan, which reads literally "engaging in its own activity is abandoned"; the sense of removed hindrance is supplied from context. In the list of the nature of the factors of awakening, samādhi is not named, so only six are listed; this is rendered as it stands. In the list of the six causes of mindfulness, the first item is written "seeing mindfulness" (dran pa mthong ba); the explanation that follows shows it should read "seeing something similar" ('dra ba mthong ba), and it is translated so. In the explanation of the order of the limbs, "as one thinks, so one utters words" reads rtog pa ("thinking") for the written rtag pa ("permanent"), which makes no sense here. The fruit named in the example of association is the mango (Tibetan a mra). The six limbs and the five aspects (object, nature, companions, cultivation, result) closely follow the treatment of the path limbs in Asaṅga's Abhidharmasamuccaya; "the limb of ascertainment" renders yongs su gcod pa (pariccheda). The four qualifiers of cultivation correspond to the familiar formula viveka-, virāga-, nirodha-niśrita and vyavasarga-pariṇata. The text assigns right effort to the aggregate of wisdom, as written. As the text stands, the procedure described under "counting one by one" (counting "one" on the in-breath and "two" on the out-breath up to ten) is concluded with the words "this is counting two as one"; it has been translated as written. Kalala and arbuda (Tibetan nur nur po and mer mer po) are the first two stages of embryonic development. The piece ends in mid-sentence; the next piece completes the description of the special counting. In the account of the truth of origin, the text as transmitted reads srid pa ("existence"), but the next sentence refers back to "that craving (sred pa) which produces suffering," and the truth of origin is craving; the evident reading sred pa, "craving," has been translated. In the long in-breath, the text reads thung du ("short"), which contradicts the context of the long breath; the similar-looking thur du, "downward," has been translated. In the definition of the short in-breath, the text as written concludes "this is called attending to the short out-breath"; this has been kept as transmitted, though the sense requires the short in-breath. In the fifth item the text once reads lus kyi 'du shes ("bodily perception") where every parallel phrase has lus kyi 'du byed ("bodily formation"); the evident reading "bodily formation" has been translated. "Calmed" renders shin tu sbyangs pa (praśrabdhi), elsewhere translated as "pliancy"; the alternative explanation draws on that sense. "The not-incapable stage" renders mi thob tu mi rung ba (and, in its second occurrence, the evidently miswritten mi thos su mi rung ba), the Tibetan equivalent of the term anāgamya, the stage preparatory to the first dhyāna; the second occurrence has been read in line with the first. "The three elements" (khams gsum) here are the elements of dispassion, cessation, and relinquishment, not the three realms of existence. The thirteenth item is given in the text only in its in-breath form. The phrase rendered "being thus free of confusion when breathing out and breathing in" reads in the Tibetan as given "the confusion that has thus arisen"; this makes no sense in context, and a dropped negative (reading "non-confusion," Skt. asaṃmoha) seems evident. The commentary's gloss on "Blessed One" depends on the Tibetan rendering bcom ldan 'das, whose first element means "vanquished," hence "the Vanquisher." "Endowed with knowledge and conduct" (vidyācaraṇasampanna) is in Tibetan literally "endowed with knowledge and feet," which underlies the gloss that it shows "that by which he went." The "four kinds of faith born of understanding" render avetyaprasāda. The two glosses on Sugata play on the Sanskrit su-, read as "thoroughly, never to return" (like a fever completely cured) and as "well, completely" (like a vase filled to the brim). In the last sentence, "fruits of the ascetic life" renders dge sbyong gi 'bras bu (śrāmaṇyaphala), the four fruits from stream-entry to arhatship. The Tibetan announces "ten respects" and then introduces the list with "the four are," though ten items follow; "four" appears to be a slip, and "ten" is translated. In "timely," the Tibetan reads "the offerings ('bul ba) to be brought to maturity," which does not fit; the similar-looking gdul ba, "those to be trained," is evidently meant, and the rendering "those to be brought to maturity" follows that sense. In the list of the tīrthikas' failings, the text reads "exhaustion of craving" (sred pa zad pa) where the corresponding epithet above read "exhaustion of becoming" (srid pa zad pa); the two differ by one vowel, and both make sense, so the reading is kept as written. The quoted line rendered "O, it is that which is to be won" is obscure in the Tibetan (kye des mnyes par bya ba yin no) and the rendering is tentative. "Right way" translates rigs pa (Skt. nyāya), and "the Dharma to be pursued" translates gnyer ba'i chos (Skt. anudharma). "Perfect in the vision of liberation" follows the evident sense of the sūtra epithet (vimuktijñānadarśana); the Tibetan as given reads "teacher of liberation" (rnam par grol ba'i ston pa), apparently a slip for "vision" (mthong ba). The second gloss on arhat plays on the Sanskrit sense "worthy." The commentary's counts of the afflictions abandoned at each stage are given here as the text states them. The text writes rtogs pa ("realization") in "thoughts of sensual desire and thoughts of aversion"; the sense requires rtog pa ("thought," Skt. vitarka), and it is translated so. "Uninterrupted" renders ma bral ba, apparently corresponding to the quality later listed as ma ral ba ("untorn"). "Untainted" renders ma god pa, which in this list of the qualities of moral discipline seems to correspond to Skt. aparāmṛṣṭa ("not clung to"), as the commentary's gloss about wrong views and aspirations suggests. "Ground" and "not a ground" render gnas and gnas ma yin pa (Skt. sthāna, asthāna), which the commentary glosses first as cause and non-cause and then as possible and impossible. The passage as it stands repeats the explanations of the second and third kinds of action (unpleasant now but happy later; unpleasant now and painful later) a second time, with only a slight wording difference in the second occurrence ("through the influence of bad companions"); both occurrences are translated as they stand. "The five courses of rebirth" renders lam rgyud lnga, understood as the five destinies (pañcagati). "Divine eye" renders 'phrul gyi mig (Skt. divyacakṣus); the commentary explains "divine" as referring to the meditative concentrations. The Tibetan dan rtags, one of the three "aspects," is an unusual word; it is rendered "labels" following the commentary's own gloss, which explains it as recollecting the names in each life. Where the sūtra speaks of the "purified" (rnam par dag pa) divine eye, the commentary as transmitted glosses the word as "liberated" (rnam par thar pa); it is translated as it stands, though "purified" may have been intended. The final sentence names this ninth power "the power of recollecting other lives" as the Tibetan reads, although its content is the knowledge of beings' death and rebirth. Three evident copyists' slips are read as intended: ngang gi mtshan nyid as rang gi mtshan nyid ("particular characteristic"), nyen thos as nyan thos ("śrāvakas"), and skyob ("protect") as skyo ba ("disenchantment"), which pairs naturally with "faith." "Delivered" renders bsgral ba here in its ordinary sense of ferrying beings across, not the wrathful ritual sense. "The Mighty One" renders mthu che ba, apparently a deity revered by non-Buddhist teachers (possibly Maheśvara); it is translated by meaning because the identification is not certain. Two evident slips are read as intended: thar zug as ther zug ("eternalism"), and dbyung ba ma yin pa as 'byung ba ma yin pa ("not leading to deliverance"), matching the commentary's own explanation. "Delivers" and "door of deliverance" render sgrol ba and bsgral ba in their ordinary sense of ferrying beings across, not the wrathful ritual sense. "The five courses of rebirth" renders ལམ་རྒྱུད་ལྔ, which I take as the five destinies (gati); the phrase is terse and the reading is not certain. "In this very life" renders མཐོང་བའི་ཆོས་ཉིད་ལ, the standard rendering of Sanskrit dṛṣṭe dharme, so ཆོས་ཉིད here is not dharmatā. In the lion's-roar passage the Tibetan literally reads "seeing the sign of non-awakening and seeing that it does not stand"; I have taken this as one thought, that he examines the sign the challengers allege and finds it cannot be sustained, in line with the commentary's later "not seeing any sign for disputing." "The place of the bull" renders ཁྱུ་མཆོག་གི་གནས, Sanskrit ārṣabhaṃ sthānam, the station of the herd's leader. The lemmas quoted are abbreviated references to the sūtra's formula for the four fearlessnesses and are rendered as they stand. The text as transmitted assigns knowledge of the eighty-four thousand kinds of conduct and the eighty-four thousand heaps of Dharma to the analytical knowledge of meaning, after already treating that knowledge, and its closing sentence pairs only the analytical knowledge of language with unobstructed wisdom, leaving unobstructed words unassigned; both are translated as they stand. "Five strands of sense pleasure" renders འདོད་པ་ལྔའི་ཡོན་ཏན (kāmaguṇa). The words བར་ཆད་ཀྱི་ཆོས་གསུངས་པ་ནས་བྱ་བས appear to stand for the usual ཞེས་བྱ་བས and are rendered accordingly. Here དབང་པོ means "faculties" (indriya), not the god Indra. In the verse, གླང་པོ is the "great elephant" (nāga), an epithet of the Buddha, as in the Pāli parallel "gacchaṃ samāhito nāgo." Two readings are taken as copyists' slips: ཞེས་བྱ་བའི་སྒྲིབ་པ is read as ཤེས་བྱའི་སྒྲིབ་པ, "obscuration of the knowable," as the next clause requires; ལས་འདྲེན་མ is read as ལས་འདྲེས་མ, "mixed action." The sentences on the unattached and unobstructed wisdom are elliptical in the Tibetan, and "whereas the wisdom that does" supplies the evident contrast with the Tathāgata. "Teaching of the wisdom of liberation" follows the Tibetan སྟོན་པ, where the usual list has "the vision of the wisdom of liberation." "With regard to the present" reads དེ་ལྟར་བྱུང་བ as ད་ལྟར་བྱུང་བ, as the parallel in the preceding passage requires. In "very steadfast in his vows," the first occurrence has བསྟན་པས, read as བརྟན་པས, which the text itself uses in the gloss. "Put other beings at ease" renders the unclear expression སྦམ་བཀོང་བ; the meaning given follows the commentary's own gloss, "not causing fright or fear." "Robes" reads ཆོས་དང་ as ཆོས་གོས, since the lemma concerns giving garments. In the gloss on protection by the Dharma, མཉེན་པ is read as མཉན་པ, "listening," as the following sentences confirm. Only some of the thirty-two marks are glossed here, and they are rendered as the text gives them. The ladder of strength gives Indian terms in Tibetan transliteration, romanized here as spelled; they evidently correspond to the series known from the Abhidharmakośa (mahānagna, praskandin, varāṅga, cāṇūra), with "mighty champion" (མཐུ་བོ་ཆེ) standing at the top of the scale, where that series has nārāyaṇa. "Bull elephant" renders བན་གླང, which could also mean bull. "The seven high" (བདུན་མཐོ་བ) is translated as written, though the gloss suggests the reading may be corrupt. The text's definitions of the miracles of declaration and instruction are the reverse of the usual Abhidharma assignment, in which reading others' thoughts is the miracle of declaration (ādeśanā); they are rendered as given. "The miracle of teaching" here evidently refers to the miracle of instruction (anuśāsanī-prātihārya), which the Abhidharma tradition holds to be the supreme of the three miracles, since it alone leads to liberation. "Without appropriation" renders ལེན་པ་མེད་པ (Skt. anupādāya), which the commentary itself glosses as no longer taking up a new existence. The phrase ཕུང་པོ་གཅིག་ཏུ་མཚམས་སྦྱར་བ reads literally "linking the aggregates into one"; it is rendered in light of the commentary's own gloss "linking from one life to another."
